We have assumed that we are speaking to men who are already well versed in the practice of gardening, and for this reason we have not returned to the first principles of the art, such as, for example, the making up of beds and the construction of frames, greenhouses, etc. These sorts of things are, moreover, easy to understand. Besides, these sorts of things can be found in all gardening books. The art of forcing plants to give their produce in all seasons of the year is still far from attaining the persection of which we think it is capable; may our work excite the emulation of growers, and the goal we have set ourselves, that of being useful, will seem very close to being achieved. 2 Coogle --- - T4 PRIME GARDENER. A ABRICOTIER (Prunus armeniaca by Linnaeus; armeniaca vulgaris by modern botanists); a third-growth tree, native to Armenia, from where, we are told, it was transported to Rome and from there to the rest of Europe. The apricot tree, too well known to be described here, offers a fairly large number of varieties. We shall mention the best and most widespread. 1. ABRICOTIX, or Early Apricot. In espaliers, it ripens at the end of June, and in July if grown in the open air. Small, rounded, yellow or reddish on the sunny side. Its flesh is musky and of rather mediocre quality, tending towards yellowish. Its kernel is bitter. This variety reproduces very well from stones, so it doesn't need to be grafted, and in this case the tree lasts a very long time. As its greatest merit lies in its earliness, it is customary to raise it in espaliers with warm exposure. 2. WHITE ABRICOT. It ripens a little later than the previous variety, i.e. at the beginning of July; it is about the same size, with whiter skin and flesh; the latter has a slight peach flavor, which makes it a little more pleasant. Its kernel is bitter. It does well in the open air, but prefers a warm espalier position. 3. ARRicor ANGOUMOIS. Ripens around July 15. It is smaller and usually more elongated than the previous variety. Its color is amber-yellow, more or less reddish and speckled with brown, depending on whether it has been more or less exposed to the sun during its development. The flesh is yellow to reddish, very aromatic, melting and pleasant, but a little acidic. Its kernel is very good and often double. 4. ABRiCOr COMMUR. Ripens at the same time as the previous variety. It is fairly large, reddish-yellow in color, and may be scabby on the light side, or even cracked if the year has been rainy. Its flesh is melting and quite good when grown in the open air, but becomes mushy if ripening is too advanced. Amaude amere. A vigorously vegetating tree, but rapidly thinning out. It is never espaliered, and as it reproduces identically from stones, it is often not grafted; in this case, it is much less robust, which is common in apricot trees. 5. HOLLAND ABRICOT, or filbert almond. Ripens at the end of July. Its fruit is small, reddish, yellowish, with a reddish-brown spot on the light side; its flesh is juicy, vinous, melting, quite pleasant, and its sweet almond has the taste of filbert, from which comes one of the names it bears in the trade. It does better in espaliers than in the open air. 6. ABRICOT DE PROVENCE. Ripens at the end of July. It is small, slightly compressed on the sides, yellowish, reddish on the light side; its flesh is juicy, quite full-bodied, but still a little dry in certain warm years, sweet, vinous. The stone is rough and the tart sweet. 7. ABRICOT DE PORTUGAL, or male apricot. Ripens around August 15. The flesh is yellow, melting, fragrant, with a very pleasant flavor. The tree is delicate and, in our climate, requires a warm espalier. 8. ABRICOT ALBERGE. Ripens around August 15. Its flesh is firm, fragrant, good and excellent for jam. Sweet almond. The tree grows quite tall, is very vigorous, and bears a large quantity of fruit, which sometimes degenerates. It reproduces its variety identically by sowing. There are three sub-varieties: Abricot de Montgamet, ripening a little later, larger, less round, with more melting flesh and the same perfume. Sweet almond. Abricot de Tours, ripening at the same time, larger, more fragrant, better quality. Amande douce. Apricot aveline, differing only slightly from Montgamet, from which it has the same quality. Sweet almond. 9. ABRICOT PÊCHE, or Nancy apricot. Ripens around August 15th; very large, compressed, more or less brownish-reddish-yellow, especially in high winds, and often becomes scabby on the light side if the season is rainy. The flesh is reddish, very melting, with a distinctive and very pleasant fragrance. A distinctive feature of this fruit is the hole in the stone through which a pin can easily be inserted. The tree is of average vigor, and its foliage is usually wilted. It has given rise to a large number of sub-varieties, because it always reproduces more or less well by sowing. 10. ABRICOT ROTAL. Ripens around mid-August. Slightly smaller than the previous variety, more rounded, yellowish, lightly colored, with melted, yellow, fragrant flesh, better than that of the peach apricot. The tree is very vigorous; it is remarkable for the size of its leaves and the length of their petiole. 11. AbRicor MUSCn. ripens at the end of July. It is almost round, with very thin, yellow skin, revealing the stone through the flesh, which is very delicate, fragrant and excellent. This variety requires a very warm espalier. 12. ApRIcor caos MUSCH. It ripens at the end of July. Its flesh, which does not adhere to the stone, is melting and fragrant; its kernel is sweet; the tree is more vigorous than the previous one. 13. ABRICOT NOIR Ou DU rAPX. (Prunus dasicarpa, by Willdenow.) Round, small, dark wine-red; flesh red and clinging to the pit; bland, very poor quality, except in extremely hot years. I have seen other new varieties obtained by Mr. Noiseite, such as the Apricot Vinux, the Apricot de Paris, etc., etc., but they are not as widespread as they should be. PRIMEUR. Apricot trees are only very rarely forced to grow in Paris, either because their fruit is not valued as highly here as it is in the north, since we have an abundance of them in season; or because we suspect difficulties in this operation which do not exist; or, finally, because this carelessness too often results in France in an uncertain sale which sometimes cannot compensate for the cost of cultivation. The apricot tree, so well naturalized in France, has not yet become so in northern Europe above the fiftieth degree of latitude, and particularly in Russia. It's no more than sixty years since the apricots eaten in St. Petersburg were brought there from Paris, and although care was taken to pack them a little before they were ripe and with meticulous care, it's easy to judge their condition after such a long journey. tried growing apricots in greenhouses. He succeeded perfectly, made his fortune, and only then stopped making a secret of his process. Until then, he convinced those who came to buy them from him at an exorbitant price that he was bringing them from Paris. Since then, Russia has harvested enough of these fruits to satisfy the consumption of wealthy people. When it comes to forcing an apricot tree, the variety is not indifferent; those that lend themselves most easily to this kind of cultivation are: the apricotin, the petit musch, the common apricot and its varieties named blanc-groszeespèce, commun hatif and blanc de Provence; the angoumois hatif, and some sub-varieties of the apricot-pêche. Nevertheless, the others could succeed in certain climates, given proper care (1). The tree must be young, vigorous, absolutely gum-free, well furnished with wood so as not to occupy too much space unnecessarily, and provided with a quantity of good fruiting eyes. Without this condition, the flower appears, the fruit sets, but often deteriorates and falls for lack of sap, as the leaves alone have the ability to attract it and concentrate it on the young fruit. A tree destined to be forced must be prepared for this as early as spring, i.e. at the time of the first disbudding. All branches should be left to grow, so that the fruit can ripen in the following order: 1. apricotin, 2. angoumois hatif, 3. petit musch, 4. commun bitif, 5. blanc-grosse-espèce, 6. apricot de Provence, q. apricot-pêche and its varieties. In very cold climates, the latter and its sub-varieties are preferable to the others, although they are less likely to produce fruit, and we extract those that are only likely to bear fruit. We act in the same way and according to the same principles during the second disbudding. As soon as the sap is suspended, spread a layer of three to four fingers of compost over the entire surface of the soil to be covered by the greenhouse, mixing it with the soil by ploughing with a fork, the depth of which will always be proportionate to the more or less superficial arrangement of the roots. It is essential that the fertilizer is fully consumed, because it will immediately provide abundant nourishment for the vegetation, and because if it were still susceptible to fermentation, it would attract and fix moisture to the roots, causing them to mildew or give rise to the deadly disease known as powdery mildew. In autumn, shortly after the leaves have fallen, if you want very early fruit, or more usually in January, you prune short to obtain more beautiful fruit, especially if you want to preserve the trees; in the opposite case, you prune long, and the fruit is more numerous, but of lesser quality. If, as a result of this operation, gummy ooze forms, either on the large limbs or on the cut branches, this inconvenience is remedied by removing the gum or ulcerated part with a scraper, and by applying a little grafting wax to cover the wounds when necessary. The apricot tree flowers and ripens fruit in four months, when its vegetation follows its normal course. Forcing could hasten the process, but to avoid heat stroke, it's much better to delay vegetation a little, and allow four and a half months from the time you start forcing to the time you obtain results. To obtain certain results, it takes about a month to bring a tree into flower. This means that if you want apricots in April, you have to start heating in January. But an intelligent gardener can try to force it on much earlier, and success will crown his care in mild winters, especially in those where the sun often appears. An early season gardener can therefore, by employing the method generally in use, provide fruit from the beginning of April right through to the plein-terre season. He can extend the latter season until the end of September, by planting espaliers in the north, in cool soil and exposure, and by choosing late varieties, such as Portuguese apricots, peaches, royal, alberge and others. There are several ways of forcing apricots. We'll describe them all in detail, because we'll be giving here the general notions that can be applied to all other stone trees, and we'll often refer the reader to them. 1. forcing in crates and greenhouses. This method is scarcely used in regions where the cold does not fall below eighteen degrees, a temperature below which apricot trees cannot survive in the open ground; but it is widely used in Sweden and Russia. The trees are grown in pots or crates, just as oraugers are grown here, given the same care, and wintered in greenhouses. To fortify them, we place them in heated tarpaulins, and treat them in the same way as those we force into flying greenhouses. It would therefore be pointless to give a breakdown of the details that follow. 2. Forcing in a flying greenhouse and in an espalier against a wall. After selecting the tree to be heated and preparing it for this operation by pruning and disbudding, place the flying greenhouse as follows. This greenhouse (fig. 11.) consists of a light framework, which can be dismantled at will, and is made up of cross-members to support glass panels of varying lengths, depending on the height of the trees to be forced, but usually not exceeding eight or nine feet. If the panels are long enough to exceed the height of the trees by only a few inches, they can be placed on the ground with their tops against the wall, but more commonly they are supported on a small plank balustrade, eighteen inches to two feet high. In this case, manure stoves are set up along this support during frosts. Sometimes, to make greater use of the flying greenhouse, it is placed on a bed for early fruit and vegetables, as we're about to say, but it should be noted that this method has the disadvantage of making the trees much harder to defend against humidity. Here's how it's done. At two feet from the wall, a warm layer is raised and given a width of four feet; it must be given just the right height so that it can ferment and emit heat. When established according to ordinary principles, solid stakes are driven one foot from the outer edge, through the entire thickness of the layer and into the ground. These piles should be spaced so that each has a purlin to support, and their tops should be flush with the bed. A long cross-beam is nailed solidly to these stakes, to support the panels and hold them in place, so that they don't sink with the layer as it sinks due to the consumption of manure. Usually, this crosspiece consists of a piece of oak two or three inches wide and twelve to eighteen lines thick, but it would be better to make it from a solid plank one foot wide, covering the outside of the bed and sloping slightly to keep rainwater away from the panels. At other times, the layer is only three feet thick, and the stakes supporting the crosspieces are planted against them and on the outside. This is even the most common method, although it doesn't have the same advantages as the previous one. Here's why: when it comes to heating the layer, the stoves have to be placed outside, and can only be covered with litter, which is not enough to prevent the water from the panels from getting in between them and the layer, and cooling them both. In all cases, i.e. whether there is a bed or not, when the crosspieces are mounted, the glazed panels are placed on top, and their length must be proportionate to the height of the espalier. However, the most convenient are never more than eight feet long. They are secured at the bottom with small iron pegs set into the crosspiece, and at the top with a false coping made of plaster, wood or lime mortar. It's essential that air and cold can't penetrate through any of the joints, so we seal them with moss. On the sides of the greenhouse, which are sealed with perfectly joined planks, there is a door on the east side, and a window on the west side. I assume that the espalier faces south, because if it faced either east or west, the door would have to face south. The door would have to be glazed to make it easier to see what's going on inside, which is more difficult than through the glass of the panels. However, this is not the case. Between the door and the bed, if there is one, we build a brick furnace, or place a stove, but with the extreme precaution of isolating it from the bed, for fear of accident. We must light from outside, and the pipe, which can be made of sheet metal or cast iron, will be much better in the ground. It should cross the entire length of the greenhouse, on the surface of the ground; then, when it reaches the end, it bends upwards, runs along one side of the window, and exits at the top near the last panel. To avoid obstructing the passageway, which has only two feet, if there's a layer, we let him run along it, but never less so than by isolating him sufficiently so that we don't have to worry about him setting fire to it. To avoid this accident, and for other reasons, here's how to arrange the layer inside. Five-foot stakes will be driven against the edge, and these stakes will be used to hold planks lying against each other and encasing the layer; they can be held in place by means of a few nails. In this way, the inside of the greenhouse can be kept clean, there's no danger of fire, and when the layer sags, it can't widen. Finally, it can be loaded with tan, or even potting soil, to force flowers, strawberries, small vegetables, etc. We assume that all these preparations are complete. Now it's time to place thermometers at intervals, to check the temperature. We immediately start building a fire to warm up the greenhouse. It is essential not to let any smoke into the greenhouse, as it is fatal not only for the apricot tree, but for all the plants exposed to it. The temperature must not be raised very high all at once, as too sudden a transition will cause the tree to sprout flowing, etiolated buds, incapable of producing anything, or even preserving themselves; it may also result in fruit buds aborting and developing into wood. For the first eight days, simply set the Réaumur thermometer at between eight and ten degrees, then gradually raise the temperature each day, until it reaches between ten and twelve; maintain this temperature until the bud is very swollen and ready to bloom. In the meantime, there are a number of essential treatments we should mention. At least twice a day, the greenhouse should be visited and entered carefully, i.e. with the door open as little as possible, so as not to let the cold in. If an icy wind is blowing in the direction of the door, sew a heavy canvas curtain or mat over it, and let it fall behind you before opening and entering. If the greenhouse were considerable, and the produce capable of compensating for expenses, it would be even better to build a sort of vestibule out of planks, with a first door, which you would close behind you before opening the door. The pathway is swept and cleaned to remove any matter that might retain humidity. The espalier is carefully peeled of any dead leaves that may have remained, and which would certainly lead to contagious mould; the insects, kermes and aphids, that have hatched as a result of the heat, are carefully destroyed; and lastly, any means that circumstances may require are employed to chase away the humidity. The surest way to do this is to provide air, but this requires great precautions, even if the outside temperature is only a few degrees below that inside. If we were to open without further care, the plant's insensitive transpiration would be completely suppressed, leading to sudden death or defoliation. If the damage did not go that far, the tree would remain languid, and some or all of the fruit would abort. If the outside temperature in the shade is below five degrees Celsius, don't let any air in; if it's between five and eight degrees Celsius, let some in, but don't open the door for as long as necessary; finally, if the temperature is between eight and fifteen degrees Celsius, you can let some air in by opening the door and window, but before doing so, prepare the plants so they won't be inconvenienced. This preparation consists in turning off the furnace until the inside thermometers show that the inside and outside temperatures are more or less in equilibrium, 2 although the greenhouse temperature is still a few degrees higher. Let's return to our espalier, which we left when the flower buds were ready to develop, with the greenhouse atmozphere heated to between ten and fifteen degrees. If the gems are seen to swell much more rapidly than they usually do, it is likely that the tree has been pushed too fast. In this case, the organs of fertilization have not had time to develop fully, so they remain languid, the anthers contain little pollen and, still imperfect, the non-irritable stigma cannot be stimulated by the prolific liquor, resulting in fruit abortion. To overcome this major drawback, the thermometer is lowered by a few degrees, air is given and the sap is suspended for a few days; during this time, the fruiting bodies perfect themselves and acquire all the energy necessary for the act of fertilization. This brings us to the critical moment of flowering. If my advice has been followed to date, and the greenhouse has been heated evenly throughout, almost all the flowers will appear at once, and flowering will only last a few days. But it's during this interval that you need to redouble your care and precautions. Keep the temperature strictly between ten and twelve degrees, no more and no less. If the heat is allowed to drop, abortion will occur through weakness; if it is increased, there will be what is known as erosion and brülure, i.e. the drying out of the authenticae and stigmata. These two pitfalls are easily avoided by monitoring day and night for some time, but a third, humidity, is even more fatal. To avoid this, air is given several times a day, from eleven o'clock in the morning until three o'clock in the afternoon, and more or less loug-time each time, depending on the outside temperature. Old 3 1 13 2 ABR 13 rags are used to wipe off any drops of water that stick to the glass panels. Finally, we provide the necessary watering for the strawberry plants or other plants we grow in the greenhouse, either on the bed if there is one, or in pots placed on the shelves we have arranged for this purpose, and we give them only what is strictly essential. Smoke is particularly dangerous during the flowering period, so great care must be taken to avoid letting it get inside the greenhouse, otherwise all the flowers will abort. As soon as they have reached a third of their size, we increase the heat, but gradually, so that it takes at least eight days to raise the thermometer to twelve or fifteen degrees and keep it there. During the course of this education, the tree should be given the care that circumstances dictate. For example, unnecessary branches should be cut back, branches should be trellised as they lengthen; branches should be disbudded and removed if they are crumpled, etiolated or mouldy. When the tree reaches maturity, remove any leaves that may be hiding the fruit, to let it enjoy the sun's rays. When it's very cold, place good mats over the panels at night, and remove them whenever necessary. Finally, we'll take the most scrupulous care to maintain the interior temperature constantly at the same thermometer reading. If there's a layer, we'll probe it from time to time, and when we see that it's starting to cool, we'll place thick stoves of hot manure all along the outside. You can even pull a good portion of the old manure from the outside, and replace it with new manure from the stable. 3. Forcing into espalier frames. In the north of France, or even around Paris, when you don't have a large expanse of wall for espaliers, you can use M. Noisette's espalier à chassis. Here's how it works: in a direction from east to west, plant two rows of apricot trees, ten or twelve feet apart in each row, with the rows five feet apart. Between the two lines, behind each tree, is a wooden trellis against which each espalier is trellised. These trellis frames are six feet high, plus or minus, and iucliated dedaus between the two planting lines, so that the upper end of those in the right-hand line bears on the top of those in the left-hand league, thus A. When the trees trellised on the frames or trellises represented by the sides of this reversed A, have reached the top, their highest branches touch each other, and their growth is directed in such a way as to force them to die out to the right and left without going any higher. In addition, they are bent for pruning like ordinary espaliers. They can be trained as espaliers à la Moutreuil, à la Française, or en palmette, and this last form is the most advantageous in this case, because the tree occupies half the surface area. For heating purposes, trees are prepared and pruned as described above. The glazed paunches are installed, or a furnace is set up, with an earthen pipe running the length of the espalier, passing through the middle of the canopy formed by the coppice of the two lines of trees. Heat it up, taking care to keep it absolutely warm, as we've said, and take the same care as we've taught you. A warm layer can also be used. To do this, dig a pit two and a half feet wide and ten inches deep under the vault, and fill it with hot manure. On top of this manure, we build a hot bed three and a half feet wide at the nozzle, two and a half feet wide at the top, and we raise it three feet; to preserve its heat, we redo it every two weeks. A furnace is set up as above, but the pipe is passed over the bed, or along one of the sides. This method has been used in the past, but offers no advantage; often, in spite of every precaution, the dampness of the manure attaches itself to the twigs and leaves of the trees, causing them to mildew. In addition to this, only a very small part of the bed can be harvested, because with the exception of mushrooms, asparagus, white chicory and a few other vegetables that can only be eaten blanched, the other vegetables cannot be grown there. In northern climates, where the espalier is used only to obtain fruit in the regular season, and where it would not ripen without this precaution, it is sufficient to place nine- to ten-foot panels in the autumn, leaning them against each other in a vault (the glass must be at least six inches from the surface of the foliage). The joints of the panels are hermetically sealed with moss, so that air and cold cannot penetrate inside; along the panels, at their foot, a bed of hot manure two feet high and two or three wide is placed, and everything stops there. Air is supplied whenever the temperature permits, and the panels are removed entirely when the season is warm enough to allow the fruit to grow and ripen. In this way, apricot trees blossom and set fruit long before the outside trees begin to vegetate, and they have time to improve over the course of the summer. This method can also be used for espaliers against a wall. 4°. Forcing in greenhouses and in the open. For this type of cultivation, you need a warm greenhouse that is entirely glazed, or at least glazed on three sides and above, as described in the article on vines. Apricot trees are grown in crates, and when they are strong enough to produce fruit soon, they are removed with the root ball and transplanted into the greenhouse, in the open ground. If you wish, you can grow them as bushes, but the best way is to espalier them. The care required is absolutely the same as for the flying greenhouse. However, it should be remembered that the more light the trees receive, the easier it is for the fruit to set. So place them as close as possible to the glass. We won't go into detail here on how to build and heat the greenhouse, how to raise the layers, etc. For all these articles, we refer you to the "Greenhouse" section. For all these articles, we refer the reader to general works on horticulture. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. The apricot tree does well in all soils, provided they are not too wet; however, it prefers light, sandy and deep soils, especially when it is free-standing or grafted onto an almond tree, as in this case its roots are taprooted. It can be propagated from seed or by grafting. If you want to sow it - and this is the best thing to do in areas where it doesn't seem to do very well - choose varieties that reproduce identically from the kernel, such as early apricot, common apricot, alberge, peach, etc. Preference is given to the kernels that are the most resistant to disease. Preference is given to the largest kernels, coming from the most beautiful fruits; they are immediately stratified, i.e. placed in a crate, alternating a bed of kernels and a bed of damp sand, and then placed in a frost-free cellar over winter. In October, or better still in spring, we take them out of the sand, and, after having well loosened and ploughed the soil, we plant them two inches deep, in place, or in a nursery eighteen inches or two feet apart; and, if we have planted in autumn, we cover the seedlings with a good thickness of litter or dry leaves, in order to protect their delicate sprouts, already developed to a certain extent, from the effects of frosts. If you want a very vigorous tree, you must sow it in place; if you don't, you must replant it in its second year, and if the soil is deep, you must be careful to follow the authors' advice and cut the pivot. The good gardener falls into this error by recommending that the pivot be cut from trees that are to be trained in espaliers. He gives the pretext that the less vigorous growth is easier to manage. Far from sharing this opinion, I believe that a tree can never be vigorous enough, when it is managed by an intelligent gardener who knows his trade. In fact, a tree grown from noyan behaves and grows in exactly the same way as one that has been grafted. Its fruit is usually a little smaller, but just as good, and the tree is more difficult to gum. Apricot trees are never grafted onto themselves, as this method produces trees that are languid and much more prone to gum than others. All varieties do very well on damanoir, cerisette and Saint-Julien plums, but not on almonds; the crests of peach, angoumois and alberge apricots are prone to peeling off when placed on this species. Grafting is usually done with a dormant-eye escutcheon. I would point out that the plants on which it is placed must always come from seed and never from offshoots. In hot, dry soils, apricot trees are best planted facing east; in cold, damp soils, they are best planted facing south. Open-air varieties produce smaller fruit, but in greater quantities and with a more fragrant taste. Three years after planting, this tree starts to bear fruit, and it lasts because of the quality of the soil. Its flowers appear from the first days of spring, but are liable to be destroyed by late frosts, unless care is taken to shelter them from the slightest danger with mats or canvas. When exposed to the full force of the wind, apricot trees will rapidly thin out towards the base of the branches if they are not pruned, at least during the first four or five years. Pruning consists of hollowing out the inside of the tree's head to allow easy access to air and light, cutting off all misplaced or useless branches, and draining the sap towards the lower part of the branches. As for espaliers, they can be trained in palmette, French or Montreuil style, like peach trees; it's true that the wood is irregular and less suited to symmetrical shapes, but as this tree has the quality of growing back on old wood, with intelligence it can always be trained. Fruit-bearing branches should be pruned short, and even, when trellising, it is sometimes necessary to remove part of the shelters, otherwise the tree will be exhausted and remain sterile the following year; those that remain become much bigger and better. The small lambourdes are left intact. When ripe, if you carelessly uncover the fruits by suddenly pulling back the leaves under which they are hidden, the sun will catch them, burn them and make them fall. This operation must therefore be carried out carefully and gradually. When an apricot tree is bald, either because of age or because you've neglected to prune it, you pull it back close to the trunk, and it soon forms a new head. But it soon wears out and perishes after producing a few harvests. For further details on ordinary cultivation, not only of the apricot tree, but of all the plants mentioned in this book, please refer to the excellent Manual by our collaborator Mr. Noisette. 14 1 ABSINTHE (GRANDE), ALUINX, ARSINTEE ROMAINE, ADSINTES COMMUNE, ARMOISE AMÈRE (artemisia Absinthium); perennial, indigenous plant, aromatic in all its parts, which is taken as an infusion and used to make liqueurs. Absinth retains all its cultivation qualities when dried out, and has never been subjected to forced cultivation; we need only mention its ordinary cultivation here. It thrives in all types of soil, but prefers those that are substantial, light and slightly damp, albeit warm. In the south, it lasts fourteen or fifteen years, and acquires much more energetic virtues. It could be propagated from seed, but it's much easier and quicker to spread it from clumps or suckers, which you separate and plant in autumn or spring. What's more, it is very often self-sowing, and all that's needed to multiply it abundantly is to care for the young plants that grow naturally around the mother plant, and transplant them in spring. In Issv, near Paris, some inhabitants grow this plant extensively, to supply it to the capital's distillers (1). GARLIC (Allium sativum); this plant, which is little used in Paris but much used in the south of France, has no need to be grown as a primeur, since its bulbs keep very well all year round, if placed in a dry, frost-free place. PRIMEUR. Nevertheless, as some people are in the habit of using, as a seasoning, what they call garlic tails, i.e. the green top of the leaf, it is worth saying how one can (1) Absinthe "energetic properties; it is tonic, stomachic, vermifuge, etc. obtain it all year round. All you need to do is choose large, very healthy, well-fed bulbs, plant them in potting soil in a tied layer, among other vegetables grown under cloches or trays, and repeat this planting every fortnight during the bad season, so that the leaves can be cut off until you can get some in the ground. The holes in which the plants are planted are made with a planter; the bottom is filled with a little sand to reduce humidity, and the bulb is sunk to an inch and a half. As the stem must be a beautiful green, care is taken to place the garlics in the brightest part of the bed. ROcAmRolr (Allium scorodoprasum) is preferable for this type of cultivation. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Garlic should be planted in the open ground towards the end of February or the beginning of March; however, it still has time to succeed if planting is delayed until the beginning of May, or even later, in light, warm and substantial soils. In southern France, it is sometimes planted in November, but more often in February. It is planted in borders or beds, three inches deep and six bulbs apart. The plant is hardy, and requires only a few weedings to rid it of weeds. When the leaves dry out, maturity is recognized; the plants should then be pulled up and left to lie on the ground for a few days, to allow the air to absorb their excess moisture. When the garlics are quite dry, they are cleaned of any earth that may be attached to them, bundled together and hung in the place where they are to be kept. I've seen a few people use a particular cultivation method, with the sole aim of obtaining extraordinarily large garlics. Here's how they do it: in August, they plant preserved garlic bulbs from the previous year in substantial soil; they leave them in the ground until the heads have grown to the size of walnuts; then they pull them out, dry them in the shade, exposed to a draught, and keep them until spring, to replant them whole in February and March. This method produces monstrously large heads of garlic, but is this advantage enough to offset the doubled cost of cultivation? Garlic, like rocambola, is a very rare seed-bearer, so we only propagate them by their bulblets. Both like warm, light, sandy soil, but they do well everywhere. As with all bulbous plants, they need much-consumed fertilizers, as fresh manure causes them to rot. ALLELUIA, OXALIDE, PETTTX OSEILLE, SURELLE, PAIN-DE-cOUCOU (Oxalis acetozella); a small, perennial, native plant that thrives in the shade of woods and in rock crevices. In some provinces, it is grown on a large scale to produce what is known in the trade as sorrel salt (1). Few people grow it in the kitchen garden, because it is only used in cooking to make seasonings, so we have never tried to force it. This could easily be done, by pulling out the small tubers that make up its roots, and planting them, after having (1) Acid oxalate of potash or sorrel salt, is refreshing; it soothes the pulse, calms thirst, reduces body heat, increases urine secretion, etc., eic. The plant is antiscorbutic and diuretic. This species should not be confused with OXALIDE CORNICULEE, (Oxalis comniculata,) an annual plant more commonly found in the Paris markets, but which does not have the same qualities. Oxalide petite Oseille has white or pink flowers, while Ozalide corniculée has yellow ones. Leave them to dry for a few days in the shade, in the compost of a warm bed, where they should be sunk half an inch. In winter, they can be placed on a bed under a chair, or under cloches housing other early plants. Twenty-five to thirty days after planting, the first leaves can be cut, whatever the season. REGULAR CULTIVATION. Oxalis, which some people eat in the same way as sorrel, although it is more acidic, can be grown in light, sandy, slightly damp soil in the vegetable garden, with a northern exposure. The best fertilizer for it is threshold compost or heather soil. In the spring, propagate from seeds, barely covering them with soil, to allow the roots to sprout. If you want a seedling to succeed, mulch it with chopped moss. Mice are extremely fond of its root tubers, so every care must be taken to keep them away. If you are forced to grow it in open, warm areas, it needs a lot of watering. AMANDIER (Amygdalus communis); Medium-sized tree, native to Asia, whose fruit and various uses are well known. The best varieties are: 1. sweet, hard-shelled almonds. Amandier commun ou franc. Very productive tree, but small, late fruits. Common almond tree with large fruit. Very productive tree; very large fruit, ripening in September. Amandier de Tours. Largest fruit of all, elongated, compressed, with semi-hard shell. 2. Sweet almonds, soft-shelled. Princess or ladies' almond. Very productive tree, very good fruit, very soft-shelled, easily broken in the fingers. Sultana almond. Rather delicate tree; rounded fruit, as esteemed as the previous one. 3. Bitter almonds. Almond tree with large fruit. Very vigorous tree; rounded fruit. Medium-fruited almond tree. Productive tree; rounded fruit. Soft-shelled almond tree. Not very vigorous or productive; medium-sized, slightly compressed fruit. Almond-peach tree. Quite vigorous, productive tree; fruit sometimes resembles an ordinary amald, other times large, rounded, fleshy, but whose pulp always retains a bitter flavor. PRIMEUR. As the amaudier does not succeed above the 48th degree of latitude, where spring frosts constantly destroy its flowers, in northern France we are obliged to subject it to artificial cultivation. It is planted at the foot of a wall in the south, in an espalier, and depending on how cold the climate is, it is sheltered during flowering by canvas, straw mats or glass panels. In Europe's most northerly countries, it is treated in the same way as the apricot. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Almond trees thrive in light, warm, deep soil. They are propagated by sowing or grafting onto themselves, or, more rarely, onto plum trees, and only in shallow, strong, damp soil. Almonds are stratified in January and planted in nurseries or, better still, in the spring. Ordinary weeding, hoeing, etc. is carried out, and the young trees are usually ready for grafting the following year. Grafting is done in buds, with a growing eye in spring, or with a dormant eye in September. All other care is the same as for peach trees. AMBROISIE, CHÉNOPODE AMBROISIE, TEÉ DU MEXIQUE, ANSÉRINE (Chenopodium ambrozoides); annual plant, native to Mexico, which is only cultivated for use in tea infusions, because of the sweet smell it gives off. As it retains its sequential qualities after drying, we don't usually force it into the soil. It grows well in any soil, provided it is warm, but it prefers light, sandy and substantial soils. It is propagated from seed, sown in spring, and when the plant is strong enough, transplanted into the soil. It requires no special care, and does well in all seasons if sown under a bell or frame, on a warm bed with supplies (1). ANGELICA, ANGELICA OFFICIMALE, RACINZ DU SAINT-ESPAIT (Angelica archangelica); biennial plant, growing naturally in the Alps. The ribs of its leaves and young stems are preserved in sugar, its leaves are used to make liqueurs, and its seeds are used to make dragées, from which it follows that, since it can be preserved by these various preparations, it would be absolutely useless to subject this plant to aruficial cultivation (a). (4) This plant can be usefully employed in medicine for pulmonary catharsis, humid asthma, and all diseases of the chest which are not yet declared phthisis; in a word, it can be placed among the bechic stimulans, whose properties are best proven. (a) Angelica is considered stomachic, carminative and diaphoretic. It is a mildly stimulating tonic. Angelica is hardy and fairly easy to grow. It likes open, substantial, slightly damp soil with a sunny exposure; nevertheless, it does well everywhere, provided it is given some watering during summer droughts. It should be sown as soon as the seeds ripen, or in September, because if you wait until spring, the seeds often fail to set. It is undoubtedly on this particularity that a widespread prejudice has been established in the south of France: it is believed that its seeds lose their germinative capacity when harvested with the bare hand. They need little covering and frequent soaking, and usually sprout after a month and a half or two months, sometimes only the following spring. If you leave the young plants in place, they require little care, but if you transplant them, you need to take a few precautions to ensure they take root again. Transplant them into a well-trodden, loosened soil, amended with reconstituted potting compost, and give them a few weedings and waterings. In winter, when the stems have disappeared, the plants are covered with a little compost, and in May and June of the second year, the stems can be cut. Although this plant is biennial, it often grows back in the third and even fourth years. ANISE, ROUCAGE, ARIS VEAT (Fimpinella anisum); a biennial plant, native to the Orient, the seeds of which are used to make small sugared almonds and liqueurs. There would be no point in forcing you to plant it, so we'll just talk here about its ordinary cultivation (1). (1) Anise has the same green characteristics as angelica. The seeds: are tonic, stimulating, carminative, etc. warm, perfectly loosened, amended with old compost. Sow it in spring, in March or April, and give it frequent basins to help germination. Weed the young plant, or water it during droughts, and it sets seed in August. To collect them, the stems are cut close to the ground, left to dry in the sun, then beaten. The plants usually regrow in spring and provide a second crop of seeds, but the roots often perish in winter, so it's wise to sow them every spring. This is also the practice of the inhabitants of southern France and Touraine, where anise is grown extensively. Anise seeds retain their germinative properties for three years. ARACHIDE, PISTACHE-DE TERRE, AMANDE-DE-TEREE (Arachys hypogœa); annual plant, native to Mexico where it is highly cultivated, has many similarities with the bean, and can be grown in much the same way. Its seed, roughly the shape of a large bean, is eaten cooked in water or under ashes; an oil is extracted that is good to eat and suitable for a variety of uses. After flowering, as soon as the pod has formed, the stalk inclines towards the s.I., the pod sinks into the earth, and the seed is found two or three inches below the surface when ripe. Peanuts were brought to France around thirty years ago. Agronomists, struck by the benefits it could bring, strongly recommended it to farmers in the south of France (as it can only succeed in large-scale cultivation in provinces where melons produce good yields in the open ground). In the Landes department, its cultivation succeeded perfectly, but it was soon abandoned because growers could not afford to dispose of its products, which were unavailable on the market. We have never tried to force peanuts, but it could be done with the greatest of ease. All you have to do, in any season, is sow three or four seedlings in a pot six to seven inches in diameter, which you then press into the tan of a warm layer, either in a pineapple greenhouse or under a frame. Leave the plant in this way until it is strong enough to be germinated in the pot, and then place it with the rootball in the potting soil of a warm melon bed under a frame. The same care should be given as to early beans. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Peanuts like warm, substantial, light or sandy soil, so that they can easily sink their pods into the soil, amended with well-consumed potting soil or, better still, leaf compost. In spring, when the soil is sufficiently warmed up, sow in rays or clumps, covering the seeds with two inches of soil. We hoe, weed, and finally give it the same care as beans. M. Noisette recommends soaking the seeds in water forty-eight hours before sowing, to hasten germination. If sown in rays, plants should be spaced fifteen inches apart; two feet apart if sown in clumps. ARROCHE DES JARDINS, BONNE-DAME, BELLÉDAME, IRIBLE, FOLETTE, CHOU D'AMOUR Ou D'ARMOU (Atriplex hortensis); annual plant that comes to us from Tartary. We use it to mix with sorrel to soften its acidity; despite its insipidity, in the south of France we use a lot of it in herb broth. Three varieties are grown: blond, with yellowish-green leaves; red, with pale-red foliage; and very red, with dark-red leaves. PRIMEUR. This plant rises very easily, grows rapidly and is not at all delicate; these reasons make it very easy to grow as a primeur. From October onwards, it is sown thickly in the compost of early bedding, along with supplies, radishes, etc.; fifteen or twenty days later, leaves have to be cut, and it must be sown again in a warmer bed. Sowing continues fortnightly until February, when the seeds can be planted in the open ground. As it is quite water-loving, it should be placed on beds where plants requiring watering are grown. ORDINARY GROWING. It can be sown in March, and as it grows very quickly, sowing should be repeated every twenty days, so that the plant is available all year round. The seed must be harvested a little before maturity, otherwise it is easily scattered by the winds. The stalks are cut, dried in the shade, then shaken on a cloth; the seeds detach and are collected with the greatest of ease. ARTICHAUT (cynara Scolymus); perennial plant, native to Barbary. Several varieties are grown: Le gros vert, or Artichaut de Laon, very large, excellent, the most widely grown around Paris. The gros camus de Bretagne, with a broad, flattened head, paler green than the previous one, a little earlier but less fleshy. The violet, of mediocre size, early, with scales washed purplish-red at the tip, excellent for peppering, but of mediocre quality when cooked. Red, smaller than the previous one, with purple-red circumferential scales, the best of all if eaten raw when young, but becoming hard and fibrous when fully grown. It is less successful around Paris than in the south of France. The white, even more difficult to preserve than the previous one, is very early, assex tender but small. Its head is very flat, and its scales are terminated by a prickly spine. It degenerates quite rapidly in Paris. Sucré de Géne, very small, more spiky than white, pale green, yellow flesh and excellent raw. It degenerates even more quickly than the previous variety, and in the Paris climate, if you want to have it in good condition, you have to bring in carnations from Italy every two years. PRRZUR. There are several ways of obtaining early artichokes, which we will describe in detail below. To obtain artichokes in April, we force them into place. Carnations are planted in the middle of May, and cared for in the same way as full-grown plants, until the cold sets in. You don't want to plant earlier, nor choose carnations that are too big, so that they don't set fruit in autumn, but only form strong, vigorous plants. You get wicker cages, with bars spaced a good inch apart, cylindrical, about like a barrel split at both ends, eighteen inches wide and two feet high. (fig. 3.) As soon as the first frosts threaten, each artichoke plant is mounded with a little soil taken from a nearby square, the leaves are bundled together, after cutting off those at the bottom that threaten to dry out or rot, and a cage is placed over each plant. Arrange the foliage in the cage in such a way as to give the leaves, especially those in the heart of the plant, as much air and light as possible. Once all the cages are in place, spread a well-trampled bed of manure, at least six to eight inches thick, over the entire surface between each cage. When frost arrives, we surround the cages up to the top with a bed of dry litter, against which we raise a good bed of manure. Close the top of the cage with a good plug of broken straw, and cover the whole with a cap of long straw, to keep out rainwater. Whenever the thermometer indicates that the outside temperature is above freezing, remove the plug to let in air and light. You can even leave the plants uncovered during rain, as long as it doesn't last several days in a row, as artichokes are more afraid of shrivelling than humidity when they have air. Every three or four days, as soon as the plants are uncovered, we visit them to remove any rotten leaves, and to replace in a more favorable position those which the deprivation of air and light would threaten to shrivel. Care is taken to avoid being caught out by the frost, and when the thermometer falls, the straw pads are replaced exactly on the cages every evening. When spring arrives and frosts are no longer to be feared, the cages and surrounding fumter are removed, but left on the square until the soil has warmed up enough to allow the first hoeing to take place without any inconvenience. This easy method produces artichokes from Easter to May. It is essential, I repeat, that the plant be young if you want to obtain them at the times indicated. Older plants treated in this way are more likely to be preserved and produce fruit earlier, but only fifteen to twenty days earlier than the others. Artichokes can also be forced in situ, to produce fruit in February and March. Here's how it's done. You dig three pits one foot wide and as deep, parallel to each other and eighteen inches apart; their length is indeterminate; you fill these pits with old manure and potting soil, on which you can make small spring and summer seedlings. In May, strong artichoke eyelets are planted in the beds between the pits. They can be cultivated early in the summer, just like those from an ordinary plantation. When the cold weather is about to arrive, we remove the old manure and compost from the three pits, and replace it with warm manure; or enclose the two rows of artichoke plants in wicker cages similar to those we've already mentioned, but only eighteen inches high. Around the cages and over the pits, a warm layer is established, which is consequently six feet wide by an indeterminate length, and in the middle of which the artichoke vines are placed in voids held by the cages. The layer is raised eighteen inches, and when it is level with the cages, a two-foot-wide, fourteen- to fifteen-inch-high verrine is placed on each plant (fig. 5)." The whole is left in this state until the frosts become intense, at which point the layer is replenished with six or seven inches of warm manure, and, during the night, the verrines are covered with straw mats and large bedding. Needless to say, the bed must be welded often, to see if it loses heat, and whenever this happens, it must be reshaped, new manure added, lined with good stoves, and every means employed to keep it at a constantly high temperature. Whenever the thermometer is between five and eight degrees, or higher, we take advantage of moments of sunshine to give the air by lifting the verrines a few inches. We wipe off the moisture and water droplets that cling to the glass, peel the plants and clean them of aphids, rid them of rotten or moldy leaves, etc., etc.. The third way of forcing artichokes is the one to be preferred, because produce can be obtained from December to April. In spring, baskets or manes are purchased, fourteen to fifteen inches wide and eighteen inches deep. These are filled with a mixture of good, free-flowing soil and well-consumed loam, and in April, one or two artichoke carnations are planted in each basket. These baskets are buried in one of the garden's flowerbeds, and the plant is cared for as if it were in the ground. A gardener with a little experience will easily see if the plant is in danger of remaining too weak to produce in December and January; if so, he'll push it a little with water to get it going. If, on the other hand, the vigor of its vegetation makes him fear that it will bear fruit in the ordinary autumn season, he will remove the basket of soil, and give the rootball only the humidity strictly necessary to prevent the plant from perishing. At the end of October or the beginning of November, depending on whether the plant is more or less advanced, the baskets are removed and carried to a tarpaulin where they are placed in a warm layer. The plants are peeled, aphids are destroyed, air is given, ponds are made where necessary, and all the general care necessary for the plants is taken. In this way, you'll soon obtain prôduits that succeed one another throughout the winter, if the plants are not all of equal strength. Some people don't take such precautions. In October or December, they simply remove old artichoke plants with the root ball, and replant them in a hole made for them in the warm layer of a tarpaulin, and in which they have placed a certain quantity of the soil whose composition we have indicated. This method has the advantage of being more economical, but the results are neither as rapid nor as certain, because whatever precautions are taken to lift the plant, the roots are always damaged to a greater or lesser extent, and transplanting is an operation that tires and delays the plant. If you have a tarpaulin and you don't want to speculate on the product, you can make use of the lost space by forcing a few artichoke plants into it. To do this, all you need is a few eighteen-inch-diameter, deep boxes. Fill them with the soil mentioned above, and in September, plant a strong artichoke plant in each one, and let it recover in the open air. When the first white frosts make themselves felt, we cut off some of the leaves at the base to save space, and transport the crates to the tarpaulin, where all we have to do is give the plants the usual care. We would nevertheless point out that, treated in this way, they require more frequent watering. Finally, we'll end this article on early plants, by teaching you a very simple procedure which can be used to keep arlichauts for part of the winter at low cost. When the cold weather arrives, we cut the fruiting artichoke stems on the ground and, in the vegetable greenhouse or simply in a good cellar, we plant them in damp sand; the heads are thus preserved for a very long time with almost all their qualities. A preferable way of destroying an artichoke patch is to pull up the whole plant and plant it as we have just said, taking care to remove most of the leaves. The teles continue to grow and can supply part of the winter, and this resource is all the more precious as it requires no expense. Sometimes artichokes may run out in a jar din between the two harvests, i.e. in August and early September. This inconvenience, caused by summer drought, is easily avoided by daytime trussing of plants that failed to produce in spring. This method brings fruiting forward by one and a half to two months. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. The artichoke thrives in free soil, rich in nutrient juices, but especially deep, because its roots are pivotal. It can be propagated by graiues, which often produce the same product in the same year, but as they do not reproduce their summer variety, it is customary to propagate only by crilletons. In spring, when the leaves of the mother plants have reached about a foot in length, i.e. in April, the crown is removed, taking care not to damage the roots, and the eyelets growing around the stump are detached by hand. Only three of the most vigorous are left. Of these, the strongest are chosen, with a few roots in the nut, i.e. the crown that forms the collar. However, a carnation will grow well without them, and if you didn't have many, the lack of roots would not be a reason to discourage them. Horticultural experts recommend cutting the leaves to six inches long before replanting the carnations, but three years of comparative experiments at my place have shown me that they are mistaken; the artichoke plants to which I have subjected this operation have always been less vigorous, and above all much less eager than those to which I have left their leaves in their full length. Moreover, this is easy to understand: we know that leaves, as much as roots, contribute to plant nutrition, and if you remove these organs from a plant that already lacks roots, it will no longer be able to draw anything from the air, nutrition will be almost nil, and consequently, recovery will be much longer and more difficult. This is a general rule that applies to all plants. Whatever the case, after having perfectly cleared and loosened the ground intended for planting, and after having suitably amended it with a consummate fertilizer, the eyelets are planted in staggered rows, two and a half feet apart in mediocre soils, and three apart in good soils. Two of them are placed four inches apart, so that there is no gap in the plantation if any are missing. They should not be planted too deeply, to avoid heart rot. From time to time, you give the plants a little water to help them get back on their feet; you hoe and weed to destroy weeds, and that's all you do until winter approaches. If a few plants are missing, they can still be successfully replaced in May, either by detaching new eyelets from the mother plants, or by removing one where both plants have recovered. When a plantation has been well cared for and planted at the right time, two-thirds of the plants start producing the following autumn. As soon as the first frosts appear, all the stems are cut as close as possible to the stump, the base leaves are removed and the others are bundled together. This operation consists in bringing the soil around the stem to protect it from frost. A very pernicious and, unfortunately, all-too-common practice is to take the soil from the bed itself, between the feet, thus uncovering the roots and exposing them to the rigors of the frosts. When this first operation is complete, dry leaves, fern or barley bales are heaped around the plants, and the whole thing is covered with a large bedding. In wet soils, "it is necessary," says M. Noisette, "to open gullies between each row of artichokes, and to use the earth removed to butter the feet. The entire square, except for the gutters, is covered with a layer of dry sumier, which is made thicker around each plant, taking care not to bring the manure into contact with the plants, which are placed at the bottom of a funnel. This funnel is covered with dry litter during the frosts, and the straw is combed to keep rainwater away from the plants. "Whenever the weather gets milder and the sun appears, we remove the litter and replace it during the night. In my opinion, the best way to protect artichokes from the cold, to which they are less sensitive than one might think, and from humidity, shrivelling and the resulting rot, which are the ordinary causes of their loss, is to house them in the cages I mentioned in the article on early vegetables, and to manage them absolutely as I said on page 31. For the last three winters, I have used no other method in my garden, and since then I have always obtained very satisfactory results. The artichoke cover can be removed entirely at the end of March, or earlier if frosts are no longer to be feared. A good ploughing is done, burying the manure from the cover and scattering the soil used to butter the artichoke. The artichoke is then oiled as we said, and other care is limited to a few hoeings to open the pores of the soil and destroy parasitic weeds. Artichokes, though perennial, are only profitable for three years; after the fourth, their yields diminish considerably in quantity and size. For this reason, planting should be renewed every three years. When an old square is destroyed, the leaves can be blanched like cardoons, and used in cooking in the same way. Some authors have printed that they are more delicate; I've experienced this myself, and I can't agree with them. Sowing artichokes can only be useful if you want to obtain new varieties, or if you are short of eyelets. The seed is collected from the best terminal heads, and kept on the artichoke's receptacle until sowing. From flowering to maturity, the stem must be bent to prevent rainwater penetrating through the scales into the heart of the fruit, which would cause the seeds to abort and rot. In February and March, sow in pots sunk into the warm layer of a frame, or wait until the end of April to sow in situ. In the former case, transplant the young plant as soon as it is strong enough; in the latter, thin out, hoe and give it the usual care. Artichokes sown on a bed almost all bear fruit the following autumn, but the others hardly produce their first heads until spring. Seed artichokes are not equally good; many are very thorny, hardly bigger than a thistle head, and must be pulled up; but if you have sown Laon seed, you will get many that are large and excellent. ASPERGE (Asparagus officinalis); native perennial, growing naturally on the sandy beaches of almost all the rivers of France and Germany. Its uses are generally well known. There are several breeds, for example: white asparagus, or Dutch asparagus, green asparagus, violet asparagus, etc.; but the slight differences between them are purely local, and we must not hope to fix them when we change country and culture (1). (1) Asparagus is a very good vegetable, but it has the singular disadvantage of imparting a foul odor to the urine of those who eat it. This odor is easily destroyed by putting muriatic acid or vinegar in the chamberpot; if, after eating asparagus, you swallow a small pill of turpentine, the urine, far from smelling bad, absolutely exalts the odor of violets. PAIMEUR. Market gardeners in Paris produce a lot of early asparagus, using two processes that we'll describe in detail below. In one case, they heat on the spot; in the other, they force the asparagus into a frame. On-site heating. In dry, light, warm soils, the soil is laid out in planks or beds four feet wide; in cold, strong, low-porosity soils, only three feet wide. A two-foot-wide path is left between each bed. The beds are perfectly loosened, and the soil is lightened as much as possible with old topsoil that has been completely used up. In spring, in March in the south of France and in April in the Paris climate, asparagus claws are planted in the beds in the usual way. The difference, however, is that the plant is spaced less widely; ten inches between each plant is sufficient. The main thing is to leave six to eight inches of empty space along both sides of each bed. The plantation is raised and cultivated without any special care until its third year, that is, until the plants are strong enough to be heated. Then, from December to April, depending on whether you want to produce earlier or later, you dig the two-foot-wide paths you've left around the planks to eighteen or twenty inches, and remove the soil after loading the two- or three-inch-thick strips. The pits are filled with well-packed hot manure, and these stoves are raised a foot above the board, and the board itself is covered a foot thick with the same manure. A bed of long litter is placed on top to keep rainwater away from the bed. If the planting is to be done on soil with some consistency, transverse vaults can be dug from distance to distance, under each bed, and filled to measure with hot manure. 8If you have frames, you can save a lot of manure; after raising the stoves as we've just said, cover the board with only six inches of manure, and place the frames with their panels over them at night, especially when it's freezing; cover the whole thing with straw mats, and it's best not to remove them until it's sunny. Fifteen or twenty days later, the asparagus should have emerged from the soil. Remove the manure from the top of the bed, cut off those that are long enough, and replace the manure. If it has lost its heat, mix it with new manure, or even replace it entirely, as well as the manure from the stoves. This is de rigueur whether or not there are frames. However, some market gardeners do things differently: when the asparagus start to grow, they remove the manure from under the frames, and never put it back in. They simply renew the stoves more often. Here's why they do it this way: asparagus growing in manure is deprived of light, and remains white along its entire length, whereas asparagus growing in light under the glass panels turns green or red along its entire length outside the soil. As the heat of the soil is greatly reduced by not replacing the manure, vegetation is delayed, and only by covering with dry leaves and straw mats is it possible to avoid suspending vegetation entirely during very cold weather, this method seems to us to be vicious. All the more so as nothing is as easy as giving asparagus its color. To do this, we gather the asparagus into small bunches of ten to twelve, prick the bunches up to half their length into the soil of a warm vegetable layer, and cover them with a transparent glass cloche. Twenty-four or forty-eight hours later, the asparagus is perfectly colored. So, far from recommending this method used by some market gardeners, who may be unaware of the very simple practice I've just taught, I'd advise you, on the contrary, to load the boards under the frame with a foot of manure when the cold becomes more intense. It's enough for the glass of the boards to be six inches away from the surface of the layer. However, care must be taken not to generate enough heat to burn the asparagus. If you take care to keep rainwater away from the layers, which would cool them down, and if you maintain their heat by reheating the stoves every fortnight, you can cut asparagus every other day for a month and a half or two months. When they stop producing, the soil is thrown back into the paths, and if there are still frosts to be feared, a certain thickness of manure is left on the beds, which is not removed until April, to preserve the roots. In spring, the asparagus grows only a few skinny, flabby stalks, which you must be careful not to cut off; but the following year they have regained enough vigor, if you don't cut them off in spring to be able to be heated again. A plantation in good soil, well managed, can last ten years, and, by alternately heating it two years at a time, provide five harvests of early crops. We should point out that forcing beds can also be encouraged by sowing; only, instead of heating in the third year, you'll have to wait until the fourth, but the yield will never be as good. As we have said, market gardeners hardly start forcing asparagus until the beginning of December, and they continue until February, or March at the latest, because it is during this part of the year that asparagus is most valuable. But the same success can be obtained by heating as soon as the stalks begin to turn yellow, and even as soon as the seed has acquired that red color which heralds its maturity. Heating on a bed. In November, we raise a warm layer eighteen to twenty inches high and three feet wide; if we want to heat later, when winter is in full force, we make the layer three feet thick and four feet wide: the manure, being in a larger mass, heats up more easily and retains its heat better. The layer is filled with three to four inches of very light earth, or old potting soil mixed with half garden soil. Place glass frames over it, and leave it there until the heat has passed. When there's no longer a risk of burning the asparagus roots, we get at least four-year-old claws, shorten the roots so that they are only eight or nine inches long, and lay them upright, very close together, so that they support each other, and take care that all the collars are roughly level. Once this has been done, use your hand to slide potting soil between the roots until all the interstices are well filled, and then spread half a finger's worth of potting soil over the whole plant. A fortnight later, the asparagus begins to grow, and you can start cutting. They are very green, but very thin, so we usually eat them as peas. After fifteen or twenty days, the claws are exhausted and no longer produce; all that's left to do is undo and throw the claws away, because they're burnt. Old squares of asparagus that are destroyed provide excellent roots to be heated in this way. This is the ordinary method used by most of the market gardeners in Paris. Mr. Noisette has made a few modifications, the results of which have been bigger produce, in greater quantities, and lasting longer. Let co tavant agriculteur speak for himself. "At the same times as for the other on-site cultivation (from November to March), layers of hot manure are prepared, eighteen to twenty inches high and four or five inches wide. - When they're at their hottest, we "cover them with ten inches of compost, to which we've mixed" a third of light earth. We place on top, six inches apart, claws aged four years or more, and on top of which we spread a two-inch bed of the same potting soil, and on top a little hot fu mier. Leave the bed in this state for a few days, and when you think the asparagus "are about to start growing, remove the manure and replace it with three inches of new compost," after which the frames are placed. The "chi" of the layer is renewed by means of heaters, which are changed every - time they are needed. After "twelve to fifteen days, the asparagus begin to produce, and we cut for as long as they "give, which is about a month, more or less. - Gardeners who have tarpaulins sometimes make use of places lost in the dark, such as the underside of tiers, back corners, etc., by forcing asparagus into baskets. Nothing is as simple as the means they employ. They procure mannes or baskets, ten inches deep by an indeterminate width; at the bottom they place a bed of straw or litter, over which they spread a layer of sand one inch thick. They finish filling the basket with consumed potting soil mixed with a fifth of light garden loam. In each basket they plant four old claws at the rate of each square foot, and, instead of cutting the roots, they intertwine them with each other. They give a light basking and place these baskets in lost places. The asparagus develops rapidly, and when the claws are exhausted, they replace them with new ones. Some growers place similar baskets in the 2 21 * * Y 2 2 i63 L 16 13 empty places of the warm layers they have in baches, and even in the open air under a frame. ORDINAIRX CULTIVATION. Asparagus like light, sandy, substantial soil; they become superb in fertile sands. They can be propagated from seed, which can be dried in situ or in a nursery. In the latter case, the roots, known as pates or claws, are planted in situ. Sowing takes place in November or October in southern France, in March in the Paris climate, and in April in more northerly regions. To sow in a nursery, prepare the soil with good ploughing and suitable amendments; when the soil is perfectly loosened, sow by broadcasting or in very light rays, and cover the seeds with a fingerful of reconstituted compost. Oh gives a few light basins, if the season is dry, and the seeds usually rise in forty days. We hoe lightly, pull out weeds carefully so as not to uproot the young plant, and give a few sprinklings if necessary. As winter approaches, all stems are cut off, and the plant is covered with three inches of potting soil, which is mixed, if desired, with half light or sandy soil. The following year, if sowing has been perfectly successful, you can remove the claws and put them in place. If not, wait until the second year, but never later. When pulling out the roots, great care must be taken to avoid breaking the large fibers that extend laterally around the collar. If the plant is to be sown in place, a few modifications are made to what has just been said; after preparing the ground as for planting, a hole one inch deep is made in each place where the plant is to be, into which five or six seeds are sown, covered with potting soil, and when they have risen, the most vigorous plant is left and all the others pulled out, or better still, cored below the collar, so as not to undermine the roots of the one that remains. During the first year, watering, hoeing and weeding are carried out in the same way as for the nursery, and in subsequent years the seedling is managed in the same way as the plantation. An asparagus plant established by this method produces its crop a year later, but the plants are more vigorous and last longer. Asparagus plants are planted in different ways. It is done at the same time as sowing, in beds or squares. For both, it's best to prepare the ground the previous autumn. To plant in beds, you open pits two feet deep in dry soils, three feet deep in wet soils, four feet wide and of any length. Between each plank, a sufficient interval is left to receive the soil from the excavation, without forming too high an ados. In wet soils, a foot-thick bed of plaster, gravel, pebbles or anything else capable of allowing water to pass through is placed in each pit. Once this is done, a foot-thick layer of good fertilizer is placed at the bottom, consisting of manure, turf, street mud, etc., which is trampled down as much as possible to prevent it from settling. This is the method used by all growers; but I must confess that I don't quite understand its usefulness, as the asparagus claw only produces a very short, very short hair, always extending laterally; each claw lasts only three years, after which it perishes, and a new one forms above it to replace it. What influence, then, can this fertilizer have on a root that never reaches it, that moves away from it every year, and that can receive no nutritive juices from it since it is placed above it? In any case, we spread three inches of well loosened soil from the excavation on top of this fertilizer, and mix it with enough compost to make it very light. Using a chalk line, draw three equally spaced lines, and fifteen or eighteen inches apart on each line, mark the places where the claws are to be placed, by raising a very small mound of compost. Choose good claws, with long, white, healthy roots; if any are fractured, cut them very cleanly with a serpette; then spread one on each mound, taking care to incline the roots a little. Cover the whole with three fingers of soil similar to that on which you planted. During the course of the year, ordinary care is given, consisting of weeding, watering and so on. In November, the beds are replenished with three fingers of soil taken from the ados which, until then, have been used to sow lettuces, supplies and other plants whose cultivation requires no more than six months' time, and which are not tall enough to provide shade. The following year, the same care is taken, and in autumn, after cutting the dried stems, three new fingers of soil are covered, on which a certain thickness of consumed manure is spread. In the third spring, while hoeing, we bury this fertilizer, and already we can start cutting the biggest asparagus. Every year, you continue with the same cultivation, loading the bed with three fingers of new soil and giving it new fertilizers. In this way, an asparagus plantation is in full production by the fifth year, and can last fourteen or fifteen years. Never cut later than July. If you plant in squares, the costs are a little higher, but the produce is more beautiful, and asparagus usually lasts from fifteen to twenty years. The soil is prepared in August of the previous year, and fertilizers are mixed in to lighten it; if the soil is strong, sand, heather and leached ashes are best. The following spring, in March or early April, the soil is divided into five-foot planks, the first of which is dug with a good spade-shot, and the soil is dumped onto the next one; then it is dug to a depth of eighteen inches in dry soils, and two feet in wet ones, and the soil is placed in a storage area. If two feet have been dug, a six-inch bed of rubble or gravel is placed at the bottom of the pit to facilitate drainage. On top of this, a second bed a foot thick is laid with consumed manure or other fertilizer. The pit is then completely filled with the soil first removed during excavation. In this way, it is in the middle of the rest of the soil. Once this has been done, the second bed is prepared in the same way, then the third, and so on, until the end of the square, which is now at the same level as before the operation. We sow or plant as we have said, giving the same care throughout the year, and by autumn, the soil has settled enough to allow us to top up with three fingers of compost. Every year, in autumn, new fertilizer is brought into the square, which is buried in the spring when the first ploughing is done, and so that in the long run the square does not become too raised, an inch or two of soil is removed from the surface, to be replaced by better soil. The asparagus seed is harvested from the most vigorous stalks, which should not be cut in the spring for this purpose. The stalks are cut off, the berries removed and left to dry in the air for two weeks; they are then crushed in the hands and washed in a vase filled with water. The husks float to the surface, and the seeds sink to the bottom. B BASELLE ROUGE, ÉTINARD DU MALARAR ( Basella rubra); biennial, climbing plant, native to the Indies, cultivated

like Rancux BAselle (Basella alba), and used for the same purposes. These two plants are ideal for replacing spinach, which has the same taste and qualities. They produce much more, and all summer long. PRIMEUR. I don't think that baselle has ever been grown as an early crop, probably because, as it is not well known on the Paris markets, market gardeners would not be able to sell it. It would, however, be very easy to grow for those who use tarpaulins, flying greenhouses or hothouses. In May, plant it in baskets in a bed against a wall facing south. In September, it would be tucked into the tarpaulin, planted with its basket in a bed against a wall, which it would line by trellising against it, and, by this means, it would make use of wasted space. If it had enough light, it would produce very good leaves, which could be harvested until spring. A single plant of boselle can provide, in four or five months, enough leaves for three or four dishes. CULTURX ORDINAIRX. Baselles are sown in March, on a warm bed under a frame, in substantial, loose soil. When frosts, to which they are very sensitive, are no longer to be feared, they are lifted with the rootball, and transplanted into the ground, at the foot of a wall facing south. Weed, hoe and, in hot weather, water frequently, as these plants love water.
