They should be planted against a trellis, to give their climbing stems (red or green, depending on the species) an easy grip. One of the most vigorous plants is left untouched to obtain seeds, which usually ripen very well. BASILIC (Ocymum basilicum); small annual plant, very aromatic in all its parts, native to Persia. It has several varieties: large-leafed, purple-leafed, lettuce-leafed, aniseed-flavored, etc. They are used as an assortment, and sometimes as a salad ingredient. Basil can be sown in any season, on a warm layer of tarpaulin, under a frame or under a bell. It germinates very easily and grows quite rapidly. If you want to use it for seasoning, wait until its stems have grown to at least five or six inches; for salad, cut it very young, otherwise its strong, aromatic flavor may displease some people. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Basil is sown in the soil in March. In May, it is transplanted to a warm spot in light, well-dressed soil. It requires copious and frequent watering throughout its life. The seed is harvested in autumn. BÉNINCASE GLAUQUE (Benincasa cerifera), and BÉNINCASE CYLINDRIQUE (Benincasa cylindrica); in this article, I bring together these two species or varieties of cucurbits, which I believe belong to the cucumber genus. Believed to have originated in Senegal, they have only been cultivated in Paris since 1826. They are still not very widespread. Their fruits are about the size of a cucumber, elongated, dark green and covered, especially the first, with a thick, viscous, glaucous dust. The flesh is white, firm and quite similar to that of cucumbers, but with a preferable taste; moreover, it can be eaten in the same way. PRIMEUR. These plants are still too little known in France for us to have tried to grow them early, but it is almost certain that, to obtain them in the off-season, we would have to force them in the same way as melons, and manage and prune them in the same way. We therefore refer the reader to this article. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Benincases can be grown in two ways: on a bed or in the ground. In both cases, they are sown in pots at the beginning of April, on a warm bed and under cloches, or under a frame. Seeds germinate with difficulty if they are not given sufficient warmth and humidity. When the plant has risen to a certain strength, it is removed from the soil and placed on an old bed, or at least in an earthen bed, at the foot of a south-facing wall. The stem is pruned over two eyes, and the branches over three or four nodes; moderate watering is given, as they fear too much humidity, and they are steered like melons. I believe that, sown in the open ground around April 15th, they could also succeed in ordinary years, i.e. those that are not too cold or rainy. But to do this, you'd have to sow in bunches, and here's how. Holes eighteen inches deep and two feet wide should be dug in good soil and exposure, and filled with warm, well-packed, trampled manure. In the middle of this manure, we'd make a new funnel-shaped hole, eight inches deep in the middle and a foot wide, which we'd fill with a mixture of three-quarters old topsoil and one-quarter light earth. Sow in this compost, and cover with a cloche or verrine, around which place a hot manure stove, seven to eight inches wide. The seeds would certainly sprout, and all that remained was to remove the stoves and verrines, around the fifteenth of May, perhaps earlier, if the season was favorable. A valuable quality of benincase fruits is that they keep perfectly well throughout the winter, if stored in a very dry place and protected from light frost. In 1828, I saw some at my colloborateur's, Mr. Noisette, on the first of March, and they seemed healthy enough to give him hope that they would keep for some time yet. BETTERAVE (Betta vulgaris); biennial plant, growing spontaneously in southern Europe. It is too well known to list all the uses to which it is put. Its main varieties are: grosse rouge, fairly long and the most widely cultivated of all. Petite rouge, more precocious, with a milder flavor. The round red, also early, of the same quality, but rounded. Yellow, large, elongated and the sweetest of all. Large and small white, very tender, delicate, but a little bland in flavor. Yellow with white flesh, the best for providing sugar. Detterave champétre, or starvation root, is grown only in large quantities, and for sourrage. We don't usually force beet, because the root, the only part we use, keeps perfectly well in a vegetable greenhouse, or simply in a dry cellar, until March and April. If at this time you choose the healthiest roots, place them in fine, very dry sand, in a box and put them in a dry, poorly ventilated place, you can still have them in May and June, and longer if you take the precaution of visiting them often and cutting off the buds as they grow. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Beets do more or less well in all soils that have been suitably loosened and amended, but they take on enormous dimensions in warm, deep, free and light soils, well broken up, and heavily amended from the previous year, or with fertilizers consumed from the beginning of March until May; after two good ploughings, they are sown broadcast or in rays. When the plant has risen, which happens after a fortnight, it is thinned out so that the plants are fifteen inches apart in mediocre soils, and eighteen or twenty in good soils. We hoe, weed, destroy parasitic weeds and, if the season is dry, water deeply. November is harvest time. We pull out the roots and leave them to dry out, then we twist or cut the leaves, and put them in the vegetable greenhouse to pull them out as we use them. Beet has a slight fear of transplanting, so when thinning out the seedling, if you want to transplant where there is a shortage, you must do so immediately and before the air has dried the surface of the roots. Sometimes, when the rácine is as big as a finger, you sow it in a nursery before transplanting it into the field. Transplanting should be carried out with the same care, and care should be taken to avoid injuring or breaking the root, which should never be cut back. If you have to wait before transplanting, soak them in a mixture of soil, water, manure and cow dung, to protect them from contact with the air. At harvest time, some of the best beets are left in the ground to provide seed. During hard frosts, we cover them with straw or dry litter. Sometimes, however, they cannot be preserved, in which case the best and healthiest beets in the vegetable garden are planted in the spring. Let grains retain their germinative virtues for only two or three years. C CAPUCIN. Two species are grown: the CrArD* CAPUCINE (Tropœolum majus), whose stems are very long and climbing, and the reTire CArUCINE (Tropœolum minus,) whose short stems need no support. The flowers of both are used to garnish salads; the buds are candied in vinegar before they open, and the flowers before they ripen. For this purpose, the smaller variety is preferable, as it flowers more abundantly than the larger. It would be of little importance to force the nasturtium if its use were limited to decorating salads, but its double-flowered variety is a very pretty ornamental plant, and can be used in the same way. This is the only one we're going to talk about. Nasturtiums, native to Peru, are perennials in their own country; but as they ripen their seeds in the space of a summer, we are accustomed to growing them as annuals. The double-flowered variety, which does not set seed, is propagated by taking cuttings under a bell or under a frame, in pots sunk in a warm layer. They can be taken from March to August. From September onwards, especially if there are cold rains, the pots are transported to a warm greenhouse, and if you want to enjoy the flowers all winter long, they are placed in a warm layer of tannin, in as much light as possible. The care they require is limited to removing dead leaves, directing the branches so that each enjoys the same amount of light and air, and moderating watering, as this plant is very sensitive to rot. I think that by sowing the little nasturtium in autumn and transporting it to the warm greenhouse before the weather turns sour, it will also flower in winter, and keep for several years. It's a good idea to take cuttings from double nasturtiums every year or two at the most, as experience has shown that young plants are the most resistant in the greenhouse. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Large and small nasturtiums grow well everywhere, but they do best in open, light soil with warm exposure. They are sown in April, and watered frequently during the heat of summer. They sometimes resow themselves. CARDON or Carde (Cynara cardunculus); perennial plant, native to Barbary. Everyone knows that it is grown for its leaf ribs. It has produced several varieties, including Cardou d'Espagne, which has no thorns, but grows very quickly, and has thin ribs. Cardon de Tours, preferred to the first, although very thorny, because it holds well and its ribs are wider and softer. Cardon plein sans épines (full cardoon without thorns), which has the same qualities as the previous one, but with the added advantage of being thornless. Artichoke-leaf cardoon, with very full, reddish, excellent ribs that are also thornless. PRIMEUR. In ordinary cultivation, cardoons supply from October to at least February. We will now describe the procedures to be used to obtain cardoons from March to September. At the beginning of November, prepare a warm bed under a frame. Sow two seeds of Tours cardoon or full cardoon without thorns, in small pots four inches in diameter, filled with a mixture of half light soil and old potting soil fully consumed. This first sowing can be done on a warm layer in the open air, using cloches or verrines. A similar sowing is repeated in December, and a third in January, but these last two should be done under a frame or tarpaulin. In any case, all three can be carried out as described below. When the plant has emerged, one of the two is cut off below the collar, if both seeds have germinated, and only the most vigorous is kept. When it begins to show its third leaf, remove it from its pot with the rootball, and place it in a larger vase with the same soil. A new warm layer of soil is then placed under the frame, and the pot is pushed into it. When the plant is strong enough, we establish a three-foot-thick muted layer at the foot of a south-facing wall, in very dry soil, covered with a foot of good compost mixed with a quarter of light earth. The cardoons are unstuck and planted in the potting soil, two feet apart, in all directions; the frame is placed on top, and surrounded by good stoves that are changed every time they are needed. If you notice that the bed is getting cold, you open a trench two feet wide and four deep around the frame against the bed; you lard it with new manure from underneath, and fill the pit with it to serve as stoves. If the cultivation is carried out in such a way as never to allow the heat to drop in the bed, nor the cold to penetrate into the frame, cardoons can be blanched for kitchen use as early as the month of murs; in April, from the second sowing, and in May, from the third. In March, choose the most vigorous plants from the second and third sowings and plant them out in a bed at the foot of a south-facing wall. Each will be covered with a bell to shelter them from the cool nights and gradually accustom them to the open air. If, during this month, the sun is strong and prickly, they should be sheltered from its rays for part of the day, using light-colored cloths. Otherwise, they should be cared for in the same way as full-earth seedlings, and will start to bind well in June and July, but many will fail; nevertheless, with careful watering and cultivation, they will produce until the time when full-earth cardoons start to yield. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Cardoons like substantial, light, deep soil, as well as abundant watering. You can easily get trained ones as early as August, with the precaution of sowing them in small pots, under a frame, in March, and transplanting them in April, when they require only ordinary care. For late-season and winter plants, sow them in the ground in April and May, and proceed as follows: loosen and fine the soil, which should be perfectly loosened and warm. Three feet apart in all directions, dig holes one pled wide and as deep, fill them with well-consumed manure mixed with a small amount of soil to give it body, then sow two or three seeds in each and cover with two fingers of the same soil. Water frequently, weed and hoe, and keep insects, especially aphids, away from the young plant. When the plant has two or three leaves, we thin it out so as to leave only one leaf in each hole. If watering is not spared, the cardoons will grow with great vigor, and their leaves can reach up to six feet in length. When they are sufficiently formed to be ridged, the soil from the holes is brought together around the plants, and piled as high as possible with a wooden fork. The leaves are brought together to form a sort of bundle, and held in this position with wicker, straw or string ties. They are then covered with a thick shirt of long straw, which is held in place in the same way. The plant is left hidden for three weeks, and this time is always sufficient to soften and whiten the rib. If you were to wait any longer before releasing it for consumption, it would inevitably rot from lack of air and light; as a result, cardoons should only be buttered and tied as necessary. When white frost begins to set in, remove the cardoons and place them in the vegetable greenhouse to protect them from the cold, as they are very sensitive to it. Place them with their root ball in damp sand, after lifting their leaves to make them take up less space, but without squeezing them too tightly, so that air can still circulate between them, which is essential to prevent them from rotting. Some gardeners, lacking sufficient space, are content to place them upright, against a wall and leaning against each other; they often visit them to remove rotten leaves, and to deliver for consumption those whose rot threatens to spread to the heart. If you have a frame at your disposal, it's easy to preserve cardoons without a greenhouse. Choose the hottest, driest spot, open a trench the width of the trunk and three feet deep. Place the box on top, and throw the soil from the trench against its outer walls. A good bed of long, dry straw is placed upright against one side of the trench, and leaned against a row of cardoons; against these a second bed of straw is laid, then a second row of cardoons, and so on until the chassis trunk is filled. Care should be taken to leave the upper third of the length of the leaves exposed to light and air. Once this is done, the glass panels are placed to prevent cold and damp from penetrating to the plants. Whenever the air is dry and it's not freezing, the panels are removed, but they must be rigorously replaced every evening. Some people are content to place their cardoons in an uncovered trench, as described above. They protect them from frost and rain by spreading a thick blanket of dry litter over them, which they take care to remove whenever air can be supplied. Cardoon seeds are harvested in the same way as artichokes. In autumn, a few plants are sheltered as we said for artichokes (see page 31); in spring, they are uncovered and cultivated in the same way as artichokes. The seed retains its germinative properties for five or six years. CARROT (Daucus carota); biennial plant that "sprouts spontaneously in southern Europe, and of which several varieties are grown, namely: The RED ones, often preferred to the others for cooking, because they are generally found to have a spicier taste. The sub-varieties are Short red hative or Dutch, excellent and very early, preferable for autumn and early spring sowing. Its smaller, slightly fatter sub-variety is used almost exclusively by market gardeners in Paris. Rouge-longue, very good, but grown more in Germany than in France. Red pale, from Flanders, good, with the same qualities as yellow. JaUnes, sweet, very good. The sub-varieties are : Long yellow, one of the oldest cultivated varieties, and still considered by many to be one of the best. Young short or round yellow, as good as the previous variety. BLaNCuis, quite good, but especially esteemed for its long, easy shelf-life. Ordinary white, of mediocre quality and not widely grown. Blanche de Breteuil, half-short, often a slightly yellowish color, better than the previous variety. Blanche ronde, very mediocre. Blanche hors de terre, very large, very long, with green collar, quite good. Violet, very sweet and very large, but has the disadvantage of staining broths and stews. Spanish violet, of the same quality and size as the previous one, but usually yellow on the inside, which means it does not stain broth. It is easy to store. PRIMEUR. In order to obtain fresh carrots in winter, spring and early summer, we have subjected these plants to a special cultivation method. Spring-sown carrots keep well through the winter, but they never taste as delicate, nor are they as tender, and it is for these reasons that fresh carrots are preferred as soon as they have reached the size of a little finger or close to it. For the early season, the short rouga from Hollende is the preferred variety, because it is the earliest, the one that rises the least, and one of those that, on the bed, is content with the least thickness of soil. The choice of seeds is not indifferent either, as experience has shown that new varieties produce individuals that grow very quickly. The best seeds are therefore those that are two years old, and older ones should not be sown. It is customary to sow it from September to February on the warm beds prepared for celery, cabbage, lettuce, radishes and supplies; however, as the roots grow quite deep, the bed is reloaded in the area intended for carrots, so that there is eight inches of potting soil; it is advisable, when reloading the bed, to mix two thirds of light earth with the potting soil, which must be thoroughly used up. Carrots sown in potting soil that is too grainy and not sufficiently consumed are prone to staining and bad taste. When the layer is fully warm, sow thickly, cover the seeds with a finger of fine compost, hoe lightly and cover with the frame. In layers where only cloches are used, seeds are sometimes sown in clumps, but in other layers, seeds are broadcast, often between other plants in the empty spaces. Air is given whenever the temperature permits, watering is carried out as needed, and the hard crust that may form on the surface of the soil is carefully broken. As soon as the carrots have emerged, the plant is thinned out by cutting with the nails below the cotiledons the overabundant plants that are to be destroyed; by this means, which should always be used for delicate seedlings, you are never exposed to shaking the roots of the individuals that are to remain. Some time later, when the carrots have gained strength and are showing three leaves, they are thinned out a second time, so that the seedlings are spaced two inches apart. All that's left for the seedlings is the usual care, consisting of new stoves when the warmth of the soil decreases, watering, hoeing, etc. When the carrots have reached their full size, they are ready for planting. When the carrots have reached the right size for the intended use, we pull them out, always choosing those closest together, so that those remaining have more room to develop. Doing as we've just said, you'll have roots of varying size, very tender and very good, until the autumn seedlings in the open ground begin to produce. To grow carrots in spring and early summer, there is no need for beds or frames. Sow the indicated variety in September, with the same care as described in the article on ordinary cultivation, but in light soil with warm exposure. Carrots do not fear the cold, but they are sensitive to it, especially when young. So, when frost sets in, cover them with a good layer of bedding or dry leaves, and give them air whenever the weather permits. In hard winters, such seedlings produce very early. REGULAR CULTIVATION. The quality of carrots depends much more on the type of soil in which they are grown than on the variety to which they belong. In sandy, fertile, deep soils, it is excellent, as well as in those that are free-flowing, soft and substantial. It doesn't like fresh fertilizer, so you need to fertilize the land where you want to plant it at least six months in advance, or at least only with consumed manure; in any other case, it forks, i.e. its root splits into two or more branches. Once the soil has been well loosened and loosened, sowing can begin in February, but with warm exposure, and continue until June, with any exposure. Sow broadcast or in rays spaced six to eight inches apart, covering lightly with a rake or harrowing with a fork. In heavy soils, prone to pounding from rain or watering, spread a fingerful of three-quarter-used potting soil, or a light mulch. If the season is dry, frequent watering helps germination. When carrots begin to sprout, and until they have reached a certain strength, they require a great deal of care, as they are subject to slugs, spiders and other insects that are very avid eaters. As for slugs, there's only one way to get rid of them, and that's to hunt them assiduously in the evenings, mornings and after mild spring rains. Spiders are easily kept at bay by frequent drenching with water infused with a little soot. We hoe from time to time, and keep the seedling free of weeds. When the seedling has no more than three leaves, never later, we start to thin out the thickest areas, so that we can transplant where there is a shortage. This operation requires great care to prevent the transplanted cavottes from forking. We choose a moment when the soil is soaked by a gentle rain; with a florist's hoe, or simply a small wooden palette, we pull out the young plant, taking great care not to break the end of its root; in the place where it is to be transplanted, we make a large hole with the planter, we place the root in it, taking extreme care to place it very vertically and not to bend its end, then we pour soil all around with the tip of the planter. Water immediately and continue to do so until recovery is perfect, but take care not to over-wet. As the carrots grow, you continue to thin them out, and when they are finger-sized, which usually happens around midsummer, you start using the thinned ones for cooking. These can be removed successively for use, until the plants are five or six inches apart, at which point they are no longer touched. In November or December, depending on the severity of the season, the carrots are harvested and stored in the greenhouse. We pull them out with a flat-tined fork, so as to cut less; we clean them of any earth that may be attached to them, cut or twist the leaves above the collar, then, in the greenhouse, we place them in sand, taking care to leave the collar outside and level with the surface of the sand. If you don't have a greenhouse, you can also place them in a cellar or other place sheltered from the rigors of the cold. Air the greenhouse whenever it's safe to do so. When you realize that they have grown a lot of leaves, which can exhaust them or at least rob them of a large part of their qualities, you undo the layer of sand, cut off the collar below the new shoots, and replace them again in the sand, as they were before. With these simple precautions, they can be stored easily and for a very long time. Carrots also keep very well in an open trench in dry soil, but this method is rarely used unless they are grown in large quantities for cattle feed; nevertheless, we'll teach you how. We dig a pit at least four feet deep, four or five wide, and of a length determined by the quantity of carrots to be placed in it. A good bed of rubble or gravel is placed at the bottom, over which a layer of dry sand is spread. This is followed by a bed of carrots, with the snares turned towards the middle of the pit; a bed of sand is then added, followed by a bed of carrots, and so on, alternately, until the trench is full. A good thickness of long straw is spread over the whole, and the earth from the excavation is thrown on top, forming a steep ados to divert rainwater. Some people don't go to such lengths, especially when cultivating dry, light soil. They don't pull the carrots out of the bed where they were grown, but simply defend them against the extreme cold with a good cover of litter, dry leaves or fern. This is certainly the most convenient and economical method, but by no means the safest. It sometimes happens, when heavy frosts follow long rains, that most of the roots perish despite this shelter. Carrot seed retains its germinative capacity for two years. To obtain them, we keep the biggest and best roots in the greenhouse, taking care not to cut their collars, as they would otherwise fail to grow. Sometimes we leave them in the ground, and to protect them from frost, we cover them with a thick layer of litter, which we remove whenever the weather is mild. Those that have been uprooted are replanted in March, in very loose, well-amended soil. They bolt in May, and the seed is harvested in August. The stems are cut or hung in a dry place, and the seeds are not removed from the umbels until it's time to sow them. To rid them of the hairs that cause them to cling and curl up together, they are rubbed in the hands with fine sand or ash. CARUBEAN (ceratonia siliqua); second-growth tree, native to southern France. Its fruit, a foot long, consists of a flat, fleshy silique enclosing a reddish, sweet pulp with its seeds, good for eating when dry, but slightly purgative. PRIIXUR. The carob tree fears frost and hardly ripens its fruit in Paris without special care. It is planted at the foot of a wall in a warm, south-facing position, and trellised. For the first few years, that is, until it is strong enough to blossom, we content ourselveŝte, to protect it from the cold, with stuffing it in the manner of fig trees, but with much more care. When it has developed enough to give hope of obtaining fruit, we stuff it as we said; but in the first days of February, we remove these covers, perfectly net the branches of any garbage that may be heaped on them, and palisate those that need it. Eighteen inches from the base of the tree, a warm layer is erected parallel to the wall, on which glass panels are placed, with their tops resting against the wall above the tree. The sides of this flying greenhouse are closed with planks, the joints of which are sealed with moss to prevent the frost from penetrating. Air is supplied for one or two hours a day, and only when the sun is shining. The bed can be used to sow small vegetables. That's all it takes to advance vegetation and give the tree time to ripen its fruit. The glass panels can only be completely removed at the beginning of June. REGULAR CULTIVATION. Carob trees thrive in free, warm, amended soils with a southern exposure, where they are protected from frost as mentioned above. In the Paris climate, it is wise to have a few plants in orangeries. They can sometimes be propagated by strangling marcottes, but they grow back with great difficulty, and this method of propagation is very time-consuming. It is therefore preferable to use the seedling method. In spring, fill pots with clean, substantial or orange-tree soil, sow a few seeds in each one, and bury them in a warm layer of compost. When the young carob trees have risen, leave just one in each vase, and give them the care ordinary to orangery plants. When the plant is two or three years old, and judged capable of withstanding full soil, it is removed from the vase and 1 44 3 placed with the root ball. The roots should not be touched, and frequent watering should be given to ensure recovery; once it begins to vegetate, watering should be stopped, unless the soil becomes absolutely dry in very hot weather. Carob wood is extremely hard, and is considered incorruptible. CELERY (apium graveolens); biennial plant, growing spontaneously in the marshes of southern France. Cultivation has caused it to lose its pungency, and it is used in cooking, raw or cooked. There are several varieties. Hollow celery, small celery, chopping celery. Leaves are small and spread out, and can hardly be used for anything other than supplies. Dwarf curly celery, very tender, very crisp, used, like the previous one, as a garnish. Full white celery, very large, very fleshy, one of the most widely grown. It has two sub-varieties: Turkish or Prussian celery, which has the same qualities but is larger; and pink celery, which differs only in the pinkish tint of its ribs. Céleri gros violet de Tours. Its ribs are a more pronounced purplish-pink than the previous variety; it is the largest of all the varieties. It is remarkable for the thickness of its ribs and the size of its stalk. Celery root. There are three sub-varieties: long white celeriac, whose root is very large and elongated; round white celeriac, whose root is large and rounded; and red celeriac, whose root is veined with red and purple. The roots of the last three are eaten cooked. PRIMEUR. Early celery is grown for ordinary use, as well as for salads and other purposes. For the latter, hollow celery and curly dwarf celery are grown as follows: on a bed intended for small vegetables, laid out in November and filled with only four inches of compost, sow lightly, cover the seed lightly, baste well and cover with cloches or frames. It is not necessary to provide air until the plants have emerged; simply maintain the humidity of the compost by frequent but moderate watering. Once the celery has risen, start lifting the bells or panels, weather permitting. Thin out so as to leave two or three inches between each plant. As soon as they have three leaves, air becomes much more necessary, and you must give them air whenever you can without inconvenience. We start cutting as soon as the leaves have reached four or five inches in length, and we do this with the care of cutting one plant between two, so as to give the other remaining plants more space until the first ones to be cut have grown back leaves; then we cut the others, and so on alternately. In this way, plants can be grown much closer together without harming each other, and this is all the more advantageous as the great art of early cultivation is to obtain a lot in a little space. We continue to sow on a bed for supply, until the season allows sowing or transplanting in the open ground. Celery grown for other purposes is treated differently. With the precautions described in the article on ordinary cultivation, celery keeps very well until the end of April. Early crops should therefore be cultivated in such a way as to produce celery from May through to August, since seedlings sown in the open ground begin to yield at this time. In December, to obtain produce in May; in January, to obtain it in June; in February, to obtain it in July; in March, to obtain it in August; we sow on beds and under cloches or frames as we shall see. December sowing is done on a warm bed, with three to four inches of compost. Sow lightly, water lightly and cover with cloches or frames. When the plant has risen, air is given and the plants are governed as we have said. When they have three leaves, we transplant them onto a new warm layer filled with six inches of potting soil, two or three inches apart so they don't wilt, and continue the same care until March. At the beginning of this month, in warm, dry soil, establish a muted layer filled with eight inches of potting soil mixed with a small amount of light garden soil, and transplant the celery, staggered, in rays seven to eight inches apart; water, and cover with bells or panels to facilitate recovery. When growth is assured, simply protect the plant from cool nights and cold rains with straw mats or dry litter. Bleaching can begin as early as the end of April. Sowing in January, February and March is done and managed in exactly the same way, but the young plants, instead of being permanently transplanted on a warm bed, are transplanted into the ground as they become strong enough, but not before the beginning of April. In light soil, well amended and loosened, they are transplanted in rays spaced eight to nine inches apart, watered immediately to facilitate recovery, and as this plant is very water-loving, care is taken to keep the soil constantly moist. Needless to say, hoe from time to time, and keep the bed free of weeds. When the celery is strong enough, we blanch it. To do this, you can choose the most advantageous of the methods described below. For example, if you want to enjoy a large quantity at a time, you can butter it; otherwise, you can bind it and cover it several times. REGULAR CULTIVATION. Celery seedlings sown in April, May and June provide good yields from September until the frosts, and until the following spring when the plant is sheltered. The April sowing is usually done in a nursery for transplanting, but the May and June sowings should be done in the field, so that the celery is not delayed by transplanting, and has time to grow to full size before winter. For the nursery, choose a bed in the south, break it up, loosen the soil and mix it with as much compost as you can. Sow very lightly and water frequently. When the seedling is large enough, it is transplanted permanently into beds prepared in the same way as for in-place sowing. Let's take a look at the latter. In clean, substantial soil that is both warm and slightly moist, or in ordinary soil amended with good consumable fertilizers, we prepare beds four feet wide by an indeterminate length. If you want to bind the soil for blanching, leave a path one foot wide between the boards, but if you want to butter the soil, leave a strip three feet wide, so that when the time comes, you can pull out the soil for ridging; you use it to sow lettuces and other plants that will have produced their produce before the first frost. The soil is loosened, a rake is passed over it, and parallel rays are drawn with a string, nine inches apart. Sow the seeds in each ray thickly enough to fill the bed even if half the seeds fail to germinate. Water daily, prick out and thin out the seedlings once they have three leaves. Transplant in places where leaves are lacking, and give the usual care to seedlings. A few days later, when the plant is strong enough to be sure it won't melt any more, we thin out for the last time, so as to leave an eight-inch gap between each plant, and transplant again in the empty places if there are any. The same care is continued until it's time to blanch. To do this, choose dry weather, and bind each plant with three straw or rush ties, after bringing the leaves together in a bundle. The space between the plants is filled with litter, so that only the tips of the leaves are exposed to light and air. Sometimes, after tying, the soil from the flowerbed left next to the plant is thrown back onto the bed and heaped around the plants to a quarter of their height the first time, half that eight days later, and three-quarters another eight days later; the fourth quarter is left uncovered. Some gardeners, to avoid these embarrassments, are content to carefully uproot the celery they want to bleach, and bury it in potting soil, leaving only the tips of the leaves showing. In all cases, it usually turns white within three weeks, and can be used for consumption after that time. Winter celery can be kept in place during the bad season, by means of good earthing-up, but this method has its drawbacks; it is very difficult to dig it up for use when the ground is heavily frozen or covered with snow; then, if prolonged cold rains follow severe cold, most of it rots. It is therefore best to transport it to the vegetable greenhouse, where it is buried in sand up to a third of its length; it is given air whenever you are not afraid of the cold or damp, and is visited often to remove rotten leaves that would spoil the others if left. Celery seedlings are much less successful in very dry soils than in others, unless a method widely used in the south of France is adopted. The soil is thrown in on both sides; once this has been done, the bottom of this kind of pit is chiselled, loosened and perfectly smoked, and the celery is sown and raised as we have said. It finds there a sustained humidity which is very favorable to its vegetation. To butter it, the soil removed before sowing is thrown back onto the boards. To obtain seed, leave a few of the most beautiful plants in place, chosen from among those whose varieties have remained perfectly straightforward. To protect them from the cold, they are mounded up to half their height, and covered with dry litter or, better still, bales of barley or wheat, if you are not afraid of mice, which are often attracted by this kind of covering. Air is provided whenever the temperature permits. In March, the plants are uncovered and dethatched, the rotten leaves are removed, the plant is peeled and soon shows its stems. The seed ripens and is harvested in September; it retains its germinative powers for three or four years, but the newer it is, the better it is. Celery root can be grown in the same way as above, with the difference that it cannot be mounded. To preserve it over winter, simply transport it to the vegetable greenhouse, where it is buried in sand up to the collar. Seed-bearing plants are covered with litter or dry leaves to protect them from frost. CERFEUIL (scandix cerefolium); annual, native, aromatic plant, used in cooking as a garnish and seasoning. A curly-leaved variety is available for the same uses and can be grown in the same way. PRIMEUR. Chervil is only sensitive to very hard frosts, so it produces throughout the winter if sown at the end of September and in October. In countries where the cold is very harsh and snow abundant, it can be sown from December to February, on a warm bed, mixed with other supplies. It rises and supplies promptly, but also passes very quickly, making it necessary to sow at least three or four times during the winter, if you don't want to run the risk of running out. The seedbed is filled with four inches of loam, sown fairly lightly, lightly covered and covered with cloches or frames. The rest is taken care of in the usual way. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Chervil is not at all difficult to grow; it adapts to any soil and any exposure, but as it goes to seed very quickly, we are obliged to sow it every two weeks, in order to always have tender chervil. From March to May, it is sown in warm, loosened and amended soil; from June to the end of August, it is sown in cool or even damp soil, in the shade and with a northern exposure. Autumn sowing takes place at the foot of a wall facing south. During the heat of summer, the only care chervil requires is frequent watering and removal of parasitic weeds. The best seeds are those that ripen in June and July; they keep well for three years, but are safest when new. CERFEUIL MUSQUÉ ou D'ESPAGNE (scandiz odorata); perennial plant, native to Spain, larger than the preceding one, used for the same purposes, but with a stronger, distinctive flavor that is not to everyone's taste. Its fleshy, rather large root is used in soup like carrots and parsnips, and gives it a very pleasant taste; cooked 2 in broth, many people eat it with pleasure. PRIMEUR. Musk chervil is so widely used that it is rarely grown on the ground. Moreover, if we were to do so, it would only be to obtain supplies, as the plant is perennial and grows in the open ground, and we can pull up its roots throughout the winter for consumption. To obtain tender leaves during the frosts, all you have to do is prepare a spot on a warm bed with large vegetables, so that the soil is thick enough, remove a few plants of musk chervil sown the previous year with the root ball, plant them there, and cover them with cloches or frames. With a little watering, the plants will recover easily, and as they develop leaves, they can be cut for use. "ORDINARY CULTIVATION: This species thrives in free-light soils with very warm exposure. As it is rather averse to transplanting, it should be sown in the field as soon as the seeds are mature, otherwise they will only germinate very slowly, and several months after sowing; when the seeds are more than a year old, they no longer germinate. As the plant is quite large, we thin it out and leave at least a foot between each one. If you want to make use of the roots, water them abundantly, so that they are both tender and large. CHERRY (Prunus Cerasus.); native to Africa, where Lucullus brought it. We place next to it the MxRISIER, (Prunus avium,) which grows spontaneously in all the forests of Europe. These two species have produced a large number of varieties, of which we will mention only the main and best. As far as we can, we will relate them to the types that have been recognized by growers. 1. wild cherry. Guignier with large black fruit. Large, blackish fruit with soft, plump pulp, ripening in June. Large white fruit. White fruit, reddish on the sunny side, very good, ripening in June. Guignier with pink fruit. Watery, tender-red fruit, ripening around June 15, quite good. Guignier with large, shiny black fruit. Fruit with red pulp, excellent, ripening at the end of June. Guignier with large black fruit and short stalk. Fruit similar to the previous one, but with a shorter stalk, excellent. This guignier, which ripens at the end of June, is the best of all. Guignier à rameaux pendans. Large, shiny black fruit with elongated stalk, very good, ripening in July. 2. Bigarreautier. Bigarreautier with large red fruit. Reuge fruit, large, good, ripening at the end of July. Bigarreautier with large white fruit. Fruit very similar to the previous one, but whitish on one side and lighter red on the other, of better quality, ripening at the end of July. Belle de Rocmont, cœur-de-pigeon. Fruit less large and elongated than the preceding one, with marbled skin, tresbon, ripening around mid-July. Bigarreautier with flesh-colored fruit. Similar to the previous one, just as good, but smaller. Ripens at the same time. Gros Cœuret. Depressed, heart-shaped fruit, shiny, almost black when ripe, i.e. in August. 3. Cherry trees. Cherry from Holland or England. Large, black, very good fruit, ripening in early June. Prussian cherry. Fruit large, turbinates, dark color, very good, ripe at the end of June. Short-tailed Montmorency cherry. Medium-sized, round, compressed fruit with watery pulp, good, ripens in early August. Large Montmorency cherry. Large, compressed, bright-red fruit, excellent, ripening in July. Not a very productive tree. Gros Gobet. Medium-sized, compressed fruit, more deeply furrowed than the previous one, excellent, ripening in July. Cerise de Villènes. Small, very sweet, yellowish-pink fruit, ripening in June. Early dwarf. Small fruit, of mediocre quality, but very valuable in that it ripens from the end of April to the beginning of May, and is the most suitable variety for forcing. Late English or Royal. Large, very dark red, excellent fruit, ripening in July. It has a sub-variety with almost black fruit. Belle de Choisy, Cerise de la Palembre. Medium red fruit, ripening in July. Cerise de Varennes. Large, compressed fruit, excellent red color, ripening in August. Cerise de la Madeleine. Large, red fruit with soft pulp, good, ripening at the end of July. Cherry Duck. Large, red, slightly compressed fruit with sweet pulp, excellent, ripens in August. Late cherry or All Saints' cherry. Small, acidic, red fruit, ripening successively from June to the first white frosts. M. Noisette has obtained a sub-variety with larger fruit. 4 Griottier. Griottier ordinaire. Medium-sized fruit, black, good, ripening in September and October, and often kept on the tree until November 15th, espaliered to the north. German sour cherry. Medium-sized fruit with red pulp, very poor quality, ripening around mid-July. Griottier de Portu gal. Fruit less acid than the preceding ones, ripening in August. PRIMEUR. Using the various varieties we have just listed, cherries can be obtained naturally from the end of April to the fifteenth of November. To get cherries in winter, you need to heat them, and there are several ways of doing this. The most common is the flying greenhouse, the construction and use of which are described in the article on Apricots. Cherry trees are treated in the same way as other trees, and require similar care, so we won't come back to this point. It is also heated when planted in the greenhouse, and for details of this process, we refer the reader to the article on Vines. It is best grown as an A-grown apricot. We'll now deal with the question of how to heat them in a greenhouse vase. Several varieties can be grown in this way, but those with the best chances of success are: the early dwarf cherry, the English hätive, Dutch broad-leaved, Prussian, cherry-gin, royal hätive and late English cherry. Three or four years in advance, we prepare the trees to be forced by grafting them onto mahalèb, four or six inches from the ground. They are pruned into bushes or cattails, letting them spread out their wood only to the extent of the space they will require in the greenhouse. When the plant is the right size, in autumn, it is lifted with the rootball, and planted in a crate or basket, in good free soil, half mixed with potting compost. Leave it for a year, to allow it to recover, and during this time give it all the care it needs, such as burying the vase, watering, hoeing, etc. Persoons who are not afraid to make the necessary expenditure to raise these subjects in vases (boxes or pots), from their youth, will always achieve more satisfactory success. In any case, from October onwards, a warm layer of half horse manure and half dry leaves is prepared in a greenhouse or a tarpaulin. When the heat is at its peak, holes deep enough to accommodate the vases or baskets are made in the layer, at distances calculated according to the width of the cherry trees. A sufficient thickness of compost is then spread over the entire layer, to ensure that the trees have a well-filled collar, and that the edges of the vases do not show. The trees must be spaced far enough apart, so that air and light can circulate perfectly between all their branches, even when they are leafed out: it is also essential that they are as close as possible to the glasses. We start heating, and it doesn't take long for the gems to swell and develop into leaves and flower buds. Calculate the time of flowering, and don't be afraid to bring it forward or delay it a little, by lowering or raising the heat, so as to take advantage of a few days of sunshine, which is always found in ordinary winters, so that the flowering and fertilization are protected by the light of its rays. If you're lucky enough to seize this favorable moment, almost all the fruit will set, which doesn't always happen when the sky is dark and cloudy. This is a general rule that should be applied to all species of fruit trees, without exception. In any case, the ordinary scins are given in the article on apricot trees. When a currant tree has borne fruit, M you want to keep 1 for another year, don't hurry to put it back in the ground, especially if frosts are still to be feared. Leave it in the greenhouse until March, or move it to an orangery. In spring, we place it in the open ground with good exposure, but nevertheless leaving it in its vase, or, which is much better, placing it with its root ball in a new basket. It is pruned very short to develop wood in the first year. In the second spring, we give it the usual pruning, and the following autumn, if we think it has recovered well enough, we can heat it again. Some trees can only withstand this kind of cultivation for a few years, and perish after being heated twice; others, on the contrary, last a very long time. We need hardly say that to ensure a succession of fruit harvests in the greenhouse, you must not place all the trees in the greenhouse at the same time, but rather from month to month or fortnight to fortnight. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Cherry trees grow well in all types of soil; however, they only develop their full beauty and longevity in deep, light soils, which are dry for bigarreautier and merisier, and a little cool for cherry and griottier, but not wet. All of them also fear clay soils and stagnant humidity. In the granitic mountains of the Rhône department, I have seen wild or grafted cherry trees grow with astonishing speed and vigor, reaching a height of sixty to eighty feet, in soils where rye can only provide a small harvest every eight or ten years. Cherry trees can be propagated by seed or offspring, but the latter are never as robust as the former, and are almost always attacked by pomom disease. In many countries, we don't bother to sow seedlings, because there are plenty of young trees in the woods suitable for transplanting to the nursery. Varieties are propagated by grafting, on wildings or subjects from wild cherry seeds, if you want to grow large trees; on griottier for medium-sized trees; and on mahaleb, when you want to grow them in cattails or espaliers. We would point out that when grafting onto cherry trees, preference should be given to those with red or white fruit, as the graft is stronger and the trees less prone to gum. In marly or chalky soils, mahaleb provides the best subjects. In addition, it can be grafted with a dormant or growing eye, and even with a split when the stems are a little strong. Cherry trees are not pruned, but they should be pruned for the first two or three years, to give them a nice head. Pruning of espaliers and cattails is carried out according to the same principles as for other stone trees; only, for espaliers, the small fruit-bearing branches that have grown in front are retained. When a cherry tree begins to age, thin out and produce little fruit, it is pruned back to the large branches or even to the trunk, and vigorous scions grow which produce a lot for a few years. CHESTNUT (fagus Castanea); a large, indigenous tree, producing a generally well-known fruit. Several varieties are grown, the main ones being: Chataigne égalade, or exalade, slightly flattened, very large and excellent. The tree produces a lot, but quickly wears out. Pourtalonne, very productive, large and good. La verte du limousin, which is large, good and keeps very well. Maron, known by the names of the country where it is grown, for example: maron de Lyon, de Luc, d'Agen, d'Aubray, etc. This is the biggest, best and most sought-after variety. PRIMEUR. It's impossible to force chestnuts into cultivation, but they can be kept all year round. By leaving them in their husks and placing them in dry sand, wheat or bran, in a dry but cool place, you can easily get green ones until April. If you want to enjoy them for longer, as soon as they are picked, they must be dried first in the sun, then in an oven after being baked. Thus treated, they can be stored for two years without difficulty, provided they are kept in a dry place. ORDINAIRX CULTIVATION. Chestnut thrives in deep, granite soils, and does poorly in damp, clayey soils. It is propagated from seed, and to preserve varieties, it is grafted into flutes or growing eyes. Chestnuts are stratified in January; in spring, they are planted or nursed, producing vigorous trees that are pruned every year until their stems have reached a suitable height. If planted in a nursery, holes are dug at least a year in advance. Plant them in autumn, and when they are well established, i.e. one year after planting, graft them and leave them to nature's care. However, care must be taken to protect them from the teeth of cattle, by wrapping thorn bundles around their trunks. We should point out that the chestnut tree fears shade very much, and usually becomes sterile when deprived of the influences of sun and light. When several are planted, they should therefore be spaced appropriately. When a tree becomes old, its large branches are cut back to within three or four feet of the trunk, more or less, depending on their size; a new head is formed, which produces little fruit, to be sure, but very large and of excellent quality. CHENILLETTE (Scorpiurus vermiculata); annual plant, from the south of France, remarkable for its spiral-rolled, bristly, caterpillar-like pods. They are sometimes used as salad greens, with the intention of surprising people who don't know them, and making them mistake them for disgusting insects. In the past, they were preserved in vinegar and used in the same way as capers. It would be pointless to produce early versions of this plant, so it can only be grown in ordinary conditions. It thrives in light, sandy, warm soils, but does well in all, provided it has good exposure. Sow in April, and the seeds ripen perfectly during the summer. If you want to preserve them in vinegar, pick them before they are fully developed, otherwise they will be too hard to eat. We can also cultivate other species, all curious for their pods: for example, the SCORPIURUS RUDX, (Scorpiurus muricata,) whose pod is slender, slightly striated, with small, scattered, short and inconspicuous tubercles on the back, straight in its lower part and curving towards the top, so as to resemble the larva of certain insects. SCORPIURX VELU, (Scorpiurus subvillosa,) and SCORPIURUS SILLONNÉ, (Scorpiurus sulcata,) whose pods are spiral-shaped like snails. CHERVIS, GIROLLE (Sium sitarum); native perennial plant with fleshy, sweet, slightly aromatic roots, eaten like the scorsonera, mainly fried. 42 Since chervis does not fear frost and is perennial, it is not necessary to cultivate it early, since its roots are available all year round. It's true that those that haven't gone to seed are always the most delicate and therefore the best, so it's a good idea to adopt a cultivation method that can provide young ones all year round. To this end, we sow in spring those that are to be eaten over winter, and in autumn those destined to be eaten the following summer. This plant likes soft, fresh, deep soil, amended a few months in advance or at least with used compost, and perfectly loosened. Sow in April and September, and roots from the first sowing can be harvested as early as November. Seeds are sown in rays, or broadcast, and covered with a rake. Once they have emerged, hoe and weed them, and above all water them very frequently if you want to obtain large, tender tubers, as this plant is very water-loving. Chervis can also be propagated by separating its tubers, with the precaution of leaving a piece of the old stem neck with each one; but it has been noted that the roots obtained by this process never have the delicacy of those resulting from sowing. Chervis produces stems in the same year as sowing, but the seeds they produce are usually sterile. They should therefore only be collected from two-year-old plants. They ripen in September, and retain their germinative qualities for three years; nevertheless, the youngest are the best. WILD CHICORIUM (Cichorium intrbus); native perennial, eaten as a salad when very young, or after blanching. Three varieties are grown: The common variety, found wild on paths, and the most common in gardens. variegated, with red-veined leaves. Coffee chicory, whose long, fleshy, parsnip-like roots are used to replace and, more often, adulterate coffee when, after drying, they have been roasted and ground into powder. Its leaves are eaten in salads like those of the previous two varieties. PRIMEUR. Chicory can be grown in the ground all winter long, but it is hard and almost unbearably bitter, so if you want good winter chicory, grow it on a bed. If you don't grow a large quantity, you can grow it on a bed with small vegetables, because, as it is cut very young and when the leaves have reached two to three inches in length, four inches of soil are enough for it. The first sowing, under cloches or trays, can be done in November, and we continue to sow every twenty days so as not to run out during the winter. Sow fairly thickly, covering the seeds sparingly, and basking frequently. Don't stop sowing on the bed until you can safely entrust the seeds to the soil. This young chicory cannot be blanched. A very white, etiolated chicory known as Barbe-de-capucin is sold on the markets, and is considered an excellent winter salad. We'll explain how it's obtained. Depending on the quantity required, there are different procedures. If you only want it for home use, you grow it in barrels, but if you want it for speculation, you blanch it in layers. Here's how it's done. In autumn (rarely in any other season, although this process can be used every month of the year), take an old barrel, split on one side, and drill round holes seven or eight lines in diameter, in the following order. Assuming the barrel is standing on its bottom, make a row of holes all the way around, four inches from the ground, and spaced an inch or two apart. Make a second row four inches above, a third at the same distance, and so on, all the way to the top of the barrel, which should be placed in a warm, dark cellar. Once the barrel has been laid out in this way, obtain wild chicory roots from a year's sowing, and take care not to break them when pulling them out. After spreading a layer of sand in the bottom of the barrel, up to the level of the first row of holes, lay the roots on top, so that the collars pass through the holes, but do not overflow them by more than a line or two. Add a second bed of sand up to the height of the second row of holes, and put in a new row of roots; and so on, until the barrel is full. Water each bed of sand as you go, or use damp sand, so that you don't need to water when the barrel is full, which would cause the planting to sag and the root collars to pull out of the holes through which they are to grow. If you only had coarse sand that was too raw or washed out, you could mix in a little potting soil. If planted in October or November, it produces very good lettuce all winter long, and a row can be cut every two or three days. If you intend to sell the capuchin beard in tins, use a different method. Using sand, potting soil or even light earth, a first bed two inches thick, two to three feet wide and of indeterminate length is laid in a cellar or other dark, warm place. The chicory roots are laid on top, horizontally and with their heads outwards; then a second bed of soil and roots is made in the same way, a third, and so on, up to a suitable height. The beds should be placed slightly back, one on top of the other, to form a sloping layer and avoid 8 landslides. Water well, and soon the long, etiolated, colorless leaves appear, as in the previous crop. The roots are pulled out of the bed, bundled and delivered to the trade. Market gardeners often blanch chicory in a simpler way. They either pull it into bunches and bury the bunches in hot manure, or leave them on the ground and cover them with dry manure. This method is expeditious, but no better, because no new leaves grow, and it's the old ones that turn white and always remain hard and bitter. ORDIKARY CULTIVATION. Wild chicory does well in any soil deep enough for it to spread its long tap roots, but it thrives best in substantial free soil. To obtain young leaves all year round, it must be sown every two weeks. Sowing starts at the end of April, in light, loamy soil; later sowing continues in heavier soil. Sow thickly, water frequently and weed if necessary. Those intended for blanching or graining are sown light, in rays, in spring. The leaves it produces in the course of the year are used only to feed cattle and rabbits. The seed-bearing plants, which are left in the ground without any care, overwinter very well. The following spring, they rise, and their seeds, which keep for ten years or more, ripen in September, WHITE CHICORICE OR ERDIVE (Cichorium endivia); annual plant, native to India. It is eaten as a salad, or cooked and seasoned in various ways. Two races are grown, each of which has produced several varieties. 1° CURLY CHICORY. This breed has produced four varieties: Chicorée de Meaux or Endive, once almost the only variety grown. It fills out slowly, and rises very quickly if sown before June and given a little too much water. grosse frisée or grande endive, with the same disadvantages as the previous variety. Chicorée d'été or Chicorée d'Italie, which fills out better and more quickly than the preceding varieties, is very good for spring sowing because it doesn't climb as much, and can also be sown in autumn. Chicory is always white, with almost no filling, but excellent for cutting young like wild chicory. 2° ESCAROLE CHICORY. This breed includes four varieties: Grande Scarole or Scarole de Hollande, with broad, elongated leaves, fast-growing and therefore suitable only for second-season sowing. The round Scarole, with smaller, rounded leaves, grows more quickly, and has a very rounded, almost pomaceous heart. Blonde or lettuce-leaved, yellowish at birth, very good, but delicate and very sensitive to humidity. La courte or Célestine, very small, but very tender, with little height, and the earliest of all. It has become quite rare in Paris. PRIEUR. Preferred varieties for this type of cultivation are: Itafie chicory, white chicory, white escarole and Célestine. In November, we sow chicory that is always white, on a warm bed and under cloches or frames to cut the young leaves, or the other three varieties to be delivered for consumption only when they have formed. The first is sown fairly thickly, on three or four inches of compost, and sowing should take place every fortnight, if you want to have a constant supply. The most important thing is to give only as much water as is necessary for vegetation, otherwise the humidity, which these plants fear greatly, will melt them before they have produced their products. It is also essential that the beds are constantly maintained at a good level of heat, either by means of reheating or reshuffling, or by renewing them as needed; otherwise, the seeds will rot and grow poorly. Air is given when it is safe to do so, and to let them enjoy the light they need, doormats are removed during the day, whenever it is not freezing, or when there is no fear of frost penetrating under the glass. Seeds of all varieties refuse to germinate if buried, so after throwing them into the soil, simply press them down with the flat of your hand or a small plank palette. Chicory, which is always white, is cut as soon as its leaves have reached three or four inches in length. As for the varieties to be left to form, you can start sowing at the same time, and continue until March. The beds should be kept very warm and covered with six inches of compost. Sow lighter, do not cover the seeds, water moderately, and give air and light as much as possible. Summer chicory, or Italian chicory, is the most successful in this type of cultivation. When the plant is strong enough, it is transplanted onto a new layer, always under a bell and under a frame; it is protected as much as possible from humidity, which is very harmful, and if no care is neglected, chicory can be ready for consumption as early as February. Sometimes, when the season is warm, March seedlings can be transplanted in the open ground, but in warm conditions, in well-soiled beds under cloches. If you want to get very beautiful and good results from January and February seedlings, prepare a muted bed in dry, south-facing soil, plant the seedlings in it, and cover with glass panels. Cloches can also be used, filling the gaps between them with dry manure and bedding. In case of rain, cover with straw mats. There are two ways to grow chicory in December and January: 1° to keep plants grown in the open ground in the vegetable greenhouse; 2° to prick out on bedding. It's the latter that we're going to deal with here. From the beginning of September to October, we sow Meaux chicory in an earthen bed at the foot of a south-facing wall, and leave it there until the approach of the white frosts. Then raise a warm layer filled with six inches of potting soil, remove the plant with the rootball, and plant it in the layer, taking care to water it immediately to help it take root. Cover with cloches or trays, and grow as usual. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: All chicory varieties degenerate rapidly if not properly cared for. Sowing should begin in mid-March or, if it's not a spring year, in early April, and continue until the end of July. The varieties that do best in spring are summer chicory, always white, round escarole and Celestine; the others are prone to bolting if sown before June. In March and April, sow on a south-facing terrace, and later in a bed of light, loose earth, suitably top-dressed. After spreading the seedlings, mulch them to prevent rainwater or watering from beating the soil. It differs only in its stem, which is thin at the base and increases in size from the middle to the top. It has the same qualities, and is planted at the same distance. Chou-Caulet de Flandre. A sub-variety of the first, differing only in the red color of its stem and leaves. Suitable for the same uses and planted at the same distance apart. Chou branchu du Poitou, or Chou mille-tête, grows less tall than the preceding varieties, but produces a large number of leafy stems. It is extremely productive, and can be planted at the same distance as the previous varieties. Daubenton's perennial cabbage has many similarities with the previous one, but differs in that its lower branches are very long, and are inscribed on the ground, where they sometimes take root. It is planted at the same distance as the former. The large green Chon frisé from the north is quite similar to the cavalier, but it grows a little lower, and its paler green leaves are cut and pleated in a very elegant way. Like the two sub-varieties that follow, it is very hardy and resists even the hardest frosts. They are planted two feet apart. Last year, I propagated it with cuttings that have taken root perfectly, but I don't yet know what the final result will be. Scottish kale. A slightly smaller sub-variety, with narrower, more fringed leaves. They are planted two feet apart, as is the variety that follows. Green northern kale, which differs only in the red tint of its stems and leaves. The elegance of these last three cabbages makes them real ornamental plants. The buds that grow in spring are thin, long and very tender, and are eaten by some people as asparagus. variegated cabbage is small, with red and yellowish-white variegated leaves on a green background. It is good, but as it produces little, it is grown more for curiosity than for use. They are planted at eighteen inches. Cabbage with proliferating leaves, obtained by M. Vilmorin, is small, not very productive, but very unusual because of the foliaceous expansions that are implanted on the veins of its leaves. It is grown as a curiosity, with plants spaced eighteen inches apart. Proliferous variegated cabbage differs from the above only in the red and yellowish variegation of its leaves. It is grown in the same way. Palmetto grows taller than its predecessors; the stem is bare and very straight, ending in a rosette of long, narrow, blistered, dark-green leaves. It is an excellent, fairly productive plant, but more delicate than all its predecessors and, above all, much more sensitive to cold. Experience has shown that those sown in July and August are more resistant to frost than those sown in spring. They are planted two feet apart. Naples cabbage is another variety that is just as delicate as the previous one, and needs to be treated in the same way. It has a short, swollen stem and flat, smooth leaves with fringed edges. These last two varieties are native to Italy. Green cabbage with large ribs is an excellent variety, but only acquires all its qualities once it has been through the most severe frosts. Its leaves are smooth, large, rounded and of a beautiful green color; the petiole is broad, full and fleshy. This variety, like the next two, can be sown from May 15th to the end of June, and the young plants transplanted permanently, two feet apart, in July and August. Blond cabbage is preferred to the previous variety by many people, because its leaves are softer and have a milder flavor. It is also hardy. Cabbage with large collars and fringed leaves differs from the previous varieties only in its green leaves with fringed edges. It can be grown in the same way, and is no more sensitive to cold. As all these cabbages produce in winter as well as in summer, they do not need to be forced, so we will only consider them from the point of view of their ordinary cultivation. It is customary to sow them in nurseries in March and April, in well-soiled and perfectly loosened beds with good exposure. In April and May, i.e. when the plant is strong enough, it is permanently repotted, spacing each variety as we have said. Ordinary care is given, and it produces in winter and spring. In July and August, sow in beds with northern exposure, water frequently and transplant permanently in September and November. The plants from these seedlings produce during the following summer. Nevertheless, these times are not so rigorous that you can't advantageously sow every month from spring to autumn. All green cabbages, with the exceptions we have mentioned in each article where necessary, are grown in the same way. They do very well broadcast and in open fields, provided the soil is prepared with good fertilizers and ploughing. In this case, they are very profitable as green fodder for the cowshed during the bad season. II. MILAN cabbage, or Poutis-raisis. Cabbages of this breed are easy to recognize by their short stalks, loosely packed heads, and more or less embossed and braided leaves. They are usually softer and more delicate than cabbages, and taste much less like musk. A large number of varieties are available: The very hative Milan d'Ulm, an excellent variety, very easy to peel, of mediocre size, with a fairly high stem. Milan hatif, with the same qualities as the previous one, but with beautiful green leaves and a lower stem. Milan court, or dwarf, tender, excellent, fairly quick to peel, small and stocky, very dark green, slightly slate-colored. Pancalier de Tourrainc, very good and frost resistant, short stem, very dark green leaves, very short, crinkled at the edges, strong ribs and prominent veins. Ordinary Savoy or large Savoy cabbage. Bigger head than previous varieties; tender green leaves; excellent. Pancalier blond, quite similar to Pancalier de Tourraine, but paler green and slightly yellowish; less resistant to frost. Milan à téte longue, with elongated, small, pointed buds; dark green, blistered, frizzy leaves. Very tender and very good. Milan doré or Savoy, with very curly, blond-green leaves that turn yellowish in winter. Low stems; medium-sized, rounded, loosely packed, excellent apples. This variety has no fear of frost. Milan des vertus or large German kale. Apple as large as that of a large cabbage, which it resembles a little in the way it is made, and in its leaves, which are less blistered than in the preceding varieties and sometimes of a glaucous green. This very vigorous variety, suitable for large-scale cultivation, requires good soil; it resists the cold well and, like ordinary kite and paucalier, keeps longer in winter than white cabbage. Do not sow later than April. The second-season German milan is tender and excellent; its apple, white on the inside, is smaller than that of the previous variety; its leaves are dark green. Milan d'automne or gros Choux frisé de Saint-Denis, has a very large, slightly elongated head; its leaves are slightly blistered, rounded, broad and pale green; its stem is large and fairly high. This variety is tender and very good. Brussels sprouts, or rosette cabbage, has a fairly small main head, but its stem, sometimes two to three feet high, produces small, curly, tender, excc.ient apples that are picked as they grow. The head is cut off last. Russian cabbage, sent to M. Vilmorin from Boston, has leaves cut to the rib, in narrow, irregular and stiff strips; its stem is a foot high, its apple round, of medium size, very tender and excellent. Savoy cabbages can be stored well until spring, so there's no need to grow them as early as possible. They are sown in two seasons: 1. from the end of February to May; 2. in August and September. The earliest are sown in June, and they follow one another without interruption until the frosts. When the young plant has been sown in a well-drained and loosened bed, watered, weeded and carefully thinned out, it can be planted out after a month or five weeks. Transplant in rows or staggered rows, spacing the larger species three feet apart and the smaller ones eighteen inches apart. They require only the usual care, i.e. watering during the heat, and hoeing from time to time. When the frosts come, you can leave outside those whose head is not yet fully formed, and particularly the Tourraine pancalier and the ordinary milan, as they finish peeling very well, either in the interval between the frosts, or when they are over. Varieties that fear the cold more, such as the blond pancalier, the long-headed kite, etc., are lifted from the ground with the root ball, and after having detached part of their large leaves, they are taken to the vegetable greenhouse, where they are buried in dry sand up to the bottom of the apple. They keep perfectly well until March or even April. Savoy cabbages can also be stored without a vegetable greenhouse. Before the cold weather sets in, a little soil is removed from the base of each cabbage in the first row, and on the north side, the cabbages are tilted into these sort of trenches, but with as little uprooting as possible. The holes in the second row are started, and the soil is thrown over the roots and stems of those in the first, continuing in this way until all the rows have been laid down. In this way, rainwater slides over the leaves and prevents it from penetrating the heart of the plant, which would inevitably cause it to rot. During hard frosts, we throw a little straw or bedding over the cabbages to protect them from the excess cold. To save space, by making them occupy a much smaller area, you can also dig a pit one foot deep by a width and length determined by the number of cabbages you want to shelter. We pull them out, or carry them into the pit, and place them there, one against the other, in an inclined position, and throw a little earth on their roots. During frosts, they are covered with straw mats, long litter or dry leaves. Finally, some people are content to store them in a cellar, where they keep quite well if it's neither too hot nor too dry. 9 this breed are numerous and widely cultivated; their heads are very tight and very hard, but they often have a musky odor that many people dislike. Cabbage is used to make sauerkraut (saüer kraut). We will list the varieties in order of earliness, as established by M. Vilmorin. 1 - Yorck cabbage. Very precious and highly prized, its apples are small and very elongated. It has several sub-varieties: Cabbage of Yorck cabage or superfin hatif. Similar to the above, but smaller and a few days earlier. Hätif dwarf Yorck cabbage. As early as the previous one, and similar to it, but with a rounder head and shorter stem. The large Yorck cabbage, with a larger head but slower forming. 2. hatif sugarloaf cabbage. Excellent variety, with slightly blond, slightly hooded leaves; elongated head, sometimes in the shape of an inverted cone, very tender, not very prone to splitting. 3. Beef heart cabbage. Produces three excellent sub-varieties: Small, early, forming its head almost at the same time as Yorck cabbage. The medium-sized variety, larger and less early, is similar to the large white cabbage. Large, with a more rounded apple shape. 4 Large white cabbage or cabbage head. It offers many varieties that are generally larger, rounder and more widely grown than the previous ones: Chou de Saint-Denis or Chou blanc de Bonneuil. Very short stems; very glaucous leaves; large, flattened or round head. Second-season Alsace cabbage. Stem short; leaves rounded, a little hooded, detached; large, rounded or flattened head, forming quickly. Gros Chou d'Alzace, from Germany, or Chou-Quintal. Its stem is short and very thick; its leaves, paler green than those of the preceding varieties, are broad and a little scalloped; in new, substantial, fresh soil, its head acquires enormous volume. Gros Chou tardif d'Allemagne. It differs from the previous variety in having a slightly longer, but very thick stem, and dark green leaves with coarse, whitish veins. Its apples are very large, tight, tender and excellent, but later. Large German cabbage with purple collars. It differs from the previous variety only in the color of the leaf ribs, and in the even slower-forming head. Large Scottish cabbage. Very large, slow-growing, but hardier than the others, more resistant to cold and easy to store. Gros Chou-Cabus de Hollande, or Chou-Cauve, very similar to the above, but a little less hardy. Short-stalked Dutch cabbage, an excellent sub-variety, with medium-sized apples, but forming more quickly than other cabbages. Stubby Brunswisck cabbage. Its stem is very short; its leaves are few, short and rounded; its head is tight, flattened and of medium size. 5. Red cabbage. Two sub-varieties are grown: Large red cabbage, short-stemmed, with leaves that start out an obscure green washed with purple, then turn red, and have reddish-brown ribs. It has a large, loosely-packed head, which some people eat in salads. Blackish Utrecht cabbage, much smaller, with a slightly taller stem, blood-red leaves and a tighter head. Its head leaves, cut into small strips and pickled in vinegar like gherkins, are considered excellent. The German red cabbage has a stronger stem and larger head than the previous variety, whose color it also resembles. AUTUMN SOWING. It's easy to grow cabbage all year round, without forcing, by autumn sowing. Yorck cabbage and other small early varieties are sown at the end of August (never before), in well-soiled beds with good exposure. Ordinary soius are given, consisting of basinage, etc. In October, along a wall facing south, we prepare good ados, well loosened and amended with half-consumed manure, and transplant the young plants, fifteen or eighteen inches apart. Care is taken with them, and if winter is very harsh, it's a good idea to mulch the bed with two or three inches of good manure, which you bury in the spring by hoeing. These cabbages, if planted in good soil and with a warm exposure, start to produce around the fifteenth of April, and give until May, when they are replaced by the following varieties. Ordinary varieties can be sown from the fifteenth of August to the fifteenth of September, with the same care. In October, they can be planted permanently or transplanted from a nursery into a bed facing south, where they will overwinter and only be planted in February and March.
