Depending on the size of the variety, space them from eighteen inches to three feet apart. Don't spare them any water, especially when they're young. As we have said, they succeed Yorck cabbages and produce until August. SPRING SOWING. In February, a warm bed is prepared and filled with five to six inches of a mixture of three-quarters old compost and one-quarter light garden soil. At the beginning of the month, we sow cabbages in this layer, which we cover with cloches or frames when it freezes and during the night. In the last days of March, they are transplanted into the ground and planted out, and produce in September. The second spring sowing is done at the end of February and beginning of March. In a well-soil bed with a warm exposure, at the foot of a wall facing south, sow lightly and plant out in the first fortnight of April, and produce at the end of September and during October. The third sowing is done in March, in loosened and, above all, well-soiled soil. Transplant at the end of April, and the cabbages harvest in November and December. Cabbages from the last season keep well until spring, if treated as we said for savoy cabbages. IV. RAVES. These varieties are grown for their leaves, but more particularly for their root or collar, which becomes large and fleshy, and is used like turnips. There are three varieties, which have given rise to a number of sub-varieties, namely: I. Siamese cabbage. It is easily recognized by its swollen stem above ground, forming a ball that gives rise to leaves on its top and sides. It has three sub-varieties: White, with a white ball; it is later than the other three. Violet, with a violet stem and leaf ribs of varying shades of violet. The dwarf hative, with small, sparse leaves, forms its ball very quickly. Sown from May 1st to July 15th. a. Choux-Naveis, Choux Turneps, Choux de Laponie. This breed is recognized by its fleshy, thick root, shaped like a large, long turnip and tasting like kohlrabi. Like its predecessors, it is not affected by frost. Three sub-varieties are grown: Ordinary Turnip-Cabbage, with large white roots. Hátif, a little smaller, but much quicker to produce, and more tender; white root. Chou-Navet à collet rouge, with the ribs and veins of the leaves dyed this color. When cooking kohlrabi, be careful to remove the bark to a certain thickness. 3. Rutabago cabbage, or Swedish turnip. Very similar to turnip cabbage, but yellowish, cleaner, quicker to produce and more delicate, it is preferred for cooking. It is sown in the light from the fifteenth of May to the fifteenth of July. All kohlrabi are cold-hardy, and are left in the ground to be used as needed, so there's no need to grow them as early crops. Siam cabbages are sown in May and June, in a nursery, in a bed of light, well-soiled soil. In July and August, break up a square, smoke it with half-consumed fertilizer, and transplant in staggered rows, placing the young plants fifteen inches apart.

of the others. They require some watering and weeding, but are otherwise quite hardy. In regions with heavy frosts, it's a good idea to keep them in a vegetable greenhouse, or other dry place sheltered from the cold. To do this, carefully uproot them, remove their leaves and bury half of them in dry sand. They can also be preserved by simply opening a trench in dry soil, removing their leaves and covering them with a thick layer of litter or dry leaves. Turneps can be grown in exactly the same way, except that they don't need to be transplanted. In this case, sow in a plot prepared by deep ploughing, in rays or broadcast, and thin out the plants so as to leave fifteen or eighteen inches between each one. Transplant where necessary. Rutabaga can be sown in the field, very lightly, at the times we have indicated in this article; however, it can also be sown in a nursery, in which case it is permanently transplanted in July and August. It is very hardy and frost-resistant. Kohlrabi of all breeds can be stored perfectly well in the vegetable greenhouse or in a trench, trained as we said about Siam cabbage. V. cauliflower (Brassica botrytis, by M. Desfontaines) Two quite distinct breeds of cauliflower are grown: 1. cauliflower proper; a. broccoli. They are much more highly esteemed than the former because of their delicacy. Everyone is familiar with the use made of their heads, formed by an immense quantity of flower buds: 1. cauliflowers. Their stems are lower than those of broccoli, and they are much earlier. The tender or "hatif" variety is smaller than the next two, and its less tightly packed head divides fairly quickly into flowering shoots; its leaves are less broad, more united and straighter. The three varieties differ more in quality than in any other characteristic. The semi-hard variety is somewhere between hard and soft, both in appearance and in quality. The hard variety has a large, short stalk; its leaves are large and much more wavy than those of the two precẽdens; it makes its head less quickly, but it is heavier, tighter, and does not flower as quickly. 2. Broccoli differs from cauliflower in its higher stems, wavy leaves, generally larger size and color. They are less precocious and some do not flower. Here are their varieties. A. Head broccoli. Three sub-varieties: White, with white apples, similar to cauliflower, but of better quality. One of the best of all. Purple, with a purplish apple. Excellent. The dwarf violet hátif, smaller, quicker to form, but of less esteemed quality. These three are considered to be the best, and are therefore more widely cultivated than the others. B. Sprouting broccoli. Three sub-varieties: Green, yellow and red. PRIEUR. By means of artificial cultivation and a great deal of care, cauliflowers can be obtained quite easily all year round. First of all, we'd like to point out that cauliflowers are more delicate than other species, and that they need light, very substantial, soft soil, amended with a lot of fertilizer; what they really need is a lot of water, as they won't pump otherwise. For this reason, spring and autumn sowings are usually more successful than early ones. Autumn cauliflowers, preserved as described in the article on ordinary cultivation, provide a winter supply until mid-February. We must therefore begin this article by teaching you how to grow the cauliflowers that will succeed them. 1. Sowing in July, to produce in February and March. From the fifteenth to the twentieth of July, sow tender cauliflower in damp or at least shady soil with a northern exposure. Prepare the soil with good ploughing and amendments appropriate to the season: cow potting soil, for example, is excellent when it is only three-quarters used up; any other fertilizer must be completely used up. Sow lightly, covering the seed with two fingers of compost. Water immediately, and rigorously coutinue the arrocomens twice a day, until September, and beyond if autumn is dry or hot. A fortnight after emergence, the plants are transplanted to a bed at the same exposure, prepared in the same way, but with a foot or fifteen inches between them, depending on their strength. Give them the usual care and attention, especially plenty of watering. If the plant is seen to be advancing too much, it is transplanted a second time, at the end of September, in a well-soiled bed with good exposure. When the first white frosts set in, surround the bed with a good manure stove, two feet high, and whenever the cold sets in, cover the plantation with good mulch spread on sticks laid across the stoves. If we could cover with frame panels, it would be all the better. We try to keep the plants in this way until the middle of December, but with the precaution of giving them air and light whenever it's safe to do so. Nevertheless, if the frosts become more intense before this time, we'll transplant them immediately, as we're about to say. One observation we'd like to make here is that cauliflowers are less afraid of the cold than is generally thought. During the winter of 1827 to 1828, I kept them throughout the bad season, without any cover on the northern exposure, and we know that the thermometer went down several times to five and a half and six degrees below zero; they did perfectly well. Nonetheless, the gardener mustn't take advantage of what we've just said to throw caution to the wind and run the risk of being caught out. Prepare a muted layer four feet wide and three feet thick, and build it up with materials that are likely to retain their heat the longest. A mixture of half hot horse manure and half dry leaves perfectly fulfills this purpose. A nine- to ten-inch bed of potting soil mixed with half light garden soil is spread over the bed. When the heat has passed, i.e. eight to ten days later, which should be the fifteenth of December, if possible, the cauliflower plants are transplanted with the root ball, eighteen inches apart. The glasses should be about three or four inches from the tallest leaves, and never more. If the cabbages are getting longer, you can put in more and more fusegates as needed. Hot manure stoves, at least eighteen inches thick and the same height as the sides of the boxes, are placed around the trunks. Cauliflower wilts easily, so don't miss a single opportunity to give it air and light, as this is, after heat, the first condition for success. From time to time, we probe the layer to see if it's cooling down. If it does, we dig a pit eighteen inches wide and as deep around the frames, and fill it with warm manure, which we renew every fortnight, if winter is intense. We take care to cover the panels every night with good straw mats and bedding, but we remove these covers every day when it's not freezing, or even when it is freezing, but not so hard as to worry about the cold penetrating the frames. As a rule, we start to have a few fine days towards the end of January or in the course of February. If it's a sunny day, and the thermometer rises to four or five degrees above zero, we give the plants plenty of air during the hottest part of the day, by lifting the covers almost entirely. If you give the plants the care we've just described, they'll produce their heads in February and March, unless the winter has been extraordinarily harsh. Their apples will be small but delicious. 2° Sowing in August and September, for production in April. In the last five days of August and the first five days of September, hard and semi-hard cauliflower are sown in a light, earthen bed, in a nursery, with the usual care and attention. Give it the usual care and, above all, water it copiously. When the plant is strong enough, and the frosts begin to make themselves felt, the seedling is transplanted, four inches apart, on a warm bed, under cloches, or better still, under a frame. If under cloches, as soon as the cold becomes intense, surround them with short litter, and when necessary, cover with straw mats. Water as needed, provide as much air and light as possible, and place stoves to prevent the layers from cooling. At the beginning of February, a muted layer fourteen to fifteen inches deep is established in a trench, with half hot manure and half dry leaves, placed in salternative beds. This is filled with ten inches of potting soil mixed, as we said for the previous one, with half of good light garden soil; when its great heat has passed, i.e., ten to twelve days later, the plants are transplanted twenty inches apart. Each cauliflower is covered with a choche, which is left untouched until recovery is assured. Only then do you begin to give the plants air, little by little, to accustom them to it. If the season is cold, fill in the bell gaps with bedding, and if the frosts are still strong, cover them at night with good doormats, which are removed every morning. In March, the blankets can be removed at the beginning of the month, and even the bells around mid-month, if the season is as usual, but they must always be kept close by so that they can be replaced immediately, should unexpected bad weather make them necessary. Cauliflowers treated in this way produce in April. In both these crops, the empty spaces left by cauliflowers on the warm layers, either under the cloches or under the frames, can be used to sow fournitnres, lettuces and other vegetables. 3° Sowing in September, for production in May and June. From the fifth of September until the last days of the month, hard and semi-hard cauliflowers are sown, as described below. Sow lightly in a warm rather than cold soil-bed, or better still, in an old layer of potting soil, and take the usual care when sowing. Fifteen or twenty days after the seedling has emerged, prepare the ground on which it will be planted. Against a south-facing wall, prepare a slightly sloping ados of a width calculated to accommodate two or three rows of bells. After loosening the soil well, you fill it with three or four inches of good, well-consumed compost. We plant the young cauliflowers, spacing them so that each bell, when placed, can cover from fifteen to twenty, and less if we can without losing too much space, and that we have enough glassware. When the first white frosts begin to make themselves felt, the bells are placed every evening, and removed in the morning. When the weather turns bad and the frost becomes more intense, the bells are left on during the day, but lifted whenever it's safe to do so, as cauliflowers quickly wither and melt when deprived of air or light. It sometimes happens, in warm autumns, that the plant advances more than we'd like. In this case, we suspend watering for a while, or, if necessary, we unfold the cabbages, give them a light ploughing in the soil of the bed, and transplant them immediately as they were before. This operation is enough to stop its growth for a suitable length of time. When the frost has become severe enough to threaten to penetrate under the cloches, we fill the gaps between them with litter, and cover them at night with good straw mats. In very harsh winters, we're sometimes obliged to fill in between the bells with warm manure, and cover the whole with a good thickness of bedding, over which we spread more straw mats. In all cases, it's important to continue providing as much air and light as possible. In March, a little earlier if the season is mild, or a little later if you're still afraid of the cold, prepare a bed with good exposure by deep ploughing and fertilizing the soil to suit its quality. Transplant the plants in staggered or staggered rows, two feet apart. Covering with cloches for four or five days makes transplanting much easier, but this precaution is not essential. Hoe, weed and weed, and above all water very often if the season is a little dry and hot. If the care indicated is taken at the right time, and the cabbages have not suffered from frost, they begin to produce towards the end of May, continuing into June and sometimes as late as July. We don't think it's necessary to point out here that the beds where they are overwintered can be advantageously covered with frames instead of bells. If they were used, the cold would be prevented from penetrating inside, by means of manure piled in sufficient quantity around the boxes; a thick blanket of litter would be thrown over the panels, and good straw mats would be spread over them, if necessary. M. Vilmorin teaches a wintering method that Fon can try if he has neither bells nor frames. Let's hear what this skilled farmer has to say. - In the absence of "cloches", he says, you can "winterize" your plant in small recesses made of dry litter, held in place by stakes at the same angle as the bark, so that it "is enclosed in a kind of small wall of dry manure" at a height of five to six inches. Transverse poles" supported by the stakes hold the mulch, which is doubled in cold weather, "and removed in fine weather. 4. Sowing in January, to produce in June and July, Hard and half-hard varieties are usually used for this season's sowing. From January twenty-fifth to February fifteenth, sow very lightly in a warm layer four feet wide and three feet thick, with six to eight inches of pure, well-consumed compost. Cover the seedling with cloches, or better still, with frames, and give it the usual care. Particular care is taken to ensure that heat is maintained, and to this end, bedding blankets and straw mats are used. Fifteen days after emergence, if the seedling has not suffered from cold or lack of light, it should be ready for transplanting. A new warm layer is raised, but given the season, it can be as little as three to three-and-a-half feet high and two feet thick. This is covered with seven to eight inches of potting soil mixed with half light earth. We plant the cauliflowers four or five inches apart, and shelter them with bells, or simply with straw mats, which we spread over them every evening, and hold in place with a few stakes and sticks across the top. If the nights were cold, and we were threatened with frost, we'd place a border of short, broken litter between the bed and the mats every evening. This would prevent the cold from penetrating to the plants. At the end of March, or the beginning of April, the seedlings are transplanted into place, in beds prepared as above. Care is taken as recommended above, the tiquet is spread or watered frequently, and the cabbages produce their heads in June and July. 5. March sowing, to produce in July. Tender and semi-hard cauliflowers are preferred for this month's sowing. From the first to the fifteenth of March, raise a layer of chande in the same proportions as those we have just mentioned; fill it with seven to eight inches of similarly mixed loam, and sow lightly. The seedling is protected from the frequent inclemency of March weather, by means of frames or cloches, or simply with straw mats placed as mentioned above. The seedling can be left on the bed for a month, but only if it has been thinned out or sown very brightly, and can be given air. If the season is not favorable enough to meet this last condition, we transplant it to a new bed and treat it as we did for the previous month's seedlings. In April, it is planted on specially prepared beds at the usual exposure. Proper care is taken, and it produces in July. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. As the seedlings we are about to discuss are planted in the open ground, and cabbages no longer require shelter, we must end our early season article here. 6. April sowing, for July production. The most suitable variety for sowing this season is tender cauliflower. From mid-April to the end of the month, prepare a perfectly composted bed in a south-facing position and sow fairly lightly; rake lightly and cover with a finger's thickness of consumed compost. The seedlings are left in the nursery until they are strong enough to be planted out permanently; then they are transplanted to a bed, twenty inches apart, in a dry, half-shaded position. We give them the usual care of hoeing, weeding, etc., and above all we free them from the tiquet, or earth flea. As soon as the heat begins to make itself felt, watering must be redoubled and given night and morning, because if this rigorous precaution is not taken, if the soil is not kept in a constant state of humidity, you can be sure that the cabbages will not succeed. Moreover, they require no other special care. As cauliflower sown in April does not keep its head for long, it is necessary to sow little at a time and three or four times. 7. Sow in May to produce in September. Semi-hard cauliflower is preferred for sowing this month. Paris market gardeners even give it preference over the other two varieties, for sowing in all seasons. It is sown from the first to the fifteenth of May, in well-prepared soil with ordinary exposure. Like its predecessor, it should only be transplanted once and permanently. As for the rest, it is grown in exactly the same way as the April plant, and requires just as much watering. April and May seedlings are the most difficult to sow, especially when the summer is dry and hot. In this case, it is only with the help of ean bark and a northern exposure that they can be brought to fruition. 8. June sowing, for production from September to November. Semi-hard is still the most popular cauliflower for this month's sowing. In ordinary soils, sow from the tenth to the fifteenth of June, and in warm, light soils, until the twenty-fifth. In a northern exposure, in the coolest part of the garden, prepare a bed and amend it suitably with green manure or, better still, with half-consumed cow manure. The seed is thrown in very lightly, the rake is passed over it, and the soil is loosened as described above. To keep the soil fresh, it's a good idea to mulch the seedling, in which case you don't need to cover it with compost. Leave the seedling in place until you are ready to plant it permanently, but don't spare it any watering, either in the seedling or when it is in the bed, especially during the first few months, as seedlings in this season require the most. When the young cabbages are strong enough, they are transplanted into beds at the usual exposure, and require no further care than that given to other common cabbages. They often start to produce as early as August, and continue until November, or until the first frosts stop their growth. The last heads of cabbage can easily be kept over winter. All you have to do is cut them three inches below the head, and cut off all the leaves, even the smallest ones, which are hidden inside the head. Once this is done, tie them to a string and hang them in a dry, airy place where frost cannot penetrate, such as a fruit garden, a vegetable greenhouse, a healthy cellar, or simply a bedroom. They can also be placed on wooden shelves. They will keep until February. As for cauliflowers that have not yet sprouted when the frosts arrive - and there are a lot of them if you've sown hardy ones - strip them of their largest leaves, and plant them close together, but without them touching, in a layer of compost or sand in the vegetable greenhouse, and they form small but very good heads that produce until March. If you don't have a vegetable greenhouse, you can plant them in a cellar or storeroom; but in this case, it's better to use the method we'll describe below. At the foot of a south-facing wall, in dry soil, open a trench three to four feet wide and two feet deep; plant the cabbages close together, in potting soil and with the root ball, then cover with frames. When the cold gets colder, we cover the panels with litter, and establish good rechands, which we renew every eight or fifteen days around the boxes. Cauliflowers treated in this way produce larger, slightly earlier apples. The best cauliflower seeds are those collected from the largest and most plump plants of the autumn sowing, and the firmest, whitest apples. Seedbeds are overwintered in frames or under cloches, and come up in spring. Above all, they must be kept away from all other cabbage varieties during flowering, otherwise they will cross-pollinate and degenerate. 9. Broccoli cultivation. This breed requires absolutely the same care as other cauliflowers. In May and June, prepare a shady bed, amend it with consumed fertilizers, and sow all varieties very lightly, except the dwarf and purple, whose seeds should not be entrusted to the soil until July. We water abundantly, thin out the seedlings if necessary, and only lift the plants to transplant them when they are strong enough. A bed is then broken up, smoked and loosened, and the broccoli is pricked out two and a half feet apart. We weed, weed, weed and we water daily, especially in the heat of summer. As frost approaches, a small pit is dug at the foot of each broccoli plant, and it is gently laid down, tilting carefully and repeatedly to avoid damaging the roots. Cover the stem with soil and part of the head, but leave half of the head exposed to air and light. During hard frosts, we cover them with a good thickness of dry litter, which we remove to give them air and light whenever the weather permits. At the end of winter or the beginning of spring, they yield their products. You can also overwinter broccoli under a frame, planting it with the root ball in potting soil placed at the bottom of a two-foot-deep trench, as we said for cauliflower from June sowing. Alternatively, simply remove them with the rootball, and plant them upright to the leaves, in a hole made in a south-facing bed. During hard frosts, they are covered with litter. GENERAL CABBAGE CULTIVATION. All cabbages - green, savoy, headed, etc. - like a clean, strong rather than light soil (with the exception of cauliflowers), fresh or even a little damp, deep, amended with half-used fertilizers, and perfectly loosened. In new soils, i.e. newly cleared, they sometimes acquire a surprising volume. They can only be propagated by sowing seeds at the times indicated, in loose, light, earthy soil, facing south in spring and autumn, and north in summer. Throughout their life, they need frequent watering, but it's especially during their youth that watering is absolutely essential. When it comes to planting young cabbages, the first step is to prepare the soil by ploughing several times, to loosen it enough so that the planter doesn't compress it too much. We choose rainy weather for this operation, to hasten and facilitate recovery. Make a hole with the planting tool, insert the root without crumpling it, and press it in so that the eye or heart of the young cabbage is level with the surface of the soil, or even deeper rather than higher. The plant must not be too strong, as then it is prone to rising, knotting and hardening off; but neither must it be too weak, as then it frequently languishes for a few days and melts. Cabbages are attacked, especially in their youth, by a small, black, jumping, very agile insect called the tiquet or earth flea (I); they gnaw the parenchyma of the leaves, making a thousand holes, and cause them to perish in a short time. This plague is avoided by sprinkling cabbages with ashes or road dust when they are wet from dew, rain or sprinkling water. Another insect that is just as dangerous, but only attacks the plant when it is strong, is the red cabbage bug. The only way to destroy it is to hunt it during the day, and throw it into a vase containing soap water. It perishes in a very short time. You shouldn't be reluctant to pick it up with your fingers, as this species has no bad odor. Caterpillars are another destructive insect that must be constantly hunted down. We crush them with a small wooden paddle as we find them. It's especially in the evenings and mornings that you need to be on the lookout for them, because then they're all together and easier to crush. (1) Under this name, gardeners confuse several species, each one as dangerous as the other: they are the vegetable flea beetle (allica oleracea); garden flea beetle (A. Rortezzis); cabbage flea beetle (A. Prassicæ); turnip flea beetle (A. napi); nigriped flea beetle (A. nigripes.) The larva of an insect unknown to me is also very frequently found in the stem of cabbages, near the collar, and causes a very noticeable little tumor. As soon as it is spotted during transplanting, the nodule is opened by cutting off half of it, and the worm is removed. Cabbage is no less successful. Finally, slugs and heliconias, or snails, also wreak havoc on cabbages, especially when they are young. The best way to destroy them is to chase them away. In small seedbeds, by mixing half an ounce of fine tobacco with a pound of ash and sprinkling the soil in the evening, you can kill a large number of them. I teach this procedure only after having experimented with it myself for three consecutive years, and with consistently satisfactory success. No plant that I know of experiences adulterous fertilization as easily as cabbage, without even excluding melons. In 1828, I had a singular example. The mild winter had left me with several very fine cauliflowers. They came up and flowered at a time when no other variety was in bloom, apart from a single plant of northern kale, but which was more than thirty paces away. I carefully harvested the seeds and sowed them in May; out of more than five hundred plants, I had, at most, thirty or so that appear to me today (July 18 28) to be cauliflowers. The others have leaves much larger than kale, flat in the middle, curled and wavy only at the edges, very pale green, and the stems are already over eighteen inches high. I don't know what I'll get from any of them. To avoid similar accidents, which are all too frequent, you need to place each seedling as far apart as possible (in different varieties), and above all try to ensure that they don't flower at the same time: this is the best way to avoid hybridity and degeneration of the races. Great care must always be taken in selecting the individuals intended to bear seed, and only the most beautiful, robust and straightforward of their respective varieties should be kept. We take great care of them during the summer and autumn, then, when the cold weather arrives, we shelter the varieties that fear the cold, with litter, cloches, straw mats, etc. Sometimes in spring, when all the trees start to climb, the apples of some cabus trees are so tightly packed that they can't open to allow the stems to pass through. In this case, we split them in four with the tip of the pruning knife, taking care not to cut too far forward. It is often necessary to use a sharp instrument when a cabbage has suffered from humidity, and its head is spoiled in some parts. The rot is removed by cutting to the quick, and this operation does not prevent it from going to seed as well as any other. If you cut the head off a cabbage, and these shoots produce stems, the seed they produce is just as good as the one obtained with much more stems. A cabbage seed is ripe when the siliques become yellowish-white and begin to open. Then cut the stems gently so as not to lose any seeds, spread them out on a cloth and leave them to dry in the sun or in an airy place sheltered from the rain. Then thresh it, clean it as best you can, and after enclosing it in small canvas bags, hang it in a very dry, frost-free place. It will keep all the better if the temperature is always maintained at the same level, and the same applies to all vegetable seeds. Seeds of cauliflower will not retain their germinative qualities for more than five years, after which only one parlie is produced; but those of all other varieties can be kept for seven or eight years. CHOU-MARIN, or MARITIME CRAMBE (Crambe maritima); a perennial, indigenous plant that can be ranked among the best vegetables. Unbeknownst to us, it is extremely neglected in France, while the English consume it in enormous quantities. Its blanched young shoots can be eaten in a variety of ways. They are boiled like cauliflower, then seasoned with butter or sauce. Their flavor is a cross between broccoli and asparagus. PRIMEUR. Crambe is usually grown in March and April, and it is not usually forced to be grown in France. But English gardeners. But English gardeners put a lot of effort into it, and grow it all year round. We'll explain the methods they use. They force on the spot and on the bed, and each of these processes will provide us with an article. 1. forcing in place. When a crambe plant is to be heated, it must be sown or planted in advance. Planks four feet wide are laid out over an indeterminate length, leaving a three-foot-wide bed between each plank. The plants are planted or sown eighteen inches apart lengthwise, in three rows spaced one foot apart, leaving six inches on each side between the plants and the bed. For two years, if sown, or for one year if planted; the care indicated below for ordinary cultivation is given. But as crambe roots trace out a lot, three or four times during the year, we use the spade's edge to circle around the bed, in order to cut off any raciues that tend to overtake the six inches left next to the last rows. In this way, instead of spreading out in the beds, they are forced to vegetate in the bed. From the month of August, which precedes the winter during which we must force, we stop watering, and even divert rainwater, so that vegetation is suspended instead. Around mid-September, we cut back the leaves and stems, most of which should already have dried out. In October, the soil is removed from the beds, and they are dug eighteen inches deep. They are filled with warm, well-trampled manure, which is raised in a layer a foot above the surface of the soil. After lightly hoeing the planks, they are also covered with a foot of hot manure, and, to keep out rainwater, covered with long straw, straw mats or bedding. If you only had a plank to force, you could make the beds only two feet or even eighteen inches wide; you'd be even if you had to put new manure more often in these kinds of stoves. But English gardeners always give them three feet, because they use them as a bed for sowing small vegetables under cloches. Once things are set up in this way, all that's left to do is to move the manure away from the bed from time to time to see if the crambe is starting to give, which doesn't take long. The shoots and buds are cut when they are five to six inches long, and delivered for consumption. If the crambé is to be sacrificed, everything is cut off until the exhausted plants cease to produce entirely. If you want to keep it, you stop cutting as soon as the produce diminishes noticeably, which usually happens after a month. Then remove the stoves from the beds and fill them with earth, removing much of the fùmier that covers the bed; but always leave a certain thickness to prevent the roots from being caught in the frost. They recover very well the following spring and during the summer, and can be heated two or three times in the six years between planting, which is their normal life span. When January arrives, the crambe doesn't need as much heat to be forced. The plants are mounded with the soil from the leftover bed, taking care not to expose the roots. The mounds, which are roughly conical in shape, are wrapped and covered with a sort of long straw hood, and covered with a good bed of hot manure. Sometimes we heat according to our first method, but with a modification; instead of spreading a foot-thick layer of hot manure on the board, we give it only five or six inches, and cover the whole with glass frames. " 2. Forcing on bedding. From October to the end of January, a warm layer is raised outdoors or under a frame, and covered with eight inches of compost. Carefully uproot three- or four-year-old crambe vines, leaving as much soil as possible, and plant them ten inches apart in the potting soil. Care must be taken not to bury them too deeply, as if the roots came into contact with the manure, they would burn and perish. If the bed is in the open air, calculate the distance between the feet so that one bell can cover three of them; to do this, group them three by three in a triangle and six inches apart; space the groups one foot or eighteen inches apart, depending on the size of the bells. To achieve this easily, all you have to do is place the bells next to each other when the soil in the bed is perfectly even, and press a little on them to make them leave the imprint of their 41 edge; you plant three feet of crambé in each circle formed by these imprints. Water to ensure recovery, cover with cloches, and fill the gaps with the same bedding. Stoves are used to keep the layers constantly warm, until the crambe buds begin to appear. At this point, the bells are completely covered with a thick blanket of litter, to absolutely block out the light and force the buds to turn white. If the layer is cous châssis, we simply tip a pelit pot over each bud as it appears, lining the hole with soggy soil. A little potting soil is gathered around its edges, to keep the plant in the deepest darkness. Many gardeners are content to cover the potting soil with a thick bed of short, dry litter, either in the open air or under a frame. The aim of all the methods we have just described is to provide crambe during the winter, right up to the time when it can be obtained naturally. All that remains is to explain how to obtain crambe during the summer, and that's what we're going to do. Eighteen-inch-diameter baskets are purchased on a foot deep. In spring, we fill them with a soil composed of one third old topsoil, one third fertile sand or heather earth, and one third good light garden soil. These baskets are buried, and a plant of crambe is planted in each one, which is tended all year round like those in the open ground. The following February, the baskets are removed from the soil and placed in a shed with a northerly exposure. The soil dries out, and you only water it enough to prevent the plants from perishing. As long as they are treated in this way, they remain languid, growing only half-aborted leaves and stems. In May, and progressively every month until August, we take a few baskets, remove the soil from the top down to the root collar, and cut off the spindly stems they have sprouted. Cover with a good mixture of half loam and half sandy soil, remove the basket without breaking the root ball, and carry the plant to a cold, deaf bed prepared for the purpose. There, it is first given copious watering, and its languid growth is revived by frequent basining. Soon after, it starts to grow again with vigor, and the buds that emerge are blanched in the usual way: all the art required to succeed in this practice consists simply in suspending vegetation without harming the plant too much, just as we do with orange trees that are deprived of water to bring them to sap and allow them to flower out of season. ORDINARY CULTURR. Crambé likes deep, substantial, light or sandy soil. It can be propagated from seed or by separating the roots. To sow in spring, break up a bed, amend it with fertilizer and loosen the soil as much as possible. Rows are marked out two feet apart, and small holes two inches deep and four inches wide are made, eighteen inches apart, in each row. From March to the beginning of May, you can sow. To do this, throw a handful of terrean into each hole, place five or six crambe seeds an inch apart, and cover with an inch of compost. Water copiously, and continue to do so until the crambe has emerged, and is in its early youth. The plant is then pruned, leaving just one plant in each spot, and transplanted in places where there may be a shortage, which is often the case, as a large proportion of the seeds are usually infertile. As much as, and perhaps more than, cabbages, it is subject to tick attack during its youth; it must be treated with ashes sifted over the leaves, while they are still damp from dew or sprinkling. We hoe, weed and give the usual care. In autumn, the leaves turn yellow and dry out; they are removed in October, and the bed is covered with one or two inches of compost. The crambé reappears in spring, and during this second year, the same care is given as in the previous year. By the third spring, it is ready to produce its first crop, and the young shoots are blanched, as we shall see later. Sometimes, instead of sowing in the field, we sow in a nursery, and this method is considered the most advantageous by some gardeners. After preparing a bed as we have said, at the same time of year, sow thickly and in rays spaced a foot apart. Care is taken as indicated, the tiquet is discarded, and thinning is carried out if necessary, i.e. if the plants are less than five or six inches apart. If you wish, you can transplant the plants removed in this operation elsewhere. In autumn, March and the following April, the plant is transplanted in place in a bed prepared as we have said to facilitate recovery, and if necessary, watering continues. In the third spring, the plants can be blanched. Some gardeners, to be more sure of success, and so that their plants are stronger and more capable of defending themselves in the tick season, sow in small pots sunk in a warm layer under cloches or frames, as early as February; they put five or six seeds in each pot, and, when they have sprouted, they leave only the strongest plant. In April, they plant in the ground with the rootball. Others are content to sow on the top of the bed, and lift the young plant as much as they can with the rootball, to place it permanently. Crambé also multiplies very well by taking cuttings from its roots, which are fleshy, thick and long, tracing between two soils. In February, they are cut into sections two or three inches long, and planted in pots in a warm layer covered with cloches. It doesn't take long for them to sprout buds and put down new roots; in April, they're put in place and, by this means, vigorous plants are obtained the same year, which can often be blanched the following spring. These cuttings can also be taken in the open ground, in March or April, in a nursery or in situ, but they only produce in the second year, i.e. a year earlier than plants from seed. Entin can still be propagated by separating its rooted eyelets. Here's how to bleach it. In February, plants are mounded with soil or compost to form a cone seven to eight inches high. At other times, simply cover with a good thickness of dry litter; finally, you can tip a flowerpot over each bud as it appears. The most important thing, whatever the method used, is that the shoot is absolutely deprived of air and light, otherwise it will contract a rather unpleasant bitter taste. When the bud is five or six inches long, and perfectly white, it is cut three or four lines from the root collar and delivered for consumption. To ensure that a crambe plant lasts a long time, the plants must not be exhausted, so a few tigez are left to rot. When the harvest is complete, if the plants have been buttted, they are uncovered and grow new pouzsent to repair those that have been cut. For five or six years, they can produce harvests, but unless the soil is very suitable, they rarely last longer; however, with a few simple precautions, these plants can be kept for many years. All you have to do is cut them back two years at a time, starting, for example, in the first spring with the first, third and fifth pledges in each row, and in the second spring with the second, fourth and sixth pledges, and so on. It would be even simpler to divide the bed into two equal parts, and cut alternately, one year plants from one, and the next year plants from the other. When the old stumps perish, they will naturally be replaced by the eyelets they have produced. Crambe seed is collected from old plants, which are sewn together without cutting for this purpose. It ripens towards the end of summer. CIBOULE (allium fissile); biennial plant, perennial in one variety, used in cooking for the same purposes as onions, and as a salad garnish. Varieties include: common chives, white chives, hative chives and perennial chives. PRIMEUR. As spring onions can be harvested during part of the winter when the weather is mild, and can be stored quite well in the vegetable greenhouse, it is not very important to force them. However, it can be done with great ease, and by this means we obtain new leaves that are much better than those that are only kept for supplies. From the beginning of August to the first days of September, the early or common variety is sown in the open ground, and gold gives the young plants their ordinary soitis. In October, the seedlings are transplanted in clumps of three to four plants together, on a warm bed of vegetable compost, spaced four inches apart. As soon as frost approaches, i.e. late November or early December, transplant the September seeds onto the same layer. Cover with cloches or frames, baste as needed, and the chives soon sprout. In this way, you can produce very delicious chives right through to January, and sometimes even longer. Perennial chives are used from January through to spring. In December, a few clumps are removed and transplanted to the same bed, where they are cared for for a few days to hasten recovery and vegetation. You can grow chives through the harshest winter without taking such great care. When frost approaches, we pull it out and plant it in the sand or potting soil of a vegetable greenhouse or under a tarpaulin. If you don't have a greenhouse, open a trench eighteen inches deep in the driest part of the garden, place the clumps of chives close together and cover them with a thick layer of dry litter to prevent frost from penetrating. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Annual chives can be grown anywhere, but prefer light, substantial soil amended with old compost. It can be sown at any exposure, at two times of the year: in February and March, for transplanting in April and May; and from the fifteenth to the thirtieth of July, for transplanting in September and enjoyment in April and May of the following year. At least, these are the times fixed by the good gardener's almanac. However, in order to obtain new varieties, which are always more delicate, the market gardeners of Paris sow them every month, from February to September, across their sslade beds. In the south of France, japdiniers start sowing as early as the beginning of January, and if the bad weather of the srison means that the first sowings are unsuccessful, they start again in February and March. If the soil is strong and prone to soaking from rain or watering, a finger of potting soil is spread over the seedbed. Water moderately, weed and weed if necessary. When the plant is strong enough, transplant two or three together to form a clump more quickly, in rays or in borders, leaving five to six inches between each clump. As perennial chives only produce seeds very rarely, they can only be propagated by separating clumps of chives and planting them in autumn or, more reliably, in spring. It can also be planted in rays or in borders, but spaced more widely, as it expands very quickly. If you run out, you can multiply it by planting clove by clove, two inches apart, but you'll have to wait a long time before you get nice clumps. This variety is hardier and more spring-like than the biennial chive, but it needs some watering during the heat of summer if it is to retain its vigorous growth. We keep a few vines of biennial chives, without cutting them, to obtain seeds. When ripe, cut the umbels on a clear day and collect them in bunches in a dry place. Thus preserved in their capsules, the seeds germinate very well for three years; but if you take them out, they lose their germinative virtues after two years. CIBOULETTE, CIVETTE, APPÉTIT (allium schomnopresum); perennial plant, native to Siberia, used in cooking as a salad ingredient, and sometimes as a seasoning. Three varieties were once thought to exist, but it has since been recognized that they are purely local, passing from one to the other with the greatest of ease by a simple change of soil. PRIMXUR. When frost approaches, remove the rootball from the chives in clumps no more than four or five inches wide; make a hole in the potting soil of a warm bed of small vegetables large enough to accommodate the rootball; water very lightly; cut off all the leaves from the clump near the bulbs, and cover with a bell or frame. The leaves grow back quickly and continue to supply until the plant is exhausted. Then, whatever the season, you replace it with another plant taken from the open ground, and by this means you obtain produce throughout the winter. The more air and light you give them, the slower the plants become exhausted. Moreover, you can easily keep chives for part of the winter, by treating them in the same way as we did for spring onions. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Chives are best grown in light, substantial, loose soils with warm exposure; however, they grow well everywhere. It could be propagated from seed, but as this would take a long time, it is customary to propagate by cloves. In March or April, we separate them and plant them in clumps of three or four together, spacing the clumps eight or nine inches apart, whether for beds or borders. These clumps expand rapidly, and the more you cut the leaves, the more tender and numerous they become. If you take care to keep it free of weeds, weed it occasionally and water it during the heat of summer, the chive will keep well and can remain for four or five years, after which it needs to be renewed by moving it to another spot. In autumn, as frost approaches, cut the leaves back to the ground, leave the wounds to dry for a few days, and cover the plant with an inch of reconstituted compost. COCHLÉARIA OFTICINAL OU HERBE A LA CUILLEE (cochlearia officinalis); biennial plant, indigenous, mutilated in cooking, but nevertheless very cultivated in most gardens because of the medicinal virtues attributed to it. PRIMEUR. As we always use fresh cocbléaria, because it loses most of its properties through desiccation, we thought it would be useful to indicate here how it can be obtained all year round. As soon as the seeds are ripe, i.e. around mid-August, prepare a bed in light, cool soil with a northerly exposure, and sow, taking care to keep the soil constantly cool by frequent watering. As frost approaches, lift the young plants with the moth, and plant two or three together, in pots five inches in diameter. If you have a temperate greenhouse or lighted orangery, you can place the plants in the daylight, which is enough to preserve them. If you don't have a greenhouse, set up a muted bed in a dry place, place the pots close together and cover with a frame. It should be noted that it is when the plant is in flower that the leaves have the most energy, so it is at this time that the cochlearía should be delivered to the trade, and arrangements should be made to ensure that it is always in this state. To do this, every two weeks we remove a few pots from under the frame and bury them in the potting soil of a warm layer of A vegetable; vegetation takes on activity; we keep the soil constantly moist, and the plants are quick to rise. You can also sow in a warm layer under a bell in October and November, to have cochlearia in January and February, but it never has as much energy as when it has been sown in the ground and preserved as we have said. Its seeds keep well for two years. ORDINARY GROWING: Cochlearia grows well in all types of soil, provided they are neither too dry nor too hot, but it does much better in light, damp, north-facing soils. It is sown in spring, watered frequently, especially during droughts, and care is limited to that. Usually, there is never a shortage of it in a garden once it has been planted, because it reseeds itself. In too-dry soils, it quickly goes to seed, lasts only three or four months, and is then lost forever. In this case, it acts as an annual. COCHLÉARIA DE BRETAGNE, GRAND RAIFORT, CRANSON RUSTIQUE (cochlearia armorica); perennial, growing naturally in some damp places in France. Its roots, long, thick and fleshy, are used scraped to be eaten with boiled beef and replace mustard, of which they have the strength and pungency. As it can be rooted in all seasons, there is no point in cultivating it as an early crop, so we will only deal here with its ordinary cultivation. Lo cranson grows well in all types of soil and in all types of exposure, but it thrives best in shady spots, in cool or even damp, deep, somewhat consistent soil. To propagate it, all you need to do in autumn is transplant small particles of sca roots, which the following year provide vigorous plants. To obtain very strong, very long roots, plant in troughs, ten or twelve inches deep, throw a certain thickness of well-consumed compost over the sections, and cover with light soil. It can also be propagated from seeds sown in situ or in a nursery, in spring, in very loose, deeply loosened soil. If sown in a nursery, the plant is transplanted permanently, in autumn, and spaced one foot apart in all directions. The most necessary care for a seedling or a plantation consists of watering during summer droughts. When you have strong plants, you don't need to destroy them to obtain the product. All you have to do is dig up a plant, and as the roots are long and extend a long way, you can cut off large portions for use, without destroying the others. We must point out, however, that the ones we value most are the main roots of a plant in its second year, especially when it is uprooted at the moment of flowering. Cranson is used much more in Germany than in France. Its seeds retain their germinative power for three or four years. COGNASSIER (cydonia vulgaris to modern botanists, prrus cydonie to Linnaeus); a third-growth tree, native to southern Europe. Its fruits, unbearably hard, are suitable only for making compotes, jams and liqueurs. But the tree is no less valuable as a grafting stock for cattail and espalier pear trees. We have several varieties, including Coing de Portugal, a large, ribbed fruit, less acerbic than most of the following, Colng d'Anger, a sub-variety of the previous one. It is larger, gravellier and less bitter. Common quince, pyriform fruit in one sub-variety, very mediocre, rounded in another which is the worst of all. PRIMEUR. We have never forced a quince tree, and we don't think we ever will, because we don't use its raw fruit. CULTURX ORDINAIRX. The quince tree likes light soils, cool without being cold, but at least it succeeds more or less well in all soils, except those that are very shallow and rest on a chalky layer. It can be propagated from seed, which is sown as soon as it ripens and sprouts the following spring, or by offshoots and marcottes, or by cuttings. This last method is the easiest and quickest, and is also the most widely used. Cuttings are taken in the first fine days of spring, and when they are detached from their mother, they need to be left with a two-year stub of wood. If the year is dry, they require watering, hoeing and weeding. Sometimes, to obtain vigorous plants, varieties are grafted onto plants grown from seed rather than cuttings. Quince trees thrive in all types of exposure, although they prefer sunny and south-facing exposures, and even the south-facing exposure of the Portuguese quince. As this tree bears its flowers at the tips of its branches, it should not be pruned if fruit is to be produced. Other cultivation methods are absolutely the same as for pears. CUCUMBER, CORRICRON (cucumis zalivus); annual plant, native to Africa, too widespread to be necessary to indicate here the use made of its fruit. There are several varietiesa , namely 12 The long, elongated white, rounded at both ends, highly prized in Paris. The white hatif, similar to the previous one, a little smaller, very suitable for early cultivation. Hatif de Hollande, initially white, then yellowing rapidly; suitable for early cultivation. Gros blanc de Bonneuil, similar to the first, but larger. Long yellow, often with a curved shape. Petit vert or gherkin, suitable for pickling in vinegar. The long green, similar to the previous one, but more elongated; suitable for the same use. Russian or bouquet cucumbers, very small; prezque round, the earliest of all; suitable for pickling. Arada cucumber, the size of an elongated walnut, high yielding, but difficult to grow; delicate, not always successful; suitable for preserving. In addition to these varieties, we grow a second species, the snake cucumber (cacumis flezuosus), which differs from the others only in its elongated shape. It is green, smooth and flagelliform; it is grown in the same way as the previous varieties, and is only suitable for pickling. PRIMZUR. Early cucumbers are not usually grown until December, but they can be successfully sown as early as September or October, as we shall see. As cucumbers do not require any special cultivation during the first two months of their growth, these first sowings can be attempted without great expense, and if they are unsuccessful, they can be sown again at the end of December and in January. The two varieties to be given preference for early crops are blane hatif and hätif de Hollande. Nous allomo divise" les cultures par saisons, 1. From the first of September to the first of November, if you haven't already raised a warm bed, prepare one and fill it with six inches of compost. Get small pots four inches in diameter, and fill them with a soil made up of one-third used potting soil and two-thirds light loam. In each pot, plant four or five cucumber seeds, sinking them half an inch deep. Carry the pots on the bed, bury them in the potting soil, water them lightly and cover them with a cloche or frame. It doesn't take long for the seeds to sprout. Air is given most of the day, weather permitting, but the cloches or panels are never completely removed; they are simply lifted and held in place with the rack, so that the heat from the toleil is concentrated by the glass. On éclaireit, i.e. we leave only two planis in each pot. Fifteen or twenty days after emergence, or rather when the layer has lost its heat, the cucumbers are unstuck and put back into larger pots, where they are pressed down to the cotyledons. Raise a new layer, and press them into it. You can also cover them with bells. When two or three gems have developed, pruning begins: this consists of pinching the stem above the second gil; later, it continues by successively pinching the branches at four or five nodes. A fortnight or three weeks after transplanting, prepare a warm layer and fill it with seven or ten inches of good potting soil, mixed with a third of light earth. When the first heat has passed, the cucumbers are removed from the bed, planted permanently, two and a half feet apart, and covered with the frames. Continue to pinch the branches, water and carefully peel the plants, removing any damaged leaves, or even some of the larger ones if there are many, to allow air and light to penetrate easily. It's at flowering time that precautions must be redoubled, because humidity is then more dangerous than ever. We take advantage of every moment of sunshine to give the plants some air; we wipe off any moisture and grime that may be attached to the glass panels, and we maintain the warmth of the layer with good heaters that we renew every time this is necessary. When the weather is very cold, this operation is sometimes not enough; in this case, the layer must be covered with hot manure from the stable. To do this, remove as much manure from the sides and underside of the bed as you can without undermining the soil on top, and replace it as you go along. With skill and patience, you can renew almost half a layer in this way, without the plants on top suffering the slightest inconvenience. When the cucumbers have formed and begun to take on a certain size, they are gently lifted and a piece of tile, slate or a small board is placed between them and the potting soil, to protect them from humidity. 2. The second season runs from November 1st to the last of January, as we said for the first. However, once the plants have emerged, they should be left in their pots for only a fortnight, and in the second pots for the same length of time. This way, the layers won't have time to cool down too much, and they'll receive the same care. These seedlings can also be grown in cloches, but it is much safer, and easier, to grow them in hutches or trays. 3. From February 1st to April 15th, sow in pots, on a warm layer in the open air, and under cloches. February sowing can be done in other pots and on a new layer, as we have said, but these precautions are unnecessary for sowing from the first to the fifteenth of April. In warm weather, around mid-April, establish a muted layer filled with eight inches of potting soil mixed with half light earth; transplant the cucumbers when the plant is strong enough, and cover with frames. If the spring is warm, the cucumbers are gradually acclimatized to the open air, and at the beginning of May, the panels are removed during the day and replaced in the evening, as long as the cool nights are feared. By using the methods we have just described, you can grow cucumbers for part of the winter and from the first days of spring, but there is still a gap in the year between the end of ordinary cucumbers and the beginning of early cucumbers. Here's how to fill this gap: at the beginning of August, sow in mannes ten inches deep by a foot wide, and bury the baskets in the garden bed. The plants would receive the same care as those grown in the open ground. A few days before the first white frost, we'd set up a warm layer in a furnace tarpaulin; we'd raise the cucumber branches carefully so as not to break or injure them, and we'd hold them in a loose bundle by means of a loose tie; Remove the pots from the soil, and transport the plants with their rootballs in the soil of the bed; stretch out their branches in their natural position, or at least with as little stress as possible; in this way, not only will the little knotted cucumbers reach maturity, but new ones will be formed. During cold spells, the temperature of the greenhouse would be kept at fourteen or fifteen degrees, by means of the furnace. I know that this method would be very expensive, and that if it were used only to grow cucumbers, the results would not compensate for the expense. But nonetheless, I thought I should mention it, because this crop can be grown using the layer of a bache in which vines or other fruit trees are forced. It can even be sown in the ground on ados, then protected from the cold with portable frames and manure rechands. CULTUAX ORDINAÍRE. Cucumbers like light, substantial, loose, open soil, amended with half-consumed manure and warm exposure. From April fifteenth to May fifteenth, hoods are prepared, three feet apart, i.e. holes eighteen inches deep by a foot wide; a foot of warm manure is placed at the bottom, and the soil is filled with six to eight inches of good old potting soil mixed with a third of light earth. Five or six cucumber seeds are planted in each hood, buried an inch apart, and a few sprinklings are given if the season is dry. When the seedlings have risen, they are lightened so as to leave only two or three in each hood. We hoe, weed and give the usual care to full-grown plants. Soon it's time to prune; if you're growing for ripe fruit, follow the same principles as for melons; prune as we said for early fruit, pinching back the stem above the second eye, but leaving the branches a little longer, i.e. over five or six eyes. When you want to pick the fruit young to preserve it in vinegar, you follow a different principle; you prune the branches very long, or rather, you only stop them by pricking the middle when they spread too much, and to make them I ter lateral branches. During the heat of summer, watering should be sparing, as cucumbers love edu and heat. To preserve the true varieties, it is advisable to grow them separately, and even at some distance from each other, as otherwise the races would cross-fertilize each other by adulteration. The seed is collected, leaving the most beautiful fruit to rot on the spot. The seeds are collected, washed and dried in the shade in an airy place. They keep well for eight to ten years, and perhaps longer. CULTIVATED CORIANDER (coriandrum sativum); an annual plant said to originate in the East, but which I have found growing naturally in the south of France on several occasions, where it may have become naturalized if it is not actually indigenous. In cooking, only the seeds are used to spice up stews; they have an aromatic, sweet flavor and a very pleasant odor, but only when dried; when green, if crushed between the fingers, as well as the flower, they have an odor that is at first sweet, then stronger, fetid, unpleasant and capable of giving nausea. The other parts of the plant are odorless. Coriander seed has a number of other uses: it is used to make white beer, various ratafias and sugared almonds. The Egyptians mix it into almost all their foods. As only its dried seeds are used, it would be pointless to subject it to forced cultivation. It likes light soil and low exposure, but grows well in other soils. It is planted in April, and requires no more atitres soms than to be planted from time to time until September, when its ripe seeds must be harvested. They are bormes à scamer que perdani dioz duto COU CORNE DE CERF (PLANTAIN) (plantago coronopus); annual, indigenous plant, with a sweet, almost insipid flavor, nevertheless used in cooking as a salad dressing. Only the leaves are used, which grow back better the more often they are cut. In some countries, they are eaten like spinach. PRIMEUR. In October, sow fairly thickly on a warm bed with small vegetables, and baste frequently; cover with bells. When the seeds have emerged, thin out to leave a two-inch gap between each plant. When the leaves are three to four inches long, we start cutting, and as long as the plants are producing, we continue. If you want to have them all winter long, don't wait until one seedling is exhausted before sowing another; sow every two weeks, until the spring plants begin to produce. All cultivation care is limited to frequent but light watering, so as not to chill the soil. REGULAR CULTIVATION. In light, very loose, loamy soil, sow in March, fairly light. For the plant to succeed perfectly, all you need to do once it has emerged is to give it frequent and abundant watering; this is the only way to obtain tender leaves. You can extend sowing in cool, shady soil until the end of June. If they are not spared water, they will do very well and produce until the first frosts. The seed ripens from September to October, and keeps well for two years. F CUCUMBER, POTIROX, LEMON (cucurbita ). This name covers a large number of varieties that are said to belong to two species: cucurbita pepo and cucurbita melopepo. Squashes are large, creeping annual plants native to India. Their fruits, sometimes surprisingly large, can be eaten in a variety of ways, but are always cooked. All gourds, without exception, are excellent if eaten long before they reach maturity, i.e. a few days after they have blossomed, until they have reached half their size. But this is not the case when they reach maturity; many become tough and scabby. Each so-called species will provide us with a section to which we'll refer sez most generally widespread varieties. 1. Pumpkins. Yellow, very large, widely cultivated. Green, also large and widespread, sometimes reaching enormous size. White, less large, more delicate, rarc. These three varieties, the least esteemed of all although the largest, play a lot and pass easily through each other. There are smooth, warty, long and round varieties, of varying shades of darker or darker colors, sometimes striped or spotted. In Bresse, I've often seen them almost blackish-brown. They are usually abandoned for cultivation. The Spanish pumpkin is an excellent, high-yielding variety. Its fruits are fairly large, flattened, with smooth or slightly chapped rind, very hard and usually green, and firm, soft flesh, much better than the previous varieties. 2. Melopeon ceurges. Giraumon turban, fairly large, with a pistil scar forming a bulge and widening to sometimes occupy the surface of half the fruit, which is yellowish; firm flesh, sweeter than in the pumpkin. Butternut or melonnee squash, fairly large, yellow, firm, sweet, very popular in Provence. Gireumon noir, gres, brownish-green. Barbaris long giraumon or long hande squash, productive; elongated, medium-sized fruit, smooth rind, very wide bands, brownish-green spotted with neirish, sometimes entirely white, on the same piod. Patisson, bonnet d'électeur, or Jeruzalem artichoke, large, white, five-pointed; very shapely flesh; excellent for frying. Jerusalem strip artichoke, similar to the above but smaller, with green-striped rind; even firmer flesh. Valparaiso squash or marrow squash, small, oval, pale yellow or whitish fruit; very soft flesh, excellent before ripening, then becoming fibrous and leathery. La caucourzalle or counge d'Ialie, fruit eighteen poucca long, five or six in diameter, litse, striped or spotted with green; exoellent when eaten very young, i.e. only four or five inches long; very mediotre when ripe. Very short stems. Non-running blanchs squash, of the same shape and quality as the preceding squash, but with very short, non-creeping stems. Pleina squash, elongated, large, greenish-brown fruit, with very red flesh and almost no hollow inside. Potiromon. Fruit fairly large, elongated, almost cylindrical; rind reddish-yellow, with a few purple spots; soft, very good flesh. PRIMEUR. Squash, which occupies a very large area of land and does not produce fruit of great value, has never been subjected to forced cultivation; until now, it has been enough to have early fruit only towards the end of June and in the summer. We will first explain the steps to be taken to achieve this goal; then, in order to meet the objectives we have set ourselves in this book, we will indicate the procedures that should be used to comply with them before these times. 1. February sowing. Obtain six-inch diameter pots, fill them with old potting soil, and sow two squash seeds in each at the beginning of the month. It doesn't matter which variety you choose. Water, then place the pots on a warm layer under cloches or a frame. When the seedlings have emerged, leave only one in each pot, and to avoid shaking it, cut the other by pinching it with the fingernails below the cotyledons, close to the ground. Ordinary care is given. Around the middle of March, one or more muted layers, four feet long by the same width, are established at good exposure. If several are planted, they should be at least eight feet apart, to leave space for the squash branches to run. The layer is filled with eight to ten inches of old potting soil mixed with a quarter of light earth, or better still, half of street mud that has been in a heap for at least a year. The thickness of the layer is calculated so that, when it has settled, it will be roughly level with the surface of the soil. The seedling is removed from the soil, four holes are made in the bed, and two squashes are placed in each hole, with the two rootballs touching. It's best to place only one plant in each hole, if you want the best fruit. These holes should be made at all four corners, but one foot from the edges. Cover with a frame, and give the necessary care to the heated plants. Above all, young plants should be gradually acclimatized to the open air, and when the sun's rays are too intense, which frequently happens in March, they should be shielded from it by means of light-colored cloths, which should be used to cover the frames after opening the baskets. This is essential not only for squash, but for all plants grown in greenhouses and frames. In April, if there are no more white frosts to worry about, remove the glass panels completely, and soon afterwards the frame boxes. To give the plants the space they need, their young branches are spread out in a radial direction around the bed, but carefully so as not to injure them. From this moment on, squash require no further care than that which we will indicate for full-ground plants. 2. March sowing. Like the previous one, this is done in pots and on a warm bed. The young plant is directed as described above. When there is nothing to fear from frosts, holes one foot deep and as wide are dug in a well-exposed square prepared by good ploughing and amended with half-consumed manure, spaced at least ten feet apart. Seven or eight inches of half-consumed manure are thrown in at the bottom, and the holes are filled with half light earth mixed with the same manure. Remove the soil and place two plants in each hole. Cover with a cloche, which is removed when the young gourds want to spread out. All that remains to be done is to care for the plants in the ground. These two seedlings start yielding in June, and produce until the frosts. 3. November seedlings. These seedlings, as well as the next one, are not used for the reasons given above, but they are no less successful. At the beginning of the month, we sow in pots and on a warm bed, as we said in the article on the first sowing, but here the varieties are not indifferent. Choose those that do not run or run very little, such as the Italic squash and the non-runny white squash. Seedlings should be sown with the same care as above. When the plants have filled their pots, they are removed from the soil and placed in a larger vase with a more consistent soil, made up of two-thirds potting soil and one-third light soil. They are then placed on a new layer under a frame, where they are left until they are no longer comfortable in their vase. Then prepare a new warm layer in a tarpaulin, load it with ten inches of potting soil, unstack and transplant onto the layer, leaving three feet between each plant. Air and light are given as often as possible, and heat is maintained by means of the furnace; the heat of the bed is renewed by larding it with hot manure, from underneath, as we said in the cucumber article, and all the usual means are employed against humidity. You should also remove some of the old leaves to let the air, and especially the light, penetrate all parts of the plants, especially the flowers and their buds. Eight days after flowering, goose-egg-sized fruits of great delicacy will be ready for consumption. If the plants are inclined to spread, they can be prevented from doing so by pinching off the tips of the buds. 4° August sowing. Towards the end of the month, we sow in baskets one foot deep and as wide, the same varieties as we have just indicated for the previous sowing. The baskets are planted in a bed facing south, in the open ground, and the usual care is given. When the first white frosts threaten, remove the baskets and transport them to a warm, tarpaulin-covered bed. The plants are cared for as described above, and almost immediately produce. To be forced, gourds have this advantage over 13 other cucurbits: since their fruit can be eaten first after flowering, they don't have to take the chances of those whose ripening you have to wait for. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Squashes like light, free-flowing, substantial soil, amended with half-consumed fertilizers. They grow enormously in those that have been smoked with street sludge and fermented a year in advance. They need good exposure and lots of watering, but although they like water, they would do very badly in naturally damp soil, because the warmth of the soil is even more necessary. When there are no more frosts to fear, usually in April, prepare the soil with fertilizers and good ploughing; eight or ten feet apart, make holes eighteen inches wide and deep, fill them with manure and cover them with eight to ten inches of soil, then plant four or five seeds one inch deep. The seeds are first soaked in water for twenty-four hours, to hasten germination. The water is very lightly soaked and, if desired, covered with a bell jar. When the seeds have sprouted, we thin them out so as to leave only one or two of the most vigorous plants in each hood. Needless to say, if two plants are left, they should be at least a few inches apart. If, through some particular circumstance, the seeds of a hood had run out, young plants could be transplanted without inconvenience, and their growth would be only slightly retarded. In all cases, water often, especially in hot weather. Other care consists of weeding, hoeing and frequent weeding. Like melons and cucumbers, gourds are pruned, but in a very different way. The main branches are usually left to spread as they please, until a fruit is formed; only then are they stopped by pinching them at two or three nodes above the fruit. The secondary branches are also stopped, and the tertiary branches entirely eliminated. There's another type of cultivation that is rarely used, but which is highly advantageous for obtaining very large fruit. When a squash is knotted, instead of pinching the branch, a gutter four or five inches deep is dug in the ground, a little potting soil is thrown in, and the stem is laid down so that the second knot after the fruit is buried at this depth. Roots are thrown in, providing sap to nourish a second fruit that will knot further on, and even a third and fourth if the operation is repeated above each of them. When the autumn frosts begin to make themselves felt, we remove all the fruit and store them in a dry, well-lit place, sheltered from the coldest weather; they will keep perfectly well there for most of the winter. To obtain good seeds, choose the most beautiful, the most honest and the first to ripen; leave to rot on the spot or in the vegetable greenhouse, collect the seeds, wash them and dry them in the shade. They keep well for eight or ten years. To preserve the various varieties of squash, sow them far enough apart to avoid adulterous fertilization. FOUNTAIN CRESSON (sisrmbrium nasturtium); native perennial, growing naturally in almost all clear, running waters. Used in cooking as a garnish, salad, etc. PRIMXUR. Crescon can be found all year round in fountains where the water doesn't freeze easily, so it's hardly ever used in other ways. However, in countries where it is not common, here's how it's done: in spring, an old barrel is used to make a tub eight to ten inches deep; four or five fingeringis of garden soil are placed in the bottom, and filled with well or river water. A few watercress roots are placed in this water, and they soon grow and produce stems that are cut for consumption. The only care required for this crop is to change the water every eight or ten days, and for greater convenience, the tub is placed near a well. In this case, the soil is placed at the bottom of the tub, then sown and watered in such a way as to maintain considerable and permanent humidity, but without submerging the seeds. When they have risen, water is added, but only a few lines, so that the tips of the young shoots are always on the surface. As they lengthen, we increase the height of the water, until finally the tub is full. In this way, you can obtain plants that are more vigorous and more productive, but you have to wait longer for them. You can leave the tub outside until it freezes hard enough to freeze the water; then take it to a vegetable greenhouse or other well-lit, frost-free place. Watercress continues to grow and produce until spring, when it is harvested outdoors. To ensure that your cress is always tender, you must take care never to let it go to seed. The best seeds are those collected in August and September; they keep well for two or three years. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Watercress can be sown in spring, in damp, shady soil, with a northern exposure; but to produce good yields, it must be watered abundantly at least twice a day, to keep the soil, not damp, but constantly wet. Despite this care, watercress is always short, a little hard, and very tasty. If you have a clear, pure body of water in your garden, or a running stream, you can throw a few roots into the mud along its banks, and they will quickly grow back and form large clumps; every year, you can cut back the stems until the hardest frost. In the absence of a pond, plant in a tub, as mentioned above, but leave outside all year round. ALENOIS CRESSON, CULTIVATED PASSAGE (lepidium zativum); annual plant, native to Persia, whose leaves, when young, can be eaten as a salad or as a garnish.
