Four varieties are available: Ordinary cress, the most widely grown and hardy. Curly cress, with curly thresholds; very good as an early variety. The broad-leafed cress, which grows more slowly. Doré, with pale green leaves, less productive. PRIMEUR. In December, as soon as open-ground cress ceases to produce, sow it on the compost of the warm bed for small vegetables. Basin often, and start cutting as soon as the leaf is two or three inches long. Seedlings should be renewed every fifteen to twenty days, as the plants wear out very quickly. CULTURX ORDINAIRE. From the beginning of March to May, sow thickly on old potting soil, every two weeks, as this plant sets seed very quickly. Basin often if spring is dry. From the beginning of May to the beginning of June, sow in potting soil at any exposure. From June to September, sow in cool, shady, north-facing soil. It's especially in this season that a lot of watering is needed, if you don't want the plants to go to seed almost as soon as they've emerged. From the first of September to the first of November, sow in an earthen bed, facing south. Water if the season is dry. These seedlings often survive the winter and produce until spring. All these seedlings are usually sown thickly and in rays. CARDAMINE (cardamine pratensis); perennial, growing naturally in damp meadows. Its leaves have the pungent flavor of watercress, and are used for the same purposes. PRIMEUR. When the frosts begin to stop the vegetation of cardamine, i.e. at the end of November or beginning of December, a few of these plants are lifted with the rootball from a spring sowing, and transported in the compost of a warm bed with small vegetables. Basin the soil and cover with a cloche or frame. Vegetation takes off quickly, and can be cut for use a few days later. When the plants are exhausted, they are replaced by others taken from the same soil. Sowing on a warm bed and under a bell as early as November produces more delicate and tender leaves, but these have to be waited for longer. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Cardamine grows well in all types of soil, provided they are not too dry and are not over-watered, but it does incomparably better in very damp, marshy or even peaty soils. It is sown in thick gullies in spring, frequently watered and thinned out when necessary. With careful weeding, it can last for several years. The best seeds are harvested in September; they are good for two years. CRESSON DE TERRE, ou VIVACE, CRESSON D'AmíRIQUE (Errsimum prœcox); native perennial whose leaves have some of the flavor of watercress, and are used for the same purposes. PRIMEUR. Everything we have said about cardamine in our article on primeur applies exactly to this plant. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Earth cress thrives in clean, light, moist soil. To obtain very tender leaves, it must be sown in fairly light rays every month from the beginning of March, even though it is perennial. In summer, sow in a northern exposure. It requires no more than ordinary care. Its seeds are harvested at the end of August, and keep well for two or three years. PARA CRESSON, ABECEDAIRE, BIDENT, Prai, THRE (Spitanthus oleracea); annual, creeping plant. native to India. It is used in cooking as a garnish, as a seasoning, and as a remedy for watercress, with its pungent flavor. Another species, the BRÍSII CESSON, is also cultivated. (Spilanthus susca), which serves the same purpose. PRIMEUR. In November and December, sow fairly lightly on a warm bed with meuus vegetables. Water moderately, cover with an elorb or frame, and if the plant is too thick, thin out. Cut back when clumps are strong enough. Gorume ia planie monte agez promptement en graines, et qu'elle s'exhaustue vite, il faut semer tous les mois, On doit donner de l'air aux spilanthes, mais avec beaucoup de précaution, car le moindre froid qui pénétrant sous la cloche ou le châssis les tuer infailliblement. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Para cress grows well in all types of soil, but does best in warm, light, sandy soils with a southern exposure. In February or March, it is sown in pots, on beds and under cloches or trays, and transplanted to the open ground when there is no longer any fear of the cold, to which it is extremely sensitive. During the heat of summer, you must not spare it any watering. It flowers from July to autumn, and its seeds can be harvested in September. They are good for three or four years. D DENT-DE-LION, PISSNIIT (Leontodon taraxacum); perennial plant, very common in our meadows, where, from the first days of spring, it is sought out to be eaten in salads, when its young leaves have been blanched by chance. PRIMEUR. Not only is the dandelion not cultivated as a primeur, but even as a much sought-after salad, it is not found in any garden. A few specific experiments enable us to indicate certain procedures for obtaining excellent dandelions during the winter. Dandelion seed is collected from meadows in late spring and early summer, and if you don't want to wait until next spring, sow it immediately in free, substantial soil, amended with half-consumed fertilizer. If sown in spring, the plant does not require large corners; but, if sown late, and with seeds of the year, it is necessary to weed and give some watering during the heat, to advance the plants. The most important thing is to obtain long, thick-stemmed roots. As frost approaches, we buy a number of flower pots, or better still, crates eighteen inches wide and ten inches deep. Fill them with a mixture of half well-consumed potting soil and half sand. Carefully pull out the dandelion roots, so as not to break or injure them; cut off all the leaves two or three lines above the collar, then, using a chopstick, make holes in the crate, slide the roots in up to the collar, half an inch apart, and give a good watering to settle the soil. If you have a tarpaulin, carry the crates to a dark corner; if you don't, simply place them in a warm, dark cellar. Eight days later, it's time to visit the plantation to remove and discard any roots whose crowns have gone mouldy, but these are always rare if the cellar is not too damp. Dandelions are already starting to put out new leaves. If they are not perfectly white, this is proof that light is penetrating the cellar. In this case, a bed of dry litter, five to six inches thick, is thrown over each box. Cut off the leaves when they are long enough to be eaten in salads, and continue harvesting until the plants are fully mature.

exhausted. When this happens, we replace them with others that we take from the ground, and in this way, we don't fail to harvest throughout the winter. Dandelion can be obtained in an easier way, but to do so, you need to use more space on the beds. You transplant it into the soil of the bed, in the same way as we said for the boxes, and, instead of covering it with cloches, you pour over it flowering lice, the holes of which have been plugged beforehand, so as not to let any light in. It may be sufficient simply to spread a good bed of dry litter, or even half-consumed manure, dry and fine, over the plantation. REGULAR CULTIVATION. Cultivated dandelion seems much more pleasant to many people than wild dandelion, because it has lost some of its bitterness. In any soil, but best in open, fresh soil, it is sown in the border in spring, a little thickly, and all care then consists of weeding and watering. In the following spring, as soon as vegetation begins, the plants need to be covered to whiten them. This can be done, as we've said, with flower pots turned upside down, or with litter, leaves, etc.; but the best way is to cover the seedling with four or five inches of potting soil, or to butter it at the same height, with soil lifted up along the rays or border. Scratch a few spots to see if the leaf is long enough and white enough to be harvested. If it is, and before it pierces the bedding layer, cut it off at the root collar. If you have broadcast sown, cover the bed with six inches of dry, half-consumed manure. This last method is used to obtain dandelion all year round, and particularly during the summer. In this latter season, vegetation is suspended; but it is revived by cutting off all the leaves a little above the collar, and then giving good wetting over the manure cover. Dandelion seeds should be harvested a little before they are fully ripe, otherwise they would be blown away by the winds. It will only keep for one or two years. DOLIQUE (Dolicho.). Three species are grown, all annuals, most similar to beans, but with longer pods. Unguiculated dolicho (D. unguiculatus) from the West Indies. In southern France, where it is fairly widely grown, it is known as bannette or mongette. It produces a lot, but ripens with difficulty. Dolique lablab, or Egrpte bean (D. lablab). It has a white-flowered variety. This species is grown as a garden ornamental. It ripens with the same difficulty as the first. Long-podded dolichan or Asparagus bean (D. sesquipedalis) from the West Indies. Its pods, very long, narrow and fleshy, are excellent for eating green, like those of the preceding species. PRIOR. From the standpoint of both cultivation and use, cowpeas differ little from beans, only in their fear of cold. As they have the same taste, we have never bothered to force them. In any case, if we wanted to do so, we'd have to treat them absolutely like beans. To avoid unnecessary repetition, we refer the reader to the article on Beans. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. In the Paris climate, cowpeas can only succeed in free, light, warm soils with a southern exposure. In March, they are sown in small pots which are sown in the compost of a warm layer, under a bell or under a frame. Give them plenty of air, and the usual care for delicate plants. At the beginning of May, a little earlier or a little later, depending on how hot the season is, we prepare a bed at the foot of a wall facing south, by deep ploughing and good topdressing; we unstack the plants, and put them in place with the root ball, a foot apart. They are rowed to make them climb; weeded, weeded and watered during hot spells, and the green pods are harvested before they have reached their full length. Sometimes we're content to sow them in the open ground, in May, at the foot of a wall facing south; but in this case, it's very rare for the seeds to ripen, unless the summer and autumn are very hot. Nevertheless, they provide green harvests. Cowpea seeds are collected before the first white frost, and stored in their pods, where they mature. They retain their germinative properties for several years, but the newer they are, the easier it is for them to germinate. 1 ECHALOTTE (Allium ascalonicum); perennial plant, native to the East, more commonly used in cooking than ordinary garlic, because of its milder odor and milder flavor. PRIOR. Shallot bulbs keep very well throughout the winter, so there's no need to heat this plant; but, as the tops of its leaves are also frequently used for supplies and seasoning, we must indicate the procedures to be used to obtain them in the wrong season. In autumn, as soon as the plants in the open ground cease to produce, which never happens until the frosts begin, we raise a few clumps of shallots, which we plant in the potting soil of the warm bed for small vegetables. Cover with a cloche or a frame, and give little watering, as this plant fears rot. Later, we repeat the plautation, but with dry bulbs preserved for the purpose; we plant them an inch apart, and so that they are covered with only half an inch of potting soil. They are not watered, as the humidity provided by the layer is sutfying to maintain vegetation. When harvesting, be careful to cut the leaves flush with the compost, and the plant will reproduce new ones for a very long time. ORDINARY CULTIVATION This garlic is more delicate than ordinary garlic, and much more afraid of rotting; it is also more difficult to cultivate, and only succeeds perfectly in warm, substantial, light soils. However, with a little care, it can be grown in any garden. If the soil is too strong, mix it with more or less sand, depending on how much you need to lighten it, and don't give it any fertilizer unless it's all used up. In February or March, in the Paris climate, or in autumn in the south, plant in a bed or border, with three or four inches between each clump; cover with only half a finger's worth of soil, to prevent rotting, weed if necessary, and weed a few times. If the rains are abundant and long-lasting, the plants are dethatched a little, so as to uncover the upper end of the onion. At the end of May, we start pulling out a few bulbs suitable for consumption. You can tell when shallots are ripe when their leaves turn yellow, wither and dry out; then you pull them off, separate the bulbs, leave them to dry in the sun for two or three days, and store them in a very dry place, away from frost. To obtain beautiful bulbs, the longest, thinnest cloves should be selected and planted. This plant almost never sets seed, so it can only be propagated by its bulbs, which multiply greatly. When the shallot is allowed to vegetate in suitable soil, it becomes very large, and it is no doubt this particularity that once led to the belief in the existence of a variety known as groite échalotte, 14 EPINARD (Spinacia oleracea); annual plant, native to North America. Everyone knows how to use its leaves in cooking. Two varieties are grown, each with its own sub-variety, namely: Spinach with prickly seeds. Sub-variety with broader leaves. Spinach with smooth seeds, more widespread than spinach, sub-variety with broader leaves. PRIMEUR. If spinach were to be sown on a bed, the large space occupied by the number of plants needed to provide just one dish would force us to give up this type of cultivation. But it can be grown all winter long. From mid-October to the beginning of November, or a little earlier if autumn seems likely to be cold, prepare a bed with good exposure. We amend it with good, half-consumed fertilizer, plow it and loosen it as much as possible. Sow by broadcasting or in fairly thick rays, and cover with a rake. Spinach soon sprouts and sheds its broad leaves. If the weather holds up well, and you can count on a fortnight before the frosts, you can cut the plant for the first time; but if you feel that the spinach won't have time to regrow before the cold, leave it intact to enjoy its leaves through the harsh winter. With two or three planks, you can easily have enough for a house throughout the bad season. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. To grow spinach all year round, as it goes to seed very quickly, you need to sow it every month, from March to October. Sowing from March to May requires good exposure; sowing from May to the end of August requires northern exposure and lots of watering, otherwise the plants go to seed immediately and produce very little. Finally, September and October should be planted in the same way as spring, i.e. with good exposure. Otherwise, the ground is prepared, and seedlings are sown as described in the article on early crops. Spinach takes up a lot of space in the garden, but this disadvantage is offset by its rapid growth. Six weeks after sowing, it has produced its harvest, and can give way to other vegetables. The best seed is collected from plants that have spent the winter and are coming up in spring. When the act of fertilization is complete, the male plants are pulled out to give the females more room, and they are held together with sticks if necessary. The seed retains its germinative properties for two or three years. ESTRAGON (artemisia dracunculus); aromatic perennial native to Tartary. Its young stalks are used in supplies and seasoning, and the more tender ones are preserved in vinegar with cortichon. PRIMEVR. A few days before the frosts, carefully lift a fairly strong clump of sprout, planted the previous spring and not cut for use during the year. Plant it in a warm layer of potting soil with small vegetables, water moderately to encourage growth, and cover with a cloche or frame. If several plants are to be planted, they should be spaced eight inches apart, allowing four to be placed under each cloche. Tarragon receives the same care as the other vegetables on the bed, and soon sprouts young stems that succeed each other throughout the winter, if care is taken to cut one plant at a time and the other at a later date, so that each plant is cut only every month or six weeks. In spring, when the plants have not been too exhausted, they can be put back in the ground, and they recover very well in the course of the year. The old stock usually dies, but many offspring replace it. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Tarragon thrives in open, light soil that is not too damp and very well loosened. It is customary to multiply it only by separating clumps in spring and planting them a foot apart, after preparing the soil with a good ploughing. Weed to prevent the invasion of parasitic weeds, and water during droughts, especially in light, sandy soils. When recovery is perfect, you can start cutting for use, and continue every fortnight until the frosts, but with the precaution of always leaving two to three inches of stem above ground, if you don't want to exhaust the plants. Tarragon can also be propagated by taking cuttings in spring and summer, but this method takes a long time. However, if you want to use it, you'll need to place the cuttings in a shady bed, and smother them with cloches until they recover perfectly. Seeds from this plant are obtained so rarely and with such difficulty that they are never sown. F FENNEL (ancthum fœniculum); aromatic perennial, growing spontaneously in dry, hot soils in southern France. Two varieties are grown: Ordinary fennel. Its leaves are used very rarely as a seasoning, especially for cooking crayfish. Its seeds are used to make jelly beans and flavour liqueurs. Sweet or Italian fennel. Its roots are used in cooking in the same way as celery. The plant is much lower than the previous one, more tender and with a milder flavor. PRICE. As the leaves of ordinary fennel are not very useful, and its seeds retain their desirable qualities through desiccation, only sweet fennel is cultivated by force. This is also the only one we'll be talking about in this early article. In September, in good exposure and in light, warm, terra-cotta soil, make holes four to five inches wide and as deep, eight inches apart, in equally spaced rows, and fill them with good, well-consumed compost. Sow four or five fennel seeds in each hole, and water. Care is taken with frequent tarring and weeding, and watering is done every day if autumn is dry and hot. Once the seedlings have sprouted, only one is left in each hood. When the first frosts set in, shelter the fennel with cloches or frames, or simply with straw mats and bedding. Air and light must be provided as often as possible, as otherwise the plants will bleach and rot. Fennel is blanched as required for consumption, always a fortnight in advance, as this is the time needed to obtain perfectly white and tender fennel. Fennel can be blanched on the spot or on the bed. In the first case, the stalks are mounded with earth and placed closer together, then surrounded by crushed litter or dry manure. In the second case, the plants are uprooted, their stems are bundled together with a straw tie, and the plants are buried in potting soil in the vegetable greenhouse, up to seven or eight inches above the root collar. Fennel can be grown in this way throughout the winter and part of the spring, as the plant is good for blanching until it rises. Ordinary cultivation methods only produce edible roots in early June, and then successively throughout the summer, so you need to sow on a bed to get some as soon as the autumn season ends, i.e. in April and May. To achieve this, sow in January in warm, pure potting soil under cloches or frames. As this plant loves heat, the bed must be maintained with heaters, which are renewed as often as necessary. Provide air and light, water moderately, and thin out the seedlings so they don't wilt. In February, dig a slice three feet deep and four feet wide in a warm spot; lay a layer of soil ten inches thick, mixed with light earth, and place the seedlings in it; remove the seedlings very carefully, without breaking any of the roots, and leaving as much water as possible behind. Transplant eight inches apart, water and cover with a frame or cloches. If cloches are used, the plants are grouped three by three under each cloche, six inches apart, leaving a one-foot gap between each group. Protect from the cold with bedding and straw mats, and don't give air until the plants have recovered, but from then on, give air as often as possible. From the beginning of April, and sometimes earlier, until May, fennel is strong enough to be blanched as needed. Fennel is not grown in this way in France, but it is very well known in England. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Common fennel and Halie fennel also like light, warm soil with good exposure. The first is sown in March, very lightly, and in place. The plant is thinned out, watered and weeded while young, then left to nature's own devices. As long as the soil is suitable, fennel, which is very hardy, will reseed itself. Italian fennel can be sown in several seasons, in the same way as for the September sowing, in the early article, and in holes at the same distance. It is trained like any other outdoor plant, with frequent thinning and watering. After forty days, it is ready for blanching. The first sowing is done at the beginning of March and gives in June; that of April gives in July and August; that of May in August and September; and that of June, in September, October and November. Sowing can be continued in July and even into early August, if desired, but requires a lot of water, as does sowing in June. Summer fennel can be blanched by mounding, just like celery. Fennel seeds are picked as they ripen, otherwise they fall off and are lost. They keep well for two or three years. FERTILIZER (faba vulgaris major); annual plant, native to Persia. Its seeds, dry and mainly green, are suitable for various manicures; they are floury, and are thought to be less indigestible than those of other leguminous plants. Several varieties are grown, including : La grosse sève ordinairs. Two-foot stems and very large, flat seeds. Picarde is a smaller variety. The Windsor bean, with tall, strong stems like the previous one, and very large, rounded seeds. Its leaves are larger and wider than in any other variety, but usually contain only two seeds. The haitive dwarf bean, or mazagane of the English, originates from the coasts of Africa. It is very productive, very bushy, and its stems don't grow higher than a foot, which makes it very suitable for cultivation as an early crop under glass. It has the disadvantage of degenerating very quickly if care is not taken to choose the most honest plants in their variety as seed-bearers. Julienne or petlte feve, high yielding and very early. * Petite feve d'hiver (small winter feve): very hardy, little fear of cold; also productive. The feve d'héligoland, quite similar to the two preceding ones, also productive, but less precocious. These three varieties are used more for field cultivation than for gardens. Feve à longue cossc, early, with pods containing more seeds than those of the other varieties, and therefore more productive. The green bean, so named because its seeds remain green after drying. It originates from China and is very productive, but is a little later than the others. PRIMEUR. In December, we prepare a sloping bed along a wall facing south, perfectly amended with consumed fertilizers, and load it with a finger of compost. Sow ordinary broad beans in rays or clumps, cover with potting soil and, if it's cold, spread a bit of large-scale litter on top, which you leave for a few days, until the beans begin to emerge. Then remove it to prevent the plants from wilting, and replace it only during the night. When it's getting colder, we build a wall of manure, a foot thick and more or less high, depending on the height of the beans, and place poles or sticks across it to support the mats and bedding we cover the plants with every evening, and even during the day when the thermometer falls below four or five degrees. Whenever the sun shines, even if only for an hour, we provide light, and the more advanced the plants, the more light they need. During the intervals between one frost and the next, the cover is removed entirely, but although the beans are fairly resistant to four or five degrees of cold, you must be careful not to let yourself be surprised, as they become much more susceptible to it when they have been covered. Once the frosts have passed, the manure, straw mats and bedding are removed, and the beans are soon producing. Some gardeners don't bother to cover their seedlings, but they often lose them too. In January, take advantage of a fine day, and sène tur an ados with good exposure, as we said for the previous sowing. The same care is taken, and the products follow on from the first. In November, or even September, open a trench four feet wide and three feet deep, and lay down a bed of warm manure and dry leaves, then another bed of manure and leaves, and so on until the bed is made. Fill it with seven to eight inches of well-consumed potting soil mixed with a quarter of light earth, and sow it with rays of dwarf faba bean. Between each ray, small pots four inches in diameter, filled with the same potting mix, are sown on the layer, and in each pot ten or three broad beans are planted, for the use we shall indicate. The pots are wetted and covered with a frame, which is not opened until the plants begin to emerge; but from that moment onwards, as much air as possible should be allowed in, and whenever the outside temperature does not drop to zero. When the layer begins to cool, open a trench eighteen inches wide around the trunk, and place stoves in it, renewing them every time they are needed. With a little skill, you can even pull some of the old manure out from under the bed and line it with new. Plants raised on a bed receive the usual care, and the beans produce more the warmer they are. Let's get back to the beans planted in pots. When the dull layer we've just been talking about starts to lose heat, we raise a new, unburied layer with very hot exposure, and make it three feet high and as wide. We make it narrow so that the heat from the stoves can penetrate it more easily. We fill it with six or seven inches of potting soil mixed as we said, and let it go through its first fire. The beans sown in pots are taken from the old layer, unstuffed and planted with the clod on the new one. Water moderately, and cover with frames, over whose panels you throw a lot of bedding when it's very cold. Air is given whenever the air is at a suitable temperature, and new heaters are placed every twelve or fifteen days. The dwarf bean, treated in this way, produces long before the one sown on the deaf layer. Finally, in order to have early broad beans to succeed those grown in full soil from the first frosts, sow four or five seeds in late summer of the same haitive seve nalne, in seven-inch-diameter pots filled with good soil made up of half compost and half garden soil. In a north-facing bed, bury the pots, taking care to place them on a bed of sand or gravel, and give the plants the same care you would give to seedlings grown in the open ground. Above all, they would need plenty of water, given the season. In November, the beans would be removed and planted on a warm bed under a frame. They would soon start producing. REGULAR CULTIVATION. Broad beans, whatever their variety, like light, substantial, well-smoked and loosened soils. However, with the right fertilizers, they can be grown anywhere. In February, March and April, with ordinary exposure, and until May, June and July, with damp soil and northern exposure, prepare a bed by ploughing well and using fertilizer, and draw parallel lines with a chalk line, one foot apart. These lines are sown in rays or clumps. To sow in rays, open a furrow with the weeder, throw the beans in so as to leave five or six inches between each plant, and cover with an inch of soil. If you're furrowing in clumps, use the hoe to make a trench an inch deep and five or six inches wide, one foot apart on each row. Throw in three or four beans, and cover them with the soil from the second hole, which in turn is plugged with the soil from the third, etc. When the plant has risen and is four inches high or thereabouts, we give the first hoeing, and start bubbling by bringing the soil between the lines around the feet. This forces the beans to form new roots, increasing their vigor and making them more productive. A fortnight later, a second hoeing is carried out, and the plants are relined in the same way. Finally, the soil around the plants can be raised by a third hoeing, but this operation is not essential. Weed and water frequently when the weather is dry and hot. If we didn't take care to keep the soil of the plants we've sown from May to July continually moist, it's almost certain that they wouldn't produce anything. As soon as the flower has bloomed, it is customary to pinch the tips of the branches with the nails, in order to force the sap, by stopping the plant's growth, to focus exclusively on the fruit, thus increasing both its size and earliness. Many people estimate the sap only when it is very young, and at most a third or a quarter of its normal size. If this is the case, we pick all the beans in a row, more or less, and if the season is not too advanced, we cut the stems on the ground. They immediately grow back new branches, which will produce a second green harvest before the bad season. Beans are also prone to degeneration. As soon as you start to notice this, you need to renew the seeds and bring them from another country, or at least change their exposure and soil. Seeds are harvested from the best varieties of the first spring sowing. To do this, we pull up the stalks, detach the pods and dry them in an airy place, but in the shade. The seeds, enclosed in their pods, retain their germinative virtue for five years or more. STRAWBERRY (Fragaria); native perennial, producing a delicious fruit known to all. It is grown in a large number of varieties and a few species, which we will describe in the order in which M. Noisette classified them in his Manuel, an order which M. Vilmorin adopted in Le bon jardinier, with a few modifications. This skilful farmer divides them into seven races: 1. common strawberries, 2. starry strawberries, 3. goat strawberries, 4. scarlet strawberries, 5. pineapple strawberries, 6. Chilean strawberries, 7. Indian strawberry strawberries, to which he refers all the varieties known to him. I. COMMON STRAWBERRIES. They are indigenous. Their leaves are small to medium-sized, blond-green; their flowers are small; their fruit, round or oblong, are very tasty. Woodland strawberry (Fragaria vesca); flowers sometimes dioecious; red fruit, small, round or elongated, considered the best of all. Sub-varieties: With white fruit, less esteemed. With large flowers. Red fruit; flowers often dioecious. With one leaf. Obtained by M. Duchesne of Versailles. It differs from its tipe in that its leaf is composed of a single leaflet. Its first flowers are often semi-double, and its red fruit is excellent. If its cultivation is neglected, its leaves take on three leaflets. Alpine strawberry (Fragaria semperflorens). Produces throughout the year, from April to frost, in the open ground, and throughout the winter if grown in frames or hot greenhouses. Fruits are medium-sized, elongated and very smoky, comparable in flavor to wild strawberries. This species is prone to degeneration, but is easily renewed by sowing. M. Vilmorin says that if sown on a bed in March, it produces in May, and that its coulans flower at the age of twenty days. Sub-varieties: White-fruited. Its fruits are the same size, but rounded. Fraisier de Gaillon or des Alpes without coulans. It differs from its type only in that it has no netting, which makes it very suitable for growing in borders. From the other 15 it produces the same, and its fruit is excellent, but a little smaller. We owe it to M. Le Baube, who found it in Gaillon a few years ago. Strawberry of the Alnes without coulans and with white fruits. It differs from the previous variety only in the color of its fruits, which are better and more abundant. Sometimes it reproduces identically from seed, but more often it returns to its type. Strawberry bush. Varieties without coulans, suitable for forming borders, formerly cultivated. Its fruit is good, but small; it yields less than the wild strawberry, of which it is perhaps only a sub-variety. Double-flowered strawberry. This variety is hardly cultivated except as a curiosity. Its flowers, though double, produce quite beautiful, but very acid fruit. II. STAR STRAWBERRIES. These are indigenous and can be recognized by their small, dark-green or bluish leaves; their grey stems bear small, often unisexual flowers. Their fruits are round, small and of various flavors and colors. Bargemont strawberry, or majaufe. Blond-green leaves; very long stalks; numerous, rounded, firm, dark-red fruits, very good, but with a musky taste that is not to everyone's taste. Produces a second crop in autumn, but does not always ripen. Sub-variety: Fraïsier de Montreuìl. Similar in foliage to the previous variety, but with larger, alloughed fruits, often compressed and terminated by two nipples; ripens earlier and produces only once. Heterophyllous strawberry; blond-green leaves, composed of three and five leaflets; flowers first greenish and nodding, then white and on straight, stiff stalks; round, slightly hairy fruit, quite pleasant-tasting, but with seeds that crunch under the tooth. In autumn, it produces a second crop that doesn't have time to ripen. Champagne strawberry, Champagne vine, English green strawberry. Small, blond-green leaves; spindly stems and stalks; fruits of mediocre quality, rounded or oblong, brownish on the sunny side and whitish on the other. Small-leaved strawberry. Leaves small, silky, bluish-green, stalks grey and flat-lying; fruits small, oblong, light red, pleasantly flavored. III. CAPRINE STRAWBERRIES. Indigenous. They have large, hairy, blond-green leaves; their peduncles are strong and straight; their flowers are hermaphroditic or unisexual, of medium size, with a raised calyx; their fruits are large, rounded, dark red, and have a distinctive flavor. Common capron. Flowers mostly female; large, swollen fruit of mediocre quality. Sub-varieties: Capron male. Flowers all male, large, and consequently no fruit. Planted in a mixture with the preceding variety, to fertilize it. Roral capron. Hermaphroditic flowers; fruit with firm, musky flesh, with a flavor that is not generally appreciated. Capron-abricot; fairly rare sub-variety; fruit with a flavor approaching that of the apricot, very good. Raspberry capron. Purplish-red fruit with a pronounced raspberry flavor. Very difficult to tie. Prolific Hautboy. Very large, sun-colored, very smoky fruit. This beautiful variety was brought from England by M. Noisette, in 18a4. IV. SCARLET STRAWBERRIES. Most are native to America. Their leaves are very large, bluish-green; the flowers are hermaphroditic and unisexual; the fruits, of varying size, reddish to scarlet in color, are earlier than the others and often borne on weak peduncles. Their seeds are embedded in large alveoli. Virginia strawberry. Leaves composed of narrow leaflets; female, hermaphrodite flowers; small, rounded, purple-red fruit, the earliest of all, good, fragrant, bearing from May to the end of June, and sometimes in autumn. Suitable for frames. Sub-variety: Pink or very pale red fruit, medium size, very good. Strawberry Roseberry. Low, thin foliage; fruit quite large, abundant, elongated, good, borne on stalks. It bears fruit a second time in autumn, but has difficulty ripening outdoors. This is the variety preferred by the English for growing under frames. Scarlet oblong. Late-ripening, very large fruit, which holds up better than the previous variety. Grimstone. Strong, vigorous plant with numerous coulans; large, late, good, very sweet fruit. American scarlet. Vigorous foliage; strong, fairly well-supported peduncles; very late and very beautiful fruit, very numerous, oblong, very dark red. Duke of Kent strawberry. Very small, very deep-rooted plant; peduncles hold up well; fruits very early, small, rounded, very numerous. V. American ANANAS. Their leaves are very large, with very broad leaflets; their flowers are all hermaphroditic and very large; the fruit, rounded or oval, is large, red, pink or white, generally not very fragrant, but nevertheless quite good. Carolina strawberry. Mediocre fruit, fairly large, more or less scarlet-red, rounded, often shiny, sometimes dull, high yielding. Sub-varieties: Long-fruited. With white fruits. Rare. Bath strawberry. Broader, lower leaves; larger fruits, white on one side, red on the other, rounded or elongated, borne on plump stalks, watery and not very fragrant. 1 11 15 1 13 3 2 Pineapple strawberry. Very large foliage; short peduncles, thickening into a club when ripe; large, bright scarlet fruits with few seeds set in deep cells; watery, bland flavor, especially in rainy weather. Sub-variety. Long fruits. Downton strawberry. Vigorous plant; blond-green, blister-like leaves; long, well-supported stalks; large, elongated, blackish-red fruits, very abundant, firm, fragrant, excellent and long-lasting. Kean's Imperial strawberry. Fruits of very pleasant flavor, very large, very bright red on the sunny side. Brought from England by Mr. Noisette. Strawberry-Kean's-Seedling. Vigorous plant; abundant, rounded, very large, dark-red fruit, with equally red, fragrant and excellent flesh. Pine-scarlet strawberry. Very smoky, bright red fruit of medium size. Prince-black strawberry. Fruit dark purple, fairly large, pleasant flavor. These five varieties, bred in England, degenerate rapidly if not cultivated properly. VI. CHILE FRESHIXRS. From America. Leaves less tall than previous varieties; flowers very large, unisexual or hermaphrodite. Fruit erects when ripe, which is positively the opposite of other strawberry plants. Superb Wilmot. This variety appears to be a hybrid of the pineapple and the fruit tree. Its fruits are dark red and surprisingly large. I measured some at Mr. Noisette's that were five and a half inches in circumference, and Mr. Wilmot, an Englishman who obtained it, says he has seen some eight inches. They are quite good, but inferior in quality to many of the previous breeds. Chilean strawberry, or frutilier. Dioecious plant, i.e. bearing only female flowers, or only male flowers. Fruits are very large, yellowish-white, vermilion-red on the sunny side, with an unpleasant taste and almost no fragrance. This species is delicate. It needs very-warm exposure, and good vegetable garden soil or heather. To obtain fruit, you need to own the male, or at least plant it in a mixture with Carolina strawberries, pineapples or caprons, whose flowers fertilize its own. Sub-variety: Chilean fertile strawberry. In this variety, all flowers are hermaphroditic. The fruit is smaller than in the previous variety, but more abundant and of better quality. VII. INDIAN STRAWBERRY. This race contains only one very remarkable species, which has not produced any varieties. Its leaves are small and pale green. Its flowers are yellow; its fruits are oval-rounded, bright red, but tasteless. They sit on a large involucre under the calyx, composed of five crenellated lobes. PRIMEUR. We have marked with an asterisk the three varieties usually used for this type of cultivation: the Alpine strawberry, the Virginia grape and the Roseberry. We'll start with the first method. In spring, four-season strawberry plants are planted in warm conditions, in beds of indeterminate length, but only three feet wide, with a two-foot interval between each bed. Throughout the year, ordinary care is taken, and in particular, watering is not spared when the season is dry. At the start of winter, when vegetation is threatened by the first frosts, the paths between the beds are dug at least a foot deep and eighteen inches wide, and filled with well-packed hot manure. Chassis are placed on the planks, and surrounded by stoves of the same manure, up to the height of the panels. During the night, we throw good straw mats over the whole thing, and cover them in the morning. Air is given as often as possible. Strawberry plants continue to vegetate and produce throughout the winter, provided that the stoves are renewed frequently and the plants are protected from the slightest chill. Four-season strawberries and the other two varieties we've mentioned can be bedded. For the four-season strawberry, here's how it's done. As soon as the first strawberries ripen, i.e. in May and June, sow them in trays and give them the care we mention in the article on ordinary cultivation. As soon as the plants are strong enough, we fill seven-inch-diameter pots with a mixture of one-third light garden soil, one-third heather soil and one-third well-consumed potting compost, and transplant them, placing three plants in each pot. Bury the pots in a flowerbed, and give them the usual cultivation. During the first frosts, the pots are transported on a warm layer in a tarpaulin or under a frame, peeled often to rid them of dead leaves and coulans, watered moderately, kept warm, given air when possible, and they flower and fruit all year round. You don't need to bury the pots in a layer in a furnace-heated tarpaulin, or in a warm greenhouse, you can simply place them on shelves in a well-lit area. You can also use the layers of the flying greenhouse in which you grow vines or other fruit trees. As for Roseberry and Virginia fruit trees, they are lifted with the rootball, and transplanted to a warm bed under a frame, where they soon bear fruit. But as they need to rest for a long time after a first harvest to produce a second, if you want to have them all winter long, you need to lift them from the ground every fortnight to transport them under the frames. They can also be planted in pots and grown on the shelves of a tarpaulin or a warm greenhouse. To grow strawberries throughout the rest of the year, from spring to the first frosts, all you need to do is plant a few beds of Alpine strawberries and water them daily during the hot summer months. CULTURX ORDINAIRX. Strawberry plants are generally successful everywhere, but to produce abundant, fragrant fruit, they need light, warm, semi-shaded soil, perfectly fertilized with much-used fertilizers. Capronia, scarlet and pineapple are less demanding in terms of soil quality; they grow quite vigorously in strong, slightly damp soils, but their fruit is of poor quality there. Strawberry plants can be propagated in three ways: 1. by seed, 2. by shattering, 3. by separating their coulans or fillets. Propagating by seed is infinitely advantageous, because it produces plants that are much more vigorous and productive, and offers the hope of finding new varieties superior to the existing ones. But this method of propagation can only be used for species or varieties that reproduce identically through their seeds, and there aren't many of these. For the others, therefore, we must use the splinter or coulan method. Let's start with sowing. As soon as the strawberries have reached perfect maturity, usually at the end of June, we pick them and crush them with our hands in a vase full of water. The pulp is removed, and the seeds sink to the bottom. After several washes, the seeds are easily removed. Strawberries can also be dried by laying them out in the shade, exposed to a draught, after which the seeds are easily removed by rubbing them with the fingers. Sow immediately, either in the ground or in pots. In the first case, a portion of the bed is ploughed and loosened as best you can, exposed to the sunrise, then top-dressed, raked to unify perfectly, and wetted with a watering can to avoid leading. Mix the seeds with a quantity of ash or fine sand, to sow more evenly, and spread them over the soil. Cover them with no more than half a line of very fine potting compost or heather soil, which you sift over the top. All that's left is to keep the soil continually moist, especially at the surface, to facilitate germination. To do this, you need to water very carefully, because if you beat the soil, the seeds won't germinate. Other care is the same as for all fine, delicate seedlings. Shelter from the sun and drying winds with straw mats, etc. Fifteen or twenty days later, the seedling will have sprouted, and care should be redoubled. If it has not been neglected, six weeks or two months later it will be strong and can be planted; it will produce even if you have sown four-season. Terracotta sowing is done in two ways: 1. after filling the vase with a mixture of two-thirds heather soil and one-third fully-consumed compost, sow and mulch with a small layer of very fine chopped moss. Water over the top, and place the terrine in a warm but shady spot. 2. After filling the terrine with the indicated mixture, unite the soil, throw in the seed and, instead of covering it, simply press it down with a small board or the flat of your hand. To water, place the terrine in a tub containing one or two inches of water: this enters through the holes, is absorbed by the soil and moistens it to the surface. This avoids the need for a watering can, which always plummets the surface to a greater or lesser extent, and all the seeds rise much more easily. When the plants have gained some strength, and find themselves too cramped in their terrines, we transplant them indoors or in a nursery, in well-prepared soil. Varieties with large foliage are planted permanently ten inches apart, and others seven or eight inches apart. It should be noted that if you wish, you can save the seed for sowing the following spring, but in this case it takes much longer to germinate. Terracotta sowing should be preferred for carefully managed crops, because the seeds germinate in much greater numbers and much more easily than in the open ground, where more than three-quarters are always lost. While the plant is young, it should be watered often, hoeed and weeded, and the runners scrupulously removed as they appear, so as not to let it become too weak. Propagation by bursting is the most commonly used method. It is done in autumn and spring. If this operation is carried out in autumn, some of the weaker plants may melt during the winter, but the plantation will produce abundantly the following spring. If, on the other hand, you wait until spring to plant, all the plants recover easily, but only produce the following year. It is therefore advantageous to plant in autumn. The old clumps are lifted, separated into as many parts as can be obtained with good roots, and immediately replanted in beds or borders, at the distance we have mentioned. If the season is dry, water them thoroughly until they recover perfectly. With the exception of two or three varieties, all strawberry plants produce coulans or nets, a species of long, slender, creeping stems, which emit raciues and a tuft of leaves, forming a new plant at each node. When multiplication is not intended, they are pulled out as they appear, because they tire the mother plant and prevent it from producing an abundant harvest; but in the opposite case, as early as August they are left to spread at will, then, in October, they are removed to transplant all the rooted plants they have produced. Whether propagating by sprouting or coulans, or permanently transplanting seedlings obtained from sowing, planting requires a few precautions. The ground is prepared by ploughing and fertilizing, loosening the soil as much as possible, and, if planting is to be done in a bed, the first step is to mulch, because if you waited until planting was finished, this operation would be very difficult to carry out. For this, straw is chopped as short as possible. After mulching, plant in staggered rows, ten to twelve inches apart. Water abundantly, shelter from the sun with straw mats supported by stakes, and, when the plant has recovered, hoe, or weed, and pull out the runners. At the beginning of winter, a good fingerful of three-quarter-used manure is spread over the whole bed, as it is removed from an old layer. The following spring, after removing any dead leaves and coulans, we bury it in biuant. Straw is chopped up very finely. The purpose of this operation is to prevent the soil from becoming waterlogged, to keep the surface moist, to keep out slugs, and to prevent the strawberries from getting dirty on the ground. Strawberries only produce a lot in the second and third years of planting. They must therefore be renewed every three years, either by sowing them anew, or by breaking them up as we have said. The bed from which they came is ploughed and fertilized, and they can be replanted immediately; but if this can be done without inconvenience, it is even better to move them to another site. If, for any particular reason, you want to keep old strawberry plants, every autumn you should replant them with one or two inches of good soil. This would allow them to put down new roots, which would maintain their vigor and fertility. "In England," says Mr. Noisette, "the Horticultural Society places great emphasis on perfecting the cultivation of this delicious fruit, of which they have obtained a large number of new varieties. They plant from one to two feet apart, depending on the size of the plant. Precious species are lightly "butted" once flowering has passed; they are "mulched" as we have said, or slates or tiles are laid under each "foot to support or keep clean the fruits that their weight drags to the ground. This method avoids the inconvenience of washing strawberries, which takes away some of their fragrance. The biggest enemy of strawberries is the larva of the common chafer, commonly known as the white, ton or Turkish worm. Strawberry plants are attacked when their foliage wilts for no apparent reason. The larva is found and killed. When the root of the strawberry plant is not completely cut off, it is replanted, given good watering, and it usually starts growing again. It is said that horn shavings mixed into the fertilizers given to the plants keep out this pernicious insect, the greatest scourge of horticulture, but this observation seems to me to need to be confirmed by new experiments. In any case, the oldest known method seems to me to be still the most effective today. It consists in hunting beetles in their season, morning and evening, and killing them as they are caught. G CULTIVATED LEMON, SPANISH LEMON (Lathyrus sativus); annual plant, more commonly cullivated in the field than in the garden, whose seeds are eaten dry as a purée, and green as peas. As peas are of a much higher quality than chickling peas, we have never tried to force chickling peas onto the soil, as they would take up a lot of space and produce a very small product. Nevertheless, if someone wanted to try this kind of cultivation, it could easily be done by applying to the chickling plant what we said in the article on peas. Chickling grows in all types of soil, provided they are not too damp, but it prefers those that are a little light, substantial and well exposed. In March and April around Paris, and in autumn in the south of France, seedbeds are prepared with good ploughing and half-consumed fertilizers, and sown in clumps or rays. The holes in the clumps are made ten inches apart, and when sowing in rays, eight inches apart. If you want to make the harvest last longer, you can sow as early as February, in a bed facing south, and in warm, sandy soil. In all cases, care is limited to hoeing, weeding and watering during drought and heat, until harvest time. As the cultivation of chickling vetch is absolutely the same as that of peas, we won't go into further detail on this subject, to avoid unnecessary repetition. 16 GOMBO, KETMIE COMESTIBLE ( Hibiscus esculentus) annual plant, native to South America. In the kitchen, we use its still-green fruit, which we eat as a stew, and its young shoots, which we prepare in a variety of ways. Its flowers resemble those of the marshmallow. PRIMEUR. As okra is not yet widely grown in France, it has not yet been subjected to forced cultivation, at least as far as we know. However, if it were to be subjected to forced cultivation, here's how it would be done. In May, June and July, we would sow the plants under a frame, in pots filled with half potting soil and half well-mixed light garden soil, and we would press the pots into the potting soil of an old layer. We would give the usual care to a seedling, and above all we would not scruple about watering. In autumn, the plants would be strong. As soon as the first light frosts occur, which we must take great care to prevent, we would remove the soil and place the plants with the root ball in a warm layer of potting soil, in a tarpaulin heated by the furnace, or under a frame, but then we would have to maintain the warmth of the layer by frequently renewing the stoves. It is also essential to place the plants as close as possible to the glass, so that they can enjoy the light, especially during flowering. If you had a few empty spaces on the shelves of a hot greenhouse, you could place a few plants there, after having put slightly larger pots in them. Their flowers would make a pleasant ornament, and their fruit would quickly reach the right size for consumption. Whatever the means used to heat them, they should be watered often, but little at a time, and given plenty of air and light. To have okra fruit in spring, you'd have to sow in pots in autumn, and winter the young plants in a tarpaulin heated by a furnace. In March, take them out and place them, with the root ball, in a warm layer of potting soil, under a frame. As the plant sometimes reaches a height of four feet, it should in all cases be pinched back as soon as it reaches a foot or eighteen inches, to force it to produce laterally spreading branches. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: In February, sow three or four seeds in pots filled with the same mixture as above. The pots are sunk into a warm layer of potting soil, and covered with cloches or frames. When the seedlings have emerged, leave just one in each pot. In March, a new warm layer is raised and planted. Water, air and light are provided until May, when the plants are removed and planted in a bed at the foot of a south-facing wall, in loosened and perfectly top-dressed soil. All that's left to do is provide the usual care and daily watering, as this plant loves water. Okra fruit can also be obtained simply by sowing in a warm layer of soil, in February and March, without pots, and transplanting to another warm layer, where the plant is raised until it is strong enough to be placed indoors. Sometimes, however, transplanting delays this, and the seeds don't have time to ripen before winter. However, it can be obtained by transplanting it permanently on a new bed under a high frame, or at least on a well-sheltered hillside in light, amended soil. If, in the Paris climate, you want to be sure of collecting good seeds, you must keep at least a few plants under chassis. I4 HARICOTS (Phaseolus vulgaris); annual plant, native to India, which is too well known to mention here its various uses in cooking. Cultivation and climatic differences have produced a large number of varieties, which we will mention here, according to the table presented by Mr. Noisette in his manual. SECTION ITe. - GRIMPAN BEANS, which require rowing. 1. those with white seeds. Soissons bean. Large, flat bean, very thin when dry. This is nothing other than the common flat-seeded bean, generally cultivated, but acquiring a superior quality in Soissons that it does not usually have in other soils. We therefore regard it only as a local variety. Haricot-Sabre, Sabre d'Allemagne, Haricot cossé. Pods of extraordinary width and length, excellent when young, still tender, fleshy and very good when almost fully grown. They are cut into strips and preserved in salt for winter eating. The seed of this variety, which produces a lot, is medium-sized, flattened and the best of all beans, either green or dry. This vigorous plant climbs very high; it needs long oars. Prédome, Prudhomme, Prodommet. Small pod, absolutely parchment-free, excellent, still edible even when semi-dry. Seed white, round, small, very good, new or dry. I have an excellent sub-variety from Caen, whose seeds make the pod appear articulated. Another sub-variety with yellow seeds is known. Sophie bean. Medium-sized, flat, thick-skinned, hard, watery bean of very mediocre quality. This variety is best eaten green, when the pods are three-quarters full. Rice bean. White, oblong, very small bean, excellent when eaten new, often mediocre when dry, especially in damp soils; its green pods are tender and quite good. This variety produces a lot. Lima bean. Short, broad, rough pods, good green color; thick, very large, dirty-white seeds, very floury and excellent dry. This variety climbs very high and requires long oars; it produces a lot, but is late and delicate in the Paris climate. To obtain perfectly ripe seeds suitable for sowing, raise a few plants in pots in March, and transplant them with the rootball in the sin of April or beginning of May, in the open ground, in good exposure. Only one seed is sown in each pot, and when planting, space the plants ten inches apart. Haricot bean. Medium-sized seeds, very good when new, but a little tough-skinned when dry. Excellent green pods. This variety is the earliest of all. Haricot de Picardie or de Liancourt. This variety produces a lot; it has a great analogy with the Soissons bean, but the plant is much more vigorous, rises higher, and the seeds are larger; these are flat, with a slightly hard skin when dry. The pods are quite good when green. Round bean or Pea bean. Numerous pods, good in green; very small, rounded, reddish-white seeds, soft, tasty, very good when dried. Gigantic bean. This variety, which produces little, provides an extraordinarily vegetative plant; it needs reams eight to nine feet long. Very large, flattened seeds, good when new, a little tough-skinned when dry. .2. Colored beans. Prague beans or red peas. A very late variety, but extremely productive in warm autumns. Requires very high raking. Pods without parchment, excellent when green; round, reddish-purple seeds, very good when new; when dry, they are very floury, not at all watery, with a pleasant flavor somewhat similar to that of chestnuts, but with a slightly thick skin. Prague bicolor. Very late, with the same qualities and the same defects as the previous variety; seeds variegated red and purple. Doe belly. Oblong, tawny-yellow seeds, quite good dry, but better mashed than with their skins. Excellent green pods. This variety remains dwarfed in dry, light or sandy, warm soils. It is a sub-variety of the Swiss Bean. Grivelé. A very productive variety, but its seeds, which are good dry, are flax-gray mottled with black, but have the disadvantage of turning the water in which they are cooked purple-black. Green pods. Purplish marbled. Reddish seeds, mottled brown. Very good green pods. Pink bean. Low-yielding variety; tender pods, good in green; more or less grayish-pink seeds, fairly good in dry conditions. Kidney bean. Vigorous, high-yielding plant requiring long oars; pods good for greening; red seeds, fairly good dry. Cardinal bean. Very large, flattened, white seeds with a broad purple crown around the germ, of mediocre quality when dry, and very difficult to ripen. Pods quite tender and quite good green, late variety. SECTION II. - DARK BEANS, 1. those with white seeds. Hatif de Hollande. This variety is the earliest of all, and for this reason should be preferred for early cultivation under chesnut trees. Its pods are long, narrow and excellent for green cultivation; its seeds are quite good for dry cultivation. Hatif d'Argenson. Similar to the previous one, but even earlier; very good for cultivation under chassis. Haricot Flageolet or hátif de Laon. Very early variety, very low growing, high yielding, very suitable for under-frame cultivation. Long, narrow pods, excellent when green; narrow, elongated, almost cylindrical seeds, quite good when dry. This variety is one of the most widespread and highly esteemed around Paris. Haricot dain de Soissons or gros pied. Almost as early as the previous variety; large pods; seeds of mediocre size, flattened, excellent when new, quite good dry. White parchment bean. This variety, like the next, forms very large, very branched clumps. They are very much afraid of damp soil, as their pods, which are very long and attached very low down, drag along the ground and often rot. Seeds small, flattened, excellent new and dry; pods very good in green, without parchment until they have reached three-quarters of their size. Very productive variety, as well as the following one. Sabre dwarf. The plant is very similar to the previous one, but its pods are wider and longer; otherwise, the bean has the same qualities. These two varieties should be sown light, in dry soil, and two or three seeds are enough for a clump. White American bean. A low, bushy, highly branched and highly productive plant, but with the disadvantage of becoming a bit of a climber if sown in open, slightly cool soil, or if the season is damp. Pods large, swollen, a little arched, strongly colored red-brown, especially at the tips, absolutely parchment-free, and therefore excellent in green. Seeds are small, a little elongated, white and very good when dry. This plant should not be sown thickly: two or three seeds per clump are sufficient. Deuz per clump. Low stem, even more branched than the previous variety; excellent green pods, no parchment; very good seeds when newly shelled. Very productive variety. Swiss bean. Fairly early variety, but prone to bolting; elongated, cylindrical, reddish-white seeds, rather mediocre when dry; excellent green pods. a. Dwarf beans with colored seeds. Swiss red. Elongated, cylindrical, red seeds, variegated with different colors depending on the soil, very good dry; excellent green pods. Swiss gray. Elongated, cylindrical seeds, blackish-red with white mottling, very good when dry; excellent green pods, very good when picked at three-quarters of their size and dried for winter consumption. This variety is one of the most widely grown in Paris, although it has the disadvantage of bolting when the season is both hot and rainy. Suisse gris de Bagnolet. This variety is very similar to the previous one, but its seeds are grayish with black and brown spots. It is also widespread in the Paris area, and more highly esteemed because it is less prone to bolting and is earlier; it also has the same qualities. Black or negro bean. It is very attractive and very productive, but has the disadvantage of rising very easily. However, in light, sandy, warm soils, it remains very low. Its seeds are quite good dry, but it is much more interesting if grown to be eaten green. It is widely grown and highly prized in Touraine. Haricot rouge d'Orléans. Quite low stems; small, flat, red seeds, highly prized for dry eating. Canada yellow bean. Extremely low-growing, very early, suitable for under-frame cultivation, very tender pods, no parchment, excellent in green, and even when they have reached most of their size; rounded, pale yellow seeds with a small brownish circle around the umbilicus, very good for dry eating. Yellow-brown bean. Low, productive plant; medium-sized, round seeds, very good when dried. China bean. Highly productive plant; fairly large, rounded, pale sulfur-yellow seeds, excellent when newly shelled, very good dry. We have a sub-variety with the same qualities, but with light bronze-colored seeds. Yellow bean without parchment. This very vigorous variety is very prone to bolting and degeneration; when this happens, the seeds turn from yellow to black. They are small, oval and very good when dried. Long, arched pods, without parchment, excellent green. Yellow with parchment. An even earlier variety than the previous one, and very productive. Seeds are similar, very good dry. Red bean without parchment. This plant forms large, very productive clumps; pods are good when green; seeds are very good when dry, making excellent purée. We have already mentioned all the varieties that deserve to be cultivated: it remains for us to talk about a species that for a long time was cultivated only as an ornamental plant, but which today holds a very advantageous place in the kitchen garden; this is the SPANISH BEAN (Phaseolus coccineus), originally from the West Indies. It has produced two climbing varieties, which are grown in the same way as pole beans. These are Scarlet-flowered runner bean. Stems climb to nine or ten feet high; pods long, thick, good in green; seeds large, grayish-linen mottled with black, of mediocre quality in dry conditions. White-flowered runner bean. Similar to the previous variety, but more productive; pods good when green; seeds large, of the same shape, mealy, with a slightly thick rind, but good when dry. PRIMEUR. Green beans can be harvested in the open ground right up to the first frosts, so it remains for us to teach you how to obtain them from then on, throughout the winter, until the open ground starts yielding again. As each season requires different care, we will divide this chapter into paragraphs. 1. Sowing in September, to supply in December. Towards the beginning of September, six-inch-diameter pots are filled with garden soil mixed with half a layer of well-consumed potting compost. In each pot, sow three or four bean seeds, chosen from the early dwarf varieties we have indicated as suitable for growing under a frame. Water, and place the pots in three or four rows, at the foot of a south-facing wall, without burying them. They can also be sunk into the soil of an old bed. If the season is fine, there's no need for further precautions; but if it's wet, rainwater must be kept away by means of straw mats, or better still, frames. When the plant has risen, only three or four feet are left in each pot, and the others are pinched off with the nails below the cotyledons, so as not to shake the roots of the two remaining plants. Water as needed, but only a little at a time, and in such a way as to maintain only a humidity favorable to vegetation, without drowning the soil. This rule is particularly important in cold autumns. Beans fear even the slightest frost, so cover the seedbed with straw mats every night to avoid surprises. As soon as the bad season is announced by the first white frost, we establish a deaf layer with good exposure, cover it with seven to eight inches of potting soil, and push the pots of beans into it. At the beginning of November, they are removed from the soil and planted in a new warm layer, under a frame. We continue to care for them, giving them air and light, and they soon produce. The plants can also be transported in a layer of tarpaulin heated by a furnace, or on the shelves of a hot greenhouse, or finally on the layer of a flying greenhouse, designed to force vines or other fruit trees. In order to make early beans more productive, we use a method which we will describe here, and which applies equally to all early seedlings. When the stems have reached a foot or fifteen inches in height, they are carefully placed close to the ground, so as not to break or injure them, with the top facing north, i.e. towards the highest part of the trunk, and held in this attitude with laths or sticks. The stems emit new branches, which rise vertically and produce a lot. Seedlings sown in September produce until January. 2. November sowings, to produce in January and February. From the first days of the month, seedlings are sown in pots, as we have said, but they are immediately placed in potting soil in a warm layer, under a frame. We give them air, light and warmth; we thin them out by pinching off too much below the cotyledons, and we water moderately. When the layer has lost its warmth, the plants are removed and replanted on a new warm layer with eight to nine inches of good potting soil; they are covered with stretchers and kept warm with stoves that are renewed often; the layer is even larded underneath with hot manure, when necessary, or replanted in the potting soil of a layer raised in a tarpaulin heated in the furnace, and this is always much better. In any case, the above-mentioned care is taken. February, March and April. From the beginning of December to the end of January, plants are sown and trained as described above. However, some gardeners and market gardeners use different methods, which we will report on below. Two or three seeds are sown in four-inch diameter pots. Place the pots in a warm layer under a frame, and give them the usual care. A month after sowing, the plants are placed in larger pots on a new warm layer, where they are treated in the usual way. When the beans are ready to blossom, establish a deaf layer, with eight to nine inches of well-consumed potting soil, half mixed with light earth; place the beans with or without the pot, cover with frames and surround with thick heaters, and give air and light whenever this can be done without danger. Market gardeners raise a warm layer of one-third old manure and two-thirds new manure in an oven-heated tarpaulin, which they fill with nine to ten inches of potting soil mixed with one-quarter of good light earth. They sow in rays on this layer, thick enough to take up as little space as possible, but not so thick that the plants run the risk of withering. The rays run from south to north. It would be preferable, however, to sow in clumps, at a rate of twenty to twenty-five clumps per panel, the frames supposed to be four feet wide by four and a half feet long. They give them air, light and moderate watering; they lay them down with sticks, as we have said, and, for the rest, they cultivate them like full-grown beans. 4. February sowing, to produce in May. In the first days of the month, they are sown in pots, as we said for the previous sowings, and sown in potting soil in a warm layer, under a frame, where they are raised until the end of April. At this time, we plow a bed at the foot of a south-facing wall, with a very warm exposure, and soil it perfectly. The beans are removed from the soil and transplanted with the root ball. To protect them from the cool nights, cover them every evening with glass jars, straw hives or straw mats. If you use straw mats, spread them out on sticks placed across them and supported at the right height by stakes. If you're afraid of a few cold nights, you can also cover the sides with straw mats, against which you can throw a little bedding. We don't need to tell you that early beans are only grown for their worm-like pods, because for the seeds, which keep dry with all their qualities, this care would become superfluous. If you have a warm greenhouse with some light, you can cultivate the scarlet variety of runner bean and enjoy its produce, the beautiful garlands of greenery that will climb up against the timbers supporting the glass panels, and the pleasant view offered by its beautiful clusters of bright red flowers, in a season when few or no other flowers can be seen. From September to January, fill pots nine to ten inches in diameter with good, prepared soil, and sow five or six seeds in each, leaving only three when they have emerged. If you wish, you can press the pots into the tan of the warm layer to hasten germination, or simply place them on the shelves. We'll take the same care as for the other plants in the greenhouse, but we'll place strings in the direction we want their climbing stems to go, and they'll twist around them. They will soon adorn the greenhouse with their flowers, and their pods will be picked for consumption when they are three-quarters full. As they are very large, fleshy and good from birth until they are almost fully developed, it will be easy to pick a sufficient quantity from time to time to make a dish, even though only a small number of plants are cultivated. CULTURX ORDINAIRX. Beans thrive in soft, light, slightly cool but not cold, perfectly loosened soil; they can be grown in all types of soil, but in clayey, oily and compact soils, more ploughing and much more fertilizer are needed; they are sown later, and the seeds are covered less, so that as the soil is more heated and the seeds closer to the surface, they are less likely to rot. In the Paris climate, in light, warm soils with good exposure, early varieties can be sown as early as the fifteenth or twentieth of April. Once this period has passed, dry harvests can only be expected from a few early varieties, which can still reach maturity by sowing from the fifteenth to the thirtieth of May. Later, until the beginning of July, some varieties are sown for green harvesting, particularly Swiss and flageolet. However, all the above times may vary by ten to twenty days, depending on soil, climate and how early the season is. Beans can be sown in clumps or in rays. Beans should preferably be sown in clumps in light, dry, warm soils, as they retain their moisture better this way. In strong, damp soils, on the other hand, it is much better to sow in rays. If you sow in clumps, first prepare the soil with good ploughing and fertilizer, then rake the soil together and, using a chalk line, draw lines eighteen inches apart. On these lines, with the hoe, you dig large holes eighteen inches apart and in a chessboard pattern, but only two or three inches deep; you throw five or six beans into each, if the soil is dry, light and good, and eight or ten in strong, damp soil where you think some will rot without rising. In the first case, cover with nn inch of soil, or better, potting mix, and in the second, only half an inch. If you have sown more than six seeds, when they have risen, leave only that number, and thin out where there are more. If the season is very dry and the soil light, you can give the seedlings a few sprinklings to facilitate germination, but sparingly, so as not to chill the soil. Sometimes, either as a result of this watering or of rainfall, the soil becomes compacted and forms a hard crust of varying thickness on its surface: the young plants, unable to break through, perish between two layers of soil. This inconvenience is remedied by light sowing, the aim of which is to break up this crust, and is done with a hooked trowel. The seedbed is weeded and kept clean. Fifteen days after sowing, or sometimes a few days later, depending on variety and season, the beans should be up. When they begin to gain some strength, they are given the first hoeing, and when they are a month old, they are given the second hoeing and ridged. The purpose of this operation is to facilitate the emergence of small roots, which increase the vegetation and vigor of the plants. If you have sown climbing varieties, now is the time to row them, so that their long stems can twist and support themselves as they lengthen. The oars should be from five to nine feet long, depending on the variety you're growing. If heavy rains have caused weeds to grow, we pull them out by hand, and that's all we need to do until harvest. Let's get back to sowing in rays. After preparing the ground as we have said, we draw parallel furrows with a chalk line and a hoe, twelve or fifteen inches apart and one inch deep. The beans are thrown in grain by grain, spaced about three inches apart. Cover with a rake, and all other care is the same as if sowing in clumps. Beans, especially those sown early, are prone to being eaten in the ground, before germination, by a very small, elongated insect known to gardeners as the millipede (1). Beans with only the bark left are often found to contain up to fifteen of these insects. There is only one known way to combat this disastrous plague in certain localities, and that is to soak the seeds, forty-eight hours before sowing them, in a strong decoction of chimney soot. It is said that the odor and flavor they acquire in this way ward off millipedes and other destructive insects. Beans should be harvested from the most distinctive varieties, but it is still very difficult to keep them healthy for long, as most are prone to rapid degeneration (:) This insect seems to me to be very close to the sand jule (ule¬"abalo*ς), but much smaller, and a tawny yellowish color. in soils that are not suitable for them. Some of them even have all their qualities only in the place where they were born. Preference should be given to the first seedlings sown. If they are climbing beans, we pick the pods when they are dry and perfectly ripe, which can be easily recognized by their yellowish to blackish color; we keep the seeds in their natural envelope until it's time to sow them. If they are dwarf beans, uproot the stalks, bundle them together and store them in a dry place out of the reach of frost. Seeds kept in their pods retain their germinative properties for several years. Mr. Noisette claims that, once shelled, they will not germinate after three years. 14 CULTIVATED LETTUCE (Lactuca sativa); annual plant, native to Asia, grown in the vegetable garden to provide salads, and sometimes eaten cooked. There are two breeds, 1. head lettuce and 2. romaine lettuce, which have produced a large number of varieties. FIRST RACE. - HEAD LETTUCE, Varieties of this breed are easily distinguished by their rounded, more or less frizzy leaves, tightly packed in the center and forming a more or less large head. Their flavor is generally much less sweet than that of the Romans. They are divided into four sections: 1. spring lettuces; 2. summer lettuces; 3. winter lettuces; 4. lettuces for cutting. We will describe the varieties in the same order: 1. spring head lettuces. This is the name given to lettuces grown in February and March. These are the following varieties: Lettuce-goite. This variety has the advantage of eating very quickly, but also rises quickly. It is small, very blond, with blistered, wrinkled leaves and white seeds. It is generally grown as an early variety, as it is very resistant to air deprivation. However, it can also be sown outdoors in spring. It has given rise to two sub-varieties, one with black seeds, which buds just as quickly, but retains its apple much better. The other is called slow to bolt, because even in summer it bolts very-difficulty, even though it is also early. Its seeds are also black. Red cordon or red-edged lettuce. A small variety, but larger than the preceding ones; blond-green leaves, a little shiny, the tops of those forming the head a little tinted red. Apples are quick to set, but set very quickly; white seeds. It is very good for spring sowing, and nevertheless survives the winter very well. Dauphine lettuce. Very large, early variety, with almost smooth, blondish-green leaves, with the tops of the leaves that form the head slightly tinted red; head forms quickly, but only holds well in spring.
