Black seeds. This variety does well in all soil types, but requires a lot of water, and the suckers it grows around its first leaves must be carefully removed. Yellow-red lettuce. Medium-sized variety, sweet, tender, very good; almost smooth, round, blond-green leaves with a few light red spots; well-formed head, very yellow at the core, rising fairly quickly; black seeds. Pancake lettuce, round, spring-forming, small, short, degreen. Fairly early variety; yellowish-green leaves; well-shaped, tender, good head. White seeds. This lettuce apples very well under cloche, without needing air. Lettuce grosse george. Smooth, yellowish-green leaves; tight, very tender head, but quick to bolt; white seeds. Performs well under glass if given air and watering. a. Summer head lettuces. Sowing begins in early April, and continues until July. Lettuce de Versailles. A very large, slightly tall variety, excellent for summer sowing; thin, bumpy, pale-green, whitish leaves; head fairly quick to form, well-filled, tender, very difficult to climb; black seeds. Lettuce cocasze. Very blistered, glossy green leaves with a few freckles. These characteristics sufficiently distinguish it from the previous variety, with which it has often been confused; very large head, very long-lasting; white seeds. It wants to be grown in soils that are neither too strong nor too light, unless it is given a lot of fertilizer and watering; when planting it, it sinks a little deeper than the others. Blonde lettuce with black seeds. Leaves are blond-green and shiny, a little golden; the head is medium-sized, firm, tender and holds quite well. Berlin blond lettuce. Leaves tender green, a little yellowish, slightly edged with red; very large head, forming quickly and holding well. Black seeds. This variety, like the following one, is very close to the previous one. Royal lettuce with black seeds. Darker green leaves; very large head, slow to form, but very firm. Very water-loving. White-seeded royal lettuce. Outer leaves lunantes, a little blistered, a beautiful green; apple; now very long, tender, good; seeds blauches. Requires frequent watering. Yellow summer lettuce or lazy blond. Very round leaves, united especially on the head; very well-shaped head, a little flattened, tight, large, holding very well in summer; white seeds. Stubby blond lettuce. Folded, crenate, spreading leaves; broad, flat, tight head, holding even better than the previous variety; white seeds. Batavia blond or Silesia lettuce. When successful, this variety is the largest and best of all lettuces, although its head is never very full, and becomes slightly bitter if left without water. For it to succeed perfectly, it needs light, free soil, and watering in the evening and morning, but never during the day, because if you wet the leaves during this time it will stain and rot. Its leaves are very wrinkled, wavy at the edges, yellowish-green, slightly golden, with a red border; the head is slightly concave, with an imperfect whitening; the seeds are white. Maltese lettuce. This appears to us to be a variety of the previous one, but its leaves are smoother and paler green. The head is flattened and very tender, and the seeds are white. Chou lettuce or yellow Batavia. This variety is very large and beautiful, but better cooked than raw, because it has the disadvantage of being a little tough. Its leaves are dark green, tending to brown; the head is very large and the leaves are white. Turkish lettuce. Large, dull-green, almost smooth leaves; very large, tight, firm, excellent head; black seeds. One of the best and most beautiful summer lettuces. Imperial lettuce. Very similar to the previous one, with all its qualities, but its seeds are white. Lettuce de Génes. Smooth, shiny, tender-green, slightly golden leaves, tinged with red at the top; tight, well-shaped, slightly flattened head, forming quickly and holding well. Black seeds. This excellent lettuce has three sub-varieties, all of which are of equal quality: 1. blond, with smooth leaves, a slightly pointed head and white seeds; 2. red, with curly, russet leaves speckled with brown; rounded, tight head, forming fairly quickly; black seeds; 3. green, with green, curly leaves; yellow, hard, large head; white seeds. This sub-variety should be watered only when necessary, but needs to be hoeing often. Lettuce méterelle. Thin, very wrinkled, bumpy, fairly pronounced green leaves; very tight, very large head, a little slow to form, but difficult to climb; white seeds. Big brown lazy lettuce or big gray market garden lettuce. Leaves large, rounded, a little blistered, grayish-green with a few pale-brown spots; very large, rounded head, tinged red at the top, forming very slowly but rising fairly quickly; black seeds. Lettuce palatine, Laitue rousse, Brune Hollandaise, Petite brune. Leaves almost smooth, strongly tinted red; medium-sized head, very hard, tight, holding very well. Black seeds. This hardy variety is very suitable for late summer planting. Blood or flagellate lettuce with white seeds. Its green leaves are remarkable for their pleasing red variegation; the head is rounded, tender and very good, but rises very easily in hot weather. For this reason, this variety is better suited to spring and autumn sowing than to summer sowing. It succeeds only in mild soils, and does not fully form its head until spring. Black-seeded blood lettuce. Very similar to the previous variety, but with more variegated leaves. Its head holds better in summer, and its seeds, which are difficult to obtain, are black. It needs soft soil, and only apples in spring. Lettuce grosse crépe. Very curly, yellowish-green leaves. Rounded head, quick to set, very tender, very sweet, but quick to bolt; white seeds. White george lettuce. Almost smooth, yellowish-green, brittle leaves; large, tender, excellent, but very quick-growing head; white seeds. Big red lettuce. Very large variety; outer leaves rounded, almost smooth, dark green tending to blackish, shaded a dull, dark red; apple yellow tending a little to orange, tender, sweet, very good. Black seeds. This variety acquires all its qualities only in oily soils, where it grows in all seasons. Italian lettuce. Fine, pale-green leaves, edged with red; tight, tender, very good head; black seeds. Requires lean, light soil and little watering. Bapaume lettuce. Very blond-green leaves; large, full head, much tighter at the bottom than at the top, holding well. Black seeds. Green Perpignane lettuce. Leaves almost smooth, with very strong ribbing; very large, very yellow head, soft, tender, very good, hard to climb. White seeds. This variety is precious in that it holds up well in hot weather, even in light, dry soils. Lettuce Perpignane mouchetéc. Smooth, thick-ribbed, green leaves with yellow spots; very large, excellent, firm head with the same qualities as the previous variety. White seeds. Aubervilliers lettuce. Dark-green, smooth leaves; very small, tight, yellow, excellent, very firm head; white seeds. This variety is rarely grown, although it is very good, due to its lack of head. 3. Winter lettuces. These do not grow as well. They are sown immediately after summer lettuces, and continue until mid-September. Their main merit lies in their hardiness, and in the season in which they set, as they are generally quite hard. Passion lettuce. Given this name because it usually apples around Holy Week. Leaves are green rather than bluish, blistered and slightly reddish-spotted; medium-sized head, fairly hard, better cooked than raw; white seeds. It has a sub-variety which is no better, resembling it, but whose leaves are entirely speckled with red. Shell lettuce with white seeds. Smooth, rounded leaves, green rather than blond; hard, bitter, poorly-forming head, can only be eaten cooked; white seeds. Despite its poor qualities, this variety is quite widely grown, as it is the most resistant to cold. It has a sub-variety with black seeds, which is no longer appreciated. Morine lettuce. Very similar to passion lettuce, but with smaller, greener leaves. Its head is just as large and holds longer; white seeds. Small crepe lettuce. Small variety, difficult to pick, but very successful under glass during winter. Black seeds. It is rarely grown for this reason alone. It has a sub-variety, the petite noire, which resembles it, but does even better under cloche without needing to be given air. Its seeds are similarly black. 4. Cutting lettuces. This is the name given to some varieties that pomace and others that do not, but are sown to be cut before they are formed. All early and blond-leaved lettuces can be used for this purpose, such as gotte, crepes, etc., but there are some that are more particularly cultivated for this purpose, and we will mention them here. Spinach lettuce. This variety does not apple; its leaves are blond, very tender, and cut almost like oak leaves; they have the precious quality of growing back after being cut several times. Chicory lettuce. Folded, curled leaves, rather like those of the small yellow chicory. This variety does not peel, but is good only for cutting. II° RACE. - ROMANS. These lettuces are generally distinguished from the preceding ones by their more elongated shape, their more easily developed heads, and their much sweeter flavor. Romaine hátive or green hative. Blond-green leaves, pointed at the tip; fairly large, full, slightly flat, very good apple; white seeds. This variety has the invaluable advantage of peeling perfectly under a bell, so it is preferred for bedding and spring planting. Romaine verte maraichère or green chicory. Green, long, nacelle-shaped leaves; flattened apple, elongating when it degenerates, forming itself, i.e., without needing to be bound, one of the largest of all; black seeds. This variety is the most widely cultivated because of its hardiness and self-capping quality. Romaine verte d'hiver. Very similar to the previous variety, but smaller and more difficult to comb. White seeds. Romaine grise maraichère, chicon gris, grosse romaine grise. Very similar to the previous variety, but with darker green leaves. It also has a milder flavor, which makes it more highly esteemed. Its seeds are bluish. Romaine blonde maraichère, blond chicon. Its leaves are blond-green, a little less pointed at the tip than in the preceding varieties; the apple is flattened, large, full, more tender and more delicate to eat than the others, but rises quickly. White seeds. Romaine rouge d'hiver, red chicon. This variety is the hardest to withstand winter cold, but often melts as soon as it is tied, if the soil is not suitable. Green outer leaves

reddish; apple yellow, very tender. Black seeds. Romaine panachée, flagellate, sanguine, Silesian lettuce. Green leaves, variegated with red; apple tender, sweet, excellent. Black seeds. There is a sub-variety known as English variegated chicory, whose outer leaves are less variegated, but those of the apple much more so; it has the same qualities, but the seeds are white, and it is often capped without being bound. Romaine alphange; fine, pointed, smooth, slightly yellowish-green leaves, slightly tinged with red at the top; tender, excellent apple, but with the defect of rotting or rising quickly. White seeds. It has a sub-variety, the blond alphanx, whose leaves are fat, thick and yellow; its head is very large and its seeds black. This concludes the list of the most commonly grown lettuces. It is far from certain that all these varieties are constant; many depend on the climate and the quality of the soil. So, after sufficient trials, you should base your decision more on your experience of the varieties that do best in a given locality, than on any particular nomenclature. However, this does not apply to early varieties. PRICE. We will divide this chapter into three articles. 1. Early head lettuces, 2. early cut lettuces, 3. early Romaine lettuces. 18 1. early head lettuces. Head lettuces can be grown throughout the winter without the help of layers. Here's how: from the fifteenth to the twentieth of August, prepare a perfectly earthen and well loosened bed, and sow the varieties we have classified as winter lettuces, particularly Passion, Morine and Shell. We give the usual care to full-earth sowings, and above all we don't spare any watering, as lettuces are all the more tender and milder the more water we give them. New seedlings are sown from the fifth to the tenth of September. Towards the end of October for the first sowing, and from the fifth to the tenth of September for the second, the plants are transplanted to earthen beds at the foot of a wall in a very warm, south-facing position. Care is taken to ensure rapid recovery. When frosts and heavy frosts set in, lettuce plants are sheltered with bedding or straw mats spread over a few poles placed crosswise on very short stakes. Sometimes, lettuces from the first sowing advance too far and perish, but those from the second always succeed and can provide for part of the winter. To obtain good head lettuces from December through to spring, there are a number of other ways to proceed, as described below. Sowing in August, for head lettuce in November and December. For this and subsequent sowings, preference should be given to the following varieties: petile crêpe and its sub-variety petite noire, as well as crépe ronde, if grown under cloches, as these three lettuces do better the more they are deprived of air. If grown under a frame, gotte and grosse george should be preferred, as they need butter air and would be too stifled under clochez. From August twenty to twenty-five, prepare a bed with southern exposure, and sow lightly. Water daily, and give the young seedlings the usual care, until they are strong enough to be transplanted. Then a warm layer is raised with half new manure and half old manure, filled with six inches of good, well-consumed compost, and when its great heat has passed, the lettuces are transplanted. They are spaced so that one bell can cover five. When the cold rains, cool nights or first white frosts come, we cover with bells if we've sown pancakes, or with frames if we've sown gotte or grosse george. In the first case, no air is given; in the second, air must be given by lifting the frame panels whenever the temperature permits, and especially when the lettuces are three-quarters done and ready to eat. If the bed gets cold, it can be warmed up with stoves of new manure, but here we make an essential observation. Lettuces grown on beds are equally sensitive to excess heat and the slightest cold, both of which cause them to melt quickly. Every care must therefore be taken to avoid these two excesses, and you will be all the more certain of obtaining a successful crop if you constantly maintain the temperature of the bedding soil between ten and fifteen degrees of Réaumur's thermometer. Water often and little at a time. When the apple begins to form, remove all the low yellowing leaves from the potting soil, press it against the base, and the apple will swell more rapidly. October sowing, for January apple harvest. At the beginning of October, at the foot of a wall, prepare an ados of potting soil mixed with a quarter of good light garden soil, and sow under cloches. If you wish, you can sow on old potting soil and cover with a frame. As soon as the plant begins to take two leaves in addition to the cotyledons, a new bed of pure potting soil is made, in the same ex position, and the young lettuces are transplanted an inch and a half or two inches apart. Water them, cover them with cloches, and raise them in this suffocated way, without ever giving them air. Towards the end of November, a layer is built up with two-thirds old manure and one-third new, so as not to generate too much heat, and the plants are transplanted into it at a rate of five per bell. Moderate heat is maintained with stoves, and the bed is protected from frost and rain with litter around the bells and straw mats spread over them. The covers are lifted during the day, weather permitting, to provide light for the plants, but care is taken not to give them air, unless you have sown the two varieties that require it. These lettuces flower at the end of December and the beginning of January. Sow in November, to harvest in late January and early February. In early November, prepare a warm bed of half old and half new manure, and fill it with four inches of pure loam. Sow after allowing the greatest heat to pass through, and cover with cloches or frames. The plant is thinned out so that the stalks are two inches apart, and transplanted where they are lacking. It would even be better to transplant the whole plant, but on the same layer, and this should be done when the lettuces have two leaves in addition to the cotyledons. When the plants are strong enough, build a new, warm layer with six inches of potting soil, plant them at a suitable distance from each other, and give them the care indicated above. Sow from December to February, fortnightly, to harvest from February to March. Warm layers are planted with four inches of pure loam, and sowing takes place only when the greatest heat has passed. The seeds are lightly covered, and cloches or frames are placed over them. A few days later, when the first leaf begins to develop, we place good stoves of new sumier to increase the heat of the layer, and we unfold the lettuces to transplant them in the same place, but two inches apart. We surround the cloches with litter and cover them with straw mats, to prevent frost from penetrating the plants; we give air to the varieties that require it, and light to all of them, whenever the temperature allows it. When the lettuces have acquired sufficient strength, i.e. when they have four or five leaves, a new layer is established, filled with six inches of potting soil, and the plants are transplanted in such a way as to hold five under each bell, more or less, depending on the size of the varieties to be sown. The most important thing is that each plant should be able to spread out comfortably, without wasting space. We keep the bed warm with heaters, and give the lettuces the care we've already mentioned. When the lettuces are ready for harvesting, we peel them, removing all dead and yellowing leaves, and place a small amount of potting soil around each plant, pressing it against the crown. 2. Early lettuces for cutting. All varieties of lettuce can be sown to cut young, especially those with blond leaves, but the small, early species mentioned for early cuttings in previous articles are generally preferred. The reason for this is that there is no need to sow them on purpose, because after having chosen the seedlings to be pricked out, from the seedlings sown on the bed from December to February, we still give some care to those that remain to cut them when they are strong enough to do so. Two varieties deserve preference for sowing, if you have no other intention than to have lettuce to cut. One is lettuce-chicory, which, being very tender, can be cut stronger than gottes, crêpes, etc. The other is lettuce-chicory, which, being very tender, can be cut stronger than crêpes. The other is spinach lettuce, which has the same qualities as the previous one, to which it adds the precious advantage of growing back and being able to be cut several times. Throughout the winter, from one week to the next, lettuce for cutting is sown in the empty spaces of warm beds for large or small vegetables, either under cloches or under frames. If you were to raise a bed specifically for this purpose, it would have to be filled with four inches of potting soil, and constantly kept at a moderate temperature. In any case, sow thickly enough, water often but sparingly so as not to flood the bed, which would cool it, and give it the usual care. Lettuce can be cut for consumption when its leaves are an inch and a half to two inches long. 3. Early Romaine. By sowing winter varieties, such as winter greens, red chicon, etc., and treating them in exactly the same way as we did with passion lettuce in our first early article, the same results can be obtained. Romaine lettuces can be grown in the same way as pomme lettuces, the only difference being that they require much more air. They are also sown at the same time of year. Romaine hative, or green hative, is the variety that Paris market gardeners prefer for bedding, because it has the advantage of being more easily capped than the others under cloche. At the beginning of October, they sow it at the foot of a wall facing south, on an ados of potting soil and under a bell. When it has two leaves, in addition to the cotyledons, they transplant it onto another ados of potting soil, spacing the seedlings two inches apart. When the Romaines have acquired sufficient strength, i.e. around November, the larger ones are transplanted onto a warm layer, and are tended in the same way as gotte, but with more frequent airing. We continue to care for those that have remained in the seedbed, and do a second and third transplanting on new warm layers, in December and January. We won't repeat here all the precautions that need to be taken from sowing to the moment when they start to bloom, because we've gone into them in sufficient detail in the preceding paragraphs. REGULAR CULTIVATION. The cultivation of lettuces varies according to variety and time of year. In general, they thrive in open, light, soft soil, loosened and amended with fertilizer; several varieties require the special care we have described in their respective articles. In our first article on early lettuces, we explained how to sow and manage winter lettuces, so all that remains is to talk about 1. spring sowing and 2. summer sowing. 1. Spring sowing. Spring lettuces are sown in March on old potting soil, or on a dull layer of old manure, or simply on a layer of potting soil in a well-exposed bed. Some gardeners are not so fussy and are content to sow in place, in February and March, through beds of onions, salsify, carrots, etc., or on the edges of the paths between these beds. When the seedling is sufficiently strong, usually in April, it is transplanted in place and in beds. To do this, after preparing the soil, parallel lines are drawn ten inches apart with a chalk line, and the plants are transplanted onto these lines, leaving seven to nine inches between each one, depending on the size of the variety being grown. Lettuce roots are extremely delicate, so care must be taken not to press them too hard when planting; care must also be taken never to bury the heart of the plant, otherwise it would inevitably melt. Water immediately, and continue watering whenever necessary. It should be noted here that lettuces are more tender and less bitter the more water they have received, but some varieties are exceptions to this rule, and we have mentioned this in their particular articles. All that remains is to weed often to open the pores of the soil and destroy weeds. Romaines can be grown in exactly the same way, except that varieties that don't top themselves need to be tied with one or two straw ties in dry weather, when there is no moisture on the leaves, otherwise they will rot. We don't need to say that you should refrain from watering the leaves as soon as this operation has been carried out. Cutting lettuces can be sown in the same way, but there's no need to transplant them, since they can be cut as soon as their first leaves are developed enough to be eaten as a salad. To have them all year round, you need to sow new seedlings every two weeks, and not be sparing with watering during hot spells and droughts. a. Sowing summer lettuces. Start sowing in the first days of April, on old potting soil or earthen flowerbeds, and continue fortnightly until July, in order to have successive harvests until the first frosts, by which time you already have winter lettuces and early lettuces. The plant is transplanted as described above, and cared for in the same way. Above all, don't forget that abundant, daily watering is essential in this season, if you want to obtain soft, tender lettuces. To obtain good-quality lettuce seed, you need to choose the most beautiful and vigorous seedlings that have retained the most intact characteristics of their varieties. Each variety should be planted far enough apart to prevent cross-fertilization. When the plant's leaves have turned yellow and the seeds are crowning, the stalks are pulled off and left to dry in the sun, then the stems are beaten, the seeds are winnowed and kept in a dry place for four or five years. COMMON LAVENDER (Lavendula spicata); perennial, frutescent plant native to southern France, cultivated in gardens for its aromatic scent. For the same reason, we also cultivate LARGE LEAVED LAVENDER or ASPIC (Lavendula latifolia), whose leaves are evergreen. These plants are used desiccated for medicinal purposes, and as they are not edible, they are not cultivated in gardens except to form borders; consequently, they have never been forced. They like light, warm soil, and usually multiply by sprouting in September or spring. To ensure recovery, they need to be sunk deep into the soil. They can also be propagated from seed, sown in April in an earthen bed at the foot of a south-facing wall. Cover the seeds lightly and water moderately. When the plant is quite strong, i.e. in June, transplant it permanently to the border. Finally, lavenders multiply very well from cuttings taken in the spring, in a warm layer of potting soil. When perfectly rooted, remove the cuttings, leaving as much potting soil as possible around the hair, and transplant them into the ground. For lavender to form well-stocked clumps, the borders need to be redone every three or four years. LENTILLE COMMUNE ou BLONDE (Ervum Lens); annual plant from southern France, whose dried seeds are eaten in a variety of ways. In addition to the common variety, a much smaller, reddish, rounded seed is grown under the name of lentille à la reine or rouge, and is more highly regarded in some countries. It would be pointless to force lentils, as they are only used dried. We will therefore only deal here with their ordinary cultivation. They like dry, light or sandy soil. In strong, moist soils, they vegetate with great vigor and produce a lot of foliage, but few seeds. In the south of France, they are sown in autumn, but in the climate of Paris, you have to wait until the middle of March or the beginning of April. After ploughing the soil well and giving it suitable fertilizer, sow in clumps or in rays. In the former case, clumps are spaced twelve to fifteen inches apart; in the latter, rays are spaced fifteen to eighteen inches apart. Cover with a rake, and the plants require no more care until harvest than two or three weedings to rid them of weeds. The ripening period can be recognized by the dark-colored pods and yellowing stems. The pods are then pulled out and bundled together, air-dried and transported to a dry place. It is threshed only as needed, and by this method the seeds are preserved, either for eating or sowing, for two years. 215 MACHE ou DouceTre (Valerianella locusta); a small annual and native plant, eaten as a salad throughout the winter. Two varieties are grown outside the commune: Round maiche. Smooth leaves, broader, rounder and more tender than common lamb's lettuce. Maiche d'Italie, régence, grande mache. Very large, yellowish-green leaves, highly esteemed by some people, not so appealing to others because of the short down covering them. PRIMEUR. Lamb's lettuce produces very well in the open ground during the winter, but it is very difficult to get hold of it during the snows and the heaviest frosts, if you don't use the method which is the subject of this article. At the end of August, raise an ados along a wall facing east, and load it with two fingers of good potting soil. Sow as described in the article on ordinary cultivation, and manage the plants in the same way. When the frosts begin to become more intense, a small mound of earth seven to eight inches high by an equal width is raised around the seedling; a cord of manure the same height and a foot wide is used around this mound, then poles are laid across it, and, during snows or heavy frosts, doormats and more or less bedding, depending on how cold it is, are thrown over these poles. Whenever the temperature eases, we uncover the seedbed. You can also sow with the same advantage on an old layer of compost, and cover in the same way, or with frames or cloches. If you have a few empty spaces in warm beds for small vegetables or other early crops, you can use them by sowing lamb's lettuce from autumn onwards, continuing to sow every fortnight until January. It's much harder to get hold of lamb's lettuce in summer, because in that season it rises immediately. However, this can be achieved by sowing in spring, a few at a time, lightly and every eight days, in a shady spot with a northerly exposure, and in strong, moist soil. Water in such a way as to keep the soil in a permanent state of high humidity. REGULAR CULTIVATION. Lamb's lettuce thrives in light, soft, perfectly loosened soil, amended from the previous year, or at least with well-consumed fertilizers. Sowing begins on August 15th, and continues every eight to ten days until the end of October. The first sowings provide successive yields throughout the winter, and the last ones in spring. We broadcast the seed quite thickly, because we thin out the crop, always removing the largest plants for consumption. The seeds are lightly covered with a rake, and if the season is hot or dry, watered as needed. All other care is limited to destroying weeds, either by a few weedings, or by pulling them out by hand. We leave a few of the most beautiful and straightforward plants from the October sowing to obtain seed. To avoid losing them, we harvest them several times by shaking the stems on a piece of paper or cardboard. Then, when you think all the plants are nearing maturity, you pull them up, bundle them together and hang them in a place that's not too dry, so that they can finish ripening. Or - when the fennel turns yellow," says Mr. Noisette, "we carefully pull out the stalks and put them in a heap in a cool, poorly ventilated place, so that they can ferment for two weeks, which is useful for the seed, which can be detached by shaking them with a fork. Then we pick it up, air-dry it for a few days, winnow it, and keep it "good for seven to eight years. A rather peculiar remark is that, contrary to the norm for other seeds, lamb's lettuce seeds germinate very poorly when they are new, whereas on the contrary they germinate very well and very quickly when they are two or three years old. MELISSA OFFICINALE, CITRONELLE (Melissa officinglis); perennial, indigenous, aromatic plant, grown in vegetable gardens only for its young shoots, which are used as a salad garnish, or in omelettes to replace parsley. It is also used to make an edible drink known as eau des carmes or melissa. This plant has never been forced into cultivation, because of the limited use to which it is put. It thrives in light, warm soil and multiplies by seed or splinters. The former are sown in April, in soft, loose, terra-cotta soil. Transplant when it has five or six leaves, if you haven't sown it in place. Shattering is done in autumn. Every year, at the first frost, cut off all the ground-level stems, and the plant remains vigorous and in the same place for a long time. MELON (Cucumis Melo); annual, and native to Asia. Its delicious flavor and fragrance have made it widely cultivated, with the result that we now have a considerable number of varieties, divided into three races, as follows: I RACE - BRODEN MELONS. They are considered to be febrile in the late season. These are the oldest cultivated varieties. Market or common melon, it is usually 39 rounded, sometimes a little depressed from the umbilicus to the stalk, without ribs, very rounded, of medium size. Its flesh is very thick, with a very mediocre flavor. In recent years, it has been the most cultivated of all in the Paris area. Sucrin de Tours. It has given rise to three sub-varieties, each of which easily falls into the other if not cultivated with the utmost care. These are: r. Petit Sucrin de Tours, very small, sometimes no bigger than an orange, round, with green, very embroidered rind; firm, red, very sweet flesh 2. Le gros Sucrin de Tours. Round, with fairly pronounced ribs, lightly but evenly embroidered. Red flesh; firm, vinous, pleasant. 3. Sucrin long de Tours. It differs from the preceding variety only in its elongated shape. Small-seeded sacrin. Small, round, with thick, red flesh. This variety is very early, and does well under glass. *White-fleshed sacrin. Very similar to the previous variety in shape, but its flesh is white, very deep, sweet and very good. This variety is quite precocious, easy to grow, and does well under glass. Melon des Carmes. The ribs are quite visible, the flesh pale, but melting, sweet and very good. It has two sub-varieties: 1. the round Melon des Carmes; very small and round. a. the long Melon des Carmes; of medium size and elongated shape. Melon de Langeais. Medium-sized, oval, with prominent, lightly embroidered ribs; sweet, vinous, red flesh, very good. Sub-variety: Petit Melon de Langeaie. Same shape, but smaller and earlier. Melon d'Honfleur. Very large, sometimes acquiring enormous volume, elongated, with wide and millantei ribs. Flesh a little coarse, not very melting, but vinous, fragrant, good quality. Melon de Coulommiers. Somewhat similar to the previous melon, it becomes just as large, but is greener and more rounded and less regular in shape. Although of inferior quality to the Honfleur melon, it is nevertheless quite good. Melon d'Appoigny. Medium-sized, oval, embroidered; flesh quite firm, fragrant, very good. This variety, brought from the Auxerre area by Mr. Noisette, is precious in that it does very well in the open ground, even in the fields, if sown in May and given little care. Melon d'Angers. Its size is average, its shape quite variable, and its rind mediocrely embroidered. Its flesh is sweet, fragrant and quite good. Melon de la Grave. Medium size, elongated, with regular ribs and finely embroidered green rind. Flesh not very melting, but very sweet. Melon d'Avignon. This is considered a sub-variety of the previous variety, with all its qualities. It is larger and less elongated. Melon de Rugonnant. Two varieties are known, the round and the long. Both have a blackish-green rind, very well embroidered. Their flesh is sweet and quite tart. Melon-Ananaz- Originally from the United States, where it was brought a few years ago. It is small, round, with fairly prominent ribs and lightly embroidered rind. Its flesh is greenish, melting, fragrant, sweet and of perfect goodness. Minorcan melon. Medium-sized, slightly rounded, with finely embroidered rind. Fairly good flesh. IInd RACE - CAnTalour melons. Much more highly esteemed than most of the preceding breeds, they originate from Armenia, from where they were brought to Italy around the beginning of the fifteenth century. In 1495, Charles VIII brought the first ones to be cultivated in France. "Cantatoup orange. It is small, round, with staggered ribs; its rind is light green, or brown. Its firm, orange-red flesh is quite good. This variety has the great merit of being the earliest of all, and of doing very well under glass; it is therefore widely used as an early variety. "Cataloup fin hatif. It is a little smaller and much more compressed than the other, with more prominent, slightly embroidered or scabby ribs. Its flesh is red, sweet, fragrant and very good. It is as early as the orange; it also does well under glass, which is why it would be preferred if it were as large. Brúlot harif. It differs from the previous variety only in that its ribs are more embroidered and more prominent. Otherwise, it has the same qualities. "Melon noir des Carmes. Very early; round, greenish-black fruit, without scabs, with inconspicuous ribs; red flesh, melting, vinous, excellent. Although a little strong in wood and leaves, it does very well under glass. Petit Prescott or hátif. Slightly compressed, small, scabby-ribbed, crowned, with a small protruding point in the middle of the crown; brown or blackish bark; red flesh, excellent quality. This very early variety is one of the best for cultivation under frame. "Gros Prescott. There are two sub-varieties: one blackish, the other whitish. Both resemble the petit prescott, but are larger and more compressed. In addition, these melons are almost as early and of very good quality. Twenty-eight-day Haitif. Round, pale green, slightly ribbed. This variety is very good, very early, but is more widespread in Germany than in France. Boule de siam. Of medium size, very compressed at both ends, with a black base, wide, raised, very pale ribs, but without protruding points; narrow crown; flesh a little less fine than in the preceding varieties. Astracan Cantaloupe. Large, with raised ribs; flesh orange-yellow, melting, quite good. Mogol Cantaloupe. Oblong; blackish rind; large scabs and green flesh in one sub-variety; small scabs and white flesh in the other. Both are highly valued. "Big black Cantaloupe from Holland. One of the largest of this breed; bark black; flesh red, melting, very good. * Cantaloup noir galeux ou des Saints. Small, rounded, blackish-green, very mangy, not yellowing as it ripens; red, melting, sweet flesh. IIIrd RACE. - GREEN MELONS or SMOOTH SHADES. Distinguished by their generally smooth rind and much larger seeds. White-fleshed Malle melon. Medium size, elongated, with green rind; sweet, melting flesh. Early variety. Melon de Malle with red flesh. Earlier than the previous variety, it differs only in its red flesh, which is slightly more aromatic. Muscade des États-Uais, very small, oblong, green and embroidered; green, melting flesh, excellent. Peruvian melon. Oval, with thin, blackish-green rind; white, melting, very sweet flesh. Melon de Morée, de Candie, de Malte d'hiver. Medium-sized, elongated, with smooth rind; greenish flesh, fragrant, melting, very good. If stored in a dry, sheltered place, wrapped in straw, it can be copserxed for most of the winter, and even until the end of February. Persian or Odessa melon. Very elongated shape, green with yellow stripes; green, melting flesh. Can be stored in winter like the previous one. Melon from Cyprus. Peiit and elongated, green marbled with brown; excellent red flesh. It is ascez hiuif, but should be eaten as soon as ripe. We won't mention here the other species of melon mentioned by the authors, because they are no longer available commercially. PRDEUR. Melons have greatly exercised the industry of Paris growers, so it can be said that they excel in this type of cultivation. By combining various processes, sowing times and choice of varieties, they manage to obtain excellent melons from the end of March, and to have juice until the frosts. Morée and Persian melons, which can be kept until February, as mentioned in their article, are used to fill the gap between frost and spring. 1. Sow in December, to produce in April. As this crop is very prone to failure, it is rarely grown except by amateurs; however, if the winter is mild, it is not difficult. Towards the end of December, pots four to five inches in diameter are procured; they are filled with good consumed terracotta, which is very lightly treaded, and three or four seeds are sown in each. The seeds are covered with only half an inch of compost. Bury the pots in a warm layer, cover them with cloches or chágsis, and place them in the tan of a warm greenhouse, if you have one. Seeds soon sprout, so only one seedling is left in each pot, and to avoid uprooting it by removing the others, they are simply cut off with the nails below the cotyledons. They are protected from the cold by mulch, which is removed during the day, and given a little air during the hottest part of the day. If frames or cloches are used, care should be taken to wipe off any moisture that may have adhered to the glass. In addition, all other zoins ordimaires are given to delicate plants grown under chassis. Commo, for example, to maintain the warmth of the bed, to give moderate watering, without wetting the leaves, and so on. When young plants have two leaves, in addition to the cotyledons, the stem above the second leaf is cut with the nails. The result of this operation is that the vertical stem is destroyed, and two or three lateral stems sprout from the leaf axils, which bear fruit much more quickly than the first leaf would if left to develop. After two or three days, the wound is completely healed, and the new layer is prepared to receive the seedling. So far, these are easy enough to take, and within everyone's reach; but for December sowings, since melons have to rot almost without air, they absolutely need a tarpaulin built as we're about to say. At the foot of a south-facing wall, we open a trench three feet deep, ten feet wide and of indeterminate length. A wall one foot thick is built inside, to support the earth, and this wall) on the north side is given a height of five feet, i.e. it rises two feet above the surface of the ground. The southern wall should be roughly flush with the ground. The ground is perfectly joined with this kind of tarpaulin, in the middle of which is built, with poorly joined planks, a box four and a half feet wide, running the entire length. The north side of this coffte must be three and a half feet high, and the side da midistroin piedo trcix poucur, afta that the layer that will be inside is a little inclined to the south. The entire tarpaulin is covered with glass panels. In the trunk, we lay a good layer of horse manure and leaves, so that it can retain its heat as long as possible, and we fill it with eight inches of consumed compost, mixed with no more than one eighth of light garden soil; when the heat is at the right point, we plant the young melons, with the precautions we will indicate. They are removed from the soil with the utmost care, so as not to break the root ball, then three holes are made in each panel, and the melons are planted right up to the collar, fortifying it and causing it to put out a few new roots. The soil is then pressed around the root ball, without pressing too hard, and lightly watered. When planting, arrange the plants as if they were to be staggered; that is, place the first one a foot from one edge of the bed, the second a foot from the opposite edge, the third a foot from the first edge, and so on. In this way, they enjoy to a greater degree the warmth sent into the bed by the rechands. We continue with the usual care, and above all we cover with good doormats, which we place every evening and remove every morning. Plants require a lot of light and air; to provide them with the latter, we raise the mats by an inch or two whenever the outside temperature permits, and especially when it's sunny. When the layer begins to cool, we fill the paths between the walls and the trunk with good, hot manure from the stable, and renew it whenever it's needed. Sometimes the branches grow very vigorously. In this case, to stop them and divert the sap, we make them describe several curves, and mềme a whole circle with a more or less narrow diameter, depending on the bezoin. However, a man who knows perfectly how to prune his melons doesn't need to use this procedure, which delays results. If nothing has been neglected, the plants soon flower. The male flowers appear first, followed a few days later by the female flowers. It's during the flowering period that you need to redouble your efforts to provide light and air without losing heat, and to keep moisture away. When a female flower is dry and the ovary, or young fruit, continues to grow, this is proof that it is knotted. From then on, we prune to a gem or two above the young fruit, and we also prune the main branches; we lengthen those that are vigorous, and shorten those that are weak. Leave the wounds to heal for four or five days, then remove any harmful secondary branches, i.e. those that are badly placed or that cross over one another. If a branch has more than one fruit, cut them off and leave only the most beautiful, especially the one closest to the stump. If it had only one, and it was deformed, it would be removed, and others would soon form. Eight days after this cutting, the tips of the fruiting branches are pinched off, to force the sap to flow to the melon, and all useless branches are removed for the second time. A well-managed plant should never produce less than two or three fruits. When these have reached a certain size, they are gently lifted and placed on a piece of tile or a board to protect them from the humidity of the bed. During pruning, melons need plenty of air and light to help wounds heal; the direct influence of the sun's rays is especially useful, but they must be treated with care, as they can sometimes burn, especially at the end of February and beginning of March. Water for irrigation should not be taken at random either. River or pond water is infinitely better than well or fountain water. In all cases, it should be left to stand for at least forty-eight hours before use, in a greenhouse or other place where it can warm up enough not to chill the roots of the plants. Those wishing to try December sowing, without the expense of a melon tarpaulin as just described, can also succeed in an ordinary tarpaulin heated by a furnace, and even on warm layers in the open air, provided they have at least three parallel rows, and cultivate on the middle row and under a frame. 3. January sowing. This is absolutely the same as the previous one, except that more air is given in spring, and the sun's rays are guaranteed in March. 4. February sowing. This can be done in the same way as the previous one, and the plant can be trained in the same way. But you can also start sowing on a bed in the open air, using bells, although success is more likely. At the beginning of the month, a warm layer of horse manure is laid, filled with six inches of potting soil, into which pots are sown with four or five seeds, leaving only one or two plants. The pots are placed close together, so that several can fit under each bell or glass. The spaces between the cloches are filled with short litter, and mats are thrown over them to keep out the heat. Light is not given until the melons have emerged, but then it's essential, as is air. The young plants are directed as we have said, and after the first pinch, they are replanted on a new bed filled with nine inches of potting soil mixed with a quarter of light earth. Cover with cloches, and take the same care as for plants grown in trays. Only one plant should ever be grown under each cloche, and if two have been kept in a few pots, they are separated by carefully cutting the rootball down the middle, so that the soil does not separate from the roots. When pruning, keep the branches as short as possible, without harming the plant, so as not to let them come out from under this shelter as long as frosts are to be feared. If a plant gets so carried away that it can't be maintained, we replace the bell with a larger glass. When the season permits, we don't just lift the bells on one side, we lift them to a height of a few inches, and hold them in this position by means of three small wooden racks sunk into the soil of the bed. They are removed during the day when the season is more advanced; finally, towards the end of May, they can be made to disappear altogether, unless a few are placed on fruits whose ripening is to be hastened. We prune and manage the plants in the same way as if we were growing them under a frame or tarpaulin. To achieve easy success in the four preceding crops, the choice of varieties is very important. Petit orange is one of the most successful melons for the first season. For the following months, you can also use meloh noir des carmes, brulot hatif, petit prescott, cantaloup fin hatif, canAaloup rouge, sucrin à petites graines; and other varieties chosen from among the earliest, and those that provide the least foliage. 5. March sowing. Most market gardeners do most of their sowing in this month. A few sow on beds in the open air or under cloches. In this case, they build their beds with no more than a third of warm manure, and two-thirds of dry leaves, or other materials, such as garden debris, street manure, etc. The main thing is to have good seedbeds. The key is to have materials that, while not giving a very high degree of heat, retain it for a long time. They make the beds two and a half feet thick by four wide, and fill them with eight to nine inches of potting soil mixed with one-third light earth if they're growing small, delicate species, or with two-thirds light, free-flowing earth if they're sowing large cantaloupes or embroidered melons. When the first heat has passed, they sow a few seeds four inches apart, leaving two or two and a half feet between each group, and bury the seeds an inch and a half deep. They give a light watering, and cover with cloches and litter, as we said for the previous sowing. When the seedlings have four leaves, in addition to the cotyledons, they stop the stem by cutting it cleanly below the axil of the third leaf. At this point, to facilitate healing, they provide plenty of air by raising the bells by two or three inches. As far as they can, they maintain the balance of the vegetation, by placing in a tortuous position those that seem to want to get carried away, and straightening the weakest. As for the rest, they govern their cultivation as we have said. Other growers sow on high beds under frames, and manage their plants in the same way. They remove the glass panels when the season permits, and leave the trays on for a few days longer, so as to be able to replace the panels immediately, should they have to fear sudden changes in temperature. It is essential, when the melon is in the open air and the season is warm, to maintain a slight humidity on the surface of the earth where it vegetates. This is achieved by spreading a finger's worth of finely chopped straw over the layer. Three years ago, a private individual tried to cover the entire surface of the bed with flat bricks, forming a kind of tiled floor: his experiment had the best results. 6. April sowing. This is the month for the last sowings of early melons, and as they are so easy to grow, they are also the most widely used in amateur gardens. A bed is prepared with all kinds of fermentable materials, made two feet high by five wide, and filled with nine inches of potting soil mixed with one-third ordinary earth. Sow as we have said, cover with cloches or verrines, and, when the plant is high, place the cloches on it only during the night or when the weather is bad. If the month of May is very hot, mulch the soil to prevent moisture evaporation. All other care is the same as for previous crops. Before concluding this article, I should mention a method used by an amateur in Lyon to grow melons during the height of winter, i.e. in December, January and February. In August, he fills six-inch diameter pots with potting soil mixed with a quarter of garden soil; he buries them in a flowerbed facing south, sows three or four melon seeds in each, and spares no expense in watering. Around the middle of September, a little earlier or a little later, depending on the season, he procures baskets one foot deep and eighteen inches wide; on four sides of the basket, he firmly attaches a small stake, or rather a piece of stick two feet long, i.e. about six inches higher than the top of the basket. These four 20 stakes are used to support the trellis, which we'll talk about later. He fills the baskets with the same mixed potting soil as the pots, and plants his melons in them as one would in the potting soil of a bed, with the precaution of putting only two in each dummy. He builds a muted layer into which he sinks his baskets, and covers them with frames. He gives the usual care; but he only stops the main stem above the fourth leaf, and if there are more than three side branches, he removes the lower ones. When the branches are eight or ten inches long, he begins to place the trellis on which he makes them crawl. To do this, he attaches four sticks in a cross at the end of the four basket posts, and on this kind of small framework, he establishes a horizontal trellis with flat, wide slats, about an inch wide, with square meshes an inch and a half wide. The entire trellis is four feet in diameter, and this space is enough for him to conveniently spread the branches of two plants over it. Until the first white frosts, he gives the usual care to the frame melons, and the fruit is usually tied when he transports the baskets in the early harvest tarpaulin. The inside of this tarpaulin is sixteen feet long and seven feet wide, sunk four feet into very dry ground. The back wall is seven feet high, and the front wall is three or four inches above ground level. Along the front wall, there's a recess four and a half feet wide to receive a layer made up of one-third horse manure, one-third dry leaves and one-third tan. When the deaf layer has lost its heat, he chooses a moment when the thermometer is a few degrees above zero, removes the baskets without disturbing the small trellises supporting the melon branches, and transports them to the tarpaulin layer. From that moment on, he lights the furnace so as to keep the thermometer constantly between eighteen and twenty degrees. Whenever the weather permits, he provides air by lifting the greenhouse panels to a greater or lesser extent, covering them with straw mats only at night, no matter how cold it is. This cultivation, which requires more meticulous care than expense, produces twenty to twenty-five melons a year, all the better because they are eaten at a time when there are no others, apart from a few green melons preserved in straw. According to Mr. C., who kindly shared these details with me, the trellis serves less to transport the plants conveniently than to protect them from the pernicious fumes of the bed. He is so convinced of this, that he cultivates the melons he grows in the open ground during the summer in the same way. As soon as the fruit has formed, he takes the precaution of placing it on a small board, to prevent it from deforming. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: the quality of melons depends to a large extent on the choice of the right seeds; as these monoecious plants fertilize each other very easily, if the varieties are cultivated at very close distances, the result is hybrids which more often than not lack the qualities of their mothers. Two observations are essential here. The first is that adulterated fertilization has no effect whatsoever on the fruit resulting from a flower that has undergone it, but only on its seeds; so its influence is only to be feared for the following generation. Secondly, experience has shown me that there can be no cross-fertilization between melon, cucumber and squash. I am all the more convinced that such fertilization cannot occur naturally, as for the last three years I have used all the resources of the art to bring it about, without ever having been able to succeed. The melon is usually monoecious, i.e. it produces both male and female flowers on the same plant; but according to the observations of M. Sageret, one of our most knowledgeable amateurs in the history of cucurbit cultivation, it has been proven that this plant is also very often polygamous, producing both male and female flowers, and hermaphrodites, on the same plant. Mr. Sageret has taken great advantage of this discovery to preserve intact the many varieties he grows. As soon as he sees a hermaphrodite flower bloom, he covers it with a glass bell until fertilization has taken place and the fruit has set; In this way, he can be sure that the dust from the stamens of another variety will not have been carried onto his stigmas, and that the seeds will therefore reproduce identically the individual that supplied them. Before sowing, therefore, you need to be sure that the seeds will reproduce their true variety, and the safest way to do this is to collect them yourself. Leave the fruit to rot on the spot if you can; if you want to eat it, the seeds will still be good if you don't pick it until it's perfectly ripe. Seeds retain their germinative virtues for many years, but the best are those that are two or three years old. Newer ones produce over-vigorous individuals that branch out to the detriment of the fruit. Before sowing them, some people soak them for twenty-four hours in water, but I don't think this is very useful. When they come out of the fruit, they are washed, dried in the shade and kept in a dry place. In May, we make a dull layer eighteen inches to two feet thick, with manure that has already been used once, mixed with dry grasses, prunings and other detritus likely to ferment. If the season is poor, a certain amount of hot manure can be added to liven it up. We fill it with ten inches of good soil mixed with a third of consumed loam. Sow as we have said, cover with bells until the plant can no longer stand underneath, and prune according to the principles we have taught. There is, however, one difference: if the plant is vigorous, and especially in the smaller varieties, two fruits may be left on each branch. Throughout the south of France, up to the latitude of Lyon, we sow without such precautions, in April and May, in warm, light soil with good exposure. Holes are dug five or six feet apart, one foot deep and eighteen inches wide; eight inches of old, half-consumed manure is placed at the bottom, and six inches of soil mixed with half the same manure is thrown on top. Sow five or six melon seeds, water, and when they have sprouted, leave three feet in each hole, and lead the plant according to the ordinary method. In Honfleur, where melons weighing as much as twenty-five and thirty pounds are produced, the process is a little different, and very similar to that used by growers in Paris. We dig a pit two to two and a half feet wide and two feet deep, and fill it with hot manure that we compact with our feet. Nine inches of good, light, substantial soil are spread over it, with a quarter of the manure from the previous year's pits mixed in. These muted layers are left to warm up for two weeks, then, in April, five or six seeds are sown in each one, four inches apart, and covered with a cloche or verrine. When the seedlings have sprouted, we thin them out so as to leave only two, and when they have three or four leaves, we pinch off the end of the stem. When the season is poor and the nights are cold, we throw a little litter around the bells, and cover them with straw mats at night. When the branches are large enough that they can no longer be contained under the bells, they are raised by means of racks, and finally, when there is no more bad weather to fear, they are removed altogether. Watering, weeding, hoeing and other ordinary care are then carried out, and when pruning, only three fruits are left on each plant. One thing that is quite difficult, and on which everyone is prone to error, is knowing the precise moment of maturity of a melon. Let M. Noisette tell us, in his Manuel, which signs are the least ambiguous. 1. most melons ripen a few days "in advance", i.e. they change color or "hue". 2. The tail circles and tears as if it's about to detach. 3. The fruit gives off a pleasant odor, especially in the morning. In spite of all these signs," adds this learned farmer, "we are often mistaken, because the quality of the melon depends on a pre-cis point of ripeness which, for certain types, lasts only a few hours. A melon is judged to be of good quality when the characteristics of its variety are well indicated, when it is heavy, firm under the finger, well shaped, and when its stem is short and thick. WATERMELON or Pasrìqur (Cucurbita citrullus); annual plant, native to the Orient. Watermelon differs from ordinary melon in that its leaves are coarse, covered with whitish hairs, sharply cut and digitate-like. Its fruits are large, with a smooth, shiny, green rind, mottled or spotted with brown. The flesh is almost transparent, wine-red or whitish, very sweet, slightly acidic, but always retaining a bland cucumber flavor to which one must be accustomed to find them good. In fact, even people with the most pronounced taste for watermelons will only eat them during the hot summer months, because of their refreshing quality, and after soaking them for several hours in ice-cold water, or at the bottom of a well. They can also be kept cool by wrapping them in a wet cloth and leaving them exposed to the sun for half an hour before eating. Two varieties of watermelon are grown: Watermelon from Italy or Provence, with red flesh and black seeds. It is rounded, very large, and its rind is green, mottled with brown. Watermelon from Africa, with whitish flesh and red seeds. It is a little smaller and more elongated; its rind has large greenish-yellow laches running from base to top. PADEUR. Because watermelons are only eaten in hot weather, it is not customary to force them under frames like ordinary melons. However, if we wished to do so, we could easily do so by treating them in the same way. The difference, however, would be that they would no longer be pruned once they had sufficient branches, and no fruit would be removed. In Spain, Italy and the south of France, watermelons grown with care in the open ground bear fruit and ripen quickly enough to provide their product at the most pleasant time of year, but this is not the case around Paris. We therefore need to take a few special precautions, which consist in sowing them in pots in February, on a warm bed and under a bell or frame. In April, they are replanted on a muted layer filled with nine inches of compost mixed with half light garden soil, and continue to be sheltered by cloches or frames until the season allows them to be left exposed to the open air. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: in the south of France, sow two or three seeds in a hole ten inches wide and as deep, filled with a mixture of two-thirds light, substantial soil and one-third consumed manure. The plant is then pruned and left to nature, with only the care of frequent watering. In Paris, we do much the same, but we sow in May, in a bed at the foot of a wall facing south, and on old potting soil if possible. The fruit doesn't begin to ripen until mid-September, and sometimes even later. If picked and stored in a dry, frost-free place, they can be kept for part of the winter. Seeds can be collected like those of melons or cucumbers, and keep well for many years, but the newer the better. MELONGENA or AUBERGINE (solanum melongena vel esculentum); annual plant, native to South America. Its fruits, which vary in shape and color according to variety, have a mild and rather bland taste; nevertheless, they are widely consumed in Paris. For information on how to prepare them, please refer to the Cuisinière Bourgeoise or the Almanach du bon Jardinier. Several varieties are grown, as follows: Long-fruited red melongene. It has the shape and size of a medium-sized cucumber, and is a dirty purple or reddish color. One of the most widely grown in Paris. Yellow melon with long fruit. The fruit is of medium size and roughly the same shape as the previous variety. It is green at first, whitening as it grows, and finally turning a pronounced yellow when ripe. Red melon with oval fruit. Less large than the first, swollen at the base, giving it a roughly oval shape; its color is pale purplish red. Yellow melongene with oval fruit. It has the same shape as the previous one, but is a little shorter, and its color is pale yellow. Red melongene with round fruit. The fruit is about the size of a walnut, round, compressed and pale purplish-red. Melongene ovifere (solanum ovigerum), which is generally known as the œufi plant, because of the shape and color of its fruit, is a species or perhaps a variety that should not be confused with the above, because it is considered poisonous and is certainly of poor quality. PRIMEUR. Sow in August, in sepi inch diameter pots, filled with half substantial loam and half potting soil; water and care as for full-ground plants. If you wish, you can sow in an earthen bed, raise the plant there until the approach of the bad season, and transplant it into a pot. In any case, only one plant should be placed in each vase. When the cold autumn rains start, lay a soft layer at the foot of a south-facing wall, bury the pots in it and cover them with frames. Care should be taken to gradually accustom the plants to the deprivation of air, and to let them enjoy as much light as possible at all times. When the frosts start, transport the pots in a tarpaulin heated by a furnace, and place the plants as close as possible to the glass. From then on, they require no more care than other greenhouse plants, apart from changing pots if the first ones become too small. This operation should only be carried out a fortnight before flowering or a month afterwards, otherwise the flowers will run or the fruit may abort. Unless you're growing round-fruited melongene, you should leave only three or four fruits on each plant, depending on how vigorous it is. They grow larger and are less likely to abort. These seedlings usually bear fruit in February. In September and October, seedlings are sown under a bell on a warm bed with small vegetables. When the seedling is strong enough, we transplant it into pots as we have said, and raise it under a hot bed until it is ready to flower. It is then placed in a tarpaulin and treated in the same way as August seedlings. It produces in February, April and May. In November, December and January, the plants are sown and directed in the same way, until April. At this time, we establish a dull layer at the foot of a wall facing south, fill it with eight inches of potting soil mixed with half substantial garden soil, and plant the melongenes eighteen inches apart after removing the soil. Needless to say, before planting, you must allow the layer to heat up as much as possible, so that the delicate roots lining the sides of the vases are not exposed to burning. This applies to all plants. We cover them with frames, and gradually acclimatize them to the open air by lifting the panels whenever the weather is hot enough. When the sun is very hot, it's essential to protect them from its rays with light-colored cloths or straw mats, but without accepting the amount of light they need. At the end of May, if the season permits, the frames are removed entirely, and watering is increased. These plants bear fruit in June, July and August. The February and March sowings are the most commonly used by Paris market gardeners. They prepare a warm layer filled with six inches of pure potting soil, sow, water and cover with cloches or frames. Once the plant has acquired sufficient strength, they transplant it into pots, which they immerse in a warm layer and cover with a cloche while frosts are still to be feared. In May, they prepare an earthen bed with a warm exposure, usually at the foot of a south-facing wall, and transplant the melongenes after removing the soil: all that's left to do is the usual care, and above all, not to spare on watering, as these plants love water and heat. Others, to be sure of collecting good seeds, sow as we have just said, but leave the young plant on the bed as long as it remains sufficiently warm to encourage vegetation. They then prepare a deaf layer "as we said above, transplant them there, and never take them out again. Melons sown in February and March usually produce in September and October. If you want fruit all year round, all you have to do is get it in November, December and January, and nothing could be easier. All you have to do is sow the plants in pots in May and June, and raise them outdoors in good exposure. As winter approaches, they are transported to the shelves of a warm greenhouse or to a tarpaulin heated by a fire, and the fruit grows and ripens until January. Success with early melongene depends on three essential things: 1. constantly giving the plants at least eighteen to twenty degrees of heat; 2. letting them enjoy a constant supply of light; 3. placing them in very dry tarpaulins, where humidity cannot attach itself to their leaves or flowers. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: As by ordinary cultivation I mean that which requires no artificial shelter, such as layers, cloches, frames, etc., it follows that we can only study this type of cultivation in the south of France, below the forty-third degree of latitude, or if we prefer, in the only climates where myrtle and pomegranate survive the winter in the open ground without inconvenience. There, melongenes are sown in March, in light, warm soil, and transplanted in staggered rows, two feet apart, in well loosened soil amended with fertilizer. Weed and weed, and give them copious daily watering. In Paris, I've sometimes seen gardeners grow quite beautiful and very good melongenes, but they only produced aborted seeds. At the end of March, they sowed in boxes filled with potting soil, in a sheltered spot such as a shed facing south, or even an uninhabited apartment. They sheltered their seedlings overnight with straw mats, whenever necessary. At the end of April, they planted the seedlings in the ground, at the foot of a wall facing south. They surrounded the bed with litter, and covered it every evening with straw mats supported by poles across it. In May, they gave only the ordinary care to plants grown in the open ground, and the plants usually produced their fruit in October. MORELLE NOIRE (Solanum nigrum); annual plant, growing naturally in all cultivated areas of Europe, very common around Paris. In the islands of Bourbon and France, it is widely used in cooking and known as brède; in the West Indies, it is used in the same way and called laman. It has been eaten for some time in the south of France, but only recently has its use begun to spread to Paris and the surrounding area. Its leaves can be eaten in the same way as spinach, and it has the advantage over spinach of being available during the hottest summer months. This plant has never been grown as an early crop, but as it grows quickly and in all types of soil, it is likely that if treated like spinach, it would do just as well. In ordinary cultivation, it can be sown from March to June, in loosened and amended soil. CULTIVATED MINT, BAUUX (Mentha sativa); perennial, indigenous, aromatic plant, the young stems of which are used as a salad ingredient. PRIMEUR. It's extremely easy to supply yourself with mint throughout the winter. All you have to do is pull up a clump with the root ball, bury it in the compost of a warm bed with small vegetables, and give it a few sprinklings. After a few days, it sprouts numerous buds, which are cut off when they are two or three inches long. These are often renewed before the plant is exhausted. When the plant begins to produce less, another is planted next to it, and so on, until the full-ground mints begin to produce. REGULAR CULTIVATION. Cullivy mint does well in all types of soil, but prefers light, slightly damp or cool soil with a northern exposure. It can be propagated from seed, which is sown in March and April, but more commonly, old plants are broken up in autumn, or rooted suckers are separated in spring. What's more, far from looking for ways to multiply this extremely tractable plant, we're obliged to use them to prevent it from invading much more land than we want to give it. It requires no cultivation care. PEPPER MINT (Mentha piperita) is another species frequently found in vegetable gardens, and grown to flavor pastilles and liqueurs. Everything we've said about the former also applies to this onea1 MOUTARDE NOIRE ou SENEVÉ (Sinapis nigra); an annual, indigenous plant, whose seeds are ground into flour and put into a paste with vinegar and a few assortments, to make the mustard we serve on our tables. When it's young, it's cut up and eaten as a salad, and it's only in this respect that we need to consider it here. PRIMEUR. In December, or, to put it more accurately, as soon as you realize that the cold weather is approaching and that seedlings can no longer be sown in the open ground, you sow them thickly, broadcasting them onto the potting soil of a warm bed of small vegetables. Water lightly and often, and start cutting as soon as the leaf is two or three inches long. To obtain lettuce throughout the winter, seedlings should be renewed every fifteen or twenty days, as the plant wears out very quickly. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: we will always consider mustard only from the point of view of the vegetable garden. Mustard likes sandy or light soils, a little dry and warm, but it succeeds quite well in all soils, provided they have been amended with good fertilizers and perfectly loosened by at least two ploughings. The first sowing is done around the middle of March, and the crop is cut from month to month, so that there is always something tender to cut. Spring seedlings should be sown in earthen beds with good exposure. Summer seedlings should be planted in semi-shaded areas, and require constant watering to produce fine, delicate leaves. Autumn plants are grown in the same way as spring plants. Mustard seed keeps well for three or four years. Mourarde BlanCe (Sinapis alba), also an annual and indigenous plant, is used for the same purposes and can be grown in the same way. 243 NAVET (Brassica napus); a biennial, native plant whose many uses in cooking are too well known to need mentioning here. M. Vilmorin has classified the many varieties of turnip grown in France into three series: 1. dry turnips, 2. tender turnips, 3. semi-tender turnips. According to this wise farmer, we will indicate those that are most common in jardius. IST SERIES. - Navers sEcs. Their flesh is fine and does not fall apart when cooked. Freneuse, small and half-long. Gros Freneuse, same shape, larger and of inferior quality. Navet de Meaux, very elongated, rather like an elongated carrot. Navet de Saulieu, similar to the above in shape, but with a blackish rind. Petit Berlin or Teltau, very small, with no more leaves than a radish. Varieties of this breed only thrive in poor, sandy, soft soils. In the department of Saône-et-Loire, we have a variety known as Navet de Baubry, which is no bigger than a feather-pipe, loug, brown, excellent, and the most esteemed of all turnips around Lyon. It grows in granitic soil, composed entirely of detritus from primitive rocks. All dry turnips are subject to degeneration in two or three years if grown in unsuitable soil. II° SERIES. - Tender turnips. Their flesh is less firm than that of previous varieties, and of mediocre quality; it easily falls apart when cooked. Navet des vertus, an early variety and one of the best in this series; it is oblong and very white. Navet des Sablons, very good; white and half-round. Rose du Palatinat has very tender, very good flesh and a pink neck. The gros long d'Alsace is of very mediocre quality, but is remarkable for its enormous size. The Navet de Clairsontaine is large, very long, and halfway out of the ground. Blanc plat hatif is compressed, very early, but of mediocre quality. The red flat hative differs from the previous one only in color. It is no better in quality. Turneps on rave from the Limousin region is very large, but of such mediocre quality that it is grown only for cattle feed. Turnips from this series are much poorer than dry turnips, but have the advantage of growing well in all soils, and degenerating much less quickly. In most departments of southern France, they are known by the name of rabe, and are grown in large quantities. III° SERIES. - Half-TeNDrEs. Their flesh shares the qualities and defects of the two preceding breeds. The Dutch yellow, with yellowish rind and flesh, and a rounded forine. The jaune d'Ecosse, similar to the previous one, but much more resistant to frost, which has led to its spread in Scotland and England. Malle yellow, newly imported from the United States. It is extremely early, small, round, with very spindly leaves. Alsace black, long, usually very sweet and good. Gris de Morigny, round, with firm, good flesh. These turnips, like the others, are best grown in sandier soil. PRIMEUR. Because turnips keep very well throughout the winter, it is not customary to grow them as early crops. They can easily be kept with their qualities until March, provided they are stored in a dry, frost-free and not too hot place. But if you want to have them from then until the others start to give, you need to follow a very simple procedure, which we'll explain below. In spring, when you're ready to replant the seedbeds, choose from among the turnips those that have sewn up best, i.e. those that have retained their lively color, are firm and wrinkle-free, and whose crowns have sprouted few or no buds. If these are present, we cut them off, removing with them the largest part of the neck that gave rise to them, and leave the wound to dry in the air for four or five days. Once this has been done, prepare a pine box, or simply an old barrel, and spread a four-finger-thick layer of very dry, fine sand on the bottom. Lay a bed of turnips on top, and place them so that they don't touch each other and are at least two inches from the sides of the crate. A second layer of sand of the same thickness as the first is then added, followed by a second bed of turnips, and so on until the box is full. Then the lid is put on, and the whole thing is surrounded by a thick layer of straw, to keep the temperature as even as possible. Needless to say, this should be done in a dry, frost-free place. Turnips are dug up as they are needed for consumption, and covered exactly with sand and straw. In this way, they can often be kept until autumn. Fresh turnips can sometimes be obtained in early summer, by sowing old seeds in spring in cool, shady soil facing north. But these seedlings rarely succeed if the spring is warm. OBDINARY CULTIVATION: As we have already said, turnips only develop their full potential in poor, sandy soils; in strong, substantial soils, they become very large, but lose their fragrance and their flesh softens. As they degenerate very quickly in these types of soil, if you want to keep them good, you need to change the seed every year and bring it from the country where the variety enjoys all its advantages. Turnips are usually sown from the fifteenth of June to the fifteenth of August, and sometimes up to the beginning of September for early varieties. After preparing the soil with a deep plough and loosening it properly, sow lightly, broadcasting as much as possible in dark, rainy weather. All the remaining care consists in weeding and removing weeds. Sowing sometimes takes place in May and early June, with northern exposure and fresh or even damp soil. If the year is not too hot and there is frequent rain, turnips do not bolt and do quite well. Some gardeners, in order to obtain summer turnips, sow in the same soil and same exposure as early as March and April, and they take the precaution of using only old seed for these sowings.
