Despite all their care, they frequently bolt before forming. Tender turnips, such as virtues, early flats, white or red, are the only ones that offer any hope of success for this season's sowing. When the turnips have risen, whatever the season of sowing, they are thinned out to leave four inches between the feet of the small varieties, and eight between the larger ones. When they have reached their full size, we pull them out by hand, and remove the flower before clamping them. In March, the most beautiful are chosen to serve as seedbeds, and are planted separately to prevent the varieties from degenerating through adulterous fertilization, and the seeds are harvested in August. They retain their germinative qualities for two years. Like cabbages, uavets are prone to being eaten by ticks. These pernicious insects are kept at bay by the same means, i.e. by sprinkling the leaves, when they are still wet from dew, rain or sprinkling, with ashes or simply road dust. AROMATIC NIGEL OF CRETE or FOUR-SPICE (Nigella sativa); annual plant, native to the East, grown in our vegetable gardens for its aromatic seeds, which are used to spice stews and are known as four-spice. As its seeds can only be used dried, and keep very well, it would be pointless to force this plant into the soil. It likes light, warm, substantial soils, but does well in all. Sow in place in spring; give a few sprinklings when the plant has emerged; thin out to transplant elsewhere, if necessary; weed; give a few weedings, and moderate the sprinklings as soon as the flowers appear, to let the seeds acquire all their energy. NOISETIER OR COUDRIER (Corrlus). Shrubs and trees of medium size, native or exotic, We know enough about the different uses we make of their fruit, without being obliged to repeat them here. There are several varieties and species, of which we will mention only those whose fruit is of interest. common hazelnut (corrlus avellana). There are several sub-varieties, whose synonimy we will give below, as they have been confused by some horticultural writers. Hazelnut (C. Avellana sylvestris. Lon. Ic. 2. t. 192). This variety, which grows spontaneously in our woods, has small, white fruit with a pleasant flavor. (C. Avellana sativa, fructu alho minore. Dác. fl. franc. t. 3. pag. 308.) Its fruit is white, oblong and quite small. Lea gardeners call it aveline, as well as the following. Avelinier de Procence (C. Avellana grandis, sativa, fructu rotundo, maximo). Rounded fruit, very large, hard-shelled, with white skin, very good. Avelinier with red fruit (C. Avellana rubra, sativa, fructu oblongo rubente. DÉcAxD. fl. fr. Corilus maxima. MILL. dict. n. 2.) Very large, very elongated, red fruit. I believe this is the variety that Willedenow calls Corrlus tubuloza. Noisetier à fruits en grappes (C. Avellana glomerata, nucibus in racemum congestis. DécAND. fl. fr. ) Fruit agglomerated, round, very large and very good. BLACKBERRY (Corrlus rostrata, NoIsxr. man.), from North America. Fruit very-small, round, very-good, very thick-shelled. AMERICAN NOISETIRR (Corylus américana, Micu.) Round fruit, quite good, but small. NOISTiER DE BXZANCE (Corylus brzantina, L.) Very small, mediocre fruit, but tree remarkable for its rapid growth and height, which often exceeds forty feet. PRIMEUR. Hazelnuts keep very well, and are usually eaten dry. We have never tried to force the tree that produces them, which would be very difficult. CULTURX ORDINAIRE: These trees grow best in damp, light soils, facing east and west. However, they do quite well in the shade, where they are usually found. They can be propagated by sprouting, suckering or seeding, but the latter method is rarely used because it is very time-consuming, and you can never be sure of obtaining the variety you have sown. Buds are strangled in autumn, and take root fairly quickly. The offshoots separate from the mother plants in the same season. Both types hardly bear fruit until the second year after planting. Cultivation is limited to a little ploughing, clearing away dead wood, and not allowing them to be smothered by the shade of higher trees. CULTIVATED WALNUT (Juglans regia); a large tree native to Asia. Its main varieties are:a: Common Walnut. Very productive tree; oval, small, full and very good fruit. The Chestnut or soft-shelled. Elongated, full, very soft-shelled fruit. Norer tardif or Saint-Jean. Blooms very late, and is therefore not exposed to late spring frosts. Fruit yields enough oil. Large-fruited walnut. Very large nuts, not very full; not a very productive tree. The long-fruited walnut is much more productive than the previous variety. The shell of the fruit is tender, and the kernel is excellent. The Walnut with angular fruit is one of the largest of its kind; its fruit shell is very hard, and its kernel yields a lot of oil. The Walnut with jewelled nuts has a very large, almost square fruit, with little filling. The kernel goes rancid easily. The Cluster Walnut has its fruit close together, five or six at a time, in the form of a long cluster. PRIMXUR. The size of this precious tree makes it unsuitable for any kind of forced cultivation. ORDINAIRR CULTIVATION. The walnut tree does fairly well in all deep soils, but in rocky, strong, hot soils, it becomes very large. In cool, sandy soils, it grows much faster, but its wood is of lesser quality. If it is to take on its full beauty, it must be allowed to enjoy all the influences of air and light. Seed walnut and its varieties are propagated by grafting. At the end of December or beginning of January, common or angular walnuts are stratified. In spring, they are removed sprouted, and planted as we shall say, either in situ, which is much better when possible, or in nurseries. Once the ground has been cleared and loosened, parallel gullies are laid out two feet apart. The nuts are planted eighteen or twenty inches apart, and covered with three to four inches of very loose soil. They grow quickly, and for the first two years, all you do is plough, weed and destroy weeds. In subsequent years, they are pruned to form stems, and finally, until it's time to graft them, they are raised like other trees. Once the young trees have reached a suitable size, they are prepared for transplanting. To do this, a sharp spade is driven in between each tree, cutting off all intersecting roots. A new, very abundant root ball forms around the large roots, making them much easier to transplant, as well as being easier to lift with the root ball. If the fruit is more important than the quality of the wood, young walnut trees are grafted at the top of the stem, when it is four or five inches in circumference. Grafting is carried out in the spring, using the growing eye, split, flute or ring graft. The latter is the most common and seems to be the most advantageous. The following year, they can be put in place. At least seven or eight toises should be spaced between the grafted trees, and ten or twelve between the others. If you wish, you can prune them during the first two or three years, to give them a nice head, but this is rarely done. When a walnut tree is old and starting to crown, it should be felled if the quality of the wood is important; otherwise, it should be cut back on the larger branches, and a new head formed, which can last a long time and produce a lot. OGNON (Allium cepa); perennial plant, said to be native to Africa, grown in our vegetable gardens as a biennial. We won't mention here the many uses to which this plant is put in the kitchen, as they are well known to everyone. We have several varieties of this plant: Dark red onion. It is flat, broad, excellent and above all hardy. In some countries, it is grown in fields, where it is sown after the wheat harvest with a single ploughing and harrowing. It requires only weeding to rid it of weeds. Four pounds of seed are enough to sow an acre. Sub-variety: pyriform red onion. Its flavor is a little strong, its flesh a little coarse, but it keeps perfectly well. The pale red variety is the most widely grown in Frauce; it acquires very good quality in suitable soils. Sub-variety: white pyriform onion, milder than the previous varieties. Yellow or blond onion, or ognon des vertus. This name comes from the village near Paris, where it is generally grown. It is large, very tasty and keeps for a very long time. Cambray yellow. Large, as good as the previous one, and keeps just as well. Double stem, very depressed; reddish. It is very hasty, and is easily recognized by the scantiness of its foliage. The Spanish or Catalan onion is sulfur yellow or slightly reddish, very large, broad, with tender flesh and a mild flavor. Blanc hatif is the earliest of all onions; it is very sweet and of excellent quality. The gros blanc is much larger than the previous one, and just as sweet. It is one of the most cultivated varieties for Paris kitchens. Ognon de James is quite similar to the white pyriform onion, but a little more yellowish and less elongated. It keeps for a very long time. Globe onions are a sub-variety of the above, but are absolutely globose when pure-bred. The Ognon Blanc d'Italie or Ognon Blanc de Provence is the smallest of the onions, rarely larger than a hazelnut. It is very tender, very sweet, and excellent for pickling in vinegar with gherkins, for which it is most commonly grown. The Egyptian onion, bulbous or rocambole, stands out from all the others for the bulblets it bears instead of flowers. It is also very rustic and quite good. Ognon patate or sous-terre, which M. Vilmorin regards as a sub-variety of the previous one, produces neither seeds nor bulbils, which means it has to be propagated solely by its cayeux. By sowing at different times of the year, onions can be obtained throughout the summer, and those harvested in autumn keep very well throughout the winter, so there's no need to grow them as early crops. We'll confine ourselves here to teaching you how to grow them. They like substantial, free-flowing soil, rather light than strong, amended from the previous year, as manure that has not yet decomposed is harmful to them, as it is to all bulbous plants. As a result, if you have neglected to fertilize your soil by autumn at the latest, you should only do so with extremely consummate fertilizers. Sheep manure and grape marc are considered the best fertilizers to use for this type of culling. The soil is prepared by careful ploughing in autumn or winter, the last of which should be done at least a fortnight before sowing, to give the soil time to settle and firm up, as onions sown on soil that is too divided will only succeed very poorly. So, before sowing, the soil should be trampled to firm it up, or rolled if you're cultivating large areas. In the past, gardeners used to plant their first seedlings on Saint-Antoine's day, i.e. the 17th of January, but a more enlightened practice has moved this time back from the 15th of February to the 15th of March, in the Paris area. In the south of France, it is customary to sow from the fifteenth of January to the fifteenth of February, and in the north, from the first of March to the first of April. In all cases, seed is broadcast, a little thickly, at the rate of two ounces of seed per perch of ground (the perch is twenty-two feet long). The seed is covered sparingly, and only by harrowing with a fork or rake, or, which is much better, by covering the seedbed with half an inch of half-consumed grape marc, or, failing that, old topsoil. Twenty or twenty-five days later, the seedling should have emerged. As soon as you see that the seeds are beginning to germinate, you give them a few light sprinklings to help them develop, and this is essential if the season is dry. On the other hand, if the weather threatens to be cold or frosty, we'll be very careful to cover the seedling with litter, dry leaves or fern. Throughout its growth, the onion requires nothing more than to be cleared of weeds by precise weeding. The plant is ćthinned, and the excess is eaten as a spring onion, or transplanted to places where it is lacking, taking care to leave four inches between each plant. When autumn arrives, if the onions still retain their vegetation, twist a little and lay the stalk on the ground, to force the sap to reach the bulb, which speeds up ripening. When the leaves turn yellow, a little before they dry out, the onions are pulled off and left scattered on the ground for eight to ten days, so that they can dry out properly and finish ripening. They are then gathered into botillons, and hung in a dry, frost-free place, to be removed as they are consumed. In Paris, it is customary to cut off the stalks, bag them and store them in attics out of the reach of frosts. Sometimes thicker onions are sown in nurseries, and transplanted before the bulb begins to form. This type of cultivation requires the same care as the previous one, and is very well suited to strong soils, particularly for white onions. This variety can be sown like the others in spring, but the most common season is August and September. It can be transplanted in October, or sheltered from snow and cold during the winter, and transplanted in March. By May, the onion has already grown to half its size, and can be eaten throughout the summer. If the seedling is clear, transplanting may be dispensed with. If you want very small onions, suitable for pickling with gherkins, choose the white hative or double-stem onion; sow very thickly, in dry soil, and only water the plant while it is young. When, on the other hand, you want to obtain very large onions, you have to use the planting method, "but" says M. Vilmorin, "instead of planting the plant, as usual, you lay it flat in a very shallow shelf, with its head outwards and slightly beyond the edge of the shelf. In this way, the comb is filled - with plants placed head to tail, five or six inches apart. Cover by hand with good "soft" soil; water for recovery and then as much as "needed; this operation can only be carried out - with plants that are already a little strong. = M. Vilmorin has made a major contribution to the development of the plant. Vilmorin has done much to spread the cultivation of the Egyptian onion or bulb, and as this skilful agronomist is in a better position than anyone else to provide valuable information on this subject, we're going to hear from him again: "The Egyptian onion," he says, "has, among other advantages, reliability in harvesting and economy in cultivation. Instead of raising it from seed, we plant the rocambola it produces on its stalk, each of which is transformed in the course of the summer into an onion, which is usually very large. In this respect, therefore, it has all the merit of the "new" method, but its cultivation is not as diffi cult as that of the "old" method, since to obtain these rocambolias it's - enough to plant ready-made onions, which grow up the stem and produce these little bulbs instead of seeds, or "at least together with a very small quantity" of seeds. Each year, therefore, you need to plant a number of these onions in proportion to your needs. There's no risk of exceeding the quantity strictly necessary, as the larger onions are used for cooking, while the medium and smaller ones are used for planting, except for those that are too small, which are neglected. What's more, even an onion that has been "put in the ground reproduces two or three others of "medium size, which are found at the base when it is "pulled out. So there is multiplication in the ground by "cayeux, and out of the ground by rocamboles. In addition to these qualities, bulbous onions also have their faults: their flesh is coarse, and they rot easily in winter if exposed to the slightest humidity; they must therefore be kept in an extremely dry, cold - or at least unheated - place. Placed in this way, it "keeps well. One liter of rocamboles can provide "for the planting of a bed six to seven toises by "four feet wide, and this liter is itself the product of six to eight ready-made onions that have been planted. "When these are set up, it's very useful to support them with stakes, as the stems are easily dragged down by the weight of the bulbs they "carry. Onions are planted at one foot in all directions in February and March; rocket bulbs from March to mid-April, at six to eight inches between rows and at three to five inches on the row, depending on their size.

size. Italian white onions, when grown for pickling in vinegar, can be sown thickly and grown like ordinary white onions. By sowing it at different times of the year, it can be grown at any time of the year. It requires more watering than other varieties. The potato onion is propagated by planting in May, an inch deep and twelve or fifteen inches apart. Sometimes it is very difficult to keep until this time, as it rots quite easily. If you notice that it is starting to rot, you can plant it as early as December and throughout the winter, provided you take the precaution of sheltering it from the extreme cold with a light blanket of litter. While it's growing, give it the usual care, but butter it at least twice, a little the first time and a lot more the second. Around its roots, it forms a fairly considerable mass of rounded onions and cayes, which are pulled out for use when ripe, but which are as difficult to preserve in winter as Egyptian onions. We won't end this chapter without mentioning the new method of cultivation found by Messrs Nouvellon and Lebrun de Meun, and praised perhaps a little excessively in the annals of the Paris Horticultural Society. In dry, moderately amended soil, in March and April, sow so thickly that one and a half pounds of seed should only cover a square toise of land. Water only once, and immediately after sowing. The result is a multitude of onions the size of peas or slightly larger, which are pulled up in autumn and stored in a very dry place. In spring, these small bulbs are planted in rows six to eight inches apart, with four or five inches between each bulb. Weed and weed, and you get some very beautiful onions, but they have taken up a lot of land and required a lot of care for two years, a disadvantage which I believe will prevent this crop from becoming widespread. Onions intended for seed production should be chosen from those that have retained all the characteristics of their variety. In dry, warm soils, they should be planted in November and December, but in cold, damp soils, it's best to wait until spring. Bury them two inches deep, and space them seven to eight inches apart. The stems are supported with stakes, and the seeds are usually ripe enough to collect in August. ORIGAN MARJOLAINE (Origanum Majoranoides); very small shrub, native to the East, used as a seasoning in some stews. PRIMEUR. As soon as ground marjoram stops producing, usually in November, lift a few clumps with the root ball, and plant them in the compost of a warm bed with small vegetables. Water them, cover them with a frame or cloches, and they soon produce young stems that are cut off as they are needed for consumption. When the plants are exhausted, replace them with new ones. ORDINARY GROWING: Marjoram can be grown successfully in all kinds of soil, but it does best in light, warm, amended conditions. It is grown in clumps or in borders, which are renewed at least every three years. It can be propagated by sowing seeds in earthen flowerbeds in spring, by taking cuttings in cool, shady soil in summer, or more generally by breaking off plants in autumn or spring. MARJOLAINE GENTIILE OR ORIGAN A COQUILLE, (Origanum Ægrptiacum), native to the same country, could be used for the same purposes, but it is sensitive to cold and must be overwintered in orangeries. ORPIN BLANC ou TaIQUE-MADAmE (Sedum album); native annual plant, still known in some places as vermicular, little houseleek, raziné, tripe-madame. Although its flavor is rather insipid, it is widely used as a salad. PRIMXUR. In October, prepare a warm layer with five to six inches of pure potting soil, and sow it lightly and broadcast; water very lightly and cover with cloches or a frame, but make sure to provide air very often. You can already use the plant on this first layer, if you have the precautión to maintain its heat by means of good stoves. In January, the young plants are lifted with the root ball, and transplanted onto the potting soil of a warm layer with small vegetables. If you treat them like other plants, they will produce throughout the winter. There are other ways of achieving the same goal. At the end of June, sow in the open ground on a bed of soil, and in November, transplant the young orpins to the early bed. Or, in September, cuttings are taken from a warm bed under glass and then transplanted to the vegetable bed. Sometimes, when we have vigorous plants from a spring sowing, we remove them with the rootball, and transport them to the bed where they continue to produce. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Borage thrives in sandy or at least light, dry soil with warm exposure. Sow lightly and broadcast in spring. Cover the seeds lightly, and when the plant has emerged, weed and destroy the weeds. Water abundantly during hot spells, if you want it to be tender. It can also be propagated from offshoots and cuttings, which are taken in June and July. sorrel (rumex). We won't go into the uses of this plant in cooking. Several species and varieties are cultivated, all of which grow spontaneously in France. Meadow sorrel (rumex acetosa). This is the most widely cultivated. It has given rise to the following varieties. Common sorrel, with medium-sized, very acid leaves. Belleville sorrel, with broader, less acidic leaves. Widely cultivated around Paris. Blister-leaved sorrel. Leaves broader and larger than the previous one, tormented, as if blistered, not very acidic. VIRGIN or MOUNTAIN sorrel (rumex arifolius, vel montanus). Perennial like all the others, it grows spontaneously in the Alps. It is dioecious, very low in acidity, with slightly blond leaves. It has given us a variety. Large-leaf virgin sorrel. Broader, smoother, darker green leaves. This variety is preferred because it is earlier and can be cut in spring, at least fifteen days before the others. ROUND OSRIL (rumex scutatus). Native to Provence. Distinguished by its rounded or cordate, glaucous-green leaves. More acidic. PRIMEUR. For this type of cultivation, we use Belleville sorrel, virgin sorrel, and especially its large-leaf variety. From November onwards, establish a warm bed with six to eight inches of potting soil. Raise sorrel plants with the root ball, plant them on the bed, water moderately and cover with cloches or frames. It's essential to give the plants air and, above all, plenty of light, as without this they wither with the greatest ease; the leaves turn white, and they no longer have any flavor or acidity. Renew the plantation when the plants are exhausted. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: All species and varieties are cultivated in the same way; they like light, deep soil that is neither too dry nor too hnmid, but nevertheless grow well in all types of soil. In autumn, in dry, warm soils, and in spring, in cold, damp soils, we prepare two beds, one in the south to provide for spring and autumn, the other in the north to provide for summer, a season when sorrel, grown in ordinary exposure, has an excess of acidity that is unbearable. The soil is deeply ploughed, loosened and broadcast; the seeds are lightly covered with a rake, and if potting soil is available, it should be spread lightly over the seedbed to prevent the soil from being washed away by rainwater. You can also, if you wish, sow in the border, and this is quite common in middle-class gardens. In this case, it's best to grow the male sorrel, as it doesn't produce seeds and therefore doesn't swarm the alleys like other species and varieties. Once emerged, sorrel needs only to be thinned out if it is too thick, and weeds removed with a few hoeing operations. Abundant watering during the summer helps to reduce the acidity it contracts in that season. It lasts a very long time, but to keep it beautiful and abundant, it must be renewed at least every three or four years. Sorrel also multiplies very well by sprouting in spring, and this is the only way to propagate the male sorrel. Some people are in the habit of cutting sorrel on the ground for consumption. This method is vicious in that it makes the plants very tired, so it is much better to use the market gardeners' method, which involves picking the leaves one by one, always starting with the outer ones. Sorrel seeds ripen in July and August. If left in their natural husks and stored in a dry place, they retain their germinative properties for three or four years. 4 PANAIS (Pastinaca zativa). A biennial, indigenous plant whose large, long, fleshy root has a sweet, aromatic flavor and is used to flavors soups. Three varieties are grown: Common or long parsnip, elongated, spindle-shaped, white root. Round or royal parsnip, with a rounded, top-shaped root. This variety is earlier than the previous one and, sinking less into the soil, is better suited to shallow soils. Panais de Siam or batard, slightly yellowish root, less elongated than the first variety, more so than the second, highly esteemed. PRIMEUR. As parsnips have no fear of the cold, all you have to do is grow them in the ground and let them overwinter in the bed, where they are pulled out as they are needed. To obtain them in spring, you need to sow round parsnips on a bed; here's how it's done. In September, and successively until February, warm layers are raised and filled with eight inches of well-consumed potting soil, mixed with a quarter of good light garden soil. When a layer is still warm, sow a little thickly, and cover with a fingerful of fine potting soil; baste lightly and place frames over the seedlings. Air the seedbed as often as the outside temperature allows, water as needed, and carefully break up any hard crust the soil may form on its surface. When the seeds have risen, the plant is thinned out, and some time later, when it has gained strength and has three leaves, it is thinned out a second time, so that the parsnips are spaced three or four inches apart. Sowing then requires only the usual care, consisting of new stoves when the heat of the bed begins to diminish, watering, hoeing, etc. When the plants have reached the right size for their intended use, we pull them out, always choosing those closest together, so that those remaining have more room to grow. You don't need layers and frames to grow parsnips in early summer. All you need to do is sow in the open ground, in good exposure, in September, and cover the young plant, during heavy frosts, with a light bed of dry litter. REGULAR CULTIVATION. Parsnips thrive in deep, loose, slightly cool, substantial soil. From the end of February to June, sow the seeds broadcast, covering them lightly with a rake, and spreading a finger of potting soil over the sowing to prevent the soil from fighting. Weed and water frequently. Thinning is done when necessary, to leave seven to eight inches between each plant, and that's all there is to it. In March, we pull out the best roots and replant them in another soil, eighteen inches apart, to obtain seeds. The seed doesn't ripen until the end of August, is harvested like carrots, and keeps well for only a year. Sweet potato (Convolvulus batatas); creeping perennial native to the West Indies and India. Its elongated, fleshy, soft, sweet roots, which have a slight rose odor when they begin to fade, are eaten in the manner of scorzonères, or butter cnites. M. Vilmorin thinks that the sweet potato we have in Paris is not the one grown as a food in the colonies, or, he says, it is not appreciated because it is too sweet. He adds that they prefer another, which is white or yellowish, rounded and more nutritious. In any case, in Paris, we care for three varieties: Sweet potato with white roots, small and of mediocre quality. Yellow-rooted, slightly larger, but not of the most sought-after quality. Red-rooted, excellent and very large, the tuber sometimes weighing a pound or more. Since potatoes can only succeed in the Paris climate with the help of a completely artificial cultivation method, we won't go into the details here. There are several methods that we will teach. 1. in February, prepare a warm layer in the open air or under a tarpaulin, and fill it with six inches of light, soft, pure soil. Let the first fire pass, and when it is only moderately warm, from fifteen to twenty degrees of the Réaumur thermometer, cut palate roots into sections an inch long or a little longer, and plant them two inches deep, eight inches apart. They soon produce vigorous sprays. When these are eight to ten inches long, carefully pull out the roots, cut off all but the tip leaves from the stems, then replant them immediately. Here's how: prepare a dull layer of ten inches of good, light soil, half mixed with extremely well-consumed loam. In this soil, parallel furrows are opened two feet apart, and the plautes are planted in an oblique position, so that their roots are three or four inches deep, and only the end of the stem emerges from the ground. As the roots are planted two feet apart on the rows, the result is a chessboard plantation. Water moderately, weed and weed again, and no further care is needed until the roots are harvested, which is usually around the middle of October. This is the cultivation method used by M.. Noisette in his superb establishment, and taught in his Manucl. 2. M. Vilmorin makes a few modifications to this method. Here's what he says: "In February, we take large pots and fill them with good, soft, light soil, in each of which we plant six to eight potato roots. We place these pots in a warm greenhouse, if we have one, otherwise we make a layer in which we bury the pots; we cover them with a frame, and maintain the heat at eighteen or twenty degrees by means of stoves. During this first planting, water should be used - only as much as necessary, as roots and young shoots - melt at the slightest moisture. By April fifteenth - these plants will have stems at least a foot long. At this point, we prepare a strong, dull, rounded bed - with manure and leaves that only develop moderate but long-lasting heat; we fill it with fifteen inches of good, light soil, into which only half or a third of the potting mix has entered, because the palates - make a lot of hair in the mix, but few or no large roots. The plant is unstuck, and each root and its stems are placed on this new layer, three feet apart, not in "holes, but lying in three-inch-deep gullies, from which only the ends of the stems emerge. This way of planting is already a very favorable mar" cottage for tuber multiplication. A bell is placed over each plant, and the whole layer is covered with a good mulch. As soon as the "heat arrives, water amply and - often; the branches soon cover the entire - layer; in June, pick some up twelve or fifteen - inches from the old plants: they will "root" in a fortnight or three weeks; then we "dry them, and cut them back to six inches."" 3. Some market gardeners in Paris use a much simpler method of cultivation, but one that produces slightly less. With good light soil, they raise cone-shaped plants two feet high and as wide; at the top, they plant a root 23 of palate, or a young plant raised in a pot and on a bed as we said, then they surround these mounds with stoves of hot manure, which they renew often. When the stems are a foot long, they marcott them in order to make them produce new roots, 4. A very distinguished amateur from the Paris area has singularly perfected the last type of cultivation. Here's how: In March, he prepares a bed of indeterminate length, nine or twelve feet wide, against a south-facing wall with a very warm exposure. He divides this bed, along its entire length, into three or four beds, each two feet wide, separated from each other by paths one foot wide, the first of which joins the wall. These paths are dug a foot and a half deep, and the earth is dumped onto the beds, which are shaped like the back of a donkey, a foot high not including the depth of the trenches, giving them an elevation of two and a half feet. Two rows of roots or plants raised on a hot bed are planted on top, and the spaces between the beds are filled with rechauds, which are renewed as often as necessary. With the help of a few poles supported by stakes, doormats are laid every evening, and during the day when it rains. Other care is in no way different from that required for cultivation using the previous method. Potatoes are harvested from the moment they reach maturity, which sometimes begins at the end of September, until the first frosts; but this operation must be carried out with great care, as any that are a little bruised or crumpled quickly rot. They should be placed in a very dry place, sheltered from the slightest frost, so that they can be removed and eaten as soon as they begin to smell of roses, as this is a sure sign of decomposition. The great difficulty in growing potatoes is keeping them until spring, when they can be planted. However, there are two ways of doing this. 1. in July and August, take cuttings in small pots filled with half potting soil and half light soil, on a bed and under a bell; for this, select shoots that are still only three or four inches long out of the ground, and detach them by hand as close as possible to the tubers. When recovery is assured, the pots are placed at the foot of a south-facing wall, in the open air. They are then placed in a warm greenhouse or in a barn heated by a furnace, and throughout the winter they are cared for in the same way as warm-greenhouse plants. When the season arrives, we plant them as we have said, and they provide a very bountiful harvest. 2. From among the last roots pulled out, we choose those that are perfectly healthy, among the petile ones; we let them dry out perfectly, then place them in a crate, bed by bed with fine, very dry sand. The crate is then closed and wrapped in very dry straw, placed in a second crate and placed in a pile of litter, where the potatoes are kept at an even temperature until planting. It should be noted that yellow potatoes keep much more easily than red ones. Mr. Noisètte believes that the potato could be grown very easily in the south of France, in the open ground. All you have to do," he says, "is develop the young stems in a bed, break up a four-foot-wide bed, loosen the soil perfectly, draw two lines two feet apart with a chalk line, and open them into furrows in which you lay the young plants obliquely, in the same way as on a temperate bed. If the soil was dry, we would water, weed and root the weeds, and all care would be limited to that until harvest. PATIENCE, OSEILLE-ÉPINARD, ÍTINARDS-DINORTEIS (rumex patientia); native perennial plant, recently cultivated by a few amateurs who eat its leaves in the manner of spinach or sorrel, and who claim that they share the taste of both. They are large, tender and, as they grow from the first spring, they offer the advantage of being available for cooking before any other greens. According to Miller, patience was once cultivated in England as a vegetable. It does well in all types of soil, provided they are a little deep; but it thrives in substantial, free-flowing soils that are a little damp but not cold. It can be propagated by splintering or sown in spring, very lightly and in situ, or on potting soil in a nursery, to be replanted permanently when it has four leaves. As it is very hardy, it requires little care. A single plant can last for many years, and can provide three or four months' supply each spring, if the stems are cut off as soon as they show signs of coming up. The seeds can be collected like sorrel seeds, and I think they should retain their germinative properties for just as long, i.e. for three or four years. PEACH (Amygdalus persica); medium-sized tree, native to Persia. Its fruit is too well known to discuss its qualities here. It has a very large number of varieties, but as many are of mediocre quality compared with the others, we will mention here only the best, following the selection made by Mr. Noisette. 1. Downy peaches. Vineuse de Fromentin. Very good fruit, resembling the l̀ mignonne, but larger and more elongated. Ripens around August 15. Grosze mignonne. Very good fruit, large, a little flattened, ripening at the end of August. Belle Bausse. Very good fruit, a little like the previous one, but larger. Apricot peach, admirable j'aune. Large fruit with firm, excellent yellow flesh. Avant péche blanche. Round, small, white fruit of rather mediocre quality, but precious because of its earliness; it ripens at the end of June. Violetle hásive. Medium-sized fruit, yellowish, dark violet on the sunny side, good, ripens in early September. PEACH. The peach tree is a delicate tree, which does not thrive in the open air at latitudes above Paris, and which, even in the capital's vicinity, very often suffers the disastrous effects of late spring frosts. In northern Europe, to obtain fruit from this tree, you need to give it absolutely the same care as that indicated in the apricot article. REGULAR CULTIVATION. This tree likes deep, substantial, soft, light or even slightly sandy, warm and moderately dry soil. It can be propagated by sowing or grafting. Some varieties reproduce identically through their stones, e.g. madeleine, admirable, grosse mignonne, abricotée, pêche de Malle, bourdine, pavie-madeleine, pavie alberge, etc. The others must be grafted, which can be done by hand. The others must be grafted, although their stones often produce very good fruit. To propagate peach trees by sowing, their pits must be stratified and planted in spring, either in a nursery or in the field, the latter being the preferred method. As for propagation by grafting, this is the most widespread, safest and best method. The subjects to be chosen for grafting are: almond trees with hard shells and sweet almonds, in light, warm and deep soils; in soils that do not have these qualities, and which are especially shallow, preference is given to black Mamas plum trees, and Saint-Julien or cherry trees, but grown from stones and not from suckers. Grafting is usually carried out from July to September, using dormant buds. To establish a nursery, almond or plum pits are stratified, planted in quintuncia, eighteen inches apart, the young plants are cared for according to ordinary methods, grafted and, when two years old, placed against a wall with a sunset, leVant or midi exposure, the latter being the most favorable. All that remains is to prune them to form espaliers à la Montreuil or à la Française. In either case, the principles of pruning are the same; only, when the tree is shaped à la Montreuil, it is raised on only two main branches, open in a V shape, with the two arms spread wide apart; and when it is shaped à la Française or en éventail, it is raised on four or five main branches. We'll assume that you want to form it Montreuil-style, which is undoubtedly the best method, and we'll extract verbatim from our Annuaire du jardinier et de l'Agronome, 1829, the procedures we found simplest and most natural, and those we published in the Almanach du bon jardinier, 1825, following the order indicated by nature. So, we'll take the tree from the moment it's planted, and follow it year by year, until it's fully formed. FIRST YEAR. At the time of pruning, i.e. around March, we cut back the graft on four or five good eyes or even more, but always so that there are at least two on each side; with the fingernail or the tip of the pruning knife, we prick out those on the side of the wall or on the front. It won't be long before the buds start to develop; as soon as they're strong enough to recognize the most vigorous on the right and left, the others can be removed, without damaging the bark. The two remaining lateral buds will grow rapidly, and should be left untouched until August. However, if you find that they emit sub-buds in front of and on the side of the wall, pinch these with your fingernails as they appear. In August, begin to lightly trellis the two buds, barely tilting them so as not to interfere with their development. If one is less vigorous than the other, it should not be tilted at all, but the stronger one should be tilted more. By this means, we'd soon restore the balance of the sap" During this first year, we plough several times. And, if the season is hot and dry, it's a good idea to do some watering. Fan pruning differs from fan pruning only in that it leaves four or five buds instead of two, to form the main branches. As the branches behave in the same way as if there were only two of them, to the nearest plus or minus of inclination, we won't come back to it." DRUXIEMR ANXÉR. - Once the two main branches have been removed, the graft snag is cut off at the base of the highest branch, and covered with grafting wax or Saint-Fiacre ointment to help the wound heal. If both branches are vigorous, they should be pruned to a foot or eighteen inches in length, less loug if they are thin, but always on a well-placed eye, i.e. outside or in front. All under-buds from the previous year are pruned on one eye, and if any are misplaced, they are cut off entirely. Two lower buds are left, one on each side, to form the secondary branches. After pruning, the two main branches are pruned and inclined so that they form an angle of fifteen to twenty degrees, a little more if they are extremely vigorous. The buds supplying the two secondary branches are not pruned until August. We keep a close eye on the vegetation, pinching off misplaced buds as they appear. - As early as this year, if the trees have not produced vigorous vegetation, you should start giving them fertilizer, which you bury close to their roots, but without injuring or even uncovering them. Other care is limited to maintaining the balance of the sap, by inclining more or less each branch that is too vigorous, or by pinching them if this becomes necessary." THIRD YEAR. - Once the tree has been stripped, the first thing to do is to identify the fruiting branches; they are never bigger than a feather-pipe, and their color is always light green, tinged with red on the sunny side. They are pruned from six to twelve inches long, depending on their strength. Buds intended to extend the main branches, as well as the two lower secondary branches, are pruned from ten to twelve inches in length, a little longer if they are very vigorous. Two upper secondary branches should be established, but only pruned to six inches, to encourage budding at their base and prevent them from remaining bald. Trellising is done with care, to maintain the tree in a regular shape, by inclining branches that seem to want to be carried away, and by straightening out those that are weak and languid, which determines the sap to flow to them more abundantly. The main branches are made to form a thirteen-degree angle; disbudding is carried out as described for the two previous years, and all is done with the woody branches. As these branches never bear fruit twice on the same wood, it is extremely necessary to renew them every year. If this operation, known in Montreuil as "replacement", is not carried out, the result is that the branches lengthen beyond measure, and as they never take on body, they perish. Since the tree does not grow back on the old wood, it becomes bald and soon ends up bearing fruit only at the end of the large branches. - To renew fruit-bearing branches, the eye or bud closest to their base should be carefully preserved when disbudding. If the branch did not bear fruit, either because the blossom had aborted or the peaches had fallen off after being tied, we would immediately cut back to the lower buds, whose development would be encouraged as much as possible. In the event of the branch producing fruit, it would still be necessary to monitor the bud from the base, to ensure that it was not hindered in its growth; if necessary, some fruit would be cut off, and the terminal bud of the branch would be rigorously pinched, to force the sap to flow into it. We don't need to say that the buds above must be removed as they appear; nevertheless, this removal requires a few precautions, with regard to the fruit, and here's why. Any fruit bud that is not accompanied by one or two wood or leaf buds is sterile; the flower unfurls, but does not set; and if by chance fruit is formed, it falls long before ripening. If, in order to encourage the development of the replacement bud, the buds accompanying the fruit were suddenly removed, the fruit would fall off. The only thing to do is to pinch them strongly, so as to stop them growing, until the sap has taken its course in the fruit, and it is perfectly secure; only then do we remove it, but always leaving a small tab with a few leaves. "When the replacement is thus prepared a year in advance, nothing could be easier than to oxéoier it; all you have to do is cut the fruiting branch cleanly off the bud that is to replace it, and this operation must be carried out rigorously every year. "The tree is given the same soius as in previous years, and directed in the same principles; the balance of the branches is watched; secondary and tertiary branches are formed, and directed as we have said; the main branches are lengthened, and when trellised, inclined so as to make them form an angle of forty degrees. As the tree is at its most vigorous, the pruning is lengthened accordingly, and the woody branches can be pruned to eighteen inches in length. "Throughout the year, we disbud and remove misplaced buds; we pinch off those that look like they're going to get carried away, etc., etc." FIFTH YEAR. If the tree has been followed from point to point as we have said, it should be formed, and occupy at least twenty-five feet. The angle of the two main branches is further opened, and given forty-five to fifty degrees, at which it should always remain; secondary and tertiary branches are formed, so that the space it occupies against the wall is perfectly lined. The peach tree is only successful in the open air at latitudes below Paris, and the fruit it produces there never has the fragrance or size of peaches grown on espaliers. Growing it is very simple, requiring only pruning during the first two years of planting to form a regular head, after which it is left to nature. It's not enough to have prepared the ground by ripping and fertilizing: once you've planted a peach tree, you still need to maintain the soil's fertility by covering it, every spring, with three or four inches of slightly consumed manure, which you bury in autumn 276, giving the last ploughing. Trees must also be cared for by protecting them from the disastrous effects of snow and frost, which must never be allowed to remain on their branches, and by promptly curing them when they are attacked by any disease. Blister, rust, gum and canker are the diseases that most often attack these precious trees, and unfortunately they are often incurable. Blistering always appears at the tip of the young twigs; the leaves tighten and blister; vegetation ceases; the tree remains sterile for a few more years, and eventually perishes. This disease is attributed to the sudden transition from a warm to a cold temperature caused by the north wind; but the fact is, this is an unproven hypothesis. The only remedy is to remove the affected parts at the time of pruning, and when it is judged that the new buds developed by these amputations still have time to ripen. New fertilizers are applied to stimulate vegetation. Rust is a disease particular to stone fruit trees. It seems to be an altered sap that collects between the bark and the wood, becomes corrosive and disorganizes the parts with which it comes into contact. As the deposit increases, the material filters through a large expanse of tissue, and the disease becomes all the more dangerous as it occupies a larger space. This disease seems to result from some organic bruise or lesion, caused either by a shock, or by pruning done at the wrong time. When it appears on a branch, the best way is to cut it off below the injury; if it exists on the trunk, incisions must be made to allow the gum to pass through. Chancre is a kind of dry or bloody ulceration, which hollows out, corrodes and disorganizes the part of the tree on which it is located. Often in the peach tree, canker is the result of a gum deposit, a tear, a bruise, or an amputation that 4 has not healed. When the disease begins, we can hope to cure it; all we have to do is cut to the quick and remove the whole affected part, then cover the wound with grafting wax or Saint-Fiacre ointment; but this operation, which is certainly successful on all trees other than those with stone fruit, is not always successful on peach trees, and it often happens that a new canker forms under the plaster placed over the wound to defend it from contact with the air. In our opinion, the best course of action when a tree is found to be seriously affected by one of the above-mentioned diseases is to uproot it, change the soil in which it was planted, and replace it with another. PERCR-PIERRE, CRISTE MARINE, BACILE (Crithmum maritimum); native perennial, growing spontaneously in the sands along the seashore. Its leaves can be preserved in vinegar like capers, and are also used for seasoning. The most esteemed is the one collected on the beaches of the ocean, but it is also very successful in our gardens. As this plant is only used when cut, and can be kept in this state for a year or more, there is no point in growing it early. It likes light, slightly damp soil with a warm exposure. Sow in warm soil in March, and transplant permanently when the plant is strong enough, or, much better, as soon as the seeds are ripe. During severe frosts, it is advisable to protect the plant with a bed of long dry litter. All it needs is to be cleared of weeds, and watered occasionally during hot spells. Its leaves are harvested in late August and early September. Seeds must be sown no later than the following spring, after which time they will no longer germinate. 211 1. 1 PARSLEY (Apium petrosélinum); biennial plant, native to Sardinia, known to all for its uses in cooking, either as a seasoning or as a supply. In addition to the common variety, several other varieties are cultivated: Curly parsley with curly leaves; it often reproduces common parsley by sowing. Very curly dwarf parsley, smaller than the above, very slow to grow. Variegated parsley, with yellowish-white variegated leaves. It is more sensitive to cold than the others, and should be covered in winter. Parsley with large leaves; it is distinguished by the breadth of its foliage, but is prone to abortion. Parsley with large roots; its fleshy root is used in cooking and is eaten cooked. Persil de Naples, à grosses coles, Persil-Celeri. Much larger than the previous varieties. It is cultivated because of its roots and the ribs of its leaves, which are cooked to be eaten like celery. PRIMEUR. Nothing is easier than obtaining parsley during the winter; all you have to do is sow it in a bed at midday, then cover the plant with good straw mats during the snows and frosts. In very harsh winters, you can cover with a frame, which you surround with good stoves of hot manure. A few of the most vigorous plants sown in August can also be lifted with the rootball, and transplanted into a warm layer of coarse vegetable compost. Water, air and cut while the plants are producing, and replace them with new ones when they're exhausted. Large-rooted parsley and Vaples parsley are grown in exactly the same way as celery. ONDINARY CULTIVATION. Parsley grows well in all soils, provided they have been loosened. 1 It is sown from March to August, broadcast, in beds or borders, and in autumn, in flowerbeds with warm exposure, to be available in spring. Water, and the seed sprouts in three weeks; it sometimes takes more than a month if this precaution is neglected. Continue watering frequently until the plant is strong enough to be left to its own devices. Large-rooted parsley and Naples parsley require special care. Sow very lightly, and when the plant has emerged, thin it out so that the stems are one foot apart. If you wish, you can sow it in a nursery, very thickly, for permanent transplanting, in perfectly loosened beds amended with consumed manure. As soon as the frosts begin, we mound Naples parsley to blanch it and then pull it out as it is needed for consumption. Parsley with large roots can be pulled up and transported to the vegetable greenhouse, where it is buried in sand. Parsley seeds retain their germinative power for two years. PICRIDIE CULTIVÉE, TERRR-CRÉFIR ( Picridium vulgare); annual, native plant, cultivated for a very long time in Italy, and for a very short time in France, where M. Vilmorin has made every effort to spread its use. Its leaves, cut young, are eaten in salads. PRIMXUR. From November to February, picridie is sown on warm soil under cloches or frames. Water, and don't start giving air until the plant has risen, but then it demands it imperiously. The leaves are cut off daily for use as they reach two or three inches in length. Light is very necessary for this plant, because in the dark it withers very-prom" mentally, and then it has no flavor left. ORDINARY CULTUAX. Picridie grows well everywhere, but much better in substantial, warm, moderately light soils than in all others. It can be sown in good exposure as early as March, with successive sowings continuing until the beginning of August. All the cultivation care it requires is limited to a few weedings to rid it of weeds, and very sparing watering during hot spells, if very tender leaves are to be obtained. ANNUAL PEPPER, LONG PEPPER (Capsicum annuum); annual plant, native to India. When young, the fruit can be pickled in vinegar like gherkins. As it ripens, it acquires a burning, pungent flavor, similar to pepper, but stronger. It is dried in the oven, ground to a powder and used in cooking as a substitute for pepper. If you want to give it the appearance of pepper when ground, knead it with fermented rye dough, dry the mixture in an oven from which you have just removed the bread, then grind it to a powder. In this state, pepper is sold in Paris under the name of "poivre indigène". Several varieties are grown: Piment ordinaire, with red, elongated fruit, widely furrowed when ripe. The gros doux d'Espagne, with fruit of the same color, more rounded, with a less pungent flavor. The round, or cherry-pepper, with fruit smaller than the preceding ones, of the same color, rounded. The violet variety differs from the others only in color. The Piment-Tomate, with rounded, yellow, sweet fruit, rather like a tomato. This variety ripens more slowly than the others. As chillies can only be used candied or dried, it would be pointless to grow them as early crops. This plant likes loose, warm soil, amended with old compost or consumed manure. In February or March, sow on warm soil under cloches or frames; water moderately, and the seeds soon sprout. The young plant is raised on the bed until the beginning of May, and a little earlier or later, depending on temperature, it is transplanted to a bed at the foot of a wall facing south, or to a bed with good exposure, in rows one foot apart. Weed, weed and anoint moderately. In southern départemens, simply sow indoors or in nurseries, in earthen beds with warm exposure. Chilli seeds are harvested in autumn. Select the ripest and most beautiful fruits, dry them in the sun and only open them to remove the seeds when ready to sow. Thus treated, they retain their germinative virtue for two or three years. PIMPRENELLE (Poteritm sanguïsorba); native perennial, common in meadows, used in cooking as a salad ingredient. PRIMEUR. As soon as frosts prevent field-grown salad burnet from producing young leaves, select a few of the bushiest, most vigorous plants, lift them with the rootball, transplant them into the potting soil of the warm vegetable bed, water, cover with cloches or frames, and give them the usual care. As the leaves reach two or three inches in length, they are cut for use, and the plants usually provide food all winter long. If they run out, replace them with others. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Burnet grows everywhere, but does best in substantial, light, dry soils. In spring or autumn, it is usually sown in rays or in borders. Weed it to prevent weeds from invading; water as needed, especially in hot weather, and cut often if you want tender leaves. Sometimes it is multiplied by bursting clumps in spring, but this method, which hardly hastens production, has the disadvantage of producing only skinny, short-lived plants. In good soil, a burnet may last five or six years, but it's always better to renew it every three years. The seed will keep for two years. PISTACHIER ( Pistacia veta); a shrub growing from twelve to fifteen feet tall, native to the Sprie region and naturalized in the south of France. It is dicic; consequently, it is absolutely necessary to have both the male and the female, if one wants to obtain fruit from it. PRIMXUR. As the fruit of the pistac tree is an almond that can be eaten both dry and green, this tree has never been subjected to forced cultivation. CUURE ORDINAIAE. It likes a clean, light and warm climate, and in the Parisian climate, it needs to be planted in the south, at the foot of a marsh, against which it is raised. Sometimes it is multiplied with mancoues, but individuals with semis are always the most vigorous. Pistachios are sown in spring, in a terrine planted in a warm layer under a frame. When the young plant has emerged, it is transplanted separately into pots, so that it can spend the first three or four winters in an orangery, before being transferred to the open ground. In greenhouses, it needs little watering. This shrub cannot withstand six degrees of cold when free-standing, but grafted onto the pistachio thérébinthé (pistacia terebinthus), it braves a ten-degree frost with impunity. With a little care, and only with the precaution of grafting, its growth could become very profitable in the Paris area. The Luxembourg and Roule nurseries have some very fine espaliers, which produce excellent fruit every year. PEAR, BETTX or BLàTE (Beta vulgaris); biennial plant, native to southern Europe. Its leaves are used in cooking only to soften the acidity of sorrel. The ribs of one variety, Poirée à carde, can be cooked like cardoons. PRIMXUR. Ordinary pears, being of little use and not widely grown, have never been cultivated en primeur, at least to my knowledge. Nevertheless, nothing would be easier than to obtain it during the winter. All you'd have to do is sow it in terra-cotta beds in August and September, and transplant the young plant to the warm layer of the vegetable greenhouse as soon as the frosts set in. With a little care, each plant can produce several harvests of thresholds before it is exhausted. As for chard, it can be obtained without much care; simply sow in March for winter, and at the beginning of August for spring. They are covered during severe frosts. As the cold hardens the ribs a little, it's a good idea to remove the plants a fortnight in advance, and blanch them in the greenhouse or in a cellar. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Pear is by no means difficult to grow in terms of soil quality, and it succeeds quite well everywhere, provided the soil has been previously loosened; however, it prefers substantial soil, rather strong than light, and a little humid though warm. The ordinary variety is sown in March in light soils, and from April to August in heavy soils, in rays or broadcast; the seeds are lightly covered, watered copiously and weeded often. If proper care is given to sowing, cutting can begin as early as six weeks after the seeds have been placed in the ground. Poirée-carde is sown light, at the times indicated in the "Early crops" article. Once emerged, the plant is thinned out so that it is spaced fifteen inches apart, or, if desired, transplanted at this distance. For the latter operation, preference should be given to plants sown in June and July. Weeds are weeded often, and the ribs get bigger and more tender the more you water them. As we've said, we cover it with a large amount of bedding to protect it from the cold, and in spring, we remove the covering little by little, so as not to let the sun's rays catch the more or less etiolated leaves, which would infallibly cause them to perish. The plants go to seed in their second year; their stems are supported to prevent the wind from breaking them, and, usually by September, the seeds have reached their point of perfect maturity, which can be recognized by the russet tint they take on. They are placed in a dry place, where they retain their germinative qualities for seven or eight years.
