PRIMEUR. It is not customary to force pear trees, for two reasons. The first, which is not the most essential, is that their flowers are extremely delicate, that they flow very easily, and that, consequently, the operation is extremely difficult and the success always doubtful. The second is that several very good varieties can be kept until the beginning of May, and that, thanks to them, there is no shortage of them until June, when Amiré-Joannet and Petit Muscat begin to produce. Nevertheless, if we wanted to try to force polriers, we could do so using the flying greenhouse, and giving them the same care as described in the article on vines and apricots. The earliest varieties should be chosen for this operation. We still use a method that we'll now describe, but more as an object of curiosity than usefulness. In spring, plant a quince cutting in a heddle, or simply in a pot fourteen or fifteen inches wide, which has been well-rooted in advance and is the size of a finger. Place the vase near an early pear tree, and graft a fruiting twig (never a woody branch). Care is taken with the graft as usual, and the flowers are cut off in the middle of their stalks as they appear. When the graft's weld is perfect, it is dried several times. We bury the vase in a flowerbed, and raise the subject as a bush, or to put it more accurately, we let it go as the wind blows, without pruning it. In December, a little earlier or a little later, but only when the leaves have fallen, you can cohment A forcer. Trahsporté the vase in a tarpaulin or a greenhouse chatide, très-près glasses, and bury it in the layer. During the flowering period, if the plant is able to benefit from a few rays of sunlight and can be given some air, the fruit will set quite easily; but above all, the flower must be protected from humidity. I once knew a gardener who was quite successful in this type of cultivation. He directed his trees in such a way as to make them flower in February, which he considered the most favorable time for fertilization. Once a tree had been forced once, he never subjected it to the same operation again. He planted it in a coiten in his garden, and I've noticed that these trees never grew any larger than an apple tree in paradise, even though they continued to bear fruit for several years. I attribute this singularity to the way they had been grafted. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Pear trees thrive in light rather than heavy, deep, chalky soils. In strong, clayey, damp, cold soils, it does not do well, and its stony, leafy fruit has absolutely no fragrance. In shallow soils, resting on clayey or ferruginous tuff, impermeable to water, it languidly vegetates for a few years; its leaves lengthen, turn yellowish, its bark is covered with moss, it produces very few fruits of poor quality, and finally it perishes after languishing for ten to twelve years. Pear trees can be propagated from seed, or by grafting onto quince or pear trees. Sowing requires a few precautions, depending on the type of tree you want to grow. If you want large trees, you need to go out into the woods and find wild plants from seed (not from the stump), or sow wild pear seeds. In this way, you'll obtain foris, vigorous, very long-lasting trees. If you wish to have less tall trees that will bear fruit more quickly, sow more cider or perry pears, and only remove them from the marc when it's time to put them in the ground. Finally, to obtain medium-sized, fast-growing plants, preferably sow knife fruit seeds. These provide very good subjects for cattails and espaliers intended for planting in poor-quality soils, where coguassier does not do well. Once the seeds have been removed from the fruit, they are exposed to the air for some time to dry, then stored in dry sand until they are ready to be planted. From the first fine days of spring, we prepare the soil by loosening it with good ploughing, and amending it if necessary. Sow the seeds in furrows spaced six inches apart and one inch deep; cover with soil, water lightly, and mulch with half an inch of long, half-consumed manure, to prevent the soil from fighting. When the plant has risen, hoe, weed, thin out if necessary, and finally give it the usual care. In autumn, if the young plants have reached sufficient strength, they are lifted and placed in a nursery. In cool or damp soils, we wait until spring to do this. If the plant is not strong enough, we wait until the second year after sowing. In all cases, when transplanting, leave the root pivot intact if you want to obtain suiets of good appearance and full vigor. Nursery plants are planted in staggered rows, two feet apart. If you want to grow them on stems, prune them into hooks. For cattails, espaliers, vases and other small pear trees, a quince nursery should be established, and the young trees treated until grafting, as described in the article on this tree (see Quince).) Full-grown trees are sometimes grafted at the top of a stem formed by raising the subject, but we would point out that it is always better to graft at six inches from the ground, because if the stem is broken by one of those accidents that are frequent in all cultivations, we have the resource of forming a new head without being obliged to graft again, an operation that is often very difficult when the tree has reached a certain size. Needless to say, grafting close to the ground is indispensable for cattails and other low-stemmed trees. Grafting is carried out in the form of a dormant-eye escutcheon at the second sap stage, or in the form of a cleft in spring. The latter is rarely used, except for franks and wildons, because it is claimed to produce trees of shorter duration, which does not seem to me to be sufficiently proven. In any case, grafts should be cut from healthy trees, because if they were attacked by any disease, it is certain that it would be perpetuated on the new trees. When the graft is two years old, the full-grown trees can be lifted from the nursery and put in place; but for cattails and espalier, it's better to wait three or even four years. The best transplants are those made in autumn, unless the soil is compact and moist, likely to hold the pendart lime over winter; in this case, it's better to wait until spring. The ground is dug to a depth of two or three feet, and more if possible; the soil is suitably amended; the soil on the surface is placed at the bottom, and finally the pits are dug to receive the trees; the wider and deeper they are, the more the trees will grow in them. The trees are lifted from the nursery with great care, so as to injure or bruise the roots as little as possible, and placed in position without touching or removing anything except the broken or bruised parts; they are laid out suitably on the bottom soil, in their natural position, and soil is slid between them, stirring from time to time to make it flow into all the interstices. We then finish filling the pit, and press a little around the collar to give the plant strength. Plant a little high up, so that the graft is not buried when the soil settles. Full-grown trees need pruning only in the first two or three years, and then only to give their heads some regularity. They are then left to do as they please, with the proviso, however, that they are inspected every year, in order to remove any greedy branches that may be overpowering the others, and to cut back any that are thin, poorly placed or dead. As for the pruning of cattails, gobeleta, espaliers, etc., we will limit ourselves to transcribing literally here what we have said about it in the Annuaire, and we refer to the excellent treatise on grafting and pruning, by M. Noisette, those who would like to study this diffidile subject in depth. "4. cattail pruning. We'll start with this pruning method, as it has been the most widely used for some fifty years. The tree on which it is pruned must be grafted six inches from the collar. In the first year, the stem is pruned to six or ten inches, in order to produce two lateral buds to line the cattail at the bottom, which is extremely important, and a third to form the extension of the stem. In the second year, prune the terminal bud to a foot or more, depending on your vigor, and prune the side branches so that they are always kept very short; disbud, and leave only the branches needed to fill the bottom cattail perfectly. Each year, we continue to raise the cattail by pruning the terminal bud to produce well-placed side branches: These branches must be arranged in tiers: if one branch grows in front, the second grows from the right side, the third from behind, and the fourth from the left side, the fifth from the same perpendicular as the first, the sixth from the second, and so on, so that all the insertions form a sort of spiral around the trunk; in this way, all the branches can benefit equally from the influences of air and light, and there is never any confusion. In principle, all branches should extend away from the trunk at right angles to its axis, like the rays of a rouc. Nothing is easier than forcing them to take this direction. Here's how. If a branch seems to be heading to the right, prune it on a bud placed on its left side; if, on the contrary, it is heading too far to the left, prune it on a bud placed on the right side. To prevent the bud, which is to form its extension, from heading in the same direction as the branch, we prune an inch from the bud, leaving a small snag which forces the bud to grow slightly sideways; this snag is removed in autumn. If a branch rises too much horizontally, it is forced to rise again by the same means, i.e. by pruning on an eye placed above; if on the contrary it rises too much, we prune on an eye below. We must not try to give the branches of the pear tree too arbitrary an inclination, because each variety has one which is more or less determined and even adjustable; and if we cut it too much, we could harm the vigor of the individual. A well-managed quincunx should have the shape of a regular allougé còne. = "You must be careful with the lambourdes or fruiting branches. They can be recognized by being large, short and transversely folded or wrinkled. "2. palmette pruning. A pear tree pruned in a palmette or fornyth style is nothing more than a cattail applied against a wall, whose branches are bent horizontally against this wall, and have taken on a greater extension. The branches that grow in front and behind are removed, as in all espaliers, and the tree is spurred and pruned in such a way that the lateral branches are six inches apart, and placed alternately one on the left and the other on the right. The tree is raised little by little, with the aim of obtaining two lateral branches and an extension of the stem each year. Any secondary branches that need to be replaced by joists are removed, often requiring long pruning. The balance of the branches is maintained by arching those that are carried away, and more or less straightening those that are losing weight. "3. espalier or fan pruning. The subject is trained on three or five main branches, with the middle one vertical, the other two inclined, and the lower two much more inclined. These three or five branches rise or lead like the stem of a cattail, with the difference that they and the lateral branches are palisaded, and those that develop in front of and on the side of the wall are removed. In weak subjects grafted on quince, it is sometimes necessary to remove the middle vertical branch. "4. pruning with counter-trellises. This differs from espalier pruning only in that it is carried out in the open air and not against a wall, and consequently we are obliged to trellis against stiles. Moreover, as the tree is more exposed to the influences of the air, it is also more vigorous, and often gets carried away instead of bearing fruit. There's a simple way to stop this excessive growth. It consists in letting the middle vertical branch grow upwards, and pruning it in a cattail shape. "5. goblet pruning. If we imagine an espalier tree raised on five main branches, and that we have rounded it into the shape of a cone by bending the two wings and bringing them together, we will have a perfect idea of goblet pruning. As you can see, the subject is raised on five branches, all starting from the collar, and rising away from each other to form an inverted cone. To keep them in this position, they are braced against circles which become larger the closer they are to the top. The inside of the cup must be completely empty." "The choice of exposure is not indifferent for pear trees. For example, early fruits like to be at sunrise, as do summer pears, which also like to be at sunset; but winter fruits only succeed and acquire all their qualities at noon. In principle, very fruitful varieties that would run out of fruit should be pruned short, to make them bear wood, and those that bear fruit differently should be pruned long..." Every year, for trees growing in the open as for others, a good autumn ploughing should be carried out, but with a fork or hook, so as not to mutilate the roots, and during the summer, hoeing and weeding should be carried out as often as possible. Every four or five years, to revive the soil's fertility, good fertilizers are applied halfway through. In dry, warm, light soils, cow manure is preferred; in strong, cold soils, horse manure is preferred. - Pear trees, especially in unsuitable soils, are prone to becoming covered with moss and lichen, which, in addition to keeping the bark very damp, are also a refuge for a large number of harmful insects. Great care must be taken to remove these parasitic plants as they appear." PEAS (Pisum sativum); an annual, indigenous plant, whose uses are well known to everyone. We have a large number of varieties, classified as follows by Messrs Vilmorin and Noisette. 1. SHELLED PEAS. This is the name given to varieties whose pods are covered with a tough, cartaceous film called parchment, which prevents them from being edible. a6 A. Dwarfs, which do not require rowing. Hative dwarf, very suitable for early cultivation, under a frame, because it is the most plastic of all dwarfs. Its stems rise from eighteen inches to two feet, and what distinguishes it from all other peas is that it produces its first flowers at the sccond or third node. Their pods are small rather than large, but are still highly prized. It is customary to sow them in January for the first season. It is necessary to pinch it when cultivating it under a frame, and this operation is even useful when it is in the open ground. Nain de Hollande is one of those peas that gardeners call franc-nains, because they never climb in any soil. For this reason, it is cultivated under a frame, although not early and of average season. Its stems are not as high as those of the previous variety, and they produce a lot, but their pods are smaller, as are the seeds. Of resto, it is of asscz good quality, and is often grown enbordure in mediocre soils. Nain de Bretagne. This one is of very mediocre quality, not very early, and its pods and seeds are very small; but as it rises the least of all peas, its tigez hardly exceeding five or six inches in height, it is very suitable for making borders. The gros Nain sucré is an excellent variety, productive, but late; its pods and seeds are large; the stems vigorous, large, stocky, requiring more space than in other dwarf peas. Le petit Nain vert. A taller variety than the previous ones, quite productive, with excellent seeds, remarkable for their fineness. The Nain vert de Pnusse rises even higher than the preceding variety, and forms the natural parzage between the naime and paddle peas of this region. It is more productive than the small green dwarf, but its seeds are slightly larger. The petit Pois de Blois grows to a height of only fifteen to eighteen inches; it is early and suitable for cultivation in chaszis. Its seeds are smooth, peeled and rounded. Les grimpans, qui, par cotséquent, ont besola d'être souteuus par des rames. Pois Michaux de Hollande is an excellent and very early variety. It is sown at the end of February or the beginning of March, because it is more sensitive to frost than michaux; but, as its vegetation is very rapid, it ordinarily outstrips michaux, which is sown at the end of November. This pea, which is recommendable in every respect, grows a little lower than michaux, and can do without oars if you take the precaution of pinching it at three or four flowers. It does not grow well in strong, damp soils. Pois Baron is quite similar to the previous variety, but taller. Pois Michauz or petit Rois de Paris is an excellent variety that has long enjoyed a high reputation in the capital. It is customary to give it preference for sowing in winter, at the foot of a south-facing wall. It is not necessary to row it, if you have the precaution of pinching it to three or four flowers, unless it is grown in a very substantial soil. Pois de Ruel is a sub-variety of the preceding one, which is grown in the same way, and which is preferred to it, because it is a little earlier, and its pods are larger. Black-eyed Michaxz is a very good variety, but is less or less hative than Ordian Michaxz, which has slightly larger seeds. It is cultivated in the same way. The hatif à la moelle or Pois d'Angleterre is eight joturs or so less hitive than the michaux. Its stems rise higher, its pods are larger, and its seeds are of excellent quality. Dominé has many similarities with the previous variety, but its pods are less rounded. In addition, it is productive and quite esteemed. Pois Laurent closely resembles the previous variety, but is less early. Pois hatif à grosses cosses is another sub-variety, with stronger stems and more rounded pods. It is of good quality and very productive. Pois d'Auvergne, newly introduced to our crops by M. Vilmorin, differs from the other varieties by its very long, arched pods, sometimes containing up to eleven seeds. It is of good quality. Pois de Marly is remarkable for its very high stems, large pods and very round, tender and very good seeds; but it has the disadvantage of being late. Clamart or carré fin is widely grown in Paris; it is used for late-season sowing, in the open field, and is not paddled in amended and substantial soils; in gardens, for example, it is not the same, and necessarily needs to be supported. It is late, tall and productive; its seeds are sweet and tightly packed in the pod. The Manc square grows very tall and has a very vigorous vegetation; also, in substantial soils, it often gets carried away in wood to the detriment of fruit. It is of good quality, but late. The black-eyed Susan is similar to the previous variety. It has the same qualities, and the same defect of getting carried away in wood; moreover, its flowers fear fog, and often abort. Sans-parcil has large pods, fat, elongated seeds, very tender, soft and sweet, excellent for greening; it is very productive and long-lasting, but it is late. Pois-Fève is a late variety whose ligen rises very high. Its seeds are very large and tender, but not very sweet. Pois Géant is one of those with the most vigorous vegetation and the tallest stems; its seeds are extremely large, soft, but not very sweet. Green peas from Normandy are late and grow very tall. Its seeds are good, but more prized dry than green. The wrinkled or Kniht pea, found by Mr. Kniht, of London, and introduced into France by Mr. Vilmorin, is late and grows well. Its pods are large, long and well-filled with seeds, which are large, twisted, a little square, soft, sweet and of excellent quality. Swiss Pea is an old variety that has become quite rare. Its pods are very large and very long, but with few seeds; it is also highly esteemed. 2. PEAS WITHOUT PARCHERIN or EAT-ALL. The pods of these have no cartaceous membrane, and can be eaten with the seeds. a. Dwarfs. Nain hatif is a variety native to Holland, where it is widely grown under frames, although its stems are a little tall. It grows well in the ground and produces quite a lot. The ordinary Nain grows from two to three feet tall; its pods are smaller than the preceding one, but more numerous, very tender and very good. The Flare Pea is the only edible pea that is absolutely dwarfed, its stems barely reaching a foot in height. It is branchy at the bottom, and its branches, almost all extending laterally, give it a fan-like shape, hence its name. Moreover, it is a late bloomer and produces little. b. Grimpans. The large-podded white Sans-Pareil, Corne-deBolien or Faucille, is one of the most esteemed of this secTon. Its stems are very high and very productive in good soils, and its corses are broad, large, fleshy, very tender and curved. This pea is a late bloomer. Pois sans parchemin à demi-rames is very productive and a little earlier than the previous one. Its pods are narrower and fuller. Peas without parchment with red blossoms, or QEil dePerdrir, are very productive and grow to seven or eight feet, but are very late. Its pods are hooked like those of the sans-pareil with large pods, tender, fleshy and sweet; its seeds are reddish green with purple speckles, large, round and smooth. The Turc or Crowned Pea grows very tall; its flowers, a pretty purple in many varieties, are arranged in the shape of a tuft or crown. Its pods are very numerous, very tender and very sweet. PRIMEUR. Early peas can be grown in three different ways: 1. in the ground, 2. in baskets, 3. in tarpaulins or under frames. We will deal separately with these three methods of cultivation. 1. Sowing in the ground. Along a wall in a warm location, prepare a bed by ploughing well. It is important to amend it only with well-consumed compost, if it has not been amended a few months in advance. In November (usually at Saint Catherine's Day) and throughout December, January and February, take advantage of favorable weather to sow in clumps or rays, spacing out as described in the article on ordinary cultivation. It should be noted that the first sowings do quite well in free soil, and that, for the following serials, from December onwards, sandy or at least very light soil is required. If the soil is light and dry, the peas should be sleeved, covered with soil and mulched, making them more resistant to frost. For this type of sowing, choose pigeon peas and other desirable species. Spread a small amount of dry litter over the entire seedbed. Careful attention is paid to where the protniets point out the date, so that the cover can be covered differently. A few stakes are driven into the bed, and poles are fixed horizontally to receive the mats. The mats should never touch the peas, but they should not be more than an inch or two from them, which means that the poles have to be raised as the stems rise. Whenever it's not freezing, remove the straw mats, but replace them again in the evening, and plug up the sides with litter, straw or, better still, dry leaves. When the cold becomes more intense, we leave the blanket in place, and increase its thickness with dry litter, so as to protect the peas from the slightest frost. Despite these precautions, if the peas are surprised and freeze, they should not be considered lost. By not exposing them to the sun's rays, which would kill them in an instant, and by thawing them out slowly using blankets, they recover very well from a frost of four to five degrees. When the weather eases, and there are no more frosts to worry about, usually in February in the Paris climate, we remove the shelters completely, do some weeding, put on the shoes, pinch the stems when they flower, if we think it's appropriate, and all that's left to do is harvest. In spite of all the precautions we've just mentioned, sometimes these seedlings have little or no success, if the winter is very harsh. Other methods must therefore be used to ensure good harvests. 2. Sowing in baskets. Starting in September or November, baskets or dummies seven to eight inches deep and a foot wide are purchased. They are filled with a mixture of half honest garden soil and half well-consumed potting soil; twenty to twenty-five pea seeds are sown in each basket; they are covered with the indicated soil, and a finger of well-consumed potting soil is spread on top. Baskets are buried in a warm place, at the foot of a wall facing south, and watered moderately to encourage germination. Until the first frosts, peas are given the same care as those grown in the open ground; but as soon as the cold begins to make itself felt, a warm layer is established, filled with eight inches of potting soil, and, after removing the baskets from the bed, they are sunk into it, and the young plants are covered with glasses. The glasses are removed as often as the weather permits, and the seedlings are placed in the soil.

never lose a moment of opportunity to provide air, even if only for an hour or two a day, simply by lifting the verrines on one side. When it becomes very cold, fill the space between the verrines with hot manure, and spread a good thickness of dry litter over the whole. Whenever it's sunny, the litter is lifted to provide light. When the thermometer is at or above zero, we open the top glass of the verrine, which must be hinged for this purpose. Peas are pinched off at two or three blossoms at most, and fruit can be obtained as early as February and March. It's essential to wipe off any moisture that clings to the glass as often as possible. Needless to say, for this type of cultivation, portable chàssis can be used to great advantage in place of glass jars. If you don't want to produce before April, you do things differently. When the first cold spells arrive, remove the baskets from the bed and transport them to a lighted orangery or cold greenhouse. They are given as much air as possible, and every effort is made to keep the temperature constantly at two or three degrees. If the temperature is allowed to rise any higher, the peas risk withering away, unless the greenhouse is perfectly lit. When the cold season is over, we take advantage of every opportunity to take out the baskets and let the plants enjoy the influences of the air. In February, we prepare muted beds with good exposure; we make them with horse manure, with which we mix a portion of dry leaves if we can get them, in order to increase the heat and maintain it longer. Cover with potting soil, and when the heat has passed, bury the baskets six inches apart. Soon the peas will bloom, and we pinch them at two or three blossoms. All that remains is to give them ordinary care, unless a few late frosts occur. In this case, cover them with straw mats supported by poles, as we said for seedlings in the ground, or vault them and support them with barrel hoops and laths. I've seen a few people sow peas in pots in December, winter them in apartments, then put them in the ground, in a warm spot, as soon as the season allowed, and obtain early harvests by this very simple means. If you want to grow peas in the late season, for example in November and December, sow them in baskets, as we have said, from the fifteenth of August to the tenth of September, and manage them in the same way; only take care to water them every other day if the season is dry. Pigeon peas are excellent for this cultivation, and when they have reached a height of five or six inches, they are paddled with light branches. When the frosts arrive, the baskets are removed and transported to a vegetable greenhouse or tarpaulin, close to the days. They are taken out whenever the outside temperature permits, and harvesting is not long in coming. 3. Sowing in trays and tarpaulins. In November, December and January, a good, warm layer of horse manure is laid under a frame, and covered with eight to nine inches of good loam. Sow thickly, give F --4 Ra a light watering, and plaster the panels. When the peas have risen and reached a height of three or four inches, we make a new, warm layer with a mixture of hot manure and dry excavation; we cover it with the same terrcan thinner, and transplant the young peas, in rows six to seven inches apart, two plants together, four inches apart on the rows. Lightly moisten the soil and place the panels, which are not lifted until 48 hours later, when recovery is assured. Plants can be left on the old bed, but if they are to succeed, it must have been prepared with a mixture of hot manure and dry leaves, so that it retains adequate heat until harvest time. In all cases, its temperature is maintained by means of new stoves, which are changed every fortnight if necostary. If frosts are severe, thick straw mats or dry litter are spread over the panels, but these covers should be left on as little as possible during the day, as deprivation of light is even more damaging to these plants than lack of air. Whether the peas are sown or planted, as soon as they have reached a height of eight to ten inches, they are placed on the ground, with the top of the stem facing the bottom of the frame, and maintained at this height by means of rods or narrow slats. Their heads continue to grow and soon rise again. After four or five days, the laths are removed, and the stems remain conched. This operation is considered very useful by market gardeners, because it ramifies the plants, which must necessarily increase the number of pods. The tigers are pinched on two, three or four lleurs, depending on whether there is more or less space to give them. Moreover, the zcins are the same as those required for other priors. In other words, we only water very lightly and when absolutely necessary, especially during frosts, so as not to chill the layer too much; we give them air whenever possible, and especially light, to prevent them from shrivelling. If you have a tarpaulin, cultivation becomes easier and safer. A good warm layer is established, and covered with nine inches of a soil prepared with one part light soil, an equal part potting soil, to which a small quantity of poudrette is added if desired; the whole perfectly mixed. From November to January, we sow Michaux or another early variety, and when the plant has emerged, we thin it out so that the plants are spaced as we said above, we pinch off two or three flowers, and we give it the usual care. The main thing is not to let the furnace fire get too hot, because if the temperature were too high, the stems would overextend, turn white and produce only a few flowers, most of which would abort. ORDIHAIRZ CULTIVATION. Peas are not at all fussy about the quality of the soil, and they do quite well everywhere, provided they are moved every year, and an interval of at least two years is allowed to elapse before they are returned to the same site. Without this precaution, they produce little and degenerate rapidly. Nevertheless, they prefer warm, light, sandy soils, and acquire many more qualities there than in the auins. Strong soils rich in nutrient juices enable them to grow a lot of stems and leaves, to the detriment of fruit. For this reason, it is not customary to fertilize the soil where you sow, unless it is of poor quality. Soil has a major influence, not only on the taste and quantity of peas, but also on the conservation of the varieties. Mr. Vilmoria's classification into dwarf peas and pole peas is far from rigorous. In one type of soil, dwarf peas will find abundant, suitable food, and will bolt to become a long-rowed variety; in another type of soil, poor in vegetation principles, rowed varieties will remain dwarf. Peas can be sown in clumps or in rays. In the first case, the holes should be made about a foot apart, a little more or a little less depending on whether you're growing a larger or smaller variety, and five or six seeds are thrown into each one. The soil from the second hole is used to cover the peas from the first, that from the third the second, and so on. When planting in rows, hoe parallel furrows eight inches apart, throw in the peas two by two, leaving about eight inches between them, and cover with two inches of soil. In the normal season, peas take only eight to ten days to rise. If necessary, give them a few sprinklings, pull out the weeds, and when the plant has reached five or six inches in height, cover it again by bringing a little earth around the feet. Now is the time to row the climbing varieties. As mentioned in the "Early crops" article, michaux peas and other early varieties are sown from November to February. All of them can be sown from March to the beginning of July, and at this time sowing can be extended throughout the month, and even up to the fifteenth of August, with Clamart peas, but the latter don't begin to bear fruit until October, and are often exposed to being surprised by autumn frosts. Seed peas are harvested from the best varieties; they retain their germinative power for three years if shelled, and four or five if left in their natural husks. CHICKEN PEAS (Cicer arictinum ). This species is known in various countries under the names Garvanche, Seiron, Ciseron, Pois cornu, etc. It is an annual and originates in France. It is an annual native to the Orient. It is grown very little around Paris, but is widely used throughout southern Europe. It is eaten mashed or cooked like lentils. There are three varieties: The yellow-seeded chickpea, with white flowers and fairly large seeds. The white-seeded chickpea, with white flowers and smaller seeds. The red-seeded chickpea, with pretty pink flowers and medium-sized seeds. PRIMEUR. As this species is only used in dry conditions, I don't think anyone has ever tried to force it into cultivation. Early harvests could be obtained, however, if sown on a warm bed, under a frame or tarpaulin, and given the same care as other peas. ORDINAIRK CULTIVATION. Chickpeas like light, substantial, warm soil. In the south of France, they are sown in autumn, but in the climate of Paris, they have to wait until spring, and can be entrusted to the soil from the beginning of May until the first days of July. The soil is prepared with one or two ploughings and good fertilizers, then sown broadcast or in rays; they are spaced further apart than ordinary peas; they are covered with soil, watered if necessary, weeded and destroyed, and require no further care. To harvest, we wait until the pods are yellow and almost dry. Then the plants are pulled out, bundled together and left to dry in the air, but in a place sheltered from the rain. When the seeds are absolutely dry, they are beaten with a flail. They may germinate for two or three years. 1* 1 74 POMME DE TERRR, PABMRRTRRE (Solehun Tuberosum); perennial plant, native to Peru, too widespread to need to detail here its many uses in domestic ecotomy. Some potato varieties keep fairly well from one harvest to the next, which means that very little has been spent to date on early varieties. Nevertheless, a few perzonians are able to obtain them using the two methods we'll describe below. As soon as the last frosts of January have passed, prepare a warm bed at the foot of a wall facing south, and plant whole tubers of early varieties. Cover them with three or four inches of soil, and spread five or six fingers of manure on top. If the frosts return, a good cover of dry litter is thrown over the planting, which is lifted during the day whenever the temperature permits, and put back exactly every evening. Potatoes treated in this way begin to produce tubers long before those from the previous harvest are exhausted. If you want to obtain new tubers throughout the winter, you do things differently. In May or early June, in a warm, vaulted cellar, spread a four-inch-thick bed of sand mixed with half potting soil that has been completely reduced to earth. Whole tubers are placed on top, four inches apart, and covered with a second bed of sand, a second of potatoes, and so on, until the layer is two and a half feet high. Soon the potatoes sprout spindly, small, flat-lying stalks of yellowish or reddish white. These stems are left to grow at random. As soon as winter begins, we make a hole in the sand of the bed, to check whether the new tubers produced by the roots have reached a suitable size for use in cooking. If this is the case, we start to pull them from one end of the bed, and every day we can extract a good quantity. Those that have not reached a suitable size are covered with sand, and when you have reached the other end of the layer, you can already make a second harvest, by starting again to dig the part of the layer from which the first ones were taken. Usually, this second harvest exhausts the bed, and a new one has to be made every year. We should also point out that the potatoes obtained by this method have much less flavor than those whose stems grow in the open air. REGULAR CULTIVATION. As the potato is more suited to cultivation in the field than in the garden, we will confine ourselves here to the details that are most essential to know. The potato can be grown anywhere, but its volume is greater in rich, substantial, strong soils, and it acquires its full flavor only in dry, light, sandy soils. In damp, marshy soils, it becomes pungent, and its flesh is watery and of poor quality. It can be propagated in two ways: by sowing, or by planting its tubers whole or in fragments. It is sown in early March or early April, very brightly and in rays spaced fifteen to eighteen inches apart. At the bottom of the furrows, cover the seeds with only half an inch of potting soil, and water to facilitate germination. When the plant has risen, thin out if necessary, so that there is about a foot between each plant. Some of the soil from the edges of the plant is then brought down to make a first buttaze, which is renewed as the stems rise, until they are fifteen inches high, at which point they are buttzed for the last time. They are then ridged for the last time, in the same way as planted potatoes. In good, light soils, a good quantity of tubers is obtained, more than half of which are of ordinary size. The smaller ones are excellent for planting the following spring. One of the great advantages of the sowing method is that you run the chance of obtaining new varieties, better than those you may have chosen; in any case, they are more suited to the climate and soil quality. For planting, we use whole tubers, or only cut pieces, each with at least two or three eyes. In March or April, they are planted in holes or gullies, a foot or eighteen inches apart, depending on the variety. They are lightly covered with soil, and as the stems rise, they are replanted with soil from the hole. In a good crop, potatoes should be ridged at least three times. It's extremely useful to harvest them at their ripening point, because if this point is missed and autumn is rainy, they turn green, sprout buds and lose all their quality. After allowing them to dry out on the terraip for twenty-four or forty-eight hours, they can be moved to a dry, frost-free place, where they keep very well, provided they are not overcrowded, or that you take the precaution of stirring them at least a few times. COMMON APPLE (Malus communis); native tree, of second size, having provided a large number of varieties, the best of which are Pammes de Paredis on da la Sam ham Fsuin ppit, rounded or elongated, bleno, spotted with red on one side, quite good, ripening at the end of June. Carmiz de fuin. Rounded, medium-sized fruit, greenish, bright red on the sunny side, a little acidic, but very pleasant. We mention these two species only because of their fruitiness. Calollle d'été, grosse pomme Maueteine, Passe-pomme. Small, conical, ribbed fruit, dark red on the sunny side, with dry flesh and little flavor, ripening at the beginning of the month. We omit to mention here a fairly large number of other varieties, some of which are quite good, but not comparable to those we have just mentioned. PRIMEUR. As apples are easily preserved throughout the year, it would be pointless to break into apple trees. However, if an amateur wanted to have fun experimenting on this subject, he could apply to this species everything we have said about pears. ORDIARY COETURE. Apple trees succeed almost everywhere, except in very sandy soils, clays or chalk; however, they are more at home in frank, soft and substantial turres reposenz on carcaireh rock bottoms. When sowing, choose seeds according to what you want to obtain. The seeds of wild apples produce the most vigorous, tallest trees, such as the wild apples we find in the woods. The others provide less vigorous trees, diminishing in strength in this order: 1. cider fruit seeds; 2. knife fruit seeds; 3. doucins. All trees obtained from seeds are called francs, and are generally more robust than the others. The seedlings are sown and the nursery cultivated as described in the article on pear trees. The difference is that apple trees need to be hoeed less deeply, because their roots sink less vertically into the ground. Dwarf apple trees, such as cattails, goblets, bushes, etc., can be grafted onto doucius offshoots, or onto paradise. As for the rest, apple trees are so similar to pear trees in cultivation that it would be pointless to repeat here what we have already said elsewhere. PORRUM (Allium Porrum); this garlic, native to Switzerland, is perennial like its congeners, whatever the good gardener may say, but it is cultivated as a biennial. Everyone knows how it's used in cooking. Two varieties are grown: the long variety, which supplies more and is more widely cultivated. The short variety, larger and much more resistant to extreme cold. PRIMEUR. Porreaux are usually grown one after the other with no interval, so it is not customary to grow them for primeur. However, in order to delay their emergence until July (the time when the year's seedlings begin to sow), here's how to do it. At the end of June, sow in the usual way, and water as necessary. After the first frosts, if you've sown long porreau, carefully uproot it and place it in the vegetable greenhouse, with the stem buried in sand. In spring, transplant it to the ground, where it will grow until June and July, if you take care to eradicate as needed those that are inclined to grow. When short porreau has been sown, it can be left in the ground without any problem, but it must then be uprooted and transplanted in the spring to delay vegetation. ORDINARY GROWING. Porreaux do well in all soils, provided they have not been smoked for two or three years, but they do much better in soils that are both substantial and light than in all others. They are sown in February or March, thickly and broadcast, then walked on if the soil is light, and raked to even out the soil. If the season is dry, water to facilitate germination. Until the plant has grown to the size of a typewriter pen, all it needs is a few weedings to free it from weeds. Then, taking advantage of rainy weather, we transplant it into place, and plant it in rows, six inches apart. Holes six inches deep are made with the planting tool, and the porreaux are planted in them, taking care not to bundle their roots together; on the contrary, they should be spread out as far as the width of the hole will allow, and the same applies not only to the porreaux, but to all the plants. If the soil is dry, water well to facilitate recovery. Some gardeners are in the habit of cutting off root and leaf tips: they are wrong. Weed the plantation, or water it often during hot spells; this last method hastens the development of the plants, and makes them fit for consumption from July onwards. 28 As soon as the first frosts begin, we pull up the long porreau and transport it to the vegetable greenhouse, where it is buried in sand as described above; or we open a trench at the foot of a wall on dry, south-facing ground, and place the porreau stalks in dry sand, close together but not touching. To prevent frosts from penetrating to them, they are covered with litter or dry leaves. We take them out as we need them for consumption, and keep the biggest and healthiest to make seedlings. These are planted in March, and usually go up in May. The stems are supported with sticks to prevent them being blown over by the wind, and when the seeds are ripe, the umbels are cut off and dried by laying them on a cloth and shaking them often. The first seeds to come loose are always the best, and are likely to germinate for two years. If you don't shake the umbels and keep them with their seeds, they may germinate in three or four years, but in this case you need to sow them much thicker, because not all are equally good. Before sowing other vegetables on land previously occupied by pigs, it should be properly fertilized, as this plant is a great drain on the soil. POURPIER (Portulaca oleracea); annual plant, growing spontaneously in the south of France. It is used as a salad when young, and cooked, but rarely dried, when large. Some people preserve it in vinegar. There are two varieties: Common purslane, with green leaves. Golden purslane, with yellowish leaves. This latter variety easily reverts to its type, provided its cultivation is neglected. PRDIXUR. In December, on the warm bed for small vegetables, either under cloches, under a frame, or under a tarpaulin, sow lightly and do not cover the seeds, which are very fine; just press them into the soil with the flat of your hand or a small board. Seedlings are sown month by month until the end of March. Water lightly with an apple watering can pierced with extremely fine holes, and that's all there is to it. As this plant is extremely sensitive to cold, and the slightest frost will kill it, it must be protected from it with the most scrupulous precision. To grow it from the moment when the harshness of the season no longer allows it to produce in the open ground, to the moment when it begins to produce in the beds, young plants are lifted with their root ball, and transplanted into the potting soil of the bed, where they are governed like those sown there. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. When frosts are no longer to be feared, i.e. at the end of April or the beginning of May, earlier or later depending on the climate, loosen the soil perfectly in a bed at the foot of a wall facing south. Sow the purslane lightly and cover the seed with a light stroke of a rake, or, much better, spread a layer of fine loam about a line thick. Water, and the seeds are ready to sprout. This is the best way to preserve the yellowish-golden color of the leaves, which makes them the most sought-after variety. A few of the most vigorous and golden plants from the first sowings are kept to provide seeds. These are harvested as soon as they ripen, which happens when the capsules begin to open. When stored in a dry place, sheltered from the elements, they retain their germinative power for five or six years. PLUM (Prunus); medium-sized tree, some species of which are exotic. We know a great deal about their fruits and the uses to which they are put. We will mention here the best species and varieties. MYROBOLAN PLUM (Prunus mrrobolana); from North America. Cherry-sized fruit, turbinates, attractive red color, pleasant flavor. CULTIVATED PLUM (Prunus domestica); this species is indigenous and has produced a large number of varieties, the best of which are: Prune de Catalogne or yellow hative. Small, elongated, yellow fruit of mediocre quality in wet soils. They ripen at the beginning of July. Précoce de Tours or hative black. Small, oval fruit of mediocre quality, ripening around mid-July. Royale hative. Purple fruit, fairly large, good, ripening in early July. Bisère, twice-yearly boune. Yellow fruit, long, very good, ripening on the fifteenth of July and the fifteenth of September. Monsieur. Round, large, beautiful purple fruit of mediocre quality, ripening around the middle of July. Monsieur hatif. Similar to the previous one, but darker in color. Ripens around July 15. Plum-Peach. Very large, superb fruit, quite good, ripening at the end of July. .Royale de Tours. Rounded fruit, large, purple on one side and red on the other, very good, ripening at the end of July. Damas musqué. Small, dark purple fruit, quite good, ripening around mid-August. Damas Maugeron. Medium-sized fruit, black, dark, assobon, ripening in September. Reine-Claude, ventc-bonne. Round, large, green, excellent fruit, ripening in August. Reine-Claude is generally considered the best of all plums. Reine-Claude-Dauphine, green apricot. Round, large fruit, slightly flattened at both ends, yellowish-green, excellent, ripening from August to October. Reine-Claude violette.
