Round, medium-sized, purple fruit, ascoz good, ripening from October to November. PRIMEUR. I don't think we've ever forced all plum varieties, and cola because we assume that many would be unsuccessful. That's not our opinion; on the contrary, we think that treating these trees as we did the apricot tree would produce equally good results. In any case, jordiniers are hardly ever used to force plums, whose fruit is known as Catalan plum, Saint-Barnabé plum or hative yellow plum, no doubt because it is one of the earliest. Here's how they do it. They graft the former onto the small Saint-Julien or small black damask, and place it in a pot of a size proportionate to the strength of the subject. They bury this pot in a flowerbed with favorable exposure, and give the tree the same care as if it were in the ground, with the precaution, however, of giving it a graver vase when the need arises. They don't think of training until the tree is in full bearing. Then they prune it as soon as it begins to leaf out, and transport it to a furnace-heated tarpaulin, or a hot greenhouse. They put the pot in a warm layer, and there, they give it the care we taught in the apricot article. With light, air and warmth, the tree gathers and ripens fruit perfectly. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Plum trees like open, light, chalky, slightly cool soil; however, they do fairly well everywhere, provided the soil is neither too dry nor boggy. In Paris, they are most productive when exposed to the east or south, but below this latitude they can be placed in the north. They can only be propagated by sowing, as they can only be grafted onto their own species. Some varieties reproduce from the stone, for example, monsieur plum, musk damask, greengage, September damask, etc., but never identically, and the fruit usually loses more or less of its size and quality. For sowing, robust varieties such as Saint-Julien, cherry, etc., are preferred. The pits are stratified in January, and planted or nursed once they have germinated and there are no more frosts to worry about. Young trees are cared for in the same way as other stone fruit trees. Plum trees are also sometimes propagated by suckering, but as these trees are never very vigorous, and have the serious disadvantage of always growing back by their roots, which exhausts them, they are rarely used except for grafting trees intended for espalier or cattail training. Plum trees can be slit-grafted in spring, but budding and dormant eye grafting are usually preferred. For the operation to be perfectly successful, it is important to pinpoint the right moment, i.e. the moment when the bark can easily detach from the sapwood, without tearing, and without the sap being too abundant to drown the bud. A few sprinklings can help determine this precise moment. Plums are cultivated and pruned in the same way as peaches, apricots and other stone fruits, only with slightly longer pruning. Great care must be taken to destroy offshoots as they appear, and to cut them right down to the root to prevent them from growing back. These trees often bear such an abundance of fruit that we are obliged to cut off part of them or support the branches with stakes. If a large quantity of fruit is left, it should be pruned back short the following year. 14 WILD RAIFORT (Cochlearia armorica); see COCHLEARIA. RAIPONCE (Campanula rapunculus); native perennial plant whose fleshy, mild-tasting root is eaten as a salad, and more rarely cooked. In addition to this species, two others are grown in the same way and for the same purpose: Gloved bellflower (Campanula trachelium); perennial and native. It likes shade and humidity. For excellent results, instead of potting soil, sprinkle the seedling with moss and dry leaves. Venus mirror campauula (Prismatocarpus speculum) requires sandier soil than raiponce, is cooler and less shady, and likes more manure. It is common and indigenous. PRIMRIR. We don't usually force this plant onto a bed, because it's not widely used, and perhaps it wouldn't succeed either. In fact, it could be grown all year round, by sowing in autumn and spring. The autumn crop would supply from June to September, and the spring crop through the winter. In all cases, it would be managed as described in the article on ordinary cultivation. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Campanulas like fresh, light, slightly shaded soil, amended with leaf compost; nevertheless, they do well everywhere. A good ploughing will loosen up the soil perfectly; sow the seeds in the air, in juia; then cover them lightly with fine compost. Water with a watering can fitted with a knob pierced with very small holes, and continue watering daily until the seeds have sprouted; then simply baste from time to time, especially during hot spells. All that's left to do is to destroy the weeds and do some weeding. They spend the winter very gently, without any care, and we start to pull them up for use in February, which lasts until May. At this time, they begin to set seed. Some gardeners are in the habit of covering the seedlings with chopped moss, and sowing through the camopanules a quantity of radishes, whose stems provide shade for the camopanules. The seeds ripen in July and keep for three years. RAVE, RADIS, PETITE-RAVE (Raphanuś sativus); annual plant, native to China. A fairly large number of varieties are available, distinguished into raves, whose roots are long, and radishes, whose roots are rounded. 1. Raves. Rave hative is red and very suitable for bedding. Rave de corail, or long red, does very well in open soil. Rave tortillée, to succeed perfectly, requires deep, soft soil. It should be sown lighter than the others. The white variety requires a lot of water if it is to be as mild as the others. Salmon or rose-colored. 2. Radishes. The half-long rose, sent to M. Vilmorin from Metz, is very early, not very leafy, bright pink, very good for growing. The small gray radish is sown a little light, and does not grow very fast in summer. The yellow variety has the same qualities as the previous one and should also be sown light. Ordinary white is hardy; it is the most commonly grown in middle-class gardens. Gros blanc d'Ausbourg should be sown very lightly from May to the end of August. It requires frequent watering. Petit noir is excellent for summer and autumn sowing. Horseradish or large black radish should be sown very lightly from June to the end of August. Its flesh is firm, with a very piquant flavor. It can easily be stored all winter by burying it in sand, or simply in soil, and protecting it from frost with dry litter or straw mats. The white hatif, acquiring all its delicacy only when sown in beds. Petit rouge, saumoné or petit rose, rouge paile, rouge tendre, violet, and red-fleshed are six varieties that also acquire all their qualities only when sown in beds. They are excellent and have many similarities. PRIMEUR. If you want to have small radishes during the winter, without the hassle of growing them on the bed, you can easily achieve this by doing what we're going to say. Sow them in the usual way, at the end of September. In November, prepare a layer of potting soil at the foot of a south-facing wall; pull up the radishes and plant them on this layer, taking care to bury them until the leaves begin to form. During hard frosts, we cover them with litter or leaves, on which we throw mats if necessary. This method preserves them very well until spring, but they are far from having the delicacy of those grown on bedding, by the means we shall detail. From October to March, we raise beds for this type of cultivation, either in the open air or under a frame or tarpaulin. They are filled with seven to eight inches of potting soil, which is walked on as much as possible if round radishes are to be sown. When the hot weather has passed, early varieties are sown, lightly watered and covered with cloches or glass panels. When the seeds have emerged, thin them out. When the cold becomes very intense, cover the bells or panels with litter and straw mats, all during the night. You mustn't lose a favorable moment to give the plants air, because if they don't get it, they wither and don't produce their roots. If you're growing under cloches or frames, you need to probe the layers from time to time, and as soon as you notice that they're losing heat, you revive them with heaters. The seedlings continue to be planted with the same care throughout the winter, but usually in February they can be covered with cloches. A few doormats are enough to protect them from the cool nights. In England and some northern provinces, radishes are grown to make a very good salad. Here's how: the seed is soaked in water for twenty-four hours, then placed in a small canvas bag and hung in the sun. The seeds are sown very thickly in potting soil, on a bed in winter, or in the ground in summer. Five days later, they have emerged, and the young leaves and cotyledons are sufficiently developed for them to be used by cutting very close to the ground with scissors. OBDINARY CULTIVATION: In March, start sowing turnips and radishes in light, loosened soil, covered with an inch of potting mix, and in warm conditions. We walk, water, destroy parasitic weeds, give one or two light weedings, and this care is sufficient. Seedlings can be sown again every fortnight, but when the heat starts to make itself felt, the little turnips are prone to becoming hollow, prickly and climbing. To prevent this, sow in cool, shady, north-facing soil and water so that the soil is constantly moist. Seeds are collected from the purest varieties. They may germinate for five or six years. RHUBARBE (Ahcum); two species are grown, whose thick, fleshy ribs are used in cooking, after being peeled and cut into sections. In England, they are used to replace the fruit used in pastries. The species cultivated for this purpose are: Wavy Rhubarb (Rheum undulatum); perennial and native to China. The least esteemed, yet the most widespread. Redcurrant Rhubarb (Rheum ribes); perennial like the previous one, and native to Asia. This one is much better than the other, but is still so rare that it is very difficult to obtain. Rhubarb (Rheum compactum) and Rhubarb (Rheum palmatum) are also very popular in England for cooking. PRIMEUR. I don't think anyone has yet tried to force rhubarb into cultivation. If we wanted to have it in winter, all we'd have to do is plant a few plants in baskets in spring. In autumn, the baskets would be immersed in potting soil in a warm, tarpaulin-covered or under-roof greenhouse. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: These plants do very well in free-flowing, light, round soil, amended with fertilizer and exposed to the sun. They can be propagated in two ways: by sowing and by separating suckers. Seeds are sown as soon as they reach maturity, in small pots that Ton dips into a warm layer of potting soil. When they have risen and the plant is strong enough, it is planted with its root ball. It's much quicker to propagate these plants by rooting suckers, separating them from their mother in spring and planting them out in rows and staggered rows, three or four feet apart. During the first year, they require some care: weeding, hoeing and watering are necessary to ensure a good start. In subsequent years, however, they can be left to their own devices, after being lightly ploughed each spring. ROQUETTE (Brassica eruca); a small, annual, native plant whose young shoots can be used as a salad ingredient. As it grows, it acquires a pungency that causes it to be rejected for ordinary use. PRIMEUR. A shallow plough is used to prepare a spot on the bed for small vegetables, and seed is sown every two weeks, from October to March. Lightly baste every day, and the arugula is soon ready for cutting. It grows back for a while, but soon wears out. In any case, whether grown under cloches, trays or tarpaulins, it requires no more care than that given to other supplies. ORDINARY GROWING: in light, warm soil in spring and autumn, damp and shady in summer, suitably ploughed and loosened, sow lightly and broadcast, covering sparingly with a rake. Weed, light and water as needed. It's especially in summer that you must not spare water, if you don't want the plant to contract a pungency that becomes quite unpleasant. Spring-sown seed is the best. It ripens in July and August, and keeps well for three or four years. S SALSIFIS or CERCIFIS (Tragopogon porrifolium); biennial and native plant, whose long, fleshy roots, white on the outside and inside, serve the same purpose as those of scorsonera. PRImEUa. No special equipment is needed to grow salsify all year round. Spring-sown salsify can be harvested from November26 to spring; all you need to do is sow in July and August, and you'll have salsify the following year, from spring to November. The only special care required for the latter sowings is to water them more abundantly during hot spells. REGULAR CULTIVATION. Salsify likes deep, substantial soil that has been thoroughly ploughed and loosened, and which has not been fertilized for some time. They are broadcast from February to the end of April, with frequent watering to prevent the seeds from drying out before germination. Sometimes sowing is prolonged until the beginning of June. We hoe and weed. If you fear that the soil will freeze too deep in winter for you to be able to pull them out easily if necessary, you can spread a good layer of litter over them; but it would be even better to pull them all out at the end of December, and place them in a vegetable greenhouse, in fresh sand, or else open a trench in the garden, arrange them properly and in potting soil, and cover the whole with litter or dry leaves to defend them against the rigors of frost. Salsify seeds usually ripen in July, and retain their germinative power for no more than two years. SARRIETTE DES JARDINS, SAVOURÉE ou SADRÉE (Satureia hortensis); annual plant, native to Italy, aromatic, much used in cooking to season bog beans, peas and sauerkraut. Another species, Satureia montana, is native to the same country and is used for the same purposes, but has the advantage of being perennial. PRIMEUR. In Holland, savory is dried out for year-round use. Before the flowers have developed, they are plucked, tied into bundles and dried in the shade in an airy place. Thus treated, it can be used for at least a year. Fresh sowings can be obtained throughout the winter by sowing them on a warm bed near glass; but they are quite delicate, and if they are not given plenty of air and light, they wither very easily. If mountain savory is grown, a few plants are lifted in November, along with the root ball, and transplanted into the warm bed of a vegetable bed, where they provide young stems for quite a long time. When the plants are exhausted, they are replaced by new ones. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: This small, tree-like plant grows to no more than nine or ten inches and is extremely easy to cultivate. It likes light, warm soils, where it is sown in February. It requires no special care. When the seeds begin to ripen, cut the stems, bundle them together and hang them from the floor in a dry place. They keep well for four or five years. STRAIGHT OR OFFICINAL SWEETBERRY (Salvia officinalis); native, frutescent perennial. In Paris, the only use for this aromatic plant is as a seasoning for rabe soup and to flavor fresh pork and ham. In Holland and even in northern France, it is widely used to season almost all stews. PRIMEUR. Sage is excellent dried as a seasoning, so no special care is needed in its cultivation. If you want to use it to dress fresh pork, you can pick it all winter long, as it retains its leaves even during the hardest frosts. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Sage likes light, dry, slightly warm soil, but can be grown successfully in all types of soil. It can be propagated from seed, splinters or cuttings. It is sown on a bed in spring, and when the plant is strong enough, it is transplanted with the root ball into the place it is to occupy. Cuttings are also taken in spring, in pots or terrines sunk into a warm layer of compost. To ensure that they take root again, water them well. They are replanted in the same way as seedlings. The easiest and quickest way to propagate is by splitting the clumps. The clumps are separated in spring, and replanted seven or eight inches deep. The soil is pressed down with the foot around the collar, to strengthen the plants, and watered. When sage is grown as a border plant, it must be replanted every four or five years, as it is easily thinned out indoors; but as an isolated bush, it keeps for many years. Several species and varieties are cultivated, but they are not widespread except in ornamental gardens, although many can be used for the same purposes as the one discussed in this article. SCORSONERA, SALSIFIS D'ESPAGNE (Scorzonera hispanica); biennial plant; native to Spain, whose roots, black on the outside, are used for the same purposes as those of salaifis. PRIMEUR. Scorsonera can be eaten all year round, without any particular care, because the roots remain tender and tasty even when the stems have gone to seed. CULTURX ORDIMAIRX. Scorsonera likes warm, leesy and especially deep, perfectly loosened soil. It can be grown in two seasons, from February to April, and from the end of July to the end of August. It requires the same cultivation as salsify. In substantial, mild soils, it often happens that in autumn you can already make use of those sown in spring, but more usually, you don't start pulling them up until spring. When the plant has grown and its seeds are ripe," says M. Noi- sette, "the stems are cut off at ground level, and the collars immediately sprout new leaves; the following year, the plant can still be preserved by the same means, and its roots, delicate as they are, will be much larger. = Scorsonera seed can only be preserved for two years. The best is that collected from two-year-old plants. COMESTIBLE SOW THISTLE, EARTH AMANDA (Cyperus esculentus); This perennial plant is native to Spain, where it grows spontaneously in marshy soils. Its roots bear small, numerous tubers, which are used as food and to make oil and a kind of orgeat. PRIMEUR. Yellow nutsedge has never been subjected to forced cultivation, not only because it is not widespread enough, but also because, being perennial, its roots can be enjoyed all year round. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: In March, in light, moist, perfectly loosened soil, make furrows two or three inches deep, at least ten inches apart. Swell the nutsedge tubers in water for twenty-four hours, and plant them in clumps of three or four together, a foot apart along the rows. Cover with soil; hoe, weed and water if the soil does not retain constant moisture. In October and Movember, we pull up the tubers like potatoes, and keep them the same way through the winter, for consumption and for replanting the following spring. SPILANTHE, CRESSOR DE PARA, ADÉCÉDAIRE (SpiVanthus olefacee); creeping, annual plant from the Indies, used in cooking as a seasoning because of the pungent flavor of its leaves. Another species is cultivated for the same purpose: Brazil cress (Spitanthus fusca), also an annual. PRIMEUR. In October, we sow them on the warm bed with small vegetables, and give them enough watering to keep the soil in a permanent state of humidity. They must be protected from the cold with the utmost care, as the slightest frost will kill them. In any case, they require no more than the ordinary care of other ornamental plants. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: Spilanthes are usually sown in warm soil in spring, and transplanted to light soil with warm exposure, when the plants are strong enough to withstand transplanting. All that remains is to give them plenty of water. Sometimes it's enough to sow them in the ground, against a wall facing south, in soft, loose soil, and cover the seeds with half an inch of potting soil. They often resow themselves. I Tetragonia expansa; annual plant, native to the South Sea islands, where it was brought by Dr. Banks during Captain Cook's voyage in 1772. - Like all new things (as stated in the journal of the Société d'Agronomie de Paris), Tetragonia has been praised excessively; we've tried to spread it in our vegetable gardens, where, incidentally, it hasn't made much of a fortune, and we've suggested it as a replacement for peel during the summer, a season in which peel is usually lacking because the heat makes it rise very quickly. PRIMEUR. The perpetual secretary of the Paris Horticultural Society, Mr. Soulange-Baudin, has suggested that market gardeners grow this plant as a primeur. However, we feel that this would be impractical because of the large amount of space it would take up in a tarpaulin, which would produce results of very little interest, and because it would be of no use during the nine months of the year when spinach is produced. These are undoubtedly the reasons why, to date, no grower, at least to our knowledge, has been tempted to follow the advice of the perpetual secretary of the Horticultural Society. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: Sow on beds and under cloches or trays, in February and March, either in potting soil, spacing the seeds four or five inches apart so that they can be lifted with the rootball when transplanting, or in small pots. When the plant is strong enough, and there are no more frosts to worry about, we transplant it into soft, terra-cotta soil with warm exposure, spacing the plants at least two feet apart in all directions. All that's left to do is to weed it and water it frequently during hot spells. M. Vilmorin believes that it can be successfully sown in the field at the end of April, either on ados or in terreautée beds. In the three years I've been experimenting with it, it hasn't yet succeeded. It's true that I get a few good harvests of leaves, albeit a little late, but I haven't yet managed to get seeds ripe enough to sow. The same grower, in his article Tetragone in the Bon. Jardimier, adds: "From tetragonia we take the leaves and the tender tips of the shoots, which are constantly renewed. "I can affirm, if this plant has the same mode of vegetation in other gardens as in my own, that this learned horticulturist is mistaken. Once stripped of its leaves, it doesn't grow back, only the bare stems emit a few twigs at the tips and at certain points along their length, and these twigs only produce a small number of thin, very small leaves. The seed is harvested in autumn, from plants sown on beds in February. If we consider tetragonia from a culinary point of view, it is a far cry from spinach, and even loses all its flavor if reheated. We believe that brède or black nightshade, which requires no care and produces equally well in the heat of summer, is preferable in every way. For further details on this subject, please refer to the journal of the Société d'Agronomie pratique, November 1829 issue. TOMATO or PONME-D'ANOUR (Solanum Lycopersicon); annual plant native to Mexico. Today, everyone knows how to use its fruit in cooking. There are several varieties available: The large red, most widely grown. It is furrowed. The small red, which differs from the previous one only in size. The small yellow tomato, remarkable for its color. The pear tomato, oval in shape. Cherry Tomato, very small and rounded. PRIPIRUR. Until now, we haven't made many tomato primes, because in their season we prepare a syrup that has almost the same qualities as the fruit in its freshness, when it comes to using it in sauces. Moreover, this plant is very easy to force, acting as we shall say. At three different times, in July, August and September, fill baskets ten inches wide by eight deep with good, light, substantial soil; bury them in the piod of a wall facing south, and sow a few tomato seeds in each, covering them with half an inch of compost. Water and care for the plants as usual. When the seeds have risen, only one plant is left in each basket. When the season threatens with the first white frosts, to which young plants are very sensitive, we remove the baskets and bury them in potting soil in a warm layer, under a tarpaulin or under a frame. Give them plenty of air and light, and moderate watering. When the young plants are eighteen inches to two feet tall, or rather when they are showing the buds of their first flowers, we stop them by pinching the tops of their stems and the tips of their branches. The fruits soon set and develop. When they have reached about half their size, to facilitate their ripening and coloring, they need to be given as much light as possible. To do this, remove any leaves that may hide them, and raise the flattened stems, supporting them with sticks. The fruit is harvested as it ripens, usually from December to March. To obtain fruit from March to the time of the regular harvest, we sow under a frame from the beginning of December to February. The plant is thinned out so that there are five or six inches between the young plants in all directions. When the plants are five or six inches high, remove them with the rootball, and transplant them into baskets of the same size as those mentioned above, one in each. Water to facilitate recovery, and press the baskets into a new warm layer of potting soil. The usual care is given to greenhouse plants until the frosts have passed. At this point, we set up a muted layer on which we transport the plants. Shelter them from the cool of the night with glass panels, and doormats if necessary, and remove the shelters every morning. The tomatoes soon set fruit, and require no more than ordinary care. We would point out, however, that they should only be gradually brought into the open air, for fear of over-sprouting their vegetation, or even altering it by too abrupt a transition. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Tomatoes like light, warm soil. They are sown in February, on warm soil, and when the plants are strong enough, they are transplanted into the ground, spaced at two to two and a half feet apart. We hoe, weed and, most importantly, give copious watering during hot spells. When the stems have reached fifteen or twenty inches in height, we support them with sticks, and pinch them off as we said in the early article, as well as the branches. When most of the fruit has reached half their size, or thereabouts, we start to thin out the leaves a little, so that they can benefit from the influences of the air and the sun. When they start to take on color, we remove all the leaves that obscure and surround them. When spring is warm and starts early, they can be sown in well-soiled soil, but the fruit can only be enjoyed after the end of the season. The small red variety always ripens well, but the large red variety does not. TOPINAMBOUR (Helianthus tuberosus); a perennial plant native to Brazil, whose fleshy tubers are used in cooking and have the flesh and taste of artichoke bottoms. Before potatoes were known, Jerusalem artichokes were much more widely grown than they are today. The stems of this plant commonly rise to five or six feet and end in pretty yellow flowers, which differ from those of the ordinary sun only in their smaller proportions. PRIMEUR. As only the tubers of this plant are used, and as it provides tubers all year round that can be extracted as needed, it would be perfectly useless to subject it to forced cultivation. ORDINARY CULTIVATION: Tubers are planted at the end of February or beginning of March, in the same way as potato tubers. Once the young plants have emerged, they are weeded and hoeed once or twice. They require no further care, and multiply so much that it's very difficult to destroy them once they've appeared in a garden. What's more, they are frost-resistant and can withstand the coldest weather. Jerusalem artichokes grown in France quickly become sterile; if you want to sow them, you have to bring seeds from America, or at least tubers, to harvest them. TOUTE-ÉPICE; see NIGELLE. TRIQUE-MADAME; see WHITE ORPIN. VINE (Vitis vinifera); the vine is a shrub native to Asia. It was brought to France in very ancient times, and if we are to believe ancient historians, it was the Phocaeans who founded Marseilles who were responsible for its cultivation. It soon spread to all parts of Gaul where it could succeed, i.e. as far north as the forty-eighth degree of latitude. My learned collaborator Mr. Noisette has described nearly forty varieties of table grapes in his Manual; but he considers it unnecessary to mention them here, as we are not in the habit of forcing them in greenhouses. The varieties he indicates as preferable for this purpose are: 1. Chazselas de Fontainebleau, also known as Cheselas doré, Raisin de Champagne. Its bunches are long, large, loosely packed, with rounded berries of varying size, light green or yellowish in color, slightly shaded on the sunny side. Its flavor is mild and sweet. 2. Petit Chasselas hätif. Its bunches are loose and elongated; its berries are small, less colorful and less pleasant-tasting than those of Chasselas. Its merit lies mainly in its earliness. 3. Muscat blanc, Frontignan grape. Its bunches are narrow, elongated and of medium size. Its berries are tightly packed, hard, greenish to dark yellow on the sunny side, with a very distinctive flavor. If it is sometimes grown in greenhouses, it is less to force it than to ensure its maturity, which rarely occurs in the Paris climate, unless the summer is very favorable and care is taken to remove the tightly packed seeds. 4. Muscat d'Alexandrie, Passe-Longue musquée. Long, stalked bunches, very large. Oval berries, nearly an inch long, light green, slightly shaded on the sunny side. Their musky flavor is very appealing. It is only found in a few flying greenhouses for the same reasons as the previous variety. To ripen in the open air, in the Paris climate, it needs very warm exposure. 5. Morillon blanc. Slightly elongated, compound bunches; medium-sized, loosely packed, rounded, whitish-green berries with a sweet, musky flavor. 6. Précoce de la Madelelne, July grape. Small, tightly-packed bunches; oval-rounded, small, blackish-purple berries, very covered with glaucous dust, not very sweet in flavor. The main merit of this fruit is that it is very early; nevertheless, it looks much earlier than it is, as it begins to color long before ripening, which takes place in August in the open air. We will limit the list of grapes suitable for forcing in greenhouses here, although most of the species grown in gardens, and even in vineyards, lend themselves equally well to this type of cultivation; and many of them, such as muscats in general, white and purple corinths, verdal, verjus or bordelais, white and purple gherkins, long-fruited franconias, etc., need to be cultivated in the Paris climate, need, in the Paris climate and in ordinary years, a flying greenhouse to acquire a perfect degree of ripeness. PRIMEUR. In England, where grapes ripen very poorly in the open air, the art of forcing vines into greenhouses and tarpaulins has been pushed to a high point of perfection, and it seems that it is in this country that this type of cultivation should be studied. Nevertheless, in Paris, and even in our northern départemeus, we have simple growers who are perfectly successful and can supply the rich man's table with perfectly ripe grapes every month of the year. The father of my collaborator Mr. Noisette, when he was in charge of the gardens of Monsieur, in Brunoy, cultivated vines in more than a hundred toises of greenhouse, and it is according to the various methods which he employed successfully for a great number of years, that we are going to treat this article, one of the most interesting in our work. First, we shall deal with the greenhouses, tarpaulins and panels that are necessary for the gardener who wants to practice this kind of industry. 1. Greenhouses. We assume here that you want to build a greenhouse entirely dedicated to this crop. The first step is to choose a site in the best exposed and warmest part of the garden, on condition that the soil is light, deep and substantial, and 30 capable of providing the vines with the elements for vigorous vegetation. If possible, the greenhouse should be built against a high wall, such as that of a neighboring house. If you have to build one, make it thick enough to prevent the coldest frosts from penetrating it, and raise it as high as economy allows. The higher a wall is, the more light and heat it reflects, and the less the cold winter winds have a hold on the greenhouses it shelters. As for the proportions of the greenhouse, they can vary from plus to minus. The length is indeterminate, but not the width. Six to eight feet seems to us the most manageable dimension. We base this rule on a fact that experience has always confirmed: vines always grow very well in greenhouses in all positions, but only produce fruit when they are exposed to daylight and can receive the sun's rays. The same is true of all other trees, though less remarkably so than this one. We therefore say that the greenhouse should be given a width of six to eight feet, to avoid an advanced coping or roof that would shade the espalier, since by following these proportions, the glazed panels can be applied directly against the wall, and the trellis will enjoy all the influences of light. (See figure II.) The height of the greenhouse can hardly be less than six feet, but it can advantageously be taller, and the average height seems to us to be between eight and twelve. Brunoy had much taller greenhouses, and Mr. Noisette's father found it advantageous to be able to grow a few other fruit trees, such as cherry, apricot, etc., under the vines. Some Paris establishments have square greenhouses for vines, because the walls against which they are leaning prevented them from reaching the desired length, and so they were designed to gain in width what they were forced to lose in length. In this case, the vineyard, instead of being wallpapered only against the wall, is also wallpapered against the sides, which are glazed like the front, and the roof must be entirely glazed. We won't go into the details of construction, because we must leave it up to each individual to build according to his or her means, and we'll confine ourselves to teaching the conditions that are indispensable for successful cultivation. If a gutter, or for any other reason, requires a hood to be built over the greenhouse, it should be glazed, if practicable, as shown in figure 12, or at least raised enough to receive the sun's rays and reflect them back into the greenhouse. Sometimes, for special reasons, we want to give a greenhouse a greater width than we have indicated, and in this case a roof becomes a great saving. A roof can therefore be made, but its width will never be more than a third or half that of the greenhouse, and it will have the same slope at the back of the greenhouse as that of the panels at the front. If this inclination is great enough to allow the sun's rays to strike against the plastered panelling of this roof in the greenhouse, it will do little harm to the success of the crop. Nevertheless, it will always be much better to make a glazed roof, as we have already said. To sum up, the architect building a greenhouse must not lose sight for a minute of these four indispensable conditions: to make it easy to 1. ventilate at will; 2. concentrate heat evenly everywhere; 3. leave as much freedom as possible for the greatest possible amount of light to circulate; 4. avoid humidity. If these conditions are rigorously met, whatever construction method is employed will have achieved its goal. Above all, he must avoid two dangerous pitfalls. The first and most common is petty economy in the choice and use of materials. The second, much more common in England than in France, consists in an architectural luxury that sacrifices the useful to the apparent. In our view, for a greenhouse to be beautiful, it must be good, perfectly adapted to its intended use, and nothing more. If you want it to play the role of a landscaped garden factory, as the gardens around Paris offer many examples, it's almost certain that it will only very poorly fulfill the useful purpose it should have. 2. The flying greenhouse. This is the same as the one we described in the apricot article on page 9, and which we drew in fig. 11. We won't go back over that article. We would point out that we regard the flying greenhouse as having the immense advantage of being able to be transported each year from one place to another, which means that we can with the greatest ease preserve all the vigor of a trellis, by heating it only in parts and alternately every three years. Nevertheless, we must say that this advantage is less noticeable for vines than for other fruit trees, as we have seen vines planted in hot greenhouses and heated every winter for twenty years or more without appearing less robust than others placed in the open air. 3. De la bache. This name is given to a buried greenhouse (see fig. 10), usually six to eight feet wide, covered with glass panels in the same way as a frame. It is rarely used for forcing vines, although it can be used in the absence of any other greenhouse, in which case it is necessary to forge in baskets, as we will explain below. Forcing in baskets. Few gardeners use this method, because it produces very little. However, it is useful to know about it, if only to provide the rich with the expensive pleasure of seeing on their table, in winter, a charming shrub, adorned with all its greenery and loaded with fully ripened bunches. After selecting the earliest species most suited to this type of cultivation, they are pickled according to the usual method, with the only difference being that instead of being simply planted in the ground, the pickling is done at the mother's foot, in a manna or basket filled with light, substantial soil. This basket is buried, but in such a way that later it can be removed with the rooted marcotte it contains. During the first year, the marcotte is cared for in the same way as full-ground plants, but with constant attention to maintaining a level of humidity favorable to root development. The following year, the marcotte is weaned from its mother, but without changing location. It is pruned short to produce vigorous, well-fed sarmens, and if any fruit is produced, it is cut off before flowering. If the marcotte has vegetated vigorously during the second year, i.e. after weaning, this is proof that it is sufficiently rooted to be forced. If, on the other hand, it has only produced short, weak sarmen, it should be left in place and not transported to the tarpaulin until the following year. Usually in autumn, as soon as the leaves have fallen, we lift the marcotte with its basket, taking extreme care not to break or split the root ball, so as not to tire the young roots in any way. Pruning is carried out according to the usual method, but as the wood is sacrificed, it can be loaded a little more than usual, while proportioning the pruning to the vigor it shows. The marcotte is transported in a tarpaulin or a warm greenhouse, and is placed in the bed with its basket. Care must be taken to ensure that the heat inside the layer is not too great, otherwise the root hairs will be burned and the shrub will perish. You can protect it from dangerous heat by making a hole in the bed larger than the basket, and placing a bed of light earth or loam between the basket and the walls of the hole, as thick or thin as you see fit. All the care to be taken from this point until the fruit ripens is absolutely the same as for vines forced into place, so we won't go into it here. All that remains to be done once the fruit has ripened is to lift the basket carefully, and transplant the fruit-filled plant into another vase suitable for its intended use. It should be noted that the vine should only be moved a few hours before it is to be placed on the table where it is to be planted, as the sudden transition of the roots from the high temperature of the bed to a cold temperature has a rapid influence on the plant, whose foliage loses its brightness and freshness. Forcing in place. This is the method most commonly and usefully employed by gardeners aiming for abundant produce. There are two methods of forcing in place, both of which are more or less equally used. The first is to heat an ordinary trellis using the "flying" greenhouse; the second is to plant and cultivate vines in a permanently heated greenhouse, built or adapted for the purpose. To heat with the flying greenhouse, the most suitable vines for this operation are selected from a trellis. They should be of average age, vigorous, well-trained since planting, and above all perfectly furnished with good spurs. To ensure that no space is wasted, the espalier must cover the wall perfectly, but without there being enough wood to cause diffusion. You also need to take into account the variety that is more or less suitable for this crop, and above all, it must not have been heated for at least two years. Once the choice has been made, it's time to prepare the vine, and if you need to heat the vine to obtain fruit as soon as possible, i.e. in January, it's a good idea to start caring for it the previous spring. This care consists in properly disbudding the vine, i.e. scrupulously leaving only the vigorous sarmens, and cutting back all the others with the pruning knife. All bunches must also be removed as they appear. Nature herself has marked the first favorable moment to begin the forcing operation. It's when, at the second sap, we see what gardeners call under-buds striving to pierce all over the vine, and sometimes even developing on very vigorous vines, the buds of the year's wood. We then begin to place the mounts and crosspieces of the flying greenhouse, absolutely as we said in the apricot article, page 9; but to support the panels, we establish a plank balustrade, as shown in our engraving fig. 11, and not a layer. The bed is lightly ploughed, fined with good fertilizer and pruned. This operation is carried out according to ordinary principles, with the only difference being that, as the wood is not yet mature, regular pruning must be sacrificed to the need to sometimes keep a well-ripened but poorly placed shoot, in order to cut down a well-placed but poorly grown one. For the rest, we also prune a little longer, and if there is too much wood, we are even-handed in removing the wood that is not bearing bunches as soon as the vegetation is sufficiently advanced to make it stand out. Panels are placed only at night, and only when the temperature begins to cool. The vines soon start to grow vigorously, and as the buds lengthen, they soon begin to show the rudiments of young bunches. Care must then be redoubled. Whatever the outside temperature, we place the panels every night, and even during the day when it's foggy or humid. Artificial heat can even be generated by laying a layer of small vegetables along the inside of the balustrade, as shown in fig. 11. But this layer should only be eighteen inches to two feet thick and three feet wide until the first white frost. Whenever it's sunny, lift the panels and let in some air, or even open them completely if you think it's warm enough. The moment of flowering is critical, and the success of the crop depends almost entirely on it. For the flowers to set perfectly, there must be sufficient heat, i.e. from 15 to 18 degrees Réaumur; there must be plenty of light, and the air must be free of humidity. If the gardener is lucky enough to have a few fine days during flowering, he will eagerly take advantage of them to give air while the sun's rays are beating down on the vine or in the greenhouse. But if, on the other hand, the sky is misty and humid, and the air full of fog, he will scrupulously keep the greenhouse closed. This is of the utmost importance, and people who know how fertilization works in flowers will feel it as we do. In fact, the stamen dust consists of small yellowish vesicles filled with a fertilizing liquor, which must be carried to the ovary by the stygma and the pistil. These vesicles have received from nature the singular property of bursting and releasing the liquor they contain as soon as they come into contact with moisture, so the stygma of all flowers is constantly moist. That's enough to explain how fertilization takes place. But if the air is laden with moisture, the pollen vesicles, coming into contact with it as soon as the anthers open, immediately burst, and disperse the generative liquor at random. This is the cause of crop failure when the sky is rainy during flowering. When the white frosts herald the approach of winter, care and attention must be redoubled. The warmth of one layer is no longer enough to keep the greenhouse at a suitable temperature. It's time to heat with fire. Between the greenhouse door and the bed should be a brick stove, or a stove that has been carefully insulated from the bed, to avoid accidents. The pipe, which can be made of sheet metal or cast iron, but is best made of terracotta, must cross the entire length of the greenhouse, at the surface of the ground, in the middle of the pathway. Once it reaches the end, it bends upwards and exits at the top, near the last panel. To keep the passageway clear, stacks of bricks are placed at intervals, over which boards are laid to conceal the pipes. Thermometers are placed at intervals along the wall where the trellis is installed, to maintain the temperature at a suitable level. We'll build a fire, but we'll take great care to light it from the outside, and to plug with clay any holes in the stove or pipes that might allow smoke to escape, since smoke is fatal to all plants. Every day, the trellis is scrupulously inspected in all its parts, and cleared of mold, rotting leaves and generally of any garbage that may have accumulated on the branches or leaves. Insects that heat attracts or causes to hatch are destroyed, the pathway is swept, and the utmost care is taken to maintain cleanliness. It helps to keep the air clean and prevents humidity. If you see the vine growing whitish, thick roots at its joints instead of buds, it's a sure sign that it doesn't have enough light and that humidity is harming it. The lack of light is often the result of a common ne- gligence. During the night, the steam raised by the temperature in the greenhouse condenses into water against the glass panels, attaching itself in the form of droplets, which evaporate during the day, leaving behind a kind of grime that thickens every day and causes the glass to lose some of its transparency. When this inconvenience is noticed, the glass should be wiped daily with a large sponge or cloth, to keep it as clean as possible. This kind of vine erosion we've just been talking about also sometimes proves that the mats are left on the panels too long in the morning, and replaced too early in the evening. We're well aware that this is an excellent way of keeping the heat in and saving wood for the furnace, but this misguided economy can sometimes be costly, ruining harvest hopes. As for humidity, if, despite careful cleaning and frequent airing, it invades the greenhouse, you need to find the cause and attack it at its source. In most cases, the cause will be found in the fumes from the vegetable bed. In this case, it must be destroyed without hesitation. In all cases, thick manure stoves should be placed all around the outside of the scrre, and raised two or three feet against the balustrade, i.e. as high as it. When the grapes are perfectly tied, and especially when the outside temperature allows air to be breathed in from time to time, don't be afraid to turn up the heat a little, and push it up to twenty degrees and beyond. This will save time and hasten maturity. But if you see the green of the foliage fading, and the vines inclined to wither, it might be wise, on the contrary, to lower the thermometer a little, in order to halt the force of vegetation. If a few bunches of grapes find themselves hidden by leaves that intercept the sun's rays, these leaves should be cut back, but only soberly, and only those that cannot be changed direction enough to prevent them from shading the grapes. When the cold has reached a certain intensity, every sbir, as night approaches, the greenhouse panels should be covered with good doormats, which should be doubled if the severity of the frosts so requires. These mats should be lifted every morning. In addition, you should take all the other care we have taught you in the course of this book for the various sections of fruit trees, and particularly for apricot trees. When it comes to obtaining fruit from the month of January to the time when the vine naturally produces in the open ground, the care is more or less the same, only we start heating later, and if we have a sufficient quantity of panels, we can start a new crop every month. This does not mean having several flying greenhouses, which would be too expensive, especially because of the heating, but simply extending the one you have by a few panels each time. For example, let's suppose a trellis of twenty toises, more or less, and that we start heating in September. Using panels three feet wide, we'll start by establishing a greenhouse of eight panels, which will initially give four toises or twenty-four feet. In October, we'll add eight more panels to the same greenhouse; one month later, eight more, and so on. We would point out that a single, well-built stove can easily heat twelve toises of greenhouse, especially when they have layers inside; so, with two stoves placed one at one end, the other at the opposite end, with pipes that meet in the middle of the length of the greenhouse, we can heat one hundred and twenty to one hundred and forty feet of greenhouse without excessive expense. The dwarf strawberries, peas and beans produced on such a long bed will compensate, and beyond, for the maintenance and heating costs. The same would not be true if you wanted to have as many greenhouses as seasons. Growing vines in permanent greenhouses does not always imply the intention to force. For example, in Russia, England, Holland and even Paris, for some grape varieties, we are obliged to grow in greenhouses to obtain maturity even at ordinary times of the year.

In this case, the vine doesn't need to be heated in winter; it just needs to be protected from the cold. We don't start lighting the stoves until spring, around February or March. Care is taken as we have said, up to and during flowering, and then, in summer, the panels are removed, leaving the fruit to ripen naturally. In Brunoy, Mr. Noisette's father cultivated several varieties in this way, which would otherwise never have reached maturity. Vines treated in this way require no more care for pruning and disbudding than trellises exposed to the open air. Let's now take a look at how to manage the cultivation of vines planted permanently in a greenhouse. If a greenhouse is to be built specifically for this purpose, it's a good idea to take advantage of a trellis that is already in use, and five or six years old if possible. If there are no trellises against the wall on which the greenhouse is to be built, they can be brought in by overlaying, if there are a few vines nearby, even if they are on the other side of the wall. If recoating were impossible, we could simply introduce the stems or branches into the greenhouse through a hole, and - a rather singular fact, which would be hard to believe if we hadn't experienced it several times in the superb establishment of my collaborator, Mr. Noisette, and with other growers - although the plant remains exposed to all the rigors of the season, the branches nonetheless vegetate with great strength and produce fruit as quickly as those whose plants are placed in the greenhouse. Nevertheless, if this method is employed, it would be wise to cover the soil around the stock with a good thickness of manure and long litter, and to wrap the stems right up to the greenhouse with a thick shirt of straw tied all the way round. If none of the above means could be used, planting would have to be done in the greenhouse, but then you'd have to obtain plants that have already reached a certain strength in baskets, so as not to have to wait too long for their produce. During the first two years, the greenhouse would be used for other crops. It would also be necessary, during this time, to prune and disbud the young plant, so that it gained body, and its stems, by lengthening excessively, did not remain thin and spindly. Despite all these precautions, vines grown in greenhouses always have this defect to a greater or lesser extent, no doubt due to the deprivation of air and full light during the peak vegetation period in winter. In the third spring, the vines are disbudded, fertilized and ploughed to prepare them for fruit production the following winter. As the panels are removed throughout the summer, vegetation takes place as usual, and the leaves begin to yellow and fall off at the same time, or not much later than for those in the open air. If you like, you can start heating from September 34 to January, but in Paris, this is usually at the end of October or the first few days of December. Then we lay the panels, light the furnaces, and start pruning. This last operation is carried out according to the usual principles, but there are two important modifications. The first is to save space. The arms are directed in such a way as to take up as little space as possible, and the spandrels are brought much closer together, so that no empty space is left against the wall. The second observation to make is that since the vine has to suffer throughout its life due to the effect of forced vegetation at the wrong time, it needs to be very sparing when pruned. This will not only maintain its vigor, but will also make the bunches of grapes much more beautiful if there are a few less. These observations should be taken into account when disbudding, trellising, and so on. Up to the moment when the fruit ripens, the care to be taken is absolutely the same as that described in the article on the flying greenhouse, so we won't return to it. When the grapes start to turn, extra care is needed to ensure they are as beautiful as they are good. The leaves are removed to let in as much light as possible, and if the grapes are leaning against the wall, they are brought closer to the light. We visit it every day and peel it so as not to leave any garbage inside. If the kernels are too tightly packed, intercepting the passage of air and light into the interior of the cluster, we remove some of them, but carefully, to avoid bruising or shaking the others. If any are spoiled, they must be scrupulously removed, as their contact would inevitably cause the others to rot. Finally, the leaves, like the rest of the plant, must be kept scrupulously clean. When the harvest is done, if there is still a fear of frost (which never happens if you haven't started heating until October or December), simply lift the panels, first for a few hours in the first few days, then for longer, and finally, when the full-ground vine enters vegetation, remove them altogether. Usually, around May and June, the vine makes a fresh effort to grow, and develops a few buds. But the sap soon stops, the wood ripens, the leaves come off at the usual time, and we start heating again in autumn in the same way as we said. A vineyard so artfully managed and cared for can produce for a large number of years without being much more altered than when grown in the open air. But the same applies to this shrub as to all the others. Despite all that we could say in an article even much longer than this one, despite all the efforts we would make to foresee everything, only practice and a few years' experience can put a gardener in a position to walk with a firm step and without trial and error in the difficult art of early growth. As for the others, we are convinced that we could never teach them enough, even if we had made a volume ten times larger. If we wanted to heat a vine that wasn't leaning against a wall, we could easily do so, using the espalier method with frames, invented by our learned collaborator, Mr. Noisette, and described in the article "Apricot trees", page 15. ORDINARY CULTIVATION. Vines do well in all types of soil, provided they are neither cold nor damp, but they prefer light, deep and sometimes rocky soils. It does not veg well and rarely ripens above 48 degrees latitude, and needs good exposure everywhere. In the climate of Paris, it needs the south; in our southern provinces, it does very well in the east, and even in the west. However, the earliest varieties, such as Chasselas, etc., ripen in Paris if placed in espaliers against a wall facing east. Grapevines are usually propagated by marcottes and cuttings. Cuttings are taken in spring, at the same time as pruning. The operation consists in lowering a shoot, without detaching it from its mother, and burying it in the middle of its length, so that it can take root. The following year, it can be detached from the stock, lifted and planted. The cutting or crossette is made from a previous year's shoot, which, when detached from its mother, is left with a heel of old wood about an inch long. If it is cut at the time of pruning, and the soil is not warm enough for planting, it is dipped in water up to half its length, and kept that way until the right moment for planting, i.e. until the end of April or the beginning of May. Before planting, the ground must be broken along the bed, at least four or five feet wide, and fifteen or eighteen inches deep. We amend it with good consummate fertilizer, appropriate to the quality of the soil, and loosen it as best we can. After marking the spot for each vine, holes are dug two feet wide and nine to ten inches deep. In each one, place a crossette or marcotte, taking care to incline it so that its upper end is close to the wall; first cover it with four to five inches of earth, which you tamp down a little with your foot, in order to secure the shoot in the arched position you've made it assume. Finish filling the hole, and spread three or four inches of spent manure over the soil, to keep it cool and prevent it from plummeting. The vine shoot is cut at two eyes out of the ground, and in the first year, all care consists of hoeing, weeding and giving the soil a few wettings in case of drought or excessive heat. The following spring, pruning is again carried out on two eyes; the same care is given to ploughing, weeding, etc., and the buds are placed against a trellis, or on the loqne. In the third year, we begin to establish the cords. (The first is assumed to be six inches above ground, the others should be eighteen inches by eighteen inches above). A single vine can easily provide two rows, one on top of the other, but it's much better to form only one, and plant closer together if you want several rows. A vine pruned on two branches is easier to manage and produces better-nourished fruit. The shoot is pruned just above the eye at the height at which you want to obtain the two arms, and the development of this eye is encouraged, as well as that of the eye below, in order to obtain two good buds. To do this, disbud the other eyes if they develop. When trellising, extend these two buds, one on the right, the other on the left, in the same line, to form the two arms. At each pruning, these arms are lengthened more or less, depending on the vigor of the subject, but never more than fifteen to eighteen inches. If you want to raise a vine to form two or three rows of cords, you shouldn't make the vertical buds longer each year; it would be better to wait to obtain a strong, vigorous stem. Every year, try to obtain three new buds on the arms, six inches apart. The first two should be pruned over two eyes, to form spurs; their buds should be trained vertically. The third will be trained horizontally, to form an extension of the arm. The same procedure will be followed each year, until we have obtained arms of the desired length. Nevertheless, we would point out that if you extend them too much, the sap will constantly be carried to the ends, and will abandon the part of the branches close to the stems. The inhabitants of Thomery, who supply the capital with the finest grapes, never leave them more than four to eight feet long. We would point out that a spur should never be pruned over more than two eyes, that it should never provide more than two buds, that it should be kept as short as possible, and that it should never form horns. When disbudding, the young wood is palisaded and supported in a good position, taking care not to break it; all misplaced buds which have no grapes are cut off, even those which would have grapes if they were harmful, or more than two on the same spur. Tendrils are removed, and fruit-bearing buds are pinched off after flowering. When the fruit is ready to ripen, it is gradually stripped to accustom it to the sun's rays, which should color the fruit and enhance its good qualities. We will confine ourselves to this brief overview of table vine cultivation, referring readers who would like to learn more about this subject to the excellent work of our collaborator, Mr. Noisette.
